To Mother 

Her last farewell was spoken when I was but a child still memory of her parting admonition has remained to help me best when help I needed most

OPPOSITES CHAPTER I 

"In the beginning God created Heaven and earth, and the earth was without form and void, and darkness was upon the face of the deep, and the Spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters, and God said, 'Let there be light', and there was light.' 

The late afternoon and early evening of July 22d, 19-, will probably never be forgotten by anyone who, on that particular day, happened to be in the immediate or nearby vicinity of the summer resort of Mount Hope. There are certainly four persons, of whom I am one, from whose memories can never be effaced the oppressive impression of that particular day with its seemingly unceasing, certainly most depressing and terrifying blanket of pitchy opaqueness. Even after our mortal parts are boxed and consigned to that earth, to which God gave light in the period of "In the beginning," yes-even after that, in our four minds must ever remain the horrors of that daytime night, for as the consciousness of the immortals carries with it in its flight to the place of the Eternal Future a sense of satisfaction for deeds performed that merit the commendation of "Well done," so too must it bear the remorse for things left undone and for acts committed and regretted. 

Never since previous to the moment the Great

Creator by just a word rent the hitherto unimpenetrable curtains of night, and let pour through the silver light of Heaven-never until that dark, dark July day 11

OPPOSITES 

of which I would tell has such deep, dark, dense, allenveloping inkiness encompassed this whirling mass of clay on which the human kind is born, lives a span, then passes on to be judged. The deepest pits of hades night would be less void of Heaven's light.

The morning of that fateful July day was born with the promise of a perfect day. The hour of noon had made good the promise of the early hours, for in all the dome of Heaven's blue no cloud appeared to spoil the view. The chill of the mountain air and the warmth of the summer sun mingled in a harmony that encouraged man to walk and hike and climb and feel more of man for having so done. 

Who I am need concern no one. Suffice it to say that 

I am a disappointment. A disappointment because I failed or refused to be that which had been the hope and desire of my father. My mother had not shared in that disappointment, for she had gone early to partake of the reward that awaits a saintly soul. I was without other kin. I had never married. In that, too, I was a disappointment-a disappointment to myself.

I had formed opinions-such opinions as I believed to be well-grounded ones, as to what should constitute the perfect marriage state. Marriage to me meant a harmonious union of opposites. I refer not to the opposites of good and evil; not to the opposites that produce a discord, but to opposites that blend and in the blending make for a more delightful and more nearly perfect condition than possessed by either of the opposites previous to the union. For a space of time some years prior to the "Day-time night" I believed I had found the one ordained by God and destined by fate to be my matrimonial opposite. While I was later convinced that my marriage to her would fall short of a perfect union, still in truth she was but a scant bit removed from being such as nature intended for me. 

Another man married her, but unfortunately for both

OPPOSITES 

13 of them, he failed to hold her. She was not made for him. Doubtless she was not his opposite. I might have married her, had it not been for this other man. Years later while absent from her and without desire or intent, I won this woman who was my rival's wife, but not his opposite. The knowledge of this conquest, unwelcome and unsought, came to me unexpectedly and without warning. The day I learned it I came away from where this woman was. This very unusual experience provided one reason for my being at Mount 

Hope on July 22d, 19—.

I disappointed my father in that I did not enter the ministry. I never wanted to do so. I always believed it better that I should not. I well knew the world must have religion, but in my opinion there was then and is now a tremendous lack of the right sort of religion, of which lack I did not feel that I could remove even the most minute deficiency.

We must have churches. If churches were abandoned, civilization would suffer down to the very marrow. But it is my belief that churches are not doing their full of the possibles, nor are they handled and conducted as I am convinced they should be. 

From the very first time my father had mentioned to me that which to him was the one and only thought he had in mind for my future, there had been a total absence of real enthusiasm in my response. Not that I did not have the desire to please my father—I did.

That is what made it so hard to refuse him. I considered the matter from many angles. Studiously I read my Bible. I attended church regularly. And in none of this did I find aught to make me feel that my place was in the ministry. The more I studied the 

Book, and the more often I observed the work being done by the Church, the more firmly was I convinced that the lessons taught and the instructions given for the carrying to a successful conclusion the one great

OPPOSITES 

object of Him whose inspiration of man brought into existence the pages intended to guide those who would serve Him best were not being followed and obeyed to full measure, and in a manner that could possibly make for a realization of the desires of Him who in the beginning made Heaven and earth, and then placed on the earth man-man, who from the creation up to and including this very moment, has been a disappointment to his Maker.

I could not conscientiously take a place in the religious work shops as then organized and conducted for the purpose of making the bad good, and of constructing saints out of sinful material. Of a certainty I could not, if the prevailing methods of those shops were to continue, for I did not believe in them as being the proper methods. Neither did I believe that in the span of years allotted to me I could possibly change the rule and rote of any church shop. Rather, in endeavoring to do so, I felt that I might blunt some of the tools that were really being used with good, if not the best, results. When one endeavors to revolutionize singlehanded the customs and modes and methods of centuries, the probabilities are more than strong that even if any change might be effected, its nature could not be other than of a discordant sort, producing as its very best but a rift in the tune of a prevailing selfsatisfaction. I felt that there was great need for a change in the managing and operating of the church shop, but I also felt that to bring to pass such a change would require the lifetime efforts of more than one man and of many men such as I. 

I did accede so far to my father's wishes as to enter college. The result of my studying there convinced me definitely and finally that in church work I should prove a forlorn and fatal failure. Then it was that after much argument and considerable discussion my father, disappointed as he was, gave way, for he was a kindly

OPPOSITES 

15 man and he loved his son, but this beautiful paternal loving simply intensified and rendered more acute the disappointment. However, to please Father, I finished college, even though to do so my course of study was changed.

It was while at the University of X, I met "Pinky" 

Williams. "Pinky" was the most likable fellow I ever knew. He was a good student, and in his day was the only four-letter athlete on the roster of his and my University.

CHAPTER II 

I arrived at the railroad station of Mount Hope about noon of what was to prove to be the key day to my whole life, the twenty-second day of July, 19—. 

That day determined and dominated all following days, which days were the only ones that were ever much worth while to me. 

On the night of July 21st, 19-, I went to the railroad station of Mohntown, the home of the woman I had, at one time, believed I might marry. That day

I had learned that she had in some way come to love As she was married to another, I deemed it wise and certainly best that I go away and remain away from Mohntown at least until I had sufficient opportunity to consider the matter carefully, and to reason my way out of a very delicate situation. I wanted to weigh and measure all phases, points, substantialities, possibilities and probabilities far removed from the influence of any person or persons who might in any way be concerned or interested in my problem. So it was that I went to the station and boarded the first train that would take me to a place where I might hope and expect to find none with whom I might be called upon to mingle and who might thereby interfere with my plan of secluded meditation. I too had at least one other good and sufficient reason for leaving Mohntown when I did and going where I did. 

When the train arrived at B. where a sleeper was attached, all boarding the train when and where I did had dropped off at various stations en route. Having already secured my berth, I immediately retired, and was much relieved on the morn of the morrow to find 16

OPPOSITES 

17 among the passengers none whom I recognized. I had never visited Mount Hope before that day. It was a resort seldom, if ever, frequented by any acquaintances of mine. For good and sufficient reasons I had made no hotel reservation. Alighting from the train, I climbed into a bus of Kenneth Inn. It was no chance or accident that seated me in that conveyance.

It was an effect of some force or some forces combined that influenced me to enter the bus that carried me to Kenneth Inn. Nor was it by chance or accident that it was possible for me to secure a certain desirable room, well located as to air and quietness. 

As with pen in my hand to register I had been informed by the room clerk very, very courteously but very, very convincingly that there was no vacancy, a gentleman interrupted our conversation to explain that he was surrendering his room immediately because of having received a wire calling him home. The very gentlemanly clerk anticipated my suggestion regarding that room about to be vacated and offered it to me. It is needless to say that I forthwith did accept that aforesaid room. But it was not by chance that that telegram arrived just when it did. It was because certain forces were at work to so cooperate that I should be at certain places at certain times in order that I might have a part in certain eventualities that must come to pass. 

I was escorted to my room by a colored bell-hop whose appearance seemed somewhat familiar to me, although if he recognized me he gave no evidence of it. 

My trunk had not yet arrived from the station, but I had sufficient in my hand-bag to make me presentable for the noon-day meal. After luncheon, with feigned nonchalance, I turned back the leaves of the hotel register and found no names with whose owners I was acquainted. Fortunately for the plans I had in mind, it was the old style register, so that I was enabled

OPPOSITES 

to peruse the names of all the guests who had registered from the beginning of the season. 

I then returned to my room and to my satisfaction found that my trunk awaited me. I unpacked and arranged my room according to the notions of one who intends to make that room one's abiding place for no short term of days.

Following the arranging of my room I inquired of one of the clerks on duty as to walks, paths, drives and points of interest. I intended to spend much time off in the mountains among the trees where I might be alone, and there think and reason uninterrupted and unhindered by humans who simply must converse with someone, and in some instances with anyone. How strange and how odd the confidence reposed in one by fellow guests of resort hotels. Those there are who must let one have full information as to the beautiful homes they have left for awhile, of their jewels in the lock-box of the home-town bank, of their surgical operations, the pains and joys of which one simply must share and enjoy while hearing of them, of the daughter whose hand is sought by the Duke of Ashtabula, or of the son whose entire time is monopolized by society debs and others of the opposite sex. Such generous souls-these guests of resort hotels, so unselfish in their reposing of confidences. They do so want others to share the joys and the pleasure of their experiences, which joys and pleasures I was obliged to forego, for I had gone to Mount Hope with a hope that I might work my way out of a perplexing situation and for the accomplishment of that task I much preferred solitude and the hikes that would lead me far from the babble of falsities and insincerities. I had at least one additional reason for much privacy. There was a mystery that was mine to solve and to solve it necessitated total absence of diversion. 

I forthwith set out in an endeavor to reach some

OPPOSITES 

19 high mountain point with a commanding view of the surrounding country that I might the better be able to learn its topography and thereby plan for the future hikes I intended taking. Not a great distance along the main road leading to and from the Inn, I came across a small frame building. I observed a sign thereon indicating that it was a source of information for travelers and tourists. I found therein a little old man, who was more than polite. He informed me that the hotels arranged with him to distribute their pamphlets and convey information regarding their respective locations, rates and anything that an inquiring tourist might want to know concerning them. He directed me how to reach a path leading up the mountain side that would enable me to attain the best point for observing the country around. That particular path he explained had also the advantage of being gradual in its ascent, and while greater in distance than some other paths by the fact that it was gradual, it was made less tiresome. 

I proceeded according to instructions of the little fellow and without trouble or delay found the designated path and after a time and without any event worthy of repetition, reached the summit.

CHAPTER III 

And there on a huge rock that marked the mountain peak, motionless I sat, overwhelmed and awed by the view that opened to my eyes. For a stretch of many miles was revealed to my enchanted vision beauties of nature to me hitherto unknown. Above me the azure, boundless and unlimited in its expanse, unclothed and unobscured by even fleck of cloud. To my back, series of mountain elevations, topped with trees, the branches of which reached up and swayed gently back and forth to brush and keep clear and clean the widespreading heavens. From where I stood, high above the trees through which I had climbed in my ascent, I saw below the dizzily distant winding road. It seemed a thread of black silk and the automobiles thereon were as small insects coursing its surface. And still deeper in the picture was the swerving, turning, curving river, twisting its way through the bed of the valley, a string of silver drawn through masses of closely-set emeralds. 

More firmly than ever before was I convinced of the existence of One Supreme Omnipotent Being. I knew that all the beauty above, below and around and about me had not happened by chance or accident. It was nature, the handiwork of the Creator who in the beginning said, "Let there be light" and immediately there was light. By just a thought He had made all of this and more-much more.

He had established laws, among them the laws of nature, which laws cannot be violated or abused without the exacting of a fine and a toll which no man has yet contrived how to successfully escape or evade. 

He established laws of compensation, laws of equal- 20

OPPOSITES 

21 ization, which while possibly hard by some to understand, by some hard to believe, and by others difficult to observe, nevertheless are true laws unalterable and eternal. The compensation for good or evil may take place in a day or in a decade, but "as one sows, so one reaps". Whether the seed be sown early or late, a reaping, like unto the sowing, must come. Since all thoughts, words and acts are duly compensated, so too must there be forces to adjust and readjust, a positive and a negative working as opposites and yet operating to a harmonious conclusion. So it is in obedience to nature's laws that the opposites in sex must join, if the union is to be that intended by the Maker, the Creator, and that desired by the created man. 

Oh, for a long, long time I sat on that mountain top, held to solemn silence by the vastness of the view, and engaged in contemplation and in the consideration of much that had passed within the days and years of the yestertime and of how best to meet the indefinite span of the tomorrow and the tomorrow and the tomorrow. 

Convinced and satisfied as I was that the Creator of Heaven and earth and of all that therein was, is and shall be, had established the unchangeable laws of nature and the laws akin thereto, I knew, too, there must be a God-made law that, if obeyed, would lead me from my problems with their solutions at my disposal.

Pausing in my quest for a law or rule that would untangle the knots and twists of my perplexities, I was startled to find upon consulting the watch on my wrist that I had been on that mountain peak during the passing of more than three hours. I was still more startled and somewhat alarmed as gazing toward the west I learned that much of my beautiful sky was overcast by black, grey and ominous-looking and rapidly approaching clouds. Soon followed the rumbling of warning thunder with its accompanying flash of jagged and

OPPOSITES 

forked fire, while the swaying of the distant trees gave message of rising wind.

I well knew it was imperative that I speedily return to the hotel, but how? To return by the path I had followed to reach my splendid outlook would carry me into the approaching storm. Below, I could just make out the outlines of the building occupied by the little fellow who had given me the information that had enabled me to climb to the spot that I was so pleased to reach and, at that particular moment, so anxious to leave. If I could but make a direct descent to the little fellow's office, as we shall term it for courtesy's sake,

I should be able to save considerable distance and avoid traveling into that dark menace coming out of the west. But would the descent be safe and would I be able to maintain a footing, for the grade was steep and there was no perceptible beaten way of travel?

Without further delay I must decide whether I should choose the way I had come, which would bring me face to face with the storm, and while not as steep as the route directly down the mountain side, yet was of much greater distance and although at the moment more secure, would become less and less so as the heavy rainfall made mud of the ordinarily solid ground. 

However, I hastily decided to use the path already made and traveled and with which I was familiar, rather than try to make my way down through trees, bushes and rocks that I felt sure I must encounter, should I choose the direct descent. But no sooner was my decision made than did the elements reverse it. 

Scarce had I started my return to the distant valley before I definitely realized that the velocity of the swishing wind was more than I could successfully combat, not to mention danger of the dashing dust that rendered good eyes sightless. I well knew that I could not with safety remain where I was. So, counting on

OPPOSITES 

23 the trees for some protection from murderous wind and blinding dirt, I proceeded cautiously down the mountain, hoping that I might be fortunate enough to reach the distant road without mishap, and once there find shelter with the little old man in his information office. Protected by the trees from the fury of the wind, I was for a while successful in making some progress. 

To my temporary relief I found conditions much improved, for the hurricane no longer had direct play on me, the mountain to my rear serving as a shield and protection. I made my way from tree to tree, and was soon felicitating myself on having chosen the direct route.

Then things happened which first annoyed and then alarmed me. The rain was falling as though all of the clouds of centuries past had engaged in a battle royal and in the conflict all had been felled and in the falling all the moisture contained in river and lake and ocean, having been previously caught up, had then been overturned and was flooding and drenching that particular spot of the mountain-side which I occupied and where I was then striving not to reach the road below, but to prevent being carried from my feet and plunged to the distant valley. 

With tragical terror I realized that the merciless mass of plunging water which had so rapidly formed on the mountain top and which was so unrelentingly and pitilessly pursuing me in my struggle for safety, was fast loosening rocks and stones and that they in turn were joining with that terrifying torrent in an assault upon my helpless self. 

Then, following a long, weakening and stamina sapping struggle, I managed to reach a friendly tree and attached myself thereto, fearful even then that some of the storm-mad stones would be upon me and carry me on and down to certain destruction. Over me and about me and with demoniacal delight dashed drench-

OPPOSITES 

ing and devastating water. Soon adding to my terror came tormenting knowledge that my eyes, already sore, stinging and burning could no longer see the light of day, and that all the world was as though rapidly giving way to absolute and heavy blackness. How long I held to that tree I know not, but as I clutched close its trunk I lived through all the years of my memory, as well as suffering the agonizing fears of what the future might hold for me in that mountain dungeon of direful desolation.

As these terrifying conditions became rapidly and intensifyingly worse, I had little hope of soon, if ever, reaching the safety I sought. Then in despair I wondered if, after the elements had taken from me my breath of life and had left me to mingle with the mountain dust, anyone might ever pass along that way and find me there. There were many other things too of which I thought. How long I could hold to that tree and resist the force of water, now knee deep, and the dashing, devastating rocks that were seemingly so determined to crack and crash my aching bones, I could not know-not long-I feared. Then somewhat encouraged by having so far endured the torturing assaults and having been able to maintain my defense against the attacks of those demolishing demons that had so cruelly conspired to bring about my destruction, a new terror took me in its hold. The wind must have grown in the power of its might, for to my aching ears came the sickening sound of falling and crashing trees. Above the thunder I heard the cracking of timber, and with the aid of lightning flashes I could see huge pines, which had stood the storm tests of countless centuries, bend and break under the force of the gale. Limbs and branches were torn from trunks and hurled down and down and down. 

I felt it was but a question of time alone before I, too, should be caught in the power of the fury and seized by

OPPOSITES 

25 the relentless avalanche to become a part of an unrecognizable mass of waste and wreckage. 

The tree to which I still was clinging was indeed a friendly tree and also a sturdy tree, for it stood well the powerful push of the wind and fenced and parried the crushing blows of ponderous rocks and other trees that had yielded to the tempest and were being tossed and hurled and crashed against their fellow trees, trees whose close companions they had been for ages uncountable and unrecorded. Yes, my tree was a friendly tree and I hope it still stands, for I am going back some day to see if I can find it among the other trees that withstood the storm and still remain standing on that mountain side where I had the outstanding sensation of an existence already crowded with thrilling and unusual experiences. 

But while my tree was a refuge in time of storm, the sun was less friendly, for when most needed it utterly failed me. The conspiracy of cloud and dust and rain and wind had overthrown the solar king and deprived him of his power, and as he fell from his throne of light, my tree and I and all the world for miles around became as things were before that time when was given the divine command "Let there be light." My tree stood perfectly the test, but I was as one blind-helplessly, hopelessly without sight. Neither the tree nor 

I could longer see the assailing forces that were being hurled against my strong tower, and it was this failure to see that caused our parting.

Out of the blackness came something hard and heavy which struck my wrist and loosed my embrace from my friendly tree. Then, too, at that precise moment some evilly inclined force swept my legs from under me and I was gathered with the other substances that were being catapulted to destruction. Down, down I went, striking tree after tree and other things too-I know not what. Everywhere about was thick, heavy

OPPOSITES 

and pitchy darkness, thereby obviating any possibility of my seeing some trunk of tree or other thing that I might grasp ere I had passed them by. The distance of descent I could not tell, nor did I know the length of time consumed in my descending. The great wonder was that I was sufficiently conscious to know aught of anything.

I well knew that some time my fall, with all its accompanying terrors, must come to a close. As this thought demanded "How and when?" there came a sudden flash of lightning that lit up the place for which I seemed bound, and then it was I saw that I was nearing the end of my descent, but my almost confident hope that I might yet escape with only bruises as the worst of my troubles was almost immediately dispelled, for brief as was that flaming light it was sufficient for me to see that the mountain did not descend in a gradual slope to the roadway, but that a wall had been built along the side of the road many feet up the mountain side.

All this I observed in the few moments of vision afforded by the lightning. In those fleeting seconds I recalled that I had seen the wall when seeking information from the little old man. Could but my eyes be permitted to function I might yet in some way grasp some stone or tree or part of the wall, and thereby save myself that impending plunge to the roadside so far below, but there came no helping light until at the precise moment I reached the top of the wall; then it was that a flashing flame revealed to me near at hand what appeared to be a large rock. It seemed my only hope. 

Wildly and desperately I grabbed hold of that huge stone, but it was not secure and over the wall, closely clutching the rock, I was carried by the momentum of my descent. As I fell from the top of the wall there came a flash of sufficient duration to reveal to me the figure of a man huddling close against the wall, evi-

OPPOSITES 

27 dently trying to protect himself from the fury of the destroying elements.

I saw nothing else and remember nothing else that happened for a time; for how long a time I do not know. My first returning thoughts were clouded and were concerned with the impression that I was in a boat and that the boat was sinking; that water was making its way into my boat, and that I was becoming very wet. In desperation I tried to swim, but a drowning man was holding me fast and I could not break away, for he held tight to my feet. In panic I frantically endeavored to kick myself loose, but my legs had lost their power. Then came another fiery flash, then a dash of water in my face, and I was able to better comprehend what had happened and the havoc of the happening. Evidently the rock and I had fallen upon the man on the roadside. I was lying across him, while my feet were held fast by a limb from a tree. The man was unconscious. I feared he was dead.

As rapidly as I could I released my feet from their wooden captor. Then I tried to stand, but found it hard to do. I did succeed in relieving the man from the weight of my body. Inch by inch as the lightning illumined the scene, I crawled and struggled until I reached the opposite side of the road where stood the little fellow's information office.

I tried the door, but it was locked. Evidently the little old man had fled before the breaking of the storm. I worked my aching self to one side of the office, hoping a window there might be loose, but such was not the case, for on the side to which I had made my way, there was no window either tight or loose. 

Another flash, and my eyes turned to the riotous, rampant river in the valley below. Whirling, swirling, black and sullen, far had it risen, and while it seemed impossible for it to reach to the road, yet much was it swollen and the volume it had already acquired in its

OPPOSITES 

desire to join with the other agents of destruction bore testimony that it was a menace not to be disregarded or lightly disdained. All the while water poured from the heights above, and the roadway was entirely submerged. What did not remain on the road was rushing down to join the terrifying torrent below.

There was little to prompt hope, except that I seemed to be recovering use of one leg. Instead of being broken, as I feared, I was encouraged to believe it had been only bruised and numbed by the weight that had borne down upon it. With this one relief, I tried hard to think how I could aid the man who lay so still and so white on the other side of the road. I attempted to make my way back to that unfortunate who involuntarily had preserved my life and at the loss of his own 

I feared, for, had he not broken my fall, only a miracle could have saved me. Waiting for further light from the sky to make sure where he lay, I heard approaching what seemed the sound of horse's hoofs. Then in turn from the opposite direction came the warning sound of an automobile horn. The light of the car I could not see because of a nearby turn in the winding driveway. I feared to cross the road, for the automobile might round the bend and be upon me ere I could reach that fallen form. Also to be considered was threatened danger from the approaching horse. The sound of clattering, galloping hoofs beating the mountain road bed convinced me that it was a runaway, for none but a fool would ride a horse on such a day unless it be against one's will. Around the curve to my right came the car's flashing light, which as the car straightened fell full upon the scene, revealing to my sickened sight a madly dashing horse crazily speeding to its impending doom. Upon the back, and with her arms clasped tight around outstretched neck of that fated steed, I perceived a girl with loose flung hair, which twisted and swished to the merciless wish of the relent-

OPPOSITES 

29 less and slashing wind. Ere else I saw the inevitable tragedy was before me. Automobile and mad, wild horse had met. Shrill screams-screeching brakes-a horse's mad snort-a crashing, crushing, crunching thud, then all was still, all was dark again. 

In the return to darkness I could see nothing, and as I heard nothing I feared at least two more victims had been added to the prey of the devastating wrath and fury of the berserk and merciless storm king.

"Revealing to my sickened sight a madly dashing horse..." (p. 28)

CHAPTER IV 

"After the plague of locusts had descended upon the land and the Lord said unto Moses 'Stretch out thine hand toward Heaven, that there may be darkness over the land of Egypt, even darkness which may be felt'. 

And Moses stretched forth his hand toward Heaven, and there was a thick darkness in all the land of Egypt for three days."

Dark, dark, even more dark was that July day I contend than in the time of "In the beginning" when darkness was upon the face of the deep; so dark was it that the "Egyptian darkness" was as a cloudless noontime sky as compared to the blackness of the daytime night of which I am trying to tell; so dark, so void of light was it, that with three humans in need of help, I could neither go to their relief nor summon help.

One of my legs was still as though dead and the other had failed me to such an extent that I was obliged to rest upon a platform attached to the little office, all the while quivering, shaking, quaking, as the merciless water rose higher and higher about my drenched and weary being.

Though helpless almost to the extreme, had I but possessed semi-clear vision I might have done something for those unfortunates. Had I been permitted but the slightest bit of light, I should have summoned all my will and power and have forced my way to where they lay. But in the darkness, total darkness, it was folly and worse than useless to hazard a chance.

No darker could it have been than had the Great 

Creator, instead of saying "Let there be light," have commanded-"Let there be darkness, total darkness,' 99 31

OPPOSITES 

and had immediately proceeded to pluck every last shining star from the firmament blue, than had He enveloped the sun in a shroud of blackest night, closed it tight in a coffin of total oblivion, placed the coffin in a hearse of eternal death, and then conveyed that hearse backward through the centuries to the time when time was not time, before "In the beginning", and then interred it in a grave of pitchy never was no darker could it possibly have been as I lay that evening by the side of that mountain road. Even the lightning had ceased to function. There in the inky black, helpless was I—yes, helpless and almost hopeless and tortured with a realization that succor must soon come or I should perish. As no sound came to me from my fellow victims of the storm, I feared they already had passed beyond need of human help. 

How long I lay with the water beating on me and rising about me and flowing past me, how long it was that I lay listening to the howling gale, the cracking of timber and the dashing of rocks and trees from the mountain above-I have no way of telling. It seemed that it was actual night by the clock as well as by the absence of light, when from a distance I heard the horn of an automobile and the chugging of its engine. Not rapidly, but cautiously it seemed to be approaching, and then after what seemed an interminable wait, around the turn in the road flashed its light. Above tumultuous din of the howling storm, I strove to raise my voice. 

Whether the driver heard me I do not know, but he came to a stop in front of the little old man's office, and as my eyes became accustomed to the headlight's glare I saw that the driver was the little old man himself. Hurriedly he made his way through the water to my side, but I indicated to him where he should find others needing help more than I, in consequence of which he maneuvered his car so that its light fell

OPPOSITES 

33 upon and illuminated the entire scene of human wreck and other ruin.

The horse was beyond doubt dead and upon it was the remains of the car. The little old man with what assistance I could give him managed to extricate from twisted metal and splintered wood the limp form of the luckless girl, one of whose legs was held captive by that heavy horse, while her head rested upon the chest of the poor fellow for whose plight I was responsible. 

After releasing the girl and placing her in the car, we returned for the man who seemingly was crushed and broken beyond all hope of retaining a suspicion of vital spark. Beneath the overturned automobile we found another torn and bleeding form. What remained of the clothing told us that it partly covered what was once a woman, which as tenderly as possible we laid with those other two.

The condition of these three unfortunates was such that none of them could possibly then be recognized. 

Blood, cuts, grime of automobile and mountain soil combined with the darkness to prevent our knowing what features they bore. Torn, crushed, slashed, broken, bleeding and maybe drowned, three forms lay as best we could arrange them in the car of the little old man. Then I climbed, or rather the little old man pushed me, into his automobile. He took his place at the wheel and we were off with that messy mass of bleeding flesh.

As rapidly as consistent with safety the little fellow drove to the Inn. Luckily a doctor was there. Hastily examining us all, he had me taken to my room. As I learned afterward, he gave first aid to the others, 'phoned the hospital in the nearest town to be prepared for them, and then, accompanied by the little old man, rapidly drove to that institution. 

The hotel manager who had assisted me to my room, upon advice of the doctor saw to it that I had a hot

OPPOSITES 

bath. He then had me placed in bed and endeavored to make me as comfortable as possible. Before leaving me, he placed by my bed a telephone, so that I might summon help should I be in need of same. I tried to sleep, but so worn and weary was I that slumber refused to come. I was the centralized location for all the aches, pains and bruises of the entire universe, or at least so I thought. Cramped and sore, in spite of the inclination to toss and turn, I was too stiff to change my position. I was in the throes of a mingled desire to move and yet too fearful of pain to even breathe.

After a seemingly endless waiting, during which time the manager had twice come to my room to inquire as to my welfare, the doctor returned. He carefully examined me, as a result of which he reported that aside from a small bone in one foot being fractured, some knee ligaments of my other leg being torn, my entire being a mass of bruises, I had not suffered any injury worth mentioning, although he added that the exposure to which I had been subjected might produce an ill effect.

In reply to my anxious questions regarding the other three, he informed me that they all lived, but that their conditions were quite serious. None of them was conscious. He told me he would set my breaks and get me in shape for the night, so that he might return to the hospital. He stated that it had not been possible for anyone who had seen the injured to identify any of them, but that further effort in that direction would be made on the morrow.

In response to his inquiry as to whether I wished anyone informed as to the accident, I told him that if I deemed it necessary I should attend to that matter myself. I well knew that I had no intention of acquainting anyone with word of my misfortune. 

The physician then left and after a time I passed

OPPOSITES 

35 into a fitful sleep. I dreamed myself all through the storm and the series of accidents, and must have slept a long while. As the sun was in the west when I awoke, I must have slumbered through what remained of the night into the afternoon of the day following that eventful July 22d.

When I opened my eyes the doctor, the hotel manager and my friend, the little old man, were at my bedside. The former once more revived my agony of body by endeavoring to ascertain whether he had discovered all of my injuries or had missed a few the night previous. He announced that he had no further bad news to report as to my condition, but stated that it would be necessary for me to stick to my bed for a space of time, how long a space to be determined according to how I resisted the effect of exposure and as to how well my breaks behaved. He said he would send me a nurse, but my little old friend let it be known. that he knew enough of nursing to take care of me. I told the little fellow I feared caring for me would interfere with his information bureau, but as he seemed rather touched and hurt by my hesitation to avail myself of his services, I assented to accepting his care and attention. The doctor accordingly advised my new found friend just what to do for me and left his name, address and 'phone number, in case he should be needed. The manager arranged to have a cot placed in my room for my volunteer nurse, and soon the little fellow and I were left alone.

CHAPTER V 

"My friend," I said, after a few moments of mutual inventory, "I do not know how I can ever repay you. 

Undoubtedly you saved my life, as well as the lives of those other three. None of us could have survived the night in that beastly storm. I full well know that it would have been a matter of but a short while before my mental faculties should have failed to function." "Goll boy," he said, "no credit due Doogle. The Big 

Spirit sent me to ye. When I peeked the breakin' of the storm, I knowed no tourist would be askin' for Inn or road directin' and that as everybody would hunt up shelter and keep off the road, I streaked for my little chapel beyond the village. But all the time I was eatin' and readin' and studyin' the Book there was a tuggin' and a yankin' by a Power that pulled and pushed me both toward the door and out into the storm. Goll, but 

I pushed and pulled back, but the Power said, 'And now ye has a chance to practise your preachin', ye prefers to queer your preachin' with no practisin.' But I said, 'Goll, my preachin' be true and I ain't a feered to practise when I knows what to practise. I knows what 

I preaches, but I ain't able to see what practisin' there be in simply bein' a goll fool and goin' out in the dark where it be darker in the daytime than any night time 

I ever seen, and then no chance to practise at all.' And the Power kept sayin' 'If ye got faith in your preachin', just go out and get in your car and go to your office and there ye will find enough practisin' to make up for your preachin'.' Then I done just that and ye knows the rest that be up to last night." 

I was at loss how to answer the little fellow. I 36

OPPOSITES 

37 rather feared he might not be in possession of a sound brain. So I merely said: "So your name is Doogle? 

Is that correct? What is the rest of your name?" 

He replied, "Goll, may be Doogle's all of it, may be 

Doogle ain't none of it, may be Doogle's part of it, but Doogle suits me and Doogle's been enough for everybody else for a long spell, and I be expectin' 

Doogle will have to do ye. That's more'n I knows of ye, my stranger friend. I don't know who ye be and I ain't intendin' askin. Any time ye wants to tell me, I'll listen, but till then ye be just Mr. Friend." "All right, Mr. Doogle, let it be so. You are Mr. 

Doogle and I am Mr. Friend." "Not so! Ye be Mr. Friend, but I be just Doogle.

No arguin'. Goll, I hates arguin'. It gets nobody nothin' good and the only thing that comes out of it be hard feelin's, always hard feelin's 'cept for lawyers. 

They be perfessional argyfiers. They would be starvin' to death, if it did not be for state bodies and Congress; they makes queer laws that nobody understands and the lawyers all sees diffrunt so as they can advise people diffrunt and then go to court and argie for or agin just as it suits 'em to get people's money. I beats ye up today and a lawyer goes to court and argies for ye I had no right to do it. I beats ye up next week and the same lawyer goes to court for me and argies ye got what was due an' comin' to ye. Just let the Big Spirit tell ye what to do an' do it, but don't argie about it." "Your reference to a 'Big Spirit' causes me to wonder if any of your ancestors were North American Indians." "Goll, Mr. Friend, I sees where your religious trainin' ain't had no bringin' up 'tall. The Big Spirit is the 

Spirit that handles everything or could if He be wantin' to. He lets us go our own way and that be where we gets into trouble. That be what I pretty near did last night. I wanted to have my own way, but before

OPPOSITES 

too late had decency enough to know who knows best and knows most, and just did what the Big Spirit steered me to do. Ye see, when we allows the Big 

Spirit to steer us we goes straight, but we so often gets stubborn and stiff-necked and thinks we knows best; then we gets ourselves goin' wrong and that upsets ourselves and sometimes gobs of other folks along with us. The Big Spirit gets some things goin' this way and other things goin' that way so as they meets and by averagin' right and even they both together makes things just right, if we will only just let 'em. If it rained all the time, things would drown and rot. If it never rained and the sun shined all the time, things would burn up. If we never got sick or died there would be no urgin' to take care of ourselves. If we all died young, there would be no 'centive to try to live. 

If we all was good all the time, it would be heaven right away, and if we was all bad all the time, there'd be no hope of heaven and there'd be no use of havin' such a place. "Ye must have two things opposite, a push and a pull, a comin' and a goin', a right and a left, an up and a down, a north and a south, an east and a west, workin' and sleepin', land and water, high and low, freezin' and burnin', purty and ugly, and all the other opposites so as to average things, or the earth would slip off, and if things be too easy we don't 'preciate 'em, and if everything be too hard most of us would stop tryin'. 

The Big Spirit sees that there be just enough push and just enough pull to average right, if we only let it go that way. So last night He pushed me out to go somewhere. Had I rushed regardless I mought have done nothin' of good sense. There was a pullback, just enough to not make me offset the full pull of the Big 

Spirit, and that let Him steer me to the place He wanted me to go." 99 "I think I grasp you to some extent, Doogle, but

OPPOSITES 

39 tell me. Is this the teaching of a particular religious belief that someone has imparted to you? If not, what is the foundation of this philosophy of yours?" "Yes and no, Mr. Friend. There be so many forms of religion today that the whole thing be overdone. 

Churches never was intended to be just buildin's. It is what be done in and by the folks of a church what makes it as the Big Spirit always intended." "You speak of the Big Spirit. Tell me, just who is the Big Spirit?" "The Big Spirit be the power over all things. He controls the push and the pull and tries to make everything for good. There be many spirits all about us now, but there be One Spirit over all other spirits that rules everything here and there and everywhere, and that be the Big Spirit." "I think I heard you mention your chapel. Is that a place where you preach your religion?" "It be an' it be not. I lives there and I tries to have the Big Spirit spend much time there. I consults with

Him and I tries to have people come there and talk over things with Him and me, but I calls it my chapel 'cause I tries to work out my ideas there and it be there I tries to plan the practisin' of religion more'n preachin' religion, although I does some talkin' there too. I tries to get them as will to listen and to see that religion is doin' more'n talkin', that religion is helpin', not hinderin', that religion will make people happy, not sad, if we only let the Big Spirit work His forces and we go along with 'em. These forces, some folks, for sake of a name, calls nature and we knows ye can't buck nature without payin'. It has its rules and regulations and ye has to live up to 'em or ye pays a fine. 

The law is fast and can't be changed or beat. It is like the Big Spirit had hold of levers, one for fast and one for slow, one to go right and one to go left. If ye lets yourself be pushed or pulled by the levers as the Big

OPPOSITES 

Spirit thinks and knows be right so to make a happy medium and to get proper results, all be well. If ye fights any of the levers, ye gets burnt or froze, accordin' to the rule ye breaks. Ye makes up your mind to choose a mate or some female chooses ye for her mate. 

Now, if ye lets the Big Spirit use the levers on ye as 

He knows best, He will show ye whether ye be right or wrong. There be a right Jack for every Jill. If ye lets the levers pull the strings right, ye be bound to get your own Jill. If ye bucks agin' the levers an' picks some other Jack's Jill, there be bound to be at least three folks payin' fines. Ye can't beat it. Ye just must go along with it or if ye tries to beat it, ye spends the rest of your days bein' sorry for yourself and blamin' everybody but yourself. I knows. I bucked the rule, although I didn't know it then quite so well, and I been payin' ever since."

The physician entered my room just then and interrupted the conversation. Doogle's discourse had been most interesting, but the doctor made it quite evident he believed I had been doing too much talking. As a matter of fact I had done little but listen. Doogle, taking this to himself, hastily departed, but said he would return later in the day.

CHAPTER VI 

After Doogle's departure I inquired of the doctor as to just who Doogle was and what he was. "He is a little old fellow about whom no one seems to know very much. He has been here for years. He is known only as Doogle. The hotels pay him for distributing their literature and directing tourists. At times he also acts as a guide. He has a little place he calls his office, where he distributes the hotel pamphlets. He also, unassisted, built a cottage among the trees up on the mountain side. He calls it his chapel. 

He gathers people there and I understand gives readings of various sorts, although he charges nothing for so doing. He believes he is clairvoyant. Some say he is a spiritualist. He answers that by claiming everybody who believes in any religion is a spiritualist. He preaches charity and brotherhood of man. What money he gets above what is required for his actual necessities he spends on those in need. I am told he believes that if man will but let a higher power guide him he cannot go wrong. I have intended going to his chapel and having an interview, but it seems never have an opportunity when he is there. He is a puzzle to all of the natives, but he harms no one and doubtless does a lot of good. There was a time when the boys mischievously tormented him, but his acts of kindness won the hearts of the youths of Mount Hope and they now are his best friends. They bought him an automobile last Christmas and he uses it to take out tourists. I hear he also uses it to carry provisions to the poor throughout the mountains. A peculiar, but evidently a good and remarkably valuable man to the 41

OPPOSITES 

community. He no doubt has an interesting history, but no one has been able to induce him to talk much of himself and not at all of his past.' 

I then inquired as to the injured three. The doctor stated that all were much hurt and would be confined to the hospital for a considerable length of days. None of them was fully conscious when he called at the hospital that day, but he had strong hopes of all living. 

For the man for whose injuries I held myself so largely but not voluntarily responsible, I felt much concern and was quite relieved to receive an encouraging report of his condition.

The doctor said, "No one has been found or has come forward who has been able to throw light on the identity of the unfortunates, and there was nothing among the effects found on any of them to afford any clue. Through the night the man's mind rambled in delirium. I learned this from the night nurse who told me of his dreams, describing them as they were indicated to her by the words he spoke. She said he talked much, forming peculiar or rather unusual sentences, carrying in them and with them a touch of poetical thoughts and construction. "While I was there today he muttered something to the effect that he had come away that all things might be righted, and that some persons whom he designated only as he and she might have an opportunity to obtain happiness. He also talked of a yearning for a taste of real love before it was all too late. Then he lapsed into silence for a spell. Suddenly in a tone of resentful disappointment he screamed: 'But he could not or would not understand. I gave him a free chance, but either he or fate would not let it be so. He followed me in the dark and leaped upon me from the blackness'. "Then, in wandering, nonunderstandable sentences, he spoke of Love. That possibly I might in some way

43 learn something of his identity, I asked the nurse who had formerly been a stenographer to secure a notebook and be prepared to write whatever the poor fellow might say. My hope was that in his dreams or delirium his identity might be revealed, or that by some expressions of speech he might possibly lead us to a more minute knowledge of the nature of the injury to his head. "The nurse informed me that after I left him to see another patient, he resumed talking. She followed his speech closely and I believe she was successful in getting verbatim what he said. Before I left the hospital, she and I repaired to the office, another nurse relieving her. I have here a copy of what he said. Your temperature is normal. I believe it will do you no harm to listen. According to the nurse's notes, these are his words: "'Love-love is what? They all think they know!

Whether one be student or one void of desire for even taste of knowledge, he knows or believes he knows love, but does he really know? The explorer treks far into jungle and forest and lands unknown. Oft, his going is prompted by a fear he has lost a love and hopes that in self imposed exile to learn forgetfulness; sometimes he goes to gain glory which he would hope to share with one he loves, but does that prove he knows love, really knows love? One stays home through fear of losing love if absent he be, but is that conclusive that he knows love? One struggles for gold that he may gain and hold love, but does gold buy love?

One lays fame and renown at the feet of one whose love he seeks to own, but is love by fame and honor bought? One hopes by athletic prowess or huntsman's skill to gain love, but does love bow to brawn of frame or the fleet of foot or to the hunter's gun? Do polish and good manners gain love? No, no, any of these may attract and draw together the sexes; they may be

the lure or the magnet or the bait or the possible means to love, but love they are not, neither do they buy nor hold love.' "He paused, the nurse told me. While the doctor read, I tried hard to follow him, yet my mind was much occupied with thoughts of long ago days, for somewhere in my memory there was someone, yes a someone, who had oft times expressed himself in just such a fashion as had the injured man in the hospital. Who was that man? If I could but bring his name to mind, one of the injured would be

OPPOSITES 

45 identified, I was sure, and possibly friends could be summoned to his assistance. Then another thought, a selfish thought, coursed through my mind. By making known the name of that injured man, would I not be taking quite a risk of his or some of his friends recognizing me? Yet I would, if I could, sink self and serve the poor fellow who had taken my place among the critically injured in the casualty list of Mount Hope.

Then came the nudge of conscience that I had a duty to perform and that to reveal my own identity would possibly jeopardize the cause of one whose interests I was engaged to serve. Weighing these conflicting impulses, I was interrupted by a ring of the telephone bell to which the doctor responded. Evidently the message was an emergency call for him, as he hastily and with little ceremony rushed from my room. 

Shortly afterward Doogle came in. Apparently something of importance occupied his mind. I inquired if he had any news of my fellow victims. He replied. "I has, but it must be waitin' till tomorrow. That doc. let me know he wanted me anywhere else but here and yet he has just been here hisself ever since I left. Goll, but that be what I calls bein' consistent, may be. But tomorrow I tells ye what I knows, although I ain't sure as I knows anything worth tellin' at that."

CHAPTER VII 

On the morrow Doogle departed without disclosing to me any clue that might lead to the identity of the victims of the storm. He went out, stating that he would turn the matter over in his mind a bit more. 

He had gone but a short while before there was a tap on my room door. I invited the tapper to enter.

This he did. He proved to be the colored bell-hop who had conducted me to my room on the day of my arrival at Kenneth Inn. Quietly closing the door, he stood in a hesitating sort of way, holding one of his hands behind his back. His manner rather annoyed me. As he did not speak, I inquired: "Well, what is it?" Seemingly rather ill at ease, he handed me an envelope which he had been holding in his hidden hand. 

On it was written the name which I had used in registering. I handed the fellow a coin from a bag which I had under my pillow. He accepted the money with a polite "Thank you, sir," but he did not withdraw from the room. In response to my "Is there supposed to be an answer?" he hesitated, then said "I don't know, sir, until you read the message and tell me, sir."

Nervously ripping open the envelope, this is what I read: “I am sorry for your accident, my dear (giving my Christian name). I happened to be in the lobby of the Inn the day you registered. I should have greeted you then, I think, had you not gone directly to your room. At the time of your arrival I was stopping at another hotel, but when I learned that you were to be at Kenneth Inn, I arranged that I should have a room here as soon as there should be a vacancy, which was this morning. From the register I find you 46

OPPOSITES 

47 are traveling incog., also that your address is none familiar to me. I derive great joy in ascertaining that you are really you, and that you are here under the very same roof that shelters me. Sorry your accident has prevented our having an occasional hike together. 

I recall vividly our walks through the wooded vales in those long ago years, how long ago it pains me to recall. It must be-how many years? Well, let's not count. But think of the joy you and I have missed, being apart so long, but we simply must do all possible to make it up when once you get out of the toils of that loathsome leech of a so-called doctor. I saw him today and told him I wished to visit you, but he said you were not to be seen by anyone not related to you. Well, we are not related, dear, but I once thought we were going to be and I think you will readily agree we really ought to be. I have it on good authority that you never married, so take it that you always have been sorry you allowed that Burlinson girl to temporarily captivate you and influence you to such an extent that in that horribly candid letter you wrote you did. not love me. (I still have that letter.) Well, dear, when I replied, I told you I was positive we should meet again and that I should feel better following our next meeting. Did not think it would take so long. 

However, I do want you to know that I am here, right at hand to take good care of you just as soon as possible. In the meantime one must live and to live costs money, big money, too, in these resorts, and as I am remaining just to be near you, dear, and to be with you when you can get rid of that old vulture of an M.D. (Makes enough from summer visitors in Mount 

Hope to spend his winters in Florida where he plays the same game so that he can come back here the next summer, sort of endless chain arrangement.) As I am remaining over for your dear sake, and as I have overstayed my time and exhausted my ready U. S. cur-

OPPOSITES 

rency, and as these bonifaces do so dislike accepting checks from unknown females, I am going to let you do for me what I would allow no other man to do, dear. I am enclosing a blank check for just $100. 

You can fill in the name of your bank, sign the check and return it by the boy. You will be glad I am so economical. But we can save until you are able to get around and then you and I, dear, can have our long deferred marriage performed and enjoy life together. If you think it advisable not to sign the name by which I knew you in those happy days in Coletown, you no doubt would prefer sending cash, but do whatever is most convenient and seems most expedient, dear.

Leonore Shelby. 

P. S. Dearest, you will pardon me, I hope, in my claiming that your accident possessed a blessing. Don't misunderstand me, please, dear, for I regret more than any other person in the whole world that you were injured, but nevertheless the tragic happenings of the daytime night enabled me to know what I suspected the day of your arrival at Kenneth Inn. I was quite confident, Darling Boy, as I watched you register that day that God never made another with a stature like yours, so erect, so stately, and so tall-so manly and courageous in carriage and bearing. Still, you were so well disguised that I hesitated to approach you, and while I held back you went to your room.

While I felt sure that my love could penetrate the artificial, and find behind it the idol of my fondest desires, yet it was not until the accident destroyed your make believe that I felt that I should yield to the throbbing urge of a heart yearning for the man I love, which man is you, and to you, dear, I have come. 

L. S." 

I knew who had penned that letter before I had read a line of it, even though I had not witnessed a

OPPOSITES 

49 sample of her penmanship for years. I had not seen and had not heard from Leonore Shelby since I was a youth back in Coletown, before I had finished college. 

We had once lived in the same old town and on the same street. We had played as kids together. I liked her not a little. She moved away while she was still in her early teens. I was sorry to have her go. She could outrun any boy on the street. She could spin a top. She could knock a marble out of the center of a big ring. 

She climbed many a cherry tree and many an apple tree with me. One day she took my jackknife and carved her initials and also mine in the trunk of the same oak tree. We walked to and from school together. She was a nice girl friend and I really missed. her when she went away.

Then followed some years and many days of unrecallable time, during which I did not see Leonore; neither did I hear from her directly. Before I had even word of her, I had been graduated from the old high school and had gone to college. When next I heard her name its sound had lost its thrill, and the regret of losing the companionship of Leonore Shelby had passed, as all or nearly all regrets of that sort seem to do. 

During my college days I had a summer position in a bank. One July day there came a 'phone call for me at the place of my employment. A feminine voice cautiously ascertained that it was I to whom its owner spoke and then said: "This is an old friend of yours; I am not going to tell you who I am, nor will I ask you to guess. If you really want to know who is speaking to you and you are not otherwise engaged Sunday evening next, come to Mount Olivet Church. I expect to be there with Margie Stevens. Good-bye."

CHAPTER VIII 

I had no other plans for that particular Sunday evening except to attend my own church, which plan I could conscientiously lay aside, as I should still be attending some church service, which would satisfy my father's desires in so far as my church attendance was concerned. I admit that had there been other plans, even well laid ones, I might have been inclined to disrupt them. Needless to say that on the following Sunday evening when the doors of Mount Olivet Church were swung open I was in the very immediate vicinity. of that particular edifice.

From the main street the church had two entrances. 

As from within the sexton opened one pair of doors it was with difficulty that I was able to save myself the embarrassment of falling upon him and although he did evince much surprise at the apparent eager desire of a certain young man to be present early that particular Sunday night, he nevertheless exhibited quite much zeal in his endeavor to ease the dilemma of the humiliated youth by cordially bidding him welcome. However, as the night was warm and the church having been closed for several hours was stuffy, I decided not to remain within. So it was that I apologized to the sexton and returned to the open air. I crossed to the opposite side of the street so that I might not be too much in evidence when the owner of that sweetly mysterious voice that had come to me on the bank telephone, made her appearance at the church entrance. I expected her and 

Margie Stevens to enter the door on the right of the church, as Margie's home was nearer to that door. But 

I watched in vain. She did not pass through that door 50

OPPOSITES 

51 -at least not that night. I concluded that I had been the victim of a hoax, and at the particular moment that the church chimes were so beautifully sounding their final appeal the condition of my mind was such that either a religious service was by me much needed, or else that my temperamental self was decidedly unfit to associate at that particular time with either those who preach of or with those who hear of the blessings of the meek and lowly. While endeavoring to decide how best to salvage an evening seemingly so completely wrecked and devastated, I chanced to look toward the other door; as I did so I caught a glimpse of Margie and another girl passing into the church. Whereupon immediately and completely I became as one to whom was due many blessings for meekness and for lowliness, for in the passing of a moment I had turned most meek and exceedingly lowly. 

Without delay I recrossed the street to the church, hoping that no one would know other than that I had just arrived. I entered and as I was ceremoniously ushered to a seat, I saw Margie and her friend. Vainly 

I tried to make known to the usher just where I wished to be seated, but evidently he had other things on his mind; doubtless he had other plans than the granting of my desires, for he guided me past the pew that held the two girls, whose faces without turning my head I could not perceive. Good taste forbade that. As that self willed usher led me by the pew in which were seated those two charming girls so becomingly attired in white summer dresses, I glanced as long a glance and as scrutinizing a glance at Margie's friend as permitted by that inconsiderately rushing usher. 

I take it that Margie was charming that evening, as to be charming was a habit with her, but my eyes never before and never since have beheld such a vision of radiant loveliness as that afforded me by my fleeting glance of Margie's companion.

OPPOSITES 

As the usher indicated the pew in which he desired that I should remain while waiting impatiently for the services to begin and then come to a close, I handed him my straw hat and sagged into the lap of Miss Sallie Jones, the spinsterest spinster in Coletown. 

Without ceremony or ado the usher returned my panama, and with the unsolicited assistance of Miss Jones, who insisted that I had been drinking, I was seated at her side.

What the text was I never knew. I do recall that the choir sang something about the lilies of the field and that "Solomon in all his glory was not arrayed like one of these." To this I took exception. Why, while in the presence of that vision of loveliness I had come to see, should the members of that choir or any other choir extol the glories of Solomon or lift their voices in praise of lilies or any other flower? No lily of the field, of green house or elsewhere, ever bloomed that could compare with Margie's friend. Arrayed as she was that night or any other day or night, it was unpardonable in her presence to sing of the glories of Solomon, of the lilies of the field or of any other being, human or otherwise.

The choir having done its best for the lilies, the preacher having finished his last "Finally", the long

Amen having been sounded, I with all the impetuousness of youth and all the ill manners of one oblivious. of rights and privileges of the rest of the congregation pushed and thrust myself to the side of Margie and Margie's friend. Until that moment I had not recognized that most beautiful of girls. Then she smiled. I knew that smile. It was the smile of the kid who had climbed trees with me and who could beat me in the 100, even though afterward at the University of X, I did win some medals at that same distance. 

It is impossible to describe the adorable gloriousness of that girl. Leonore Shelby, a good looking school

OPPOSITES 

53 girl, had budded and blossomed into a flower, yes, a flower more attractively beautiful and more exquisitely delightful to the senses than any upon which human eye had before or has since been privileged to feast its gaze.

Not too tall, not too shy of height, not slender, far from being even plump. But it is not of form or stature I would tell, but of her face; I defy the world to describe and do justice to that face. Hair blue-black, skin of the olive hue, a few scant shades lighter than the Italian kind, eyes of the night just before dawn, black-but yet a lurking suspicion that under a strong light one might find blue; a proud Grecian nose, mouth with lips moulded after Cupid's bow and full of crimson blood; a chin that just missed being too square. I looked and gazed in stupefied wonderment. Whether I returned the smile or whether utterance I made at all, I do not recall, but I stood transfixed on the arrow by Cupid flung-until I was aroused by Margie's voice, "Don't you remember Leonore?" 

My flight in the skies of enthrilled ecstacy and humble adoration thus interrupted, I returned to commonplace earth. Never since have I been able to bring to mind what I said then or at any time that night. I am conscious of the fact that I was allowed to accompany heaven's masterpiece to Margie's home. We spent the evening together, we three. I have a faint recollection of hoping Margie might be able to at least partly read my mind, and considerately and accommodatingly become too tired to remain with Leonore and me; but of course it was Margie's home and Leonore was Margie's friend and I was there only as a friend. of both.

But afterwards I was afforded opportunity of seeing Leonore when none but the two of us answered "present", which happening during the ensuing summer was oft times repeated. 

Then Leonore went home. I never really made love

OPPOSITES 

to the girl. Why, I do not know. I imagine I was fear ful of breaking the spell. The night before she left 

Coletown she asked me to visit her sometime; I eagerly accepted the indefinite invitation. Then at the station the next day she promised to write to me. She kept the promise; I answered her letter. Then letters became frequent and I was always glad when I heard from her. Often I was at a loss to understand when thinking of Leonore, which was not infrequently, why when she had been in Coletown I had not been more open in declaring for her other than a friendly feeling; then at other times I felt I had acted wisely and well.

One day a letter came inviting me to spend a part of the Christmas holidays with Leonore's family. I accepted. In the meantime she had sent me her autographed photograph with a request for one of me which I in turn sent to her. Then came a letter telling that her mother had placed her in an embarrassing position by one night telling some friends who had called at the Shelby home that my photo was of "the young man to whom Leonore was betrothed." 

Very seriously I had considered proposing marriage to her on the occasion of the Yuletide visit, but that letter produced within me a feeling most strangely indescribable. I recalled my reluctance to mention anything of a serious nature to Leonore during the preceding summer. Just when or how I made my decision 1 do not know, but I did decide not to visit her, regarding which determination I wrote Leonore. To my amazement she replied that many of the people in her town were expecting to have Leonore's fiancé with them, and that a number of parties in her and my honor had been arranged to be held during my stay.

She told me very plainly that she loved me and could not understand what to her was a very extraordinarily strange letter.

OPPOSITES 

55 The very day I received that letter from Leonore there came to me a 'phone request to call at the home of Margie Stevens. I promptly complied and found 

Margie quite ill; in fact, she died within the week. I had not known of Margie's illness, as I had been absent from Coletown for some months and had but returned the day that the 'phone message came to me.

She told me that she knew of my contemplated visit to Leonore's home. She advised me not to go. She informed me that for sometime past her health had been poor, in consequence of which she had gone to visit Leonore, hoping that the mountain climate of Leonore's hometown might benefit her. While there she came to believe that Leonore was not the Leonore of the Coletown days. So convinced was she that Leonore had become a different Leonore that she had made her visit very brief. She said she believed that Leonore was really fond of me; in fact, that I might possess the necessary influence to make her friend and my friend the girl she believed her formerly to be. She was very anxious, she said, to let me know that Leonore was not as she knew I would want her to be. I thanked Margie, came away and never saw her alive again.

In consequence of that interview I replied to Leonore's letter to the effect that I regretted her affection for me, frankly told her that I did not possess a satisfying assurance that I had a reciprocal feeling for her and consequently thought best that we should cease corresponding. 

From her came a most bitter letter, condemning a 

Miss Burlinson, who had done nought to influence my action. She concluded her letter with these words- "We shall meet again." There was no complimentary closing.

Except to think of her at times as the most beautiful woman I ever had seen, I had long ago dismissed her

OPPOSITES 

from my store house of memories. Forgetting that the bell-hop was awaiting a reply, my mind had been reverting to those days of my youth, from which reverie I was aroused by my telephone bell. I answered the call. Leonore was on the other end of the wire. 

Handing the boy another coin, I dismissed him with the information that there would be no answer to the message he had brought. Then tremblingily and hesitatingly I placed the mouthpiece to my lips and as best I could thanked Leonore for her solicitude regarding my welfare and endeavored to make clear to her that I was receiving all necessary attention and that she need not remain at the Inn on my account. 

To this she replied, "But, Dear, who else should take care of you and who can better do it than I?" 

I said "Your question is hardly worthy of reply. I have not seen you nor have I heard from you for years. 

You know nothing of me. I know nothing of you. You may have a husband. In fact, I have no knowledge of a single reason why you assume that you are the one and only person capable of attending to my needs." "But, dear, don't you remember that night in the old buggy when the moon made for us a lovers' day out of night?"

I recalled nought of such a night and most forcibly told her so, to which she responded, "How you pain me, darling. I thought you could never forget that night. But I have not forgotten. To not remember that you must be far from well. You need someone interested in your welfare to care for you, in order that you may be fully and rapidly restored to health."

I insisted that she abandon all thought of ever even seeing me. 

In thinly veiled intimidation came "But my dear boy, no one else knows who you really are. There may be an unfavorable turn in your condition. There should be someone to get in touch with those who

If 

I wish to communicate with anyone, I can easily do so. If I need assistance, it will be close at hand." 

Less thinly veiled the next thrust "But, Honey 

Dear," she replied, "when one feels an imperative duty resting upon one and urging one to perform that duty, one as conscientious as I must do as one sees best, especially when the welfare of one near and dear to one is concerned. I must therefore, as that one, inform the hotel manager just who you are." 

Angrily, I retorted, "Why threaten me? I have committed no crime. I have not fled from justice. I have nothing to fear from anyone. I am here for honorable purposes only and such as I desire to be known to myself alone." "Ah, you claim there are very personal reasons for your presence here, and you further state you do not wish those reasons known. Be that as it may, whatever brings you to this place neither lessens nor alters my duty.' 

Taking another tack I countered, "But tell me why you are here? Why are you at Kenneth Inn and without funds? You too must have a reason and a rather odd reason for coming here to this hotel without the means to pay your way. Are you married? If so, where is your husband? Wire him for money. Why come to me? I may not at this time want my identity revealed, but nothing of which to be ashamed brings me here.

But come, explain to me your penniless presence here." "Ah, I see. It is the lack of desire to temporarily aid an old friend that makes you so undesirous of accepting my assistance and proffered care. 

Once you were fond of my companionship and gave me more than good reason to believe your interest in me exceeded that of friendship. Your letters I have retained because I cared for you. Call me Leonore, Dear, and say that you want me near you.' if 99 "Well, Leonore, when I am able to leave this room, you are still here, probably I shall see you and discuss matters with you.' "Now you are behaving with the courtesy due from you to me. You are beginning to be like your own dear self, but in the meantime I am incurring bills, and as doubtless you are aware hotel managers have a way of being aggravatingly exacting where unpaid hotel bills are concerned. They are hospitable commensurate with the manner and promptness with which one pays. 

When it so happens that one pays them not, these hotel managers crave and demand things of one. They crave and demand one's room, and furthermore crave and demand and seize not only one's room, but in addition all that therein is. Such craving, demanding and seizing humiliates one, and one such as I should not and must not be humiliated by such seizing. To avoid such humiliation as threatens one such as I, where then must one such as I turn but to one's only near friend, such as you?" "But, Leonore, my bank account is in my own name. 

If I give you a check there would be all sorts of opportunities for people here and at the bank to learn my whereabouts."

59 "My Dear, you seem to have turned selfish. Does not that matter of signing checks apply to your own needs as well as to mine ?" "If I must explain, the cash I brought with me was,

When I came here I had no thought of an accident with all its accompanying items of expense. 99 "I am indeed sorry to learn you have grown not only selfish, but that you are exhibiting symptoms suggesting ingratitude. You would send me away, but I am one that never forsakes a friend. Here I stay until I see you.
