I intend to have an officer of the law summoned; you may tell him what you will.

Then we can discuss matters much more satisfactorily. 

In the meantime I hope the management does not become cheap and lacking in the civility due a lady-at least not until you have thrown off your nasty grouch, for which I am satisfied you are not really responsible. 

Bye, bye, dear."

OPPOSITES 

I knew not what to do or think. I could not tell the doctor of this call, neither could I tell Doogle. Yet I must do something. I must keep this woman quiet, if possible. But I must not give her money. That would simply strengthen her position and keep me indefinitely in her toils. I well knew I should hear from her again. 

How to meet her importunities for money I could not conjecture. Margie Stevens had told me that Leonore did hold some sort of affection for me, just to what extent and of what nature she did not explain and I did not know. Should any such condition still exist, the situation was still more to be feared. 

Then, too, that colored bell-hop who brought the message somehow troubled me. I could not place him, but I was unable to dismiss the thought that I had seen him on some previous occasion. I tried to pass him from my mind with the old and familiar expression, "They all look alike to me."

CHAPTER IX 

In spite of my unpleasant experience with Leonore, I must have slept, for I dreamed and in my dreams I saw myself tied fast to a cross and the cross was of stone and much taller and heavier than I, and the cross and I were on the mountain road close to Doogle's office. I looked up the mountain side and I saw my "friendly tree" and on my tree was a crown of gold and somehow I knew that the crown was mine, mine to keep and to hold and to wear, if I could climb that mountain side and reach the tree and take the crown, but my cross was heavy and I was weak and I was tired and the climb was long and steep. As longingly I gazed towards my golden crown, Doogle came from his office, took me by the hand and gave me assistance that I might bear and carry my cross. Then as we two attempted the steep ascent, there gathered around the two of us, Doogle and me, three persons that somehow I seemed aware were my three fellow victims of the daytime night. Their faces to me strangely familiar seemed, but yet I knew them not by name. Then with those three soon were joined Leonore and the black bell-hop. Suddenly and without warning the five with. clasped hands circled around and around Doogle and me and hindered us as we strived to climb that I might gain my crown. As for mercy I pleaded, met only with taunts and jeers, I saw approaching us a woman possessed of a face I could and did recognize—a face pale but divinely pure and sweet-a face that I could never forget, although for years I had been endeavoring to dismiss it from my memory. It was a face reflecting the sweetness, the goodness and the purity of one of 61

OPPOSITES 

heaven's angels. What I saw, the others saw too. As all of us, motionless and still, beheld the approach of that oncoming spirit of loveliness, she stumbled and fell. Frantically and desperately I tried to break that encircling human chain, that I might reach my angel's side, but my cross was much too heavy, and the chain was far too strong. But as I struggled against five antagonistic humans and the burdensome weight of my load, Doogle hastened to my aid, and we two broke a link in that devilish chain, whereupon the black man at the command of Leonore followed and struck me down. 

I fell by the side of the frail creature to whose aid I had struggled so hard to go, and as helpless there I lay I saw Doogle singlehanded chase all the others away; then I heard him to me say: "You have earned your crown. You did what you could. You tried to help her. Come now; your cross is loose. Go climb the mountain and take your crown. It you have earned." "I cannot leave her," I said. 

To this he responded, "But she will go too."

Out of the depth of heavy sleep I was aroused by 

Doogle's voice, "Come, come, Mr. Friend, ye must take this medicine. It be long past due." 

I could feel his hand on my forehead. Then opening my eyes I saw that it was night. He explained that he had been away all day, but felt satisfied that I would have 'phoned the hotel office, had I needed him or aught else. He felt sure, he said, that I would take my medicine, as he had left it within my reach on the table by the side of the bed, but upon his return he observed that little or no medicine had been consumed, and he proceeded to reproach himself for being so long gone. He later explained that he had been applying himself in earnest endeavor to unravel the identity of the man who was hurt. He stated that beyond a suspicion of a doubt he had previously met the unfortunate fellow, but could not bring to mind his name.

OPPOSITES 

63 "If ye be able to listen, I'll be tellin' ye of the poor boy's story. First, though, I must tell ye something of myself. I was one of twin brothers. Where I was born and was reared I be tellin' no folks. Some things in my life I cannot be tellin', for there be a stretch of time that be blank to me. There be three periods in my life.

The first I recalls vivid-like; the second be nigh a haze. 

The third be the one ye sees me in now. Most of the things I remembers most I'd like moughty much to forget. "The happenin's of my second period I be moughty anxious to remember. Well, as I said afore, I was one of twin brothers. We was, however, not much alike in physical bein'. Brother was big and strong and han'some. He was as good and straight as he was strong. 

He was the best man I ever knowed. We went to the same school and was in the same classes. We went together each day 'cept when I was not bodily able to go. 

He fit my battles for me and licked the bullies who picked on my small size and weakly frame. He helped me with my studies. He had been just gradjated from the little old school when the Civil War struck terror to our nation. We was too young to enlist and I was not physically made up to go, even though I had been 'clined to, but Brother told the only lie I ever knowed him to tell. He lied 'bout his age, just a fib, ye know, and they believed him. He was so big and so strong. 

And I went to work in a woolen mill; I 'plied myself and became what they called a wool expert. "The war went on. Once in a spell I had tidin's from Brother and for longer spells I didn't. And then we begun to get word that give us hope the war would soon end, and I was startin' to count on soon seein' my big boy again. And then one evenin' in the springtime of March, 1865, I was walkin' 'cross the old foot bridge from the woolen mill to the res'dence part of the town. My thoughts was of Brother. I was thinkin'

OPPOSITES 

of him and as was often the case as I walked 'long by myself, I talked right out 'loud to him, even as though he be by my side, and as I walked and talked I looked ahead and there from t'other end of the bridge come Brother. "I hurried for'ard to greet him. 'How did you get home, Brother?' I cried. "He raised his right hand and from the 'spression on his face I knowed I must not go nigh to him. Why, I could not tell. "Then he spoke, 'I am here to tell ye, little fellow' (that is what he always called me) 'I be not comin' home. Ye see, the rebs got me today. A piece of shrapnel cut off the top of my head.' "He took off his cap, and Lord, Mr. Friend, the top of his head be flat and I could see inside his skull and as in horror I looked, Brother was gone, and I was standin' on the bridge alone. "After a spell I went home. Father had died durin' the war. Mother, noticin' my looks, thought I be ill and so I was, but I could not be tellin' her why. The next mornin', as was the way durin' those war days, I went with the neighbors down to the railroad station, and on the bulletin board read :' (my brother's name) killed at Battle of "And then I went home and told Mother, and also told her 'bout what I had saw the evenin' afore. "And then she said, 'I often thought I saw my big boy in the nights, but then decided I must be dreamin', but I guess he was there. My lad, I believe his spirit will always be with us. May be we'll see him soon again sometime.' "The next day Mother did see him, and she been seein' him ever since. We laid her away in the old village church yard. My sister was very bitter and cried out in her agony that there either be no God or that He be an unkind God.

OPPOSITES 

65 "I told her that a God what 'lowed me to see

Brother must be a good God, and seein Brother strengthened up my faith in the hereafter promised by God. I searched and digged to learn about the ones as goes afore and tried to 'stablish some communication with them. I been classed crazy, a crank, a spook hunter, a spiritualist, and t' other things, but that don't be harmin' me none. I has had plenty of int'restin' 'speriences and believes I has had communications from Brother as well as from t' others. 

If that be a spiritualist, then I be one, but that don't say I be no Christian. I says the death, res'rection and 'scension all proves that spirits can be seen. I mentions this 'cause it was this spirit matter that bringed that poor fellow out at the hospital and me so close together. It was some spell of years after that March evenin' when I saw Brother, that there come into the 'countin' department of the 

Woolen Co., a young fellow whose name I just don't be able to call to mind. That be one of the things that be passed from me. He was a moughty splendid spec'men of a youth. 

He was fair to look on, too, with his blue eyes and blondish hair and cheery smile. He and I become fast friends. He was an athlete and I liked athletics. That was the first tie that brung us close together. Then one day I told him of Brother and of my havin' seen him on the day he was killed miles away. He wanted to know more 'bout it, he said. . "He, however, scoffed at what he called 'impossibleaticisms' or somethin' like that. More times than I can count we discussed those subjects. He seemed int'rested and was polite in listenin' to me. Had he been any other than who he was I should a either ceased discussin' the subject or else a quarreled with him. He put up many argaments that I was put to for an answer, and that did not help my temper. However, one day

I made up my mind to either convince him or own up

OPPOSITES 

that it was no use for me to be tryin' to get him to see the spirit matter as I did. If the former come 'bout, I knowed I would be havin' a co-believer in my beliefs, and could discuss with him my fav'rite line of thought and study. If I failed, I resolved never to touch on the subject again so far as he be concerned. So on the followin' mornin' I goes to his office and laid the matter afore him in just that sort a light. I told him a friend of mine who be a medium and a clairvoyant and who be possessed of other things what they calls occult powers would visit me on the comin' Saturday afternoon and stay with me over the week end. I asks him if he would have dinner with us at my home on Saturday evenin' and then put the whole matter to a test after the meal. He took up the invitation and, as he put it, the challenge. He of his own accord offered to write for me his story of the strange outcome of that and a followin' visit to my home. I told him I'd be glad to have it in writin' on condition that he did not mention my name. I had several reasons for that, some of which I believes ye will be better understandin' after ye knows the story. I'll read ye the story as told by my young friend."

CHAPTER X 

As Doogle was preparing to read the contents of the paper he held in his hand, I could not but think of his confidence in me, evidenced as it was by his disclosing to me so much of his own life. These thoughts were abruptly concluded by the sound of the little fellow's voice as he read the following: "While employed in the office of a manufacturing plant in southeastern Pennsylvania, a warm friendship was formed between one of the heads of departments and myself. "This new found friend was an honest, open-hearted, generous fellow, older than I was; in fact, so much my senior in years that I oft times wondered how he could possibly be drawn to me and held so fast in the bonds of our mutual friendship. "Upon most topics of importance which we discussed we agreed. There was, however, one subject regarding which we had vastly different opinions. That was religion. "He was a spiritualist. I was a Christian. He took no issue with my belief. He did, however, claim that without disloyalty to either his faith or mine I should and could consistently and conscientiously combine my belief with his. "He contended many times that should I but afford him the opportunity, he could demonstrate to my satisfaction that his belief was founded on undeniable facts. "He contended further that spiritualism was borne out by the Bible and that it supported the belief of the Christian rather than being opposed thereto. His claim was that spiritualism was not a religion, but a 67

OPPOSITES 

belief in the genuineness of certain revelations which he contended had been brought to light as a result of long continued search and research. He claimed that under certain conditions contact and communication was not only possible, but had been established between those still of this earth and those who had passed beyond. "He insisted that I was not fair in refusing him the opportunity to prove to me that at least some of the things in which he believed were true. "For a long while I refused to grant his request for a test. At length, confident that he could show me nothing to change my opinion, but at the same time not wishing to offend him, I yielded to his persistent importunities and accompanied him to a spiritualistic meeting in Philadelphia. "As I expected, there was nothing said or done there to convince me that his belief was well founded, as a result of which he seemed disappointed, but being fair-minded, he stated that he was satisfied that my opinion had been conscientiously formed. "For some months following our trip to Philadelphia he said little or nothing regarding spiritualism, and I concluded, with a sense of relief, that he had decided not to further discuss the matter with me, but in this I found myself much mistaken. "He was simply waiting for an opportune time to offer me what he believed would be proof positive. "He came to my office one day and his facial expression made plain to me that something important had prompted his call. He told me that the next day a friend of his named Choose, who was a spiritualist as well as a hypnotist and clairvoyant, was going to visit him at his home. "He was insistent that I have dinner with him the following evening, and that I afford his friend an opportunity to present some proofs that the belief of the

OPPOSITES 

69 spiritualist was founded on a substantial and reliable foundation. "I reminded him of our visit to Philadelphia, but as I had great regard and respect for him and as I was especially anxious not to wound his feelings, I accepted the invitation. I confess I was also curious to see by just what means he and his friend might attempt to establish their case. "However, I told him that I felt sure he could meet with nothing but disappointing failure, and that I feared my presence might interfere with the pleasure of his friend's visit, but that if he really wished it, I should gladly accept his invitation. "I also endeavored to make it plain to him that I had very little more faith in hypnotism than I had in spiritualism. "I was firmly of the opinion that hypnotism was merely an effect of a powerful mind using its force and influence over a weaker one, and I had so informed my friend during some of our discussions. "The following evening we two left the factory together, going therefrom to the home of my spooky friend, as sometimes in jest I termed him. "Choose had arrived and was awaiting our coming. 

The meeting between my friend and Choose was more than cordial. "Choose and I were then introduced one to the other in this manner: 'Fred, let me introduce my dear friend

Choose who is one spiritualist who you will be compelled to admit is no fake, as you claim all mediums are. Choose, this is my young friend of whom I have been telling you. He is first class in every respect, except that he is skeptical as to spiritualism, hypnotism and those beliefs that we hold so dear.' "Nothing further of moment was said regarding the main reason for our meeting until after dinner, when the matter was again introduced.

OPPOSITES 

"After discussing the subject in a general way for a time, my friend suggested that 'seeing was believing'; that 'experience was the best teacher'; and requested that I afford Choose an opportunity to demonstrate to my satisfaction that what was claimed by the other two as genuine did actually exist. Choose took occasion just then to state that hypnotism was not the result of a stronger mind influencing a weaker one. He claimed that one person could hypnotize his friend and in turn his friend could hypnotize the former. "He then asked whether I would give him a fair chance to hypnotize me. I readily assented and although he seemingly worked hard for quite a long while, he had little or no success. "He stood before me, had me gaze intently into his eyes, made passes and suggestions and even commands, but without avail. "Finally he acknowledged defeat and offered as an explanation that he had not been well for several days and that his sleep had not been restful the previous night. "The subject of conversation changed and we discussed other matters. It was quite apparent, however, that while both of my friends were disappointed, Choose was even more than that he was chagrined.

However, through it all they both were kindly cordial towards me. "The evening soon passed and as I was about to take my leave, Choose inquired as to whether I would afford him an opportunity to redeem himself at a later date. I consented. A time mutually agreeable was chosen. I bade them good night and went home. "The next day my friend and I had quite an animated discussion. I was jubilant, although I tried not to injure his feelings. However, he was just as confident as ever that his beliefs were well founded. He seemed of the opinion that my strong opposition to his

OPPOSITES 

71 views had influenced me to such an extent that involuntarily I had resisted Choose and had thereby rendered his efforts futile. I was satisfied, however, that I had given him every opportunity possible on my part to accomplish the result he desired. "The second date agreed upon arrived and we three again met at my friend's house. "After dinner we retired to a different room than that occupied on the occasion of my previous visit. The doors were closed and locked. The shades were lowered and a chair was placed in the center of the room. "Not a word was spoken until suddenly to my surprise and amusement, Choose, in a firm, deep voice, said, 'My friend, I have been watching you and studying you. You are doubtless clairvoyant. I know it.' "I smiled. He was rather ruffled-the only time I ever saw him in the least other than serenely calm in his attitude toward others. A discussion just a bit warm ensued, the outcome of which was that I consented to afford Choose an opportunity to make a test as to my clairvoyancy. "Seating me in the chair that had been placed in the center of the room, placing my hands on my knees, requesting me to close my eyes, with the further request and caution that I allow my mind to become and remain passive, assuring me that no harm should come to me and insisting that I allow myself to be entirely under his control and to try to remember all that I might see or hear, Choose placed himself behind the chair in which I was seated. "Lightly he stroked my head from the forehead around the sides and down the back of my neck. With a peculiar rotary motion he stroked my eyelids, asking at intervals whether I saw or heard anything. "I replied each time that I saw nothing and heard only his voice. After quite a long time-it seemed at least an hour to me-he again inquired whether I could

OPPOSITES 

see or hear. His voice I heard, but I was unable to make reply to his question, for I was without power of speech. "He continued his passes, touching my eyelids more and more lightly until once again he paused to ask whether I saw or heard. Receiving no reply, he stepped from behind my chair in front of me and took my right hand in one of his, at the same time placing his other hand on my head. "Once again he inquired as to what I saw or heard.

I continued powerless to answer him. He released my hand, and told me later that he had then stood off from and in front of me, at the same time making long passes with his hands from above my head to a point close to the floor. "It seemed to me that I was in the presence of 

Choose, yet not in the chair where he had seated me.

I could see nothing. Yet I was satisfied to be so situated and felt perfectly at ease. "When next he spoke he did not ask whether I saw or heard; he inquired as to what I saw. "At the moment he spoke I seemed to be on the shore of a great body of water with nothing in sight but ocean waves and a coast of rocks. The scene was not familiar to me, yet I was unafraid and not alarmed, and while I saw this great sea and heard the voice of Choose, still it was without avail that I endeavored to make reply. "Then while he insisted that I speak, I saw a large boat seemingly miles away. As I beheld this vessel pushing through those huge waves, my tongue was loosened and I informed Choose that I could see a boat. 66 67 'What is she doing?' he asked. "She was simply making headway, rapid headway it seemed to me, towards the very spot where I stood. 

This I told Choose. As I looked again she appeared

OPPOSITES 

73 to be in distress, for I could see people on deck rushing hither and thither as though in trouble and uncertain as to what to do. "And while I gazed, what had the appearance of a flame shot up from the boat, or it might have been that some signal of distress was being made. "I was soon convinced that there was indeed trouble, for plainly I saw small boats were being lowered, then quickly filled with precious loads of panic-stricken creatures and speedily rowed away. Others of those remaining on board leaped into the sea. "After a time I felt satisfied that all the danger threatened souls had either been saved or lost, and that their destiny-in so far as this incident was concerned had been sealed for good or bad, as the case might be. "But while with a gaze held fast, I looked with mingled wonder and pity, I saw one poor, lone man rush to the stern of the boat and wildly fling his arms in unmistakable despair. "He was alone, apparently the last of all who had been on board, left behind, all alone on a boat whose fate was fixed; alone on the ocean with no hope of succor or relief. "All this I was able to relate to Choose. "He urged me to get the name of the vessel. This I was easily able to do. Although she seemed some distance off the shore, the whole scene seemed as though viewed through a powerful glass which brought everything quite close. "In golden letters appeared the name of that boat. 

I spelled it to Choose, letter by letter: V-A-L-E-N C-I-A. "Choose then insisted that I get the name of the man who stood helpless, deserted, no boat left for him, even if he alone and unaided could have handled one. "Despair was imprinted in deep lines in every

OPPOSITES 

frenzied feature. Not alone the despair that comes to one who knows his earthly existence has drawn to a close. He doubtless realized that. But upon his face, on which for the first time I saw a scar, could be read that he was aware he was face to face with his Creator, that the events of his entire life in rapid succession were passing before his vision. "As his very existence from cradle to that very moment was being pictured so vividly, it was very evident that the recollections were not satisfying and pleasing to him, to him who had lived and who was about to receive wages for the result of his labors. 

Plainly I could see that the memories were not such as would soothe and ease the soul of one about to cross the great wide gulf. "Oh, the anguish of that distorted visage, not the horror of cringing coward, but rather realization of the fatal failure of the course he had selected to live, leaving him in the finality of his chosen career without opportunity to redeem the soul he had so deliberately and wilfully sacrificed for the pleasures of the world and the satisfaction of following the promptings of his own will. "And while Choose still urged that I secure his name, this wretched fellow raised high his hands above his head and with a shrill shrieking cry, a cry not of mortal fear, but the wailing cry of a lost soul, he flung himself into the surging sea and sank from my sight 'neath the bulging billows. "As the man faded and was lost to my vision, I heard Choose demanding, 'But his name! name!'

Both of my friends were most anxious and much concerned until I assured them that I felt once more in a normal condition. "I was convinced that hypnotism was genuine and admitted it to the two men. That subject was quickly dismissed for a discussion of clairvoyancy. "I contended that Choose had influenced me while I was hypnotized to imagine that I saw the wreck; in turn he insisted that either I had seen something that had actually happened in other days, was happening as I saw it that night or would happen; either that a wreck of past days had been repeated before my vision, that I had peered across miles of land to the ocean's shore and had seen a ship go down and that as we sat there in our friend's comfortable home human beings were sinking 'neath icy waters, or that, as he said, what would be still more remarkable, it might be that I had seen into the future, and that unless the wreck of the Valencia was then a matter of history, that at some future time it would occur just as surely as we three lived and breathed and conversed at that particular moment. "We argued the matter back and forth, and while I believed that the experience of the evening was all due to hypnotism, Choose still insisted that clairvoyancy had presented to me an actual occurrence, the loss of a boat, her passengers and her crew. "He urged me to follow further the investigation of clairvoyancy. This I did not promise nor did I intend to do. "Being thoroughly warmed and the hour being late, I bade them good night and went home, Choose insisting that both of us visit him at his home in Philadelphia.

OPPOSITES 

"We two did go to see him once, and while on the occasion of that visit he made further tests, he was successful in accomplishing nothing that impressed me as did the loss of the Valencia. In justice to him, however, I should say that some of his tests would have appeared remarkable had they not have been overshadowed by my former experience. "Soon after our visit Choose went west, I was told, and I have not seen him since. "Years passed. In the meantime I had changed my residence to another town. "One day in my new home while reading a morning paper at my breakfast table, the following caught my eye:

PACIFIC STEAMER STRIKES THE ROCKS; MANY LIVES LOST 

Thrilling Scenes When Passengers Attempt to Leave the Wreck in Boats-125 Remain in Great

Peril of Being Drowned 

Victoria, B. C., Jan. 23-The Steamer Valencia, from San Francisco, with ninety-four passengers and a crew of sixty, went ashore at midnight last night during a thick fog and a large number were drowned when attempting to leave the ship.

The Steamer is on the rocks against a high cliff, and is likely to go to pieces at any time. One boat's crew reached Cape Beale at 3 o'clock this afternoon, and nine men got ashore near the telegraph hut, about fifteen miles from the lighthouse.

The men are prisoners on the face of the cliff near which the steamer went ashore and cannot get up the cliff, or return to the wreck. The sea will probably reach them when the tide is high. 

The men report terrible scenes. One woman dropped

OPPOSITES 

77 her child into the sea when trying to hand it to her husband who was in one of the boats.

When the boat's crew left there was a little boy running about the deck crying for his mother, who was among the drowned.

There are still about 125 persons on the wreck, with almost certain death staring them in the face.

The steamer Queen City which arrived here at 4 o'clock from San Francisco landed her passengers and left at once for the scene of the wreck.

The steamer Queen City left at midnight on her regular coast cruise and should reach the scene of the wreck in a few hours. Urgent messages are being received for assistance. "I immediately wrote my old friend, enclosing a clipping of the account of the wreck of the Pacific steamer. What I wrote I do not now recall. "I also showed the newspaper article to different persons to whom I had previously mentioned my vision of the wreck of the Valencia. As I had given them the name of the boat when telling my story, and as some of them had made note of it per my request, they were of course much surprised and considerably amazed. "While I do not have a copy of my letter to my friend, I preserved his reply to me, reading as follows: 

Dear Fred:- Pa., Jan. 29, 1905.

I received your letter while I was reading about the wreck in the Ledger, and wondered if you had seen it. 

I remember the test you gave us that day and also the name of the ship. Do you remember the man's name that jumped overboard when you got the chill? 

You called him Ralph. If you had not got that chill,

While I was indeed much interested in the narrative as read by Doogle and as I so informed him, I confess I was rather more abrupt in my departure from discussion of it than I ordinarily should have been, but the fact that the name of the man who had penned that to which I had been so intently listening was Fred far over-shadowed all else, for Fred was "Pinky" Williams' baptismal name. "Doogle, are you quite sure that your young friend was not Fred Williams?" "No, his name was Fred, but not Fred Williams. I be sure, moughty powerful sure of that." "Can you not in some way communicate with the mill where you were employed, and thereby get some clue to Fred's identity?" I inquired. "I don't be able to get word from mills that don't be. I read long ago that the mill owners had failed and a spell later that the buildin's had been destroyed by fire." "But do you not know someone who was connected either with the institution or someone else who knew this man Fred to whom you might write? I does know that what I just be readin' ye be the writin' as give me or rather sent to me by the very same poor boy as be lyin' in the hospital. I has not seen him for a spell of years, and I be unable to bring to my mind his name, for as I be tellin' ye one day the second period of my life drove out of my head a smart bit of the first period, 'specially in ways of little details and where names be concerned. If I could but once hear the name, I believe I could answer definite as to whether it be correct."

I had been striving to the utmost to connect the individual of my former acquaintance, that clean cut young athlete, whose conversation so often carried with it a trace of the poetic, with this Fred of Doogle's. 

All this was prompted by the hope that Doogle and I might in some way gather up some missing threads, which he and I might knit into a finished fabric that would establish the identity of the wounded stranger. 

With the urge of this hope I asked Doogle if his young friend did at times reveal the poetic peculiarity which to me had been so characteristic of Fred Williams. 

To this he replied, "I knows moughty little of potry, but there comes back to me that he had at spells a dreamy look, and when he be wearin' that far away 'spression he talked diffrunt from ordinary folk. He was an odd sort. He 'peared sad in specks, although he was to all 'pearances a man who really had nothin' to worry about. He was a physical wonder. He had been a pert athlete, so the town folk said, and still was when I knowed him, but whether he be a poet I ain't sayin'." "Was he blonde ?"

OPPOSITES 

"Yes, he was. He had a 'normous bunch of light hair, which he combed, as we called it then, pompydore. He had blue eyes set some deep in his head. 

He had a pink and white complexion which any girl in that time would been glad to own at a big price. Today they gets the same thing at small cost. His strength was the talk of the whole mill. He could lift a moughty big case of woolen goods and with a toss throw it on the express wagon. He could take two men, one in each hand, raise them until his arms be stretched out straight and then grad'ally raise his hands above his head, still holdin' the two men, and then with ease and purty grace lower them to the ground or floor as the case mought be." 

That was a favorite stunt of "Pinky" Williams, a splendid fellow who attended college at the same time I did, although not a member of my class. "Tell me, Doogle, are you absolutely sure his name was not Williams?" "That don't sound like his name. No, it was not Williams." "Are you sure they did not call him 'Pinky' Williams?" "No, no, never heard him called 'Pinky', and his name was not Williams."

I was disappointed and yet relieved. If he should be "Pinky", my duty was plain. I could do nought but endeavor to communicate with his people; I was relieved because to have made known "Pinky", would in all probability, have led to the disclosure of my own identity, which I was most anxious to conceal. 

Mingled with the memory of the strange story Doogle had told was the impression that my little friend's peculiar dialect was not natural with him. He had wandered far from it at times in telling of his associations with Choose and his young friend, Fred.

Time passed, as time has a way of doing. The vic-

OPPOSITES 

81 tims of the accident had physically improved, although mentally they had not fully recovered. Although conscious of all the happenings of the present, they appeared to be totally helpless as to giving the least bit of information that might lead to their identity or connect them with anyone or anything of the past. If the hospital authorities had been able to learn of their family relationships, they would have discharged the patients and released them in charge of those whose obligation or desire it might have been to care for them. 

As it was, they placed them in an institution nearby where observation could be made. It was an institution semi-private and yet receiving an annual appropriation from the county and also gifts from individuals. There were two large main buildings, one for males and one for females. All this I gathered from the doctor and from Doogle.

My condition had so much improved that, aided first by a crutch and later by a cane, I was able to move about my room. I might have gone out and about the grounds, except for two things. There was a condition. of my ankle that caused severe pain whenever subjected to the outdoor air, no matter how warm the day. The other reason was that I wished to avoid any possibility of meeting Leonore. When I should leave the Inn, it was my desire that neither she nor anyone should know my destination. I had more than one reason for this. On several occasions the colored bell-hop had brought me additional notes from Leonore, asking for money. I endeavored to make it clear to her that I had little available cash with me, explaining that my stay had of necessity been long prolonged beyond my original intentions, and that the delayed departure had, in connection with bills incurred on account of my injuries, well exhausted my ready money. I did not tell her that I had been obliged to make my identity known. to Doogle, and that without his imparting any

OPPOSITES 

knowledge to anyone as to my whereabouts he had been able to establish for me a line of communication which provided me with a source of revenue. 

If from day to day I were to pay my way it had become necessary that I must repose a certain amount of confidence in someone, and I was fully satisfied that I could trust Doogle. After a time, not having had further communications from or with Leonore, I was quite pleased with the thought that I had convinced her that she could hope for no financial aid from me, and I was relieved in that apparently she had abandoned all hope of successfully victimizing me.

CHAPTER XI 

Then came the time when I was able to sit at my bedroom window and watch the passing automobiles, observing the tourists who arrived in their private cars, and the vacationists who came in the hotel bus. 

It was interesting to watch them and conjecture as to the respective stations in life they occupied. Many other things too passed through my mind as the panorama of humanity presented itself to my eager view. I was most desirous of keeping open an eye for guests whom I might recognize and who might recognize me. 

It was imperative that I avoid all such persons and to do so I must do all possible to prepare for the day when I should leave my room and make my appearance in the public places of the hotel. 

One day, as I looked out through one of my room windows, thinking little of anything in particular, my eyes were drawn to a fluttering handkerchief up among the trees on the mountain side across the road. Undoubtedly some human being was controlling the movements of that tiny piece of white. I believed it to be a female who was holding forth that handkerchief, for its texture was of a lacey sort, such as not used by men. I felt sure too it was all done to attract my attention.

As a test I withdrew from the window and gained a position from which I could see but be invisible to the waver of that bit of white, which thereupon became stationary. Returning to my window seat the handkerchief became quite animated; then as it was slowly waved back and forth its motive power made her appearance. Undoubtedly she saw me; neither can 83

OPPOSITES 

it be questioned that she was quite aware that she was the object of my gaze. It is impossible to adequately describe that woman. Not too tall, not too shy of height, not slender, far from even being plump, but it is not of form or stature I would write, but of her face. 

I defy the world to do justice to that face. Hair, blue black, skin of the olive hue, a few scant shades lighter than the Italian kind; eyes of the night just before dawn, black, but yet a lurking suspicion that under a strong light one might find blue; a proud Grecian nose, mouth with lips moulded after Cupid's bow and full of crimson blood, and a chin that just missed being too square. The morning sun shone full upon her features and presented to my view the most beautiful face I have ever seen, the most gloriously adorable face that

I believe has ever existed. Yes, it was the same face that years before held me enthralled on that Sabbath evening in Mount Olivet Church back in my own home town. Yes, it was the same face, yet the sun with its penetrating rays revealed to me that there had come a change. With passing years had come little change in form or tint. Time had wrought neither wrinkles nor semblance of crow's feet, yet there was apparent a change. That face was-what? Was it more worldly wise? Was it a mirror of experience? I could not tell and yet with all its undimmed and undiminished beauty, truly there had come a change. Leonore was little altered in feature and form, yet in some very evident but indescribable respect she had changed much. She watched me, apparently to learn how I should take this interchange of optical estimation. If I could have withdrawn from that window I should have done so, but I confess that I was held as one transfixed. Many times have I prided myself that the lures of the opposite sex held no sway or influence or power over me. I could and oft times did laugh designing womanhood to scorn, but that day it was beyond my strength of body or

OPPOSITES 

85 power of will to move my gaze from the face of Leonore Shelby. And no bird was ever more firmly held by hypnotic power of feline gaze or serpent charm than I was made captive by the mesmeric control of that entrancing and bewitching beauty who among foliage of mountain green, lighted by glows of early sun, came forth to enchant me with power of her charm.

How she was dressed I never knew, for it was the face, so perfect of mould and by nature delightfully tinted, that obscured all else, and held me with eyes gazing into her hypnotic orbs. Whatever she wore it was white or light of shade; I do recall that her head was uncovered. That is all I know of her apparel, and 

I am not sure that I know that. Once again she waved the handkerchief, smiled and disappeared among the mountain trees. She was gone, and it was with keen regret that I saw her go. Only once before in my whole existence had I been so attracted and held fast by female charms. That was when I first beheld Leonore, after she had become a woman. 

Yes, I regretted her going, yet I was glad in her departing, for by it I was afforded some opportunity to lay my plans for the battle which soon would be on, for a battle I well knew was to be mine without asking, only to be averted by my surrendering unconditionally and without a struggle. Between Leonore and me war was inevitable. She meant to conquer and subdue me. 

What her terms of conquest might be I knew not. What she intended demanding for peace was mere guesswork. 

She had spotted her prey, and no earthly power could turn her back from the attack. On my part I would not miserably and cringingly surrender, for I was not a coward and would not give way in any cause where I felt that I was right and my cause just. I felt also that a surrender weak and meek would but subject me to further demands; yes, I knew that a battle could not

OPPOSITES 

be averted, but I determined that no matter how long drawn out it might be, the contest must be to a definite and final conclusion. Quite well I knew that I should be strongly tried. That woman had many weapons of attack. She well knew I desired to hide my identity, which knowledge she could use as a weapon of deadly power. She held a charm and beauty of physical al- 

Îurement that it would be mine to combat, to which lure and bait I must not fall. That might be hardest of all, for I was but human and countless humans have yielded to attractions less enticing than those which would be mine to resist. I must be cautious; I must be courageous; I must be politic; I must be as wise as possible; I must be brave; I must be prepared to parry and give thrust for thrust. I must be strong. I mus be in control of myself and of all my passions. I must resist and withstand this woman's attacks with just sufficient defense and counter-attack that I should not arouse her to the reckless abandon of charging wildly against my fortification of strategy, for to destroy me she might be willing to sacrifice even herself. I could not afford to arouse her to desperate deeds. I felt that to such an extreme she might go as to pull down upon me the pillars of the temple, even though in the doing she buried herself in the ruins. I must be wise as a serpent, harmless as a dove. I must be a soldier, a diplomat, a strategist, seeking for peace and yet unyielding in honor. Of all this I thought while I waited for her coming, for I knew that some time she would come. I thought she would come soon, but I neither saw nor heard from her again that day.

The next day the expected did happen. Came a knock on my door. In response to my invitation to enter, the same bell-hop who had previously brought word from Leonore appeared within. Once again he was her messenger. He handed me a note from her, asking if she might see me after dinner that evening.

OPPOSITES 

87 Doogle had informed me that he would not be with me that night, as he had arranged for a meeting at his chapel. I knew Leonore was determined to see meif not that evening, then at some other time. Quite well aware was I that sooner or later I must meet her face to face-yet I hesitated and was inclined to spar for time. Finally, as one who resigns one's self to an inevitable, I penned a message to the effect that she might call for a short while, a very short while following dinner. Through the day I planned what I should do, what I should say. Following each succeeding plan to forestall whatever method of attack Leonore might adopt I tore down that which I had builded as a defense against assaults of the beautiful battler and built again, then once more demolished and rebuilt. Thus did I engage the day and when eventime fell I had no definite plans laid to offset the campaign of Leonore. 

I had eaten my evening meal, the dishes had been removed from the room and then my telephone rang. I knew it was she who called. "May I come now?" soft and strangely irresistible was the inquiry.

My reply was tart and no sooner uttered than regretted, for I knew I must not antagonize that scheming beauty, that is, if possible to avoid it. 

I said, "If you must come and invade my privacy, your unbidden visit might as well be now as tomorrow or next week or any other time." 

She parried my thrust with "Thank you, Dear, I'll be with you before you have time to change your mind."

I knew that she, the most beautiful being I ever had known; she with whom I had played in childhood days, then met at the enchanting age of budding womanhood, that she who in defiance of passed and passing years was still possessed of the most ravishing charm of face and form that my eyes had e'er beheld was at that

OPPOSITES 

moment on her way to my room. And how I dreaded her coming!

Came a tap on the unlocked door, which was slowly opened without awaiting an answering welcome. Leonore entered. She closed the door. She stood before me. 

How long she stood there I know not. What mingled, conflicting emotions and sensations passed through my throbbing, sorely perplexed and puzzled person it is impossible for me to describe, nor would I if I were able. I was high in Heaven. I groveled in hell. I was prompted to be brave. I was tempted to yield all, everything, honor, self esteem, and respect.

Í strove for discretion; I cared not for consequences; but this does not describe nor can it tell through what my tortured soul was passing, as with unchanging stare I beheld the most enticing, alluring, tempting human being God ever suffered to live and breathe. I wanted to be strong. Quite well I knew I must be strong. I had lived a life clean and wholesome. I was a bachelor and had never yielded to blandishment or charm of the opposite sex, but before me lay the hour of my supreme test. I knew it. I felt it. Satan had taken me to his highest pinnacle of temptation and lure. He whispered. that if I would but take that woman as my own, mine she should be, for he would give her to me. It is beyond power of human pen or tongue to tell the full of the maddening waiting for that devastating siren to speak and end the tearing, breaking, crushing, splitting suspense that almost had undone me. There she stooda woman of human flesh and blood-moulded by the devil-fashioned after the glories of an angel.

Oh, I cannot tell how long it was, but as there she stood the light fell full upon her. Older, but still more beautiful than when last I saw her in Coletown; her hair seemed more black, her eyes more of the night. 

Her lips were of the crimson, lips that in audible tones

OPPOSITES 

89 respond not to warm passion's call, yet plainly tell of the surging urge of the throb of the heated heart. 

She wore a gown of silver and white. It was soft and it was clinging, and in the white light of the room was revealed a form such as could only be owned by such a one as claimed and possessed that perfect face. 

And I was alone in my room with that creature. No one else just she and I were there. 

A vision of celestial splendor glowing in the caverns of yawning hell was dispelled only when that fiendish angel spoke.

She said, "Dear, we must not be disurbed, for there is much to be discussed. We must not be interrupted." 

She turned the key in the lock. I was prompted to dismiss her from the room. Yet, while I was prompted, the prompting I did not heed, for I feared to arouse in her the slightest bit of ill feeling, for such a woman scorned, with wrath aroused, would have loosed from me with the blistering fire of her heat of hate all that I might possess of favorable reputation, gold and good standing. Further than that I must confess that I somewhat tingled with the thrill that came with the tight locked door. Then, then came to me Leonore, and while 'tis said that "distance lends enchantment to the view", the closer came that vision of loveliness the more entrancing to the eye was she, and less was the power I possessed to resist her. I could but hope that some power, some force, could and would rescue me from her and from myself. I had not risen from my chair. I wanted her to believe that I was incapable of leaving my room and be seen with her in public places of the hotel; then, too, I did not desire to show overmuch pleasure in her coming. But she waived all formality and if she saw the slight she passed it by, for with a smile that revealed two rows of white, even teeth, she drew a chair nearby and faced me. "My dear," she said, "it is good to see you again

OPPOSITES 

and to be so close, just you and I. Turn your face just a trifle to the right; I want to get the proper light on you. I cannot tell whether time has been good or not to you. Your hair has withstood the snowstorm of time; your eyes are clear as of those other days. Your color is good. You have not taken on the fat of the idle, and yet there is that which tells me that for you the sun has not always been shining. You are wonderfully free from wrinkles or lines or anything giving suspicion of close approaching age, but yet there is that which bespeaks trouble met and fought and yet not entirely subdued. From what I gather you have never married, be that true ?" 

In assent, I slowly nodded my head. "And, Dear, may I hope that in spite of that letter you sent me, you still wanted me and waited for me?" gave no answer.

"Why, darling boy, I never blamed you for that letter. That Burlinson girl, jealous cat, was responsible.

I know it. And she had no claim. She had never felt the press of your lips. No iceberg ever welcomes the sun. And you, oh, well, in those other days you were just a big, bashful boy. You knew a pretty flower when one you saw and you liked the honey that therein lay, but the flower you would not pluck and the honey you would not take. It was only when at times you drew closer to the bloom to gain a better view, or to inhale its fragrance, that if a kindly swaying breeze was kind to you and gently blew the pretty flower to your embrace that then, perchance, of the honey you would sip. 

You found joy in the sweetness of the honey, but it you would not seek. It must come to you. And that 

Burlinson girl, I am sure, was not that kind of a flower. 

I think of her as a snow ball that grew on a tree. Cold by nature and jealous of the flowers that the Master 

Artist had painted with brighter hues. Your nature was such as could not be easily won by her. But she in

91 her own way did influence you, and she, not you, was and is responsible for robbing you and me of our love time of life. You have waited, Dear, I know you have waited. for this day." 

Then I spoke. "Leonore, I fail to understand just what meaning you would have your words convey or what motive prompts them, but first let me correct you in one statement you made. Miss Burlinson did nothing whatsoever to influence me in any way concerning you. I did not understand it then, but tonight I know of a certainty what severed our close acquaintanceship. Since 

I last saw you I have learned that if there is to be happiness between the sexes, both individuals to a union must be guided by forces that bring the right ones together. They must be opposites and when they meet, if both are patient and willing to wait until the proper unmistakable time arrives, then their own feelings and sensations will tell beyond doubt or dispute whether they are the opposites that by uniting will make for a harmonious and perfect union. And then I related to Leonore what Margie had told me of her. "I see it all now. She wanted you for herself. I thought as much many times, and when she could not have her way, dying as she was, she kept us apart.

If your theory be true, you are a negative and I am your positive. Dear, nature designed you for me, but I should have been the courting one instead of waiting for initiative from you.' 99 Drawing her chair closer to mine, she seized my hand and held it tight. Her flaming orbs blazed through to my brain and set all my thoughts aflame. 

Weak and faint from head to toe-nail most remote was

I, and it is well that I was so, for, if physically strong I had been at the time, moral resistance I fear might have lost. But the strange, strange pain and pleasure's thrill that coursed my bursting veins I cannot well explain, nor will I attempt to tell. Doubtless this siren was cognizant of my softening to her spell. To meet this, as well as weakened will could do, I resisted her graceless grasp, but my will to win had lost its urge and become a spongy mass, and, but for the absence of vital spark I had left on the mountain side, I might have succumbed to the lure of her gaze or been swayed by enticing caress. With moral resistance a broken thing, 'twas well I was still out of health. As strong men of all ages have weakened to their fall, so might I, and once coming, my fall no ordinary fall would have
