been. But for the time my physical weakness was my moral strength, and for the nonce from falling I was saved. My temptress plainly showed her displeasure and disappointment in that I had resisted the snare of her lure. However, with strategy changed, she quickly returned to attack. Even now I can hear her say "Dear, so you are growing old in spite of the lack of gray. Can it be you never married for want of impulse and lack of passion that makes a man a mate for his woman? Can it be your body's blood has flowed in channels chilly, or be it true it has grown cold through years of sluggish flowing? Are you a bachelor from choice or nature? At times I hoped and really believed you still awaited my coming, but it seems quite plain you were not made to love or to engage in the art of mating. If that be true, then all is answered."

The implication stung. Waxing wroth, I was about to retort, for the challenge was plain and I had pride. 

But then my better judgment, or was it the forces, came to my aid. Judging well that her aim was either to bring to pass my loss of sanely thinking, or else to taunt me into the desperate desire to prove false her innuendoes, I once again was saved. Although she fought well her planned attack, so far I had builded sufficiently strong my fortifications of defense to hold her well at bay. Then her strategy was changed, for in those eyes of brilliant night a misty field appeared, then slowly down one velvet cheek coursed moisture of a tear. 

In broken tones she sobbed: "And have I waited all these years for this? And from you for whom I cared? 

I who through love have tried always your whereabouts to know; I who when one time I learned of your being ill was much concerned and for your recovery prayed; I who when I heard of your success derived joy from your accomplishments and was proud of all you did, and who when by fate it seemed that

OPPOSITES 

95 together we had come, and that the noon day and the afternoon and the eventide of our lives were in happiness to be spent as one, I drown my pride, my woman's pride, and here in a humble way I cast myself before your feet, full of faith and in belief that you do care and that only by mistake were you kept away from me, and that you never entered married state because there was but one for whom you cared, and that I was that only one; and then, then after all this, when I come to drink my cup of bliss, to take that quaff so long delayed, so long denied, you snatch that glittering goblet of gold, that vessel so pure and so rare, fashioned and finished by love touch of Venus and by 

Cupid filled to the brim, yes, you take and you tilt my beautiful cup and love's nectar you spill on the ground.'

She wept-most copiously the woman wept. Of a truth it seemed her tears of grief must have flowed from a source of lost love. Still, I could not so believe. Yet her tears seemed so genuine, so real. "Leonore, I am sorry. If you really do love me, it is unfortunate for you and for me, for it all seems quite contrary to the prompting of my theory. Leonore, you are beautiful; you are the most beautiful creature that ever has feasted my eyes. Your charms would make most men surrender much; many men would give all they possess just to have and to call you their own; but when I love I must love only her whom I would make my wife, and my wife must be the creature of the forces that make for a happiness that comes out of nought but a perfect union. I have my theory, and as yet I have had no reason to change my views and belief regarding it. If I were seeking a wife whose beauty I wanted to flauntingly parade and whose charms I wished to use for delight all my own; if I were seeking the grace and loveliness of womanly attraction; if this were to be my ideal of wifehood, then

OPPOSITES 

my search would be at any end; tonight would experience the terminating of my journey. In truth, long years ago would have witnessed its closing. Leonore, even now I can well recall how attractive as a child you were; as a maturing girl your charm of innocence and wealth of health added to your appeal for admiring eyes. And in all womanhood never have I beheld one so worthy to wear the crown of Beauty's Queen. 

No, Leonore, as you come to me this night-majestic -matured, yet minus none of maidenly grace, if I were seeking for myself a woman or just a woman's charms, you need not bow to gain my favor. No, no,

Leonore, as I behold you even now, almost persuaded am I your subject and your slave to be, and were I in quest of pulchritude, then 'tis I your eyes would see groveling humbly at your throne; there at your regal feet would you behold your abject vassal, craving favor of his queen. But, Leonore, it is something else that my heart desires-not beauty of face or form, for beautiful as you are I am quite convinced that you and 

I are not intended to be joined. "There was something years ago that advised against our union. There is now something even more powerful that restrains and holds me back."

CHAPTER XII 

Abruptly rising to her feet and letting fall from her eyes that bit of lace she had held to catch her tears, she raised both hands above her head. I did not know what next to expect, nor did I believe that she herself was fully determined what procedure to pursue. With hands high in air, it appeared for a space, just a short space, that she was about to denouncingly assail me, to attack me with all the artillery of a woman's anger, casting volley after volley of a woman's murderous hate, thrown with all the force of resentful defeat and ignited with the fire of a woman scorned. Then she changed. Once more her attack shifted. Flinging herself toward me, her hands found a resting place upon my knees, while her head buried itself between her palms, and there she sobbed and sobbed and sobbed, and there I let her stay. After all her other attacks had failed, repulsed by a defense invulnerable to her assaults, then it was that with seeming distressing despair she broke through my protecting walls-walls constructed so well as to withstand all previous attacks. 

The element of pity behind my fortifications had weakened my protecting tower, and as it crumbled into dust she passed through the open breaches and had won the fight.

Soothingly smoothing her tresses of black, those threads so silken and sleek, gently I raised that glorious head and with my own kerchief tenderly dried her tears. In one of my hands I took one of hers -snow white and drenched with briney moisture from her eyes then stroking it tenderly to finger tips I affectionately bade her rise, and assisting her to assume 97

OPPOSITES 

her feet I drew her to my side. Then drawing her closer and nearer to me, I spoke to her as I would to a child. "Come, sit on my lap my baby girl and be little

Leonore of yore. Be Leonore who climbed the tree with me and where side by side we carved our names on its trunk. Let us talk over this matter of ours. You seem as a little child to me, like the little Leonore of old. While you and I are near of an age, yet I feel like a daddy to you."

Then Leonore did sit on my knee, and on my shoulder she rested her head, that glorious head of the night. With one arm around her waist and holding her hands tight in one of mine, different were my sensations from any experienced ever before or since.

Almost was I convinced that I had been mistakenyes, wrong all wrong, and that following years of delay the forces after all were operating to show me that Leonore was really for me and I for her. For a time neither spoke. What I wanted to say I could not for fear of bringing about fresh grief; then, too, I hesitated to break the spell, for even though the situation was full of possible happenings, I was strangely pleased, yes, more than pleased, but yet I was not happy, for I was in a mazy haze of uncertainty as to the meaning of it all. Had she uttered a word before she did, the victory might have been hers. Had I then capitulated, I could not in the years as yet unborn have condemned myself for my lack of will, for in all the world is there a man, given in his arms to hold the most entrancing, enticing human that God ever moulded, who can long or well combat the impulse to take for his own so alluring a being as she? No, no, I emphatically say. And I ask the best man in the world to come forth and tell me what he would do if the chill of his will and his manly reserve were melted and thawed by warmth of such a one, and by her snaring charms and blandishing ways his better judgment were

OPPOSITES 

99 dulled. And before you reply, "Best man in the world," have it firmly fixed in your mind that this woman whose physical charms like unto which no other has even hoped to approach is so willing, yes even anxious to be your slave.

My intentions had been by exercise of common sense reasoning to make clear to Leonore that conditions as they at that time existed made it impossible for her and for me to consider anything of love in any form; and that it had better be that we wait for what might later develop, something that might possibly show me to be mistaken after all in that the forces would yet so operate as to draw us to each other.

Following her paroxysm of disappointment displayed in the flow of her scalding tears, those were the things on the tip of my tongue, but as in sorrow so childlike and helpless she seemed, my words died ere they were born. And as she silently nestled herself on my lap with her head fitted into my neck, I was glad, I was sad, as in suspense I wondered: "What next?" 

Then lifting her head that I might hear, softly she said, "Dear, I am very sorry I gave way as I did, but I have had so much on my mind. I have worried about you and your injuries. I have also been much concerned about many other things; then, too, I have financial worries. I am without means to pay my bill here at the hotel. My funds are exhausted." 

Then flashed to me the thought, "So that's the game after all. Just as I at first suspected, it is money she seeks. Prospecting for gold, she strikes me as her mine; then staking her claim, proceeds to dig and to dig, but finding the surface resisting her drills, all her tools far too dull to break ground, to reach the vein of glittering ore she resorts to other means. Failing to sack, she tries to soften the rocky exterior with the dampness of her tears. Yes, after all, it was not for me, for me, the man, she cared. It was me, the mine that she sought."

OPPOSITES 

First strangely and surprisingly disappointed, to me soon came realization of a deep hurt to my pride, followed closely by fierce resentment. This sirenic Eve had tried playing with me. For apple of gold she would barter taste of forbidden fruit. In lack of sincerity she was the devil's delight. But at the very end 'twas a headless game she had played. At the moment of victory she had lost.

Becoming quite brutal both in manner and in words, fiercely shaking loose her arm from my waist where somehow, sometime she had placed it, roughly raising from my shoulder her head, I thrust her from me and to her feet; then, as with apparent sore amazement she wonderingly gazed, with loathing disgust I vehemently and viciously hurled, "So that is it? You a blackmailer or maybe worse. Just leave me here; go away, yes, far away, and never, never come back." 

But she did not go, neither did she speak. She only strode the floor. What were her thoughts, what her emotions, I never knew. Then, after a century of suspenseful waiting, she broke from the beat that she had been striding and stood directly in front of me. Evidently she was making a great effort to be calm. Drawing herself to the limit of her height, with transparently feigned dignity she said, "I have made a great mistake. I have made several mistakes tonight. Always have I regarded you at heart the most liberal man I ever met. From observations of years ago and from your reputation, at all times I have deemed you more than courteous. In the long ago days you claimed for yourself the generous judging of giving every one the benefit of the doubt. And how I have heard you so magnanimously proclaim that for the accused your verdict was always 'innocent' until proven guilty beyond peradventure of a doubt! In my opinion of you I find 

I have been quite mistaken. You have not proven to be generous. Not alone because you have refused to

OPPOSITES 

101 give of your purse to aid an old friend, but because you have in your refusal been lacking in generous expressions. Your brutality precludes all idea of finding in you a spark of even common courtesy, and wouldst thou tell me, Sir Knight, where thou hast mislaid thy vaunted chivalry? Without a trial, without even a hearing, you have judged me guilty and have condemned me to the cell where languish the blackmailers and those even worse. You have, Sir, demonstrated to me that those noble attributes you claimed for yourself and which I so credulously believed were truly yours, were but empty shells, flimsy, shallow shells, which at the first slight test break and reveal you as a mean and selfish one, and without just regard or fair consideration for your fellow kind. "I am going. I go without any of your gold, which

I do not want and never sought. I go without your respect, which I did believe I held, but which to me now is of no value. I go without your friendship, but that matters not, as you could not be a friend to anyone.

You cannot even be a true friend to yourself. I go without aught to pay the debts of the past or to provide for the morrow. I asked you favor as a friend, but you refused it to me. Still there are ways open to me. I can leave, conveniently forgetting to tell when 

I am going and whence I go. I can leave by way of the lane of the 'one more fortunate, weary of breath.' I can in certain ways raise currency. I can, I believe, make temporary loans from guests now here. Their terms may vary. Should I care to apply physical charm to the acquiring of necessities, I have more than a face as a fortune. You have said that I am beautiful. 

Of all the things you have said tonight those words at least were truly spoken, but it is not required that I tell others what you say, for any pair of eyes may observe what you have seen and others may appreciate what you have discarded.

OPPOSITES 

"Oh, there are many avenues open, dear friend of mine. Which I choose to journey and travel is for my reasoning of tonight and for me to execute on the morrow."

I was rebuked. Evidently I had guessed wrong again. Still, I was cautious. 

Hesitating a moment, I said, "Leonore, I hope you will pardon me. During great numbers of days and even years many tricky twists and tortuous turns have made victims of my hard pressed and tortured nerves. 

Of late my problems have not only multiplied, but matters of steadily increasing seriousness have presented themselves to me for investigation, consideration and action. I came here to think, reason and work out the solution of some of these things, most of them superlatively important. I met with an accident, of which to some extent you have knowledge. My nerves have been far below par since that affair and in consequence of that condition I have, I believe, misjudged. you. Tonight I am quite depleted of available funds, but if you will come back or see me elsewhere as we may arrange, say on Monday, I shall endeavor to relieve the tenseness of your financial condition. You may tell the hotel management that you are expecting funds which should arrive not later than the middle of the coming week, which I hope and believe will appease them."

CHAPTER XIII 

Then happened that which has oft times in my retrospection produced arguments with myself as to just why it was and for what purpose, but these debates with one's self always conclude with the conviction that forces were at work that night to bring to pass things to my advantage, provided, of course, I submitted myself to the influence of said forces. Whether that be true or not, the events of the night certainly had much to do with my future fate.

But once again came there a change in Leonore's demeanor. Approaching

Approaching a window near to where I sat, and facing me she said, "Dear, I have misjudged you, for which I am sorry. We both are much distraught. Your cares and injuries have caused you and my anxieties have caused me to see things in wrong lights. Now let us reason and discuss it all soberly and soundly and sensibly."

As my ears were engaged with the words she spoke, my eyes were occupied with drinking in the beauty and charm of that most unusual woman. But, impressed and held fast as I was by enchanting glory of face and figure, it was as nothing in the light of what was so soon to follow. "Darling," she said, "to any who may pass on the road and see me here, my presence in your room may not appear perfectly proper. The moon being full, we have plenty of light, so we need no other illumination. 

All that is desirable is that we see each other." 

Without further word she switched off the electric light. Following this, either by design or otherwise, she placed herself full in the flood light of the luminous 103

OPPOSITES 

moon, and as she stood between the window and my startled view there was revealed in unclouded vividness such fascination of face and charm of form that before the proud possessor of which the most extravagant imagination or the most rapturous dream must humbly bow in servile and speechless adoration. And as in the splendor of the lunar light I ecstatically gazed on that woman in white, almost persuaded was I that my theory of the opposites was all wrong. Yes, in the spell of full moon and that alluring white form, belief in my theory, even with its links carefully welded and so skilfully joined, was strained and stretched to the breaking point, and then with the wavering of weakened restraint woman's lure snapped my faith in my strong belief, and like a clanking, broken chain it fell to the ground, and a worthless, useless, disappointing thing it lay there. 

And then that bewitching siren spoke. "My darling boy," she said, "it is all a misunderstanding between you and me. Just that-nothing more. It is not your gold for which I have come, even needing money as I do. Think back in the past. You and I were childhood chums, we were closest pals, as together we enjoyed those happy days in the years of long ago. As a child I was sorry when my family moved away. During our separaton I thought often of you. When I came back to the old town we met again and I was glad, oh, so very glad, and I hoped you too were pleased. Those few short days were joyous ones, and then when again I went away I was glad in the thought that it was but a temporary parting, and even later when it seemed that the ties were torn I never dismissed you from my mind. It is right and it was intended that you and I should come together again, my dear. I submerge my modesty as well as my maidenly reserve in my love for you, Sweetheart, and ask why should we part again."

All this was quite far too much for virile man long

OPPOSITES 

105 to resist. This perfect being of physical womanhood, for the like of which men would fight to the death, was offering to me herself, all of herself. As a lost man, helpless and without hope, I sat, as all the while rapidly throbbing heart, greatly quickened pulse, strangely agitated nerves, conspired with straining surging blood to subdue and make numb the reason and judgment of my brain. All discretion and all the carefulness of my entire past life were as though they never had been, but I contend and always will that the flesh and blood of no full blooded man could long withstand the lure of that soulless and scheming one, as she, posing in the full glow of the enchanting moonlight, presented to my vision charms indescribable, yes, until that moment impossible even to imagine. In spite of lingering effects of my injuries, I arose from my chair. As the few remaining threads of discretion tried to draw me back and hold me fast, the burning heat of my fevered brain consumed and made cinders of those ties of reason that tried so hard to keep me safe. I stood before her. She saw; she knew. With arms outstretched she drew close, and in a strangely choking tone she whispered to me, "Dear you are mine, all mine, tonight and for always." 

With the fever of her panting breath upon my outstretched hands, once more came the tugging of a strong man's urge to be master of himself. Just one little string of self-respect tried to hold me and to avoid my impending fall, but the touch of her hand overcame every remaining restraining force. That feel of flesh, that closeness of the most beautifully moulded woman ever cast in human form, the witchery of the moonbeams, the exotic perfume with which Leonore had sprayed her gown, all of these things and still some more, and-and-I was captive in her arms. 

I had surrendered to her charms. She was in my arms, and I was in hers; my lips were on hers and I was

OPPOSITES 

drunk, drunk and lacking of all that holds a man in decency's self restraint. When most I needed moral strength I was indeed most weak. When it would have been best of all times to be physically weak, I was very, very strong. I had not stood or walked without cane for weeks, but there that night I stood firm and potent on my feet, holding Leonore in two strong arms -and then as I held her close and as in my embrace she seemed to cling and sway, it seemed that after all my theory must be wrong, entirely wrong, and that Leonore was for me, and as she seemed to cling so close and hold me in her arms so tight, I saw my bedroom door ajar. Just how long it had been so open I do not know, but from that overwhelming, overpowering spell it was by the voice of the colored bell-hop I was aroused. "Pardon, sir, but did you call?"

How he had opened the door was not long a mystery. When Leonore had locked the door she had removed the key, which made it easy for the bell-hop to use a pass key.

All that I could do, all that I was able to do was to sink into my chair. What I said to the man I never knew, but he hastily withdrew, whistling as he departed some unfamiliar air in a very novel manner. 

Collecting myself as best I could, with all the loathing and withering scorn and frenzied fury I could command, in tones of intense hate, I flung at Leonore these words: "I congratulate you. You have done your impious work well. Yes, I am tricked, but you will find that I can fight. That which during all of your snakelike scheming I suspected I now know full well to be most true. But while you played me for a fool and also played me well, I am not too foolish nor is it too late to learn and profit by my lesson. Go! Do what you will, but I am going to fight as I never fought before. 

You and your negro friend with color so appropriate

OPPOSITES 

107 for the blackmailing art can do your worst, your very blackest. You choose well your companions in crime, but try as you will you can never shake plums from this tree, nor gold can you dig from this mine."

As my outstretched finger showed her the door, the expression on her face was to me an unreadable thing. 

It was not anger; it was hardly fear; it was without semblance of hate. She was not hard. There was no threat; there was no seeking for pardon. Powerless was I to uncover what was by that beautiful mask concealed. Turning on the light, she faced me for a few brief but very tense moments. "Good night," she said. "None can foretell what the future may hold hidden in the days that are yet to be born. The telling of those secrets may solve your problems of yesterday and today, as well as mine, or it may not do so, but this I do know, we shall, we must meet again. Good night." 

She was gone.

CHAPTER XIV 

Little sleep came to me that night, and what slumber did come was of such fitful nature that it was of little if any benefit. The doctor who had not seen me for several days called the following morning. I was glad that he came, for my nerves were much in need of attention. Immediately sensing something had disturbed me, he inquired as to my upset state of mind. I told him a friend whom I had not seen for years had called and had imparted to me some unpleasant news. He pressed me to seek the open air as soon as my ankle would permit me to do so.

As usual, I inquired as to my fellow victims of the accident. He informed me that the strange darkness of the storm had seemingly affected all three in similar manner, in that while they were rapidly recovering from their physical hurts, none of the three seemed able to tell aught of self that would give inkling to identity, family connections, or any other important particular. "The injured man," the doctor said, "does certainly seem to be possessed of rather a poetical turn of mind. 

I was trying to learn from him today if he could recall the names of any friends. His reply was, 'Friends, friends, real friends, are so scarce, so rare. For years I have endeavored to find real friends. In a haze I can recall faces of acquaintances, but their names were lost in that strange darkness on the mountain side. But acquaintances are not of necessity friends, though it is possible that some of them might prove themselves so, should opportunity present itself.' "Then for several minutes he was quiet, refusing 108

OPPOSITES 

109 to answer any of my questions. Possibly he did not even hear them. If he did he ignored them. Then he requested of me a sheet of paper which I handed him from a pad of prescription blanks or forms that I carry. Borrowing my fountain pen, he wrote a bit, then paused and said, 'A friend is one who is with you and for you twelve full months in every year. A friend never suspects you of wrong. When you make a statement he never asks proof. He likes you as he finds you. When you stumble or fall, he lifts you up. When success comes your way, he rejoices. He never is envious, never jealous of you. A real friend is one that stands by you and with you even when you are wrong and he knows that you are wrong." "Then he resumed writing. When he had finished he handed me this." As the doctor finished speaking he passed to me a slip of paper bearing the following efforts of the poor fellow who had saved my life:

Just through pre-election days. 

One golden day my ship came in;

As she entered port and docked there, Many "friends" paid court and flocked there, Soon departed. Cargo thin. 

Then came darksome days of winter, Poisoned pens with green ink wrought; 

Assassin's dirk my good name sought; 

Slander's torch credit cindered. 

An humble soul, such as angels lend, Scarcely knew me, but believed me; 

Pledged to help me, not deceive me 

With Purity's white blotted Calumny's write

By Power of Right conquered slime of Might. 

Good name mended. Journey ended;

For I knew what constitutes a friend. 

When I had finished reading, I said, "I cannot believe that anybody other than 'Pinky' Williams wrote that. It is just the sort of stuff he used to write and recite when we were in college." "Well, you know I asked him if his name was Williams. He said it was not. While he was unable to tell me his real name, he was sure that it was not Williams. However, as a result of a search made at the scene of the accident there has been found what may be a probable clue. A man's watch case was found where Doogle tells me the man was lying when you and he raised him from the road. The watch was destroyed beyond all hope of ever being useful; in fact, none of the watch was found except the case and that was badly bent, dented and twisted out of shape. All that we could find that could possibly serve us was a

OPPOSITES 

111 monogram on the case. As nearly as we could decipher it the letters were L. G. D. By no twist or turn of imagination could it have contained an 'F' or 'W'." "Be that as it may, Doctor, as soon as I am able and have your permission I am going to see that fellow; I am not satisfied that he is not 'Pinky' Williams." "We can easily arrange that when I consider it wise. 

He objects so strenuously to visitors that I prefer not to cross him, but sooner or later we can lay some plan to satisfy you. The darkness of that day of accidents has strangely affected him and the others, too, as well as yourself. I might as well let you know that but for the strange effect of that same darkness there is no reason why you yourself should not have been out of this room before now. In one respect it has affected you the same as all of the others who suffered in that terrible affair. You all object to meeting people and you all seek seclusion. I can attribute that to nothing other than the effect of the awful darkness with its depressing accompaniments. "However, we must free you from this spell and if possible get your assistance in clearing up the identity of our handsome, athletic poet. I rather like his poetry. What it may lack in metre or rhythm, it more than compensates in depth of sentiment and the soundness of its truth."

CHAPTER XV 

The doctor had but gone when Doogle next appeared. For several days past his work at the chapel had kept him away from me, except to inquire as to my condition and possible needs. I determined to tell him the story of Leonore and her visit of the previous night, to which he listened attentively. When I had finished he astonished me by stating that he was not satisfied that Leonore had tried to victimize me. He gave as one of his reasons that it was his policy to give every one the benefit of a doubt. He further claimed that there were certain aspects presented by my account of my experience with Leonore, the consideration of which almost persuaded him to believe that she was either my perfect opposite or very nearly so. 

What he meant by "nearly so" he said he could best explain by raido dials. One may get indifferent results if one or two dials are set properly, but perfect results can be attained only when all dials are correctly set. 

He claimed that in a test of any electric device with positive and negative poles a result while not entirely satisfactory might yet be but a scant shade removed. from being perfect, still, that every part must be in harmony with every other part before a perfect result could be expected or obtained. He was of the opinion. that Leonore and I were possibly just a shade removed from being "perfect" direct opposites. "But why the bell-hop's appearance on the scene?" "Mought a been just a happenin'-Let me tell ye of the second period of my life or the part that influenced my career most effectively and forcibly. You may not believe it, but once I was in love with one who 112

OPPOSITES 

113 was just a shade removed from being my opposite, and I am still in love with her memory. This is a matter 

I never have confided to anyone, but I trust you, for 

I appreciate the confidence you have placed in me."

Doogle's peculiar dialect had entirely disappeared. 

Reference to a certain period of his life seemed to have caused him to forget his customary manner of speech, which led me to believe that it must have been artificial. "Previous to today I have told you a part of my early life. In those days I was never interested in the opposite sex, probably due largely to the fact that those of the feminine found in me little that appealed to them. I was never handsome. No need to tell you that. I had not an athletic physique. My schooling had been curtailed. I was not well educated. My learning was along few lines-I studied more by observation than from books. I studied people. I studied causes and effects. I studied character. I studied the habits of people and animals; what caused their habits and the effects of each habit. I studied the planets, the heavenly bodies, the changes of seasons, the tides, the ocean currents, the winds, the rain, storms and their effects. I studied electricity. I studied the trees, the flowers and in truth quite much of vegetable life. While 

I was neither attracted by the female of my specie, nor was any of that sex attracted to me, I studied humans individually and collectively, both male and female, as to their attraction one for the other and the usefulness and need for each other. Some of these things I studied from books and through listening to men of learning discuss and lecture on these subjects; I also used my eyes; I used my ears; I watched and listened.

I looked for effects produced by causes. I sought for the causes of effects that had been produced. As stated, I was neither a man's man nor a woman's man. I had my theory of the opposites and believed implicitly in it.

OPPOSITES 

As day succeeded day it was fixed more and more firmly in my mind until I was fully satisfied that it was the one and only real explanation of the requirements for perfect happiness. For a great while the only flaw 

I found was in the history of my own self. I was not clear as to whether there was or had been an opposite for me or whether in a case like my own, in the great order of things, it were better that I should have no opposite, thinking it probable none of the opposite sex could be happy with such as I. "Then one day I met her, I met her who at the time

I firmly believed was my opposite. She came to take charge of a department in the mill where I was employed. She was younger than I, about ten or twelve years my junior. But, Mr. Friend, when I saw her I felt that all the loose ends of my theory were tied tight by my finding my own true opposite. She was a fine looking woman. Almost immediately she and I became friendly. At first it was a matter of my conversing with her rather more than I did with any others in the mill. 

Then each day after the mill closed I made it convenient to walk with her in the direction of her home. 

Later I accompanied her to her residence. She seemed not to object, and as time went on I thought she looked rather with favor upon my companionship. The time came when I spent many evenings with her. All seemed going well, and I was more than satisfied that she was my opposite. Following much timid hesitancy, I at length determined to ask the one question that had been on my tongue for weeks and which I felt could have but one answer. "The day of the very evening that I had deemed opportunely auspicious to bring this important and serious matter to a head, I was called into the office of my employers by the secretary of the company. As diplomatically as suited his purpose he told me that he had observed my attention to this lady friend of

OPPOSITES 

115 mine, also that others had noticed it too. He explained that he thought it was due me that I should be apprised of the lady's past which had been of a rather doubtful sort, but added that at the time the company engaged her the result of the management's investigation was to the effect that if there had been aught wrong or questionable in her past it had been either lived down, or had been corrected. He said all would have been well, had it not been brought to his attention that she and I were much together. I insisted that our relations were perfectly proper. I was even prompted to add that I hoped to make her my wife, but this I hesitated to do, as I had no permission from her for making known such a desire. Brusquely he retorted, 'But, don't you see that it must be evident to her that you are receiving a rather nice salary and, while I am loath to say this, for I dislike hurting your feelings and hope that I do not do so, you must realize that you are not of the type of men to appeal to or interest a woman such as she. I am inclined to think that the public would be justified in believing that you are attracted to each other by something other than what I myself believe to be true, but at the same time we cannot afford to permit a scandal to be created in or about our organization.' "With vehemence I contended that Martha-that was her name—was as blameless as anyone connected with the organization, and that even if I were wrong in this the organization would still be just as vulnerable to attack, if my name should not be connected with hers, as would be the case were she and I to continue in the same relationship that had existed for some months. I argued that if I, as a man free of all domestic responsibilities and obligations, found Martha to be the one woman in the world to interest me, and that if on her part she saw no reason to discourage me it was a matter as much exclusively hers and mine as

OPPOSITES 

would be the case between even him and any woman of his choice. That unexpected outburst found him unprepared. While it was quite clear he considered. my paralleling of the secretary of the company with one of its employes most audacious, yet he was not sufficiently sure of himself to speak the words my spontaneous temerity had prompted. After some hesitation he guardedly replied to the effect that even though Martha's past was doubtful, if nothing should encourage the softly spoken whispers, the organization might remain free from public gossip, but immediately some particular man should be much associated with the girl, especially one from the organization, then significantly the knowing heads would nod and insidiously the loosened tongues would wag. My reply- 'Then, though some time in the past she may have fallen more or less short of the very high standard set by this blameless organization, so virtuously correct, but since that unfortunate happening has well paid for her mistake and has conquered self and has ceased to listen to the tempter, then, Sir, is she to be gauged, measured and weighed alone by some act committed in some unguarded moment of the long ago past? 

Would you judge yourself by what you were or by what you are? Then, so judge the woman as you would judge yourself, a man. And tell me, Sir, if this woman, who may have sinned at some time in other days, yes, more grievously sinned than either you or I, but today is more free from blame than even of all this organization the most reproachless one, would you still condemn and hold her to account?' "With an air of dismissal, he remarked, 'If we but let sleeping dogs lie then no harm can come,' to which 

I remarked, 'Then you believe that when sleeping dogs cease to lie, that lying dogs cease to sleep?' 66 "That covers it and at all costs we must guard the

OPPOSITES 

117 reputation of this firm—a reputation that has been established and kept clean for so many years.' "Then it becomes a matter of covering up a false reputation, for Martha is no worse and I am no worse with our names associated together than if we had never met, neither is this organization a bit better nor a bit worse by the existence of a friendship between this woman and myself.' "He took another tack. ""But, don't you see I am trying to take care of you? I have your interest at heart. I don't wish to offend you, but every one else would know and you yourself should know that this woman can see nothing in you but what your money would buy for her. You are quite some years her senior, and you even look older and she appears younger than your actual respective years.' "I arose and left the office. The affront was more than I could take and remain in a mood to calmly further discuss the matter. As I hastened away he called after me, 'I tried not to hurt you, but you made it necessary. Think it all over and tell me tomorrow if you don't admit that my advice has been sound and wise'."

CHAPTER XVI 

"If I do say it myself, Mr. Friend, I was a valuable man for that firm to have on its payroll. The secretary did not wish to offend me because he wanted my services. The more I considered the matter the more firmly was I convinced that there was more back of the secretary's deep interest in my welfare than that of preventing possible loosening of gossips' tongues. "That evening, because of the interview with the secretary, I had been deprived of my customary walk with Martha to her home. However, I telephoned her to the effect that I should like to discuss with her that evening an important matter. She replied that her mother was not well, and that she much preferred that I postpone the discussion until the next day, and suggested that I allow her during the day to inform me just when we could have a talk. I told her that while 

I was sorry her mother was ill and that I should gladly comply with her desires, yet I could not do so, as I should not be at the mill on the morrow, in which event she would be unable to communicate with me there, whereupon she asked for an explanation as to my contemplated absence from the mill. I told her I was not returning to the factory at any time. Evidently she sensed that my desired talk with her must have some close connection with the severing of my relations with the organization for whom so many years I had toiled, for she said, 'Come to my home this evening. If conditions prove favorable we may discuss the matter that is troubling you. If I find that we cannot do so, we must then arrange another appointment.' "Mr. Friend, that night my every last ambition 118

OPPOSITES 

119 died. I had determined that my connection with the old mill was at an end. I could not continue with an organization, the requirements of which were such that the past of a person connected with that concern, even though corrected and permanently remedied, could interfere with the free actions of that person. 

Regardless of the motive that prompted the unfair attitude of the firm, I would not go back. I decided to give the organization no advance notice of my leaving. 

I should simply write the secretary that I was through, and that he could and should retain of my salary what was due and unpaid, that I was severing all relations at that moment, and that for no reason or purpose for even one second of time would I return that day or any other day to the mill. Whatever else I did was to be determined by the result of my talk with Martha. "When I arrived at her home she was awaiting my coming, and, Mr. Friend, I never saw her look as she did that night. Always beautiful, she excelled herself.

I was almost inclined to believe that one so much my junior in years and appearance could not possibly see in me aught to admire. Then, too, there was a certain. something in her eyes, Mr. Friend, that I could not figure. Whether it was worry about her mother, whether it was anxious curiosity about what I might have to say or whether it was a certain mark of beauty I had never observed before, I could not tell, but while 

I had changed my mind as to placing my future in her hands that night, I wavered under the spell of the overwhelming beauty that was hers. "As from time to time I had studied out my theory of the opposites, I always had arrived at the conclusion that I could not feel toward one as I did towards 

Martha, unless that one were my opposite. Then, too, she was always so kind and so anxious to please me in every way that I believed she must have feelings for me such as could come only from one who knows one's

OPPOSITES 

self has met the opposite intended for one and is ready to join in the uniting with one's opposite to establish a perfect union. "In reply to my inquiry, she told me that her mother's condition was sufficiently improved that we might discuss the matter I had on my mind. Her mother was in bed, but Martha had arranged to go to her hourly to give her the medicine left by the doctor. She also had placed a small bell in a convenient place for her mother to summon her, should she desire so to do. "The night being a trifle warm, Martha and I went to the yard at the side of the house, just below the window of her mother's room, where we might hear the tinkling of the bell should it ring. I was much disturbed and uncertain as how best to begin what I had to tell Martha. I tried to frame my words and construct my sentences as best I could to save her feelings and to make plain that I had all faith in her. After much hesitation, at length in response to her urging, I began to tell my fateful tale. "Goll, Mr. Friend, I wish so often I never had started to talk on that subject, but I did. "I said, 'Martha, I have had a strangely surprising and I am well convinced a totally unwarranted conversation with the secretary today. "He said it had been noticed that you and I seemed much interested in each other, and that he thought for the best interests of the organization such a condition should not continue. "Then she spoke. 'And you came here to tell me that? Is that a matter of such importance that it could not wait?'

121 "No, Martha, it is not that which is so urgent. I wanted to explain why I am leaving the mill.' "But why leave? Has he persuaded you that for the sake of his precious organization you should sever our friendship, and that in order for you to do so you are running out?' 66 'No, no, that is not it.' "Then why should you and I heed what he may say? Is the organization to set itself up as a critical censor to pass on what each of its employes should say and do? Has the organization the right to decide who your friends or mine should be, or just how far our friendship should go?' "I was much disturbed. I did not want to tell her the whole story; yet, she had me cornered. I realized that I must either let her have the wrong impression by smothering the truth, which would be unfair to me, or I must take a chance of losing her friendship, not to speak of facing other possible disastrous happenings. "I shifted and said that which I had no previous intention of mentioning. 66 'Martha,' said I, 'it is better that I go no farther with details of that interview with the secretary. Martha, answer me one question. I love you. I want to marry you. May I?' "Her expression I cannot describe. For a long time she sat and not a word did she speak-nor did I. The letter 

I do not have, but its flaming sentences stand out as vividly as they did that night. Word for word I can read it again as it appeared to me then and as it appears before me now. This is it: 'Dear Martha: 

Doubtless you will be surprised to receive this note from me. It is the effort of one suffering the deepest dregs of remorse to in some small measure make retribution for wrongs done you. How often, oh, how often, have I yearned for the knowledge of some way in which I might make full amends for all I did to bring into your life that for which you told me when last we met that you despised yourself, and hoped for me that some woman might yet appear to make me pay part of the price that you had paid. her eyes, dead, with the tale-telling note clasped tight in her cold white hand. My fall and its disclosure killed my mother. I can marry no one. Doogle, I took his hand in mine. "I am sorry, Doogle. Let us not talk further about it tonight." "Only one thing, Mr. Friend. You doubtless wonder why my dialect and manner of speech differ from that which you have been accustomed to hear from me. 

All I can say is that the period of my life that embraced my days with Martha was different in every way. Good night, Mr. Friend."

CHAPTER XVII The billiard room of the Northwald Athletic Club in the aristocratic town of Norwald was most brilliantly lighted, but while the electric bulbs were doing full duty to illuminate even the most remote corners of the room, there existed a most heavy atmosphere of densest darkness such as that of a heavy and starless night, a night void of light by either nature supplied or human kind provided. Big, strong huskies of brain, sinew and brawn, but supinely helpless in the greyish gloom-spineless, speechless, unresisting the clamp of an on-coming cloud that was fast enveloping them in darksome despair; virile men, become palsied by a pessimistic fashion plate who but a few moments previous had dejectedly slouched into the room to cover with a pall of depression what remained of a fast dying hope, awaited the hanging of the crepe and the tolling of doleful bell. "Boys, if we win tomorrow it's because the luck that has pursued us all week has determined to reverse itself and turn as kind as it has been rotten. Why my lamentation? Why am I dejected and why cast down?

Well, listen and you shall know. 'Fan' Bellieu has pust received a wire telling that his father was seriously stricken today while visiting 'Fan's' aunt in Northern New York. The boy is leaving on the midnight train to be with his dad. As most of you know, while practising this afternoon, Bill Mayton thoughtlessly allowed Lady Luck to place his thumb just in the proper position that it could not escape being broken by a batted ball. Our first string right hander and only port sider, Fan and Bill, both lost on the eve of 127

OPPOSITES 

the big game. It is tough enough for us and those two boys that these things had to happen at all, but to crash on us when it is too late to make any plans to fill the breach, even to a limited extent, is enough to make even a real manager quit,—a real manager I saidnot to mention a dub like me who is only manager because it seems to be proper and correct for every ball team to have somebody to bear the title of manager. 

Our bunch when right needs only the players. No manager is necessary. The scrunch I am in would be a test for the best managing manager of any big league team." 

Consternation. Dismay. Nothing else, unless added consternation and dismay. A few of the spineless cravens favored canceling the game scheduled to be played with the Southwald team on the morrow, or at least asking for a postponement. But Leighton, the manager, explained that such a thing was prevented by precedent. For years the baseball game between 

Northwald and Southwald Athletic Clubs never had been played at any time other than on the afternoon of the day of the Norwald Annual Festival. There was only one thing that could prevent that ball game on that particular day, and that one thing was rain, and during all the fifteen years that the Annual Festival had been observed it never had rained on the second Saturday of September. There was a suggestion made that "Tip" Browne, the center fielder, who had done some pitching against weaker teams or who had assumed the role of relief twirler at various times, might twirl, but the occasions that "Tip" had appeared in the box were rare. Moreover, Southwald was no weak team. "No," said Leighton, "that won't help. 'Tip' has not pitched at all during the last month, not even in practice. Our two 'aces' have done all our twirling. 

Then, too, 'Tip' when playing the outfield is our best hitter, but when he pitches he takes on the habits of

OPPOSITES 

129 most pitchers and stops hitting. That is the only respect in which he is a real pitcher. When he pitches he bats like one." 

As all hope seemed dying or dead, all suggestions made having received little or no consideration and having been dismissed as being totally void of merit in the eyes of everyone, with the possible exception of the one having advanced the thought, into the room flittingly floated what at once could be recognized as the favorite son of Father Optimism. "Just heard the news. Thought I better come around. Just knew you helpless honey comb sports, who fish only when the finny ones are starved and must of necessity bite on somebody's bait, who never attempt to row a boat upstream, who never go coasting down a hill because you would be compelled to drag the sled up the hill-you spineless saps that never learned anything if you had to study to get inside 'the know'-no one had to tell me that you would be here pitying yourselves instead of getting to work on the job at hand. 

Why, this club has over two hundred members, over two hundred healthy males—at least a 'he' by birthmost of them under thirty years of age, and yet because two pitchers are taken from the list of possibilities for tomorrow's big game, instead of going over the roster and trying to find the name of at least one fellow who has pitched at some time, may be some real boy who can pitch as well or nearly as well as 'Fan' or Bill, good as they are, and I am not trying to take anything from either one of them, well, you have been sobbing out suggestions which you well knew even before you had whispered your whining wail of woe that it would. not solve the riddle, and while you have been sitting idly by hanging the crepe I took myself up the street and found me a live one that I know can pitch." "Who is he?" as one man they exclaimed. "Before I reply to that question, I want you to

OPPOSITES 

know that I feel quite sure you are going to ask me another one-how do I know he can pitch? Well, get this-I acknowledge I never saw him with a ball in his hand. But here is my story of the Christopher 

Columbus who set sail to discover a real base ball pitcher. When to my ears came the regrettable word, that to you moaning mourners was nought other than bold black type in the funeral notices, I squirted a little oil on my think machine, took from my wallet a copy of the club roster, carefully scanned the list, checked off names of all fellows that to my knowledge were college men and might have done some ball playing, then hauling my anchor on board, I, in my little barque, sailed away, fully determined to find what I sought. In quest of a twirling man, I hailed every passing skipper who might according to my notion give aid to me in my seeking or might assist in steering me to object of my searching. "Following my chart and my compass quite close and sticking hard to my definite course, in due time I encountered the captain of a useful ship, the little foreman from the mill, the tiny fellow they sometimes call 'The Medium'. I often talk with him and have benefited quite a bit thereby. He is a student, even though not the scholar some of our Norwald residents claim to be. As I approached him he quizzically said, as he oft times does, 'Well, what is it you are trying to find today?' As I imparted to him that I was in search of a base ball pitcher, one that could beat Southwald tomorrow, I dropped the thought that I was sure I had him stopped, but to my surprise he did that which repeatedly he has done by informing me he had just what I wanted. He immediately proceeded to make known to me that Fred Davis, the fine, handsome looking fellow we admitted to membership several months ago, had pitched for some big university and that he had been a star. He said the Giants and Yanks had both

OPPOSITES 

131 made him offers, but his stepfather, who is very fond of him, was opposed to the boy's playing pro ball. 

Waiting not to discuss things, I immediately set sail for Davis and soon had him on the 'phone. He is calling on Reta Jameson, but promised to try a break-away in order to run down here."

Then Jack Keating, for he it was who had held the bunch in silence as he told of the discovery of a muchneeded pitcher, exclaimed, "There he is now coming down the street. Reta is with him. He has just turned into the walk leading to the club house."

Jack went to meet him, found a comfortable chair on the porch for Reta, ushered Fred into the room and presented him to the curious crowd. "Fellows, here is Father Abraham come to lead you out of the bondage of despair-one of your own members get acquainted with him.' 

In response to the many inquiries hurled at Fred, the men learned that he had done some pitching while at college, although quite modestly he refrained from enlarging on his ability. He explained that he had been graduated the previous year, and that, although following graduation he had done some playing with an amateur team in his college town, he had not engaged in a single game since. He had, however, kept his arm in condition by pitching to some of the employes at the mill where he was employed, and had tried always to be in good condition, but explained that lack of work. in competition might affect his control, also that nine innings might be a long stretch to pitch without special preparation, especially against a competitor such as he was led to believe the Southwald team would prove to be. "But, men," he said, "I hope I'll be better than nobody, and I promise to pitch as long as my arm holds out or until the Southwald boys impress me too forcibly with their ability to hit."

OPPOSITES 

When Davis departed the crowd's state of mind was much improved. None of them had seen him play ball, but the optimistic Jack and the magnetic, gentlemanly and unassuming manner of Davis sent the bunch to bed that night firm in the belief that they could not lose.

CHAPTER XVIII 

A man-Fred Davis-a real man. Nobody meeting im and talking with him could help knowing that. 

Physically perfect, not the giant sort, but well and strongly set up, symmetrical and imposing to observing orbs-pleasing to those of feminine eyes as well as the opposite sex short of six feet, yet taller than he appeared, for the proportions of Fred were right. All his height was not confined to a pair of stilty legs. 

Broad of shoulder and deep of chest, but not the burly, bunchy-muscled, caveman kind. A perfectly formed head, crowned with an abundance of blonde hair; a kindly smiling eye of rather more blue than gray; a nose of Grecian mould and a mouth that could not scold, but yet would be firm in the saying of that which its owner believed to be right; with chin and jaw announcing unmistakable firmness without suspicion of belligerency; his skin was like that which all women crave, some of whom have it by nature and others to have must money pay. Ever and always such bearing was his as to reflect kindness, courtesy and good manners. As men of his years are regarded, his was a type exceedingly rare. 

As he rejoined Reta Jameson, this handsome fellow with smiling face and most pleasing demeanor should have thrilled a frozen Eskimo, but not so Reta, who chilled and made stiff by fancied slight thawlessly resisted all attempts to break through her frigid exterior. "Sorry to keep you waiting, Reta, but it seems the boys are in a sore plight for a pitcher and have picked on me to do what I can to help them in tomorrow's game with the Southwald team.' 133

OPPOSITES 

"That is what I gathered from our conversation as we came down to the club, but why do they choose you? They have any number of members. Certainly some of them besides you can pitch, I should think." "Apparently they believe I have had some experience that may help them. Since they have no other pitchers on their roster, I must do my best, although I may disappoint them at that." "That means that while you twirl the ball I must twirl my thumbs and count the leaves on the trees, while the umpire counts balls and strikes on you. For me that is an afternoon totally lost." "No, I believe the game is to be played in the morning. That will leave the afternoon and evening open for other things. I am sorry, Reta, if I am interfering with any plans you have made, but it would be rather disloyal to the club not to help the men if I can, especially in view of their graciousness in accepting me as a member." "Well, of course, one shows one's loyalty where one is most interested." 

Although the shaft struck home he answered not a word. While clearly showing he felt the wound, his even white teeth glistened in the light of the silver sun as he laughingly replied, "Well, we are both loyal to Northwald, aren't we?"

Her only answer was a shoulder shrug, and it was quite plain she was not pleased.

CHAPTER XIX 

How seemingly odd and most times passing understanding is the mutual attraction of certain types. To see and study Fred Davis and then ask of one's self what sort of a girl would be his choosing, one would say to one's self: None but a peace-loving, kindly sort, one that would look to him for protection, one that would fully trust him; the sweet sort of girl, naturally sweet, not the one with disposition artificially sweetened with sugar of deceit that lasts only for a little while, then leaves the disposition quite more tart for having been unreal and unnatural. 

Reta, denied the gift of beauty's charm, possessed on most occasions a way of favorably attracting. 

While neither daughter of Venus nor Apollo's pet, hers was the magnetic sort that strangely draws even strong men to them. The secret of that magic lure men have tried to learn for ages, and yet for just as long a time they have failed to find the answer. 

Crowned with an abundance of dark brown hair, Reta knew how best to arrange it. Shade of her eyes defied one to mention or try to define; not gray, not hazel, and yet a suspicion of both and under certain lights one might almost say they were green. But there were times of which more than one man could tell when those eyes could smile with appeal irresistible and most commanding. Nose rather aquiline, although far from pronouncedly so; lips a trifle warm and framing a mouth not small which fully cooperated with the appeal of her eyes to smilingly draw men to her. Keeping herself well informed she could when inclined discuss most topics in an intelligent and most interesting 135

OPPOSITES 

manner. 

Her voice, far from sweet or musical, had in it at times a rather plaintiveness that compelled those who heard it to gladly listen, and in it too there seemed a spell that gave her potency to sway or to hold whenever it pleased her fancy. Slim, but not thin, with a figure well curved and a carriage superbly graceful, and to the dint of such charm most men were given to more than one glance, should it happen that 

Reta was passing. 

Fred had met Reta Jameson soon after coming to

Norwald, and the two had been much in each other's company ever since. 

There is little doubt that she had a liking for Fred, even though her caring was of a selfish sort. While she had drawn to her many men and many had been attracted by her, none had remained long in the filing index where she figuratively listed her seriously considered eligibles. Fred had far out-lasted any predecessor, and the knowing ones seemed inclined to believe that the time had come when Reta had found the one whose name would be penned on a permanent card. 

But on the way home that evening conditions were not favoring a much extended time before Reta would be again rearranging her filing system.

The likelihood of amicable relations being re-established was little improved when Fred informed Reta as they made their way to her residence that it would be advisable for him to leave her home earlier that evening than usual, so that he might rest in preparation for the ball game on the morrow.

Sitting on the porch of Reta's home, he refused to be drawn into what might prove to be a quarrel. With neither woman nor man was he morally or physically a coward. He never sought a quarrel with either sex. 

Rather he avoided it, but while with his own sex he never backed up, with those of the opposite he care-

OPPOSITES 

137 fully avoided controversies that might require him to lose the respect of either the lady or himself. 

Sitting there that evening, for a time Reta was quite petulant, then with charm of eye and voice appeal so peculiarly winning she tried to sway Fred to her selfish way of thinking and to make him see that hers would be a lonesome lot, should he play ball on the morrow. Although she exercised all the artifice at her command, he was as dull as any newly arrived immigrant who does not choose to understand. He was strategically void of faintest comprehension. He talked, whenever permitted to speak at all, of the pleasant afternoon and evening the coming day had in store for them.

Doubtless, Reta liked him quite a bit, as much or more so than she had ever cared for any other man, but Reta liked Reta more, much more, than she ever cared for anybody else. In Fred Davis she had found the most likeable man she had ever met. In him she discerned a good disposition, such a one as would not combat her will short of an entire surrender of all just claim said disposition might have to the right of self esteem. In many respects she saw in him a desirable companion, whether temporary or for life. She saw too in him a truly handsome man, one that should attract any woman, and it was very very largely for that reason that she desired to possess him as exclusively her own property. She wanted him, but she wanted him as she wanted any other possession, as she would want the most precious jewel in town, the most beautiful dress, the most attractive home. She wanted him so that nobody else might have him. She also felt so sure that he wanted her that she was inclined to take chances, now and then, of losing him.

So it was when they parted that night that in response to his cheery "Good night, see you in the morning, Reta," that she replied: "It may be that I shall

OPPOSITES 

be obliged to miss the festival. My day is spoiled before it is born."

He was prompted to retort to the effect that if she were not going he should not call for her the follow ing day, but merely said, "Cheer up, Reta. I'll be here at nine. We'll have a good time all right. You are going to enjoy the game. Come out and root for the team. I am in need of all the help I can have, not only from the boys on the field but from our supporters in the stands. Good night, Reta."

CHAPTER XX 

Norwald was a town of beautiful residences in one of the Middle Atlantic States. It was the summer home of many families, most of them wealthy ones. 

On the outskirts of Norwald there was an immense manufacturing plant which gave employment to the people who lived in the town twelve months of the year and constituted its permanent population. Being built on high ground and having the pure waters of a beautiful lake bordering one side and one end of the town, it was delightfully cool during the warm season of the year and healthful at all times. While the summer folk came from various places, the vast majority hailed from two rival large cities. Residents of one of these cities had clan-like settled in the southern end of Norwald, while from the other had come those who established a colony in the opposite direction. Many sons of families with Norwald summer homes had attended leading universities of the country and more than one all-American athlete spent his vacation in Norwald. Thus it was that two athletic clubs were formed, one the Northwald Club and the other the Southwald Club. The Southwald was very exclusive, and the only thing that overcame a lack of azure blood to make one eligible to membership was an outstanding athletic record. All the Northwald Club required was that a fellow must be white and clean and decent and play all games in a square manner. The rivalry was keen and at times but a bit short of bitter. For years had been held what was known as the Annual Norwald 

Fall Festival. Never had it been observed on other than the second Saturday of September. Following 139

OPPOSITES 

that day of each year, departures from Norwald back to home towns were quite frequent. 

Both athletic clubs had the pick of university base ball players. Each year the base ball teams of the two clubs played together the game that was to determine for the season the championship of Norwald. Always were the games cleanly played and invariably well contested, and it had happened that the championships were rather evenly won. 

In the season of which our story would tell both teams were exceptionally strong, so strong in fact that the record of neither team bore the blemish of defeat.

Northwald's success had been largely the result of the skill possessed by its two pitchers. To that team the loss of those two main springs therefore was quite serious, and unless Fred Davis without any preparation did the superman feat of coming through to victory Southwald was destined to rule as Norwald's base ball champions for at least the coming year.

CHAPTER XXI 

To keep clean its record for clear weather on Festival Day, the sun rose untrammeled and unhidden by faintest cloud. However, it was a trifle warm for a

September day in Norwald, and as the day progressed so did the heat.

It was a long-established custom for the families. to take with them the picnic lunch basket, and so it was that when Fred called at the Jameson home, Reta and her mother had the family basket packed. During the night Reta evidently had experienced a change of mind as well as of heart, but whether for better or worse it would have been difficult at the time to say. In her pink picnic dress with a flower of same hue in her hair, she excelled her usual self. New and strange was the look in her eye which Fred, while observing it, failed to understand, but never for a moment did he betray his discerning of the change. 

Greeting Mrs. Jameson and Reta each with cheery "Helloas," he tactfully added: "Even more charming than the morning, Reta." Quite a wise stroke, for it was just the sort of thing that Reta liked. With basket on his arm, his left one, not his pitching arm, for quite fully aware was he that his good right arm must be guarded well-with mother and daughter he made his way down the street on which the Jamesons lived to Main Street at the foot of the hill, where all baskets first properly tagged were placed in one coach and the picnic folk in other coaches. Soon all were off for the picnic grounds.

After a rather short drive the horse-drawn coaches arrived at the park. On the way nothing had been said 141

OPPOSITES 

of the game, but while Reta talked of many other things, during the entire ride her eloquent eyes entreatingly made their appeal. Quite a new experience for 

Reta Jameson. Hitherto with men her word was law.

Her will was the pleasure of the opposite sex. Here confronted her something different a man with a will of his own, and, though in her heart she knew he was right, still she must have her own way. Never before had she been so put to for victory; never had she humbled herself to gain, but she was quite convinced that in words of tongue lay not her way to success that day, but in humble entreating of eye. And so it was she played her game. 

Arriving at the park, Fred remarked that as he must shortly go to the ball grounds for the preliminary practice, he would at once take the basket and seek a desirable spot and a table from which to lunch. Then it was that Reta made first reference of the day to the game, as she said: "And you still insist on playing that game?" "Why, Reta, I cannot do otherwise," is the way in which Fred replied. "All right, Fred. Everyone to his own choice. 

There is Perry Spencer from Southwald. I know he will gladly carry my basket. You may save time by going direct to the ball park without further delay." 

Fred felt the hurt, but said not a word. Mrs. Jameson hazarded towards Reta a glance of reproach, but she knew her daughter sufficiently well to add no word to the glance. Perry took the basket and he, Mrs.

Jameson and Reta were off, the latter with a chilly "Good-bye". Fred stood undecided. Should he follow after the others? Should he return home or should he go through with the game? At that instant the game could easily be counted in the lost column, but for a single moment only, for in that period of hesitation the fighting spirit of Fred Davis was aroused. With

OPPOSITES 

143 head up and teeth clinched, he stuck out his chin and straightway followed that self same chin to the dressing room at the Norwald base ball park. 

Arriving there he found some of the players already on the field, all of whom greeted him most cheerily. 

He responded in like manner, although the men discerned in him a certain something which they took to indicate his realization of the real job they had reposed in his care. But they did not know that in that something was contained a mingling of injured pride, wounded feelings and the hurt of a most unkind wrong that rendered him reticent and occupied his mind with thoughts of things far removed from the responsibility of the big task that confronted him. However, he soon changed his apparel for a base ball uniform and was in a short time out on the field, strong in the hope that he might gradually work himself into a condition that would loosen the muscles that for want of use had found themselves drawn rather tight; but he knew quite well too that he must have strength to conserve and not draw too hard on his stamina.

Soon came the call to warm up, and for him the test was near at hand, for that process should in a great measure whisper its own story as to how he should fare. Accordingly he was pleased to find that his arm responded freely to his call. He had speed and best of all he seemed to have lost nothing of his customary control. If he could but dismiss from his mind thought of Reta's wrong, or could but bring himself to see it in light of a meaningless hurt, he might feel well satisfied to believe that he would give a good account of himself. Among the rapidly gathering spectators from time to time he looked for sight of Reta, but either she had not yet arrived or else his eyes were not sufficiently keen to locate her in the fast growing and moving throng. 

In due course came game time. Southwald won the

OPPOSITES 

toss and selected to take the field. In the first inning 

Northwald was quickly retired. What would Davis do? None present had seen him in a game. Northwald was apprehensive. Even though he had been his university's outstanding base ball player of all times, even though he had been unanimously and universally proclaimed the best pitcher on any college nine-yes, even though he had been the hero of a World's series -that was another day, another year. Absence from the game, lack of actual practice meant much. But 

Northwald had no other alternative. Davis was its only hope. Certainly he had exhibited good form in his warm-up. He had speed, sharp curves, control, but was his condition sufficiently enduring to carry him through nine hard innings? That was the problem to make Northwald ponder. "Play ball!"

As Fred took his place on the mound once more he cast his eyes over that throbbing throng, but Reta he did not find, and he quickly brought his mind to the job at hand. Carefully he pitched, conserving his strength, using his best stuff only when he found it necessary to bear down. The first inning was scoreless for both sides, as were a number that followed, during which each side had played errorless ball, as all the while the rival twirlers had been masters in their respective. battles with the opposing batsmen. Fred continued to breeze along as best he could, easing the strain on an aching arm whenever permitted, which was quite infrequent, as the closeness of the contest demanded bearing down most of the way. If they would only get him a run or two, he might ease that faithful but hard pressed wing, but runs came hard, in fact not at all, and to Fred the end of the game seemed a long way off. 

Then, too, the day was unusually warm, especially for Norwald, where he had spent his summer. While the heat had served him well in the early part of the

OPPOSITES 

145 game, proving beneficial to the arm that had not seen real service for over a year, yet the long pull, the hard pull under that blazing sun was taking something out of him and his feet dragged a bit as he returned to the pitcher's box in the eighth inning. Not a player had reached second, so well had both men hurled and so well had they been supported. Really a pitcher's battle that neither could lose without being deprived of a certain credit due for pitching a winning game that would appear in the column of contests lost. 

Fred had not yet been able to locate Reta, although he had scanned the Northwald side of the field from end to end and from front to rear. The boy was enduring two contests that day, for as he fought his battle on the diamond he was warring with himself and this at times when he should have been relaxing in body and mind and conserving every last ounce of physical and nervous energy. 

Then came the ninth, with excitement intense. Every move of the eighteen men was shared by their respective followers in accordance with their sympathy. In short order the ninth without a result had become history. Then, too, the tenth and the eleventh without a score. All felt that the longer went the game the greater was the likelihood of a victory for Southwald.

That boy Davis who had struggled so hard and endured so well against many odds could not go on forever. Certainly he must break sooner than the welltrained Dunham who was pitching so well for Southwald. Did they but know the mental agony of that 

Davis boy, doubly would they wonder how he stood the tearing strain.

Then the twelfth, which many spectators seemed to sense would be the last. Likewise a certain unexplainable something made evident that the players believed the break of the game was due. Two men of Northwald were easily retired. 'Twas Fred's turn at bat.

OPPOSITES 

The opinion was expressed by many that he would and should make no attempt to reach first, deeming it expedient to take no chance of being compelled to run the bases, and thereby render himself subject to the loss of stamina and strength which he must have and must hold, if even hope of victory was to be his. Motionless at the plate he stood, while two strikes and no balls were called. It was most apparent he had no intention or even desire to run. Strategy, safe and sound, demanded of him pitching rather than hitting, and doubtless it was his aim to hold Southwald in the twelfth inning, with the hope that his team would come through in the thirteenth with the most necessary but long delayed winning run. Dunham then tried to tempt with two curves that broke outside the plate. 

Foolish Dunham. Unsound strategy. Davis would not swing at the best ball pitched. One more over the plate and the side would have been out. The Southwald hurler's lapse is what earned for him the dubbing of "Dumb" Dunham, which title is his to this day. Then with count two and two the Southwald twirler evidently tried to get the ball over, but it was a bit outside. Two and three-it was a battle within a battleit had become a personal contest. Dunham was in a hole and naturally was going to try to fight his way out. Fred felt the challenge of the duel, and personal pride urged him to the fray. A fighter's urge was in his blood, and as a warrior he was determined to win. 

Yes, it was a combat between man and man-a personal match between boxman and batsman-pitcher batting against pitcher, for Davis in accepting the defy clearly had demonstrated the abandonment of his conservative policy of saving himself to perform a pitcher's duty on the mound rather than in batter's box. A challenge given and by Fred accepted. Dunham was deliberate, so much so that once his catcher walked to the box and discussed the situation with him.

OPPOSITES 

147 The umpire warned the Southwald twirler against stalling, whereupon the anxious boxman took his position on the mound, carefully took a wind-up and shot the ball, a fast one, over the inside corner. Davis, who had dug in to take his swing, had guessed the pitch. aright, and he was set for just such a ball as was served him. Solidly he met the sphere and sent it streaking to left. 'Twas a hard line drive that had passed the infield ere was heard the sound of the bat. Although slightly rising as it sped in its flight, Brade, left fielder for the Southwald team, at the crack of the bat started in, as the ball he seemed to judge would not mount high, but would come to him on a fly or on the ground. 

His job was to either catch it or hold it to a single.
