But he had misjudged the length of drive resulting from the impact of ball and of bat, and before he had re-guessed the flight of the drive the horse hide had risen sufficiently high to pass over his head. Quickly turning, with a tremendous burst of speed he flew after that fleeting sphere like a hawk pursuing its prey. Fred apparently was inclined to take but one base, for until he saw the ball pass over Brade's head he had loitered lazily on the base path. As the left fielder turned his back to the infield and started in wild chase after the fleetly bounding sphere, Davis abandoned all thought of conservatively saving wind as well as other physical powers. Ceasing exercise of caution and care, strenuously he put his legs into ambitious operation. Turning first like a frightened deer he sped. As he left second in his wake, the ball bounced from barrier in front of seats in left field. Brade was set to catch the rebound, but eluding his grasp it caromed towards center field.

Fred slightly slackened his speed and shortened his stride after touching bag number two, seemingly satisfied with a three-base hit, but the unexpected course of the ball as it escaped Brade's pursuit impelled the coacher at third to signal the runner

OPPOSITES 

home. At that moment it seemed an easy run, but the center-fielder had gone out and as the ball bounded into his territory he hurried to meet it. As the coacher gave Fred a warning cry, the center-fielder threw. His peg was low and fast and true, and although long it was straight to the mark. At third Davis was showing the effect of his sprint and lagged a bit as he sped to the plate, that all important pentagon, that rubber slab he was striving so hard to reach. 

Payne, the catcher, was well set for the play and had the plate fully blocked, seeing which Fred forsaking his intention of going in standing up quickly hit the dirt.

As Payne had the ball a split fraction of a second before Fred arrived, seemingly Davis had lost the run, as well as paid the price for his effort by being quite physically spent. With the run not counting, Northwald's best chance,—in truth its only chance to score had passed. Also it was gravely doubtful whether the fatigued Fred could summon sufficient strength to continue holding Southwald in check. Although already hard pressed, but determined to get that precious run, even in face of further weakening himself, he threw himself to the ground, took his slide around Payne and as he hooked his toe to the plate, Payne had the ball on Fred's foot. For the length of eternity the crowd awaited the umpire's decision. Then, as hearts performed many feats of contortions, some skipping beats, others pounding as though to break through strong chest walls, others beating unevenly, as breath came hard in burdened gasps, while some there were whose breathing seemed entirely to cease, the umpire held forth two hands parallel with the ground, and the crowd then knew that the runner was safe; that the first run of the game had been scored.

As Fred much fatigued arose from the ground and slowly made his way towards the players' bench, wildly excited team mates shaking his two hands and slapping

OPPOSITES 

149 his tired, aching back, blinded by their enthusiasm, were oblivious to the plight of their hero, as very, very tired he very, very wearily dragged heavy, hot feet that he might find a place to sit down, but there was one who saw and knew, for the Christopher Columbus of the Northwald Club, discoverer of Fred Davis and leading exponent of optimism, jolly Jack Keating gave convincing evidence of observing, keen eyes, as well as kind, helping hands, as tenderly he led the tottering twirler to the sheltering shade of a nearby tree, at the same time calling on one of the players to bring water to cool his feverish head. 

Layton, the next batter experienced considerable difficulty in selecting a bat and exhibited more than slight aversion to taking his place at the plate. In the meantime "Tip" Brown, center-fielder on the Northwald nine, proceeded to warm-up, preparatory to rendering relief to Fred, if found necessary. Layton, unmindful of protesting Southwald, but careful to heed the umpire's urging warning, at length faced the fuming Dunham. First, with design to delay the play, he allowed two good ones to pass for called strikes, then skillfully fouled off a few, then witnessed the passing of two out of reach, spoiled a good one or two, but was finally out on a very long fly to left. Layton had done his work well while the tired Fred was resting and who did not leave the cool shade of the restful tree until all other members of the Northwald nine had taken their respective positions. Very leisurely then he strolled out to his place on the mound. Choosing to conserve all remaining strength to pitch to the bantering batters of Southwald, he waived the practice pitches allowed him, which may have been poor policy, for when he started to work on the first man up his control was poor, and unable to locate the plate he walked him. Doubtless Brade the hitter had been in-

OPPOSITES 

structed to wait and was soon on first to worry the already hard pressed Davis. 

That base on balls was a very bad sign. It seemed that the end was in sight. In making his run Fred had given his all and was being called to pay for that hard earned score.

Southwald needed but two runs to win. The tying run was on first base with none out. Gould the next batter tried first to bunt. Timing the pat of his bat with the zipping speed that Davis had hurled during most of the game, except when using his slow one, he was ahead of the ball and he turned it outside of the base line. For, with strength ebbing fast, Fred's speed was not there, which was one reason why Gould's try was a foul one. This was repeated, followed by two balls, and then Gould catching hold of one on the inside drove the ball to the roving ground of the gardener in left field. Easily the end of the game might be close at hand. The sphere was hit hard, and at the zing of the thing Brade was off like a startled deer, then with the thought that the ball might be caught, he halted his dash and retraced his steps towards first. With the crash and the crack of Gould's clouting club, Layton in left raced out to where he thought that fleeting thing would fall, but its course was not straight and it seemed like a being with life, as it curved and it swerved in its flight. As Layton drew near, it altered its course and turned towards the foul line in left, but Northwald's fielder, fully aware of the importance of keeping up with that elusive sphere kept his eye on his prey and swept on. At length when the strength of its wings had been spent it bent its course towards the ground. 'Twas far, far out in distant left field near where the foul line is stopped by the stand. Just across the chalk mark it had ended its flight, but whether Layton was aware of that I know no one he ever told; but whether or not that knowledge was his, with a leap and a plunge

OPPOSITES 

151 he sped on and up and when he came down his glove held the ball. Had he allowed it to strike the ground, Brade would have been obliged to remain on first. As it was the runner had no difficulty in reaching second before the ball could be returned to the infield. It had served as a sacrifice. The wisdom of catching the ball was debated until the next batter had taken his position. 

Drayton, the following hitter, evidently had been instructed to wait, believing that Davis, who was fast fading, would either pass him or at least be so much weakened that it would be an easy matter for Southwald to get at least one run in that inning, and if not more than one that they could easily finish and win the game in the thirteenth. So the batter waited until the count was three and two, then started the next one through the pitcher's box, but Davis stopped it cold and pegged to third to catch Brade, but Spiel on third spilled the throw. Two were on, one on first and one on third, with only one out. On the first pitch Drayton went to second, no effort being made to stop him. 

Fred intentionally passed Black which fully populated the sacks. It was evident he was employing the strategy of either forcing a man at the plate or making possible a double play. 

Swington was the next to hit. As he advanced to the plate, Fred's thoughts for the moment returned in full to Reta. But instead of hindering him in the battle of ball and bat, his cause was helped by his thoughts, and even more than before was he determined that for her and Northwald, the Northwald in which she was interested, he would win that base ball game. As he flashed a smoke ball past the amazed Swington, the batter was prepared to testify that strength had returned to Fred's faltering arm. Pitching like the Davis of the early innings, he fanned Swington on three hot ones that sparkled and sizzled with startling zip.

The Northwald stand rang with cheers. Two were

OPPOSITES 

out. Only one more to retire and the game was won. 

Sylon, the next batter, while a brilliant fielder at short stop, was the weakest hitter on Southwald's side. With 

Davis back in form, Northwald could not well lose, but as Sylon assumed his stance at the bat, Northwald's strong hope of victory received a rude shock, for as

Southwald's champion strike out batsman was withdrawn from the plate, there came through a megaphone the voice of the announcer: "Brooks batting for Sylon." 

Brooks, although having played ball for a number of years, had been a failure in every department and in every position attempted except that he was the surest, deadliest, timeliest and hardest hitter in Norwald.

There was nothing he could do on a ball field but bat, but how he could and did bat. The story of Brooks' and his clouting club was the topic in toto of more than one Southwald victory.

A great hush had come over all that immense field -Northwald anxious; Southwald hopeful. Brooks, all confidence, awaited the delivery of the pitch of the innocent horsehide for which he seemingly evidenced a personal grudge, for scowling and glaring as he crouched o'er the plate it was plain to the eye that in his heart there was hate, and that in the venom of his spleen was the desire unrestrained to drive and pound that puny sphere to regions far beyond hope of human recall. He well knew or believed that when came his opportunity that ball was in for a very long ride. 

Never had he faced a pitcher that he could not and did not hit. None fooled Buster Brooks. And confronting him that day was a weakened, weary man, in want of practice, suffering the penalty of lack of condition, especially to endure the grind of such a game as he was called upon to pitch that day. Then, too, he had the toll to pay for the run he had made, and in conspiracy with all the other breaks was the unusual

OPPOSITES 

153 heat to bear him down. Davis might have passed 

Brooks, but every bag already had a tenant. Still, his lion heart was unafraid, and as he drew back his arm to pitch, once again he resolved that for Reta he would win. 

Brooks, over-anxious to hit, swung too soon at a slow, lazy curve, a trifle outside the plate. One strike.

That did not disturb that giant slugger. He had not struck out all season. No tired pitcher could fan him.

He would time the next one. But, no, down and out it curved and over the plate it passed for strike two. 

Davis had come back. Brain and arm were in perfect co-ordination. He had Brooks in a hole and then started to work on him with bad ones-not real bad ones just tempting ones, but Brooks refused to take the bait and Fred had soon lost the advantage. The count became three and two. But the pitcher was still unafraid. For the sake of Reta he was going to dispose of Brooks. He had not seen her, but he knew she was there and that somewhere in that vast crowd she was rooting, yes rooting, for Northwald and for him. Taking his time, he scanned the positions of the players to make sure that they were all properly placed, and then as he looked he saw Reta, but it was not among the Northwald rooters that he found her, but on the Southwald side, seated with Perry Spencer. And as his eyes beheld, his resolution faded, and his strength again. was gone from him. Remaining was only the desire to win for those who had trusted him with that game, and he felt that they could not justly complain, even if the game should be lost, but while he must be true to Northwald and do his best for his club, had he the next ball to pitch for Reta much better than his best 'twould have been. He was tired, very tired. His arm ached; his head ached; his heart ached.

Well, it would soon be over. Unless it were a foul, the next pitch would probably be the last of the game.

OPPOSITES 

Once more he looked over his players, careful to avoid lifting his eyes to one certain spot in the Southwald stands. Making sure that the runners were not getting too much of a lead, he pitched. With all his remaining strength he desperately fired the ball. Straight for the plate it traveled and just as straight it came back to him. Weakened and tired the luckless Davis was a bit slow to recover his poise after that last pitch had departed his hand. He was off balance. His hands were lowered toward the ground when toward him came the whistling sphere. Like a rifle shot toward his head it sped and directly between his eyes cruelly it crashed him-a blow sufficient to flatten strongest of men and spread them on the earth. As from the impact the ball bounced high in the air, while the miserable Davis, crumpled up, sank to the ground, deep groans, hysterical shrieks and sympathetic tones rent the tense air, and in all that quivering throng not one eye watched the course of the ball. Base runners stood still, stricken fast in their tracks, while Brooks, the batsman, turned from his course to first to lend aid to the falling pitcher. 

Just one person saw that ball as it dropped, and prone on the mound that one person stretched forth a faint hand in which he caught that ball, and held it tight. Brooks was out. Then, as players gathered around the stricken man, they found him marble white and unconscious, a shivering mass, blood trickling from two nostrils, eyes closed, but the ball, yes the ball, was still held in that poor, limp hand.

CHAPTER XXII 

Reta's endeavors to bring about Fred's surrender to her selfish desire that he should not pitch the ball game came as the result of mingled emotions of mind. and certain conditions of heart, among them being that his companionship did really provide pleasure, another that she had selfishly determined, as was usual with her, to have her own way about things, especially where men were concerned. As she, her mother and Fred had started for the picnic grounds on the morning of the Fall Festival, she had no definite plans to win Fred from his determination to play ball, but tremendously she hoped that she might be successful in her desire to subordinate his will to hers. So strange was Reta Jameson, that to any who intimately knew her it would not have been overly surprising that had Fred's will given way to the girl's she had then expressed the desire that he play the game, which she would have gladly attended to enthusiastically root for Northwald. But before such a thing could come to pass she must have her own way-Reta's way.

So it was that when she learned she was not to bring him to capitulation that on the impulse she called 

Spencer to carry the basket, and sent Fred on his way. 

Having taken her stand she would not draw back. With no desire or intention of hurting him, she must win at all costs, or, not winning, must not let anyone know that she cared. Spencer's appearance just at the moment Fred was about to carry the basket, while accidental, was most opportune for Reta, in that it enabled her to play her game of not caring. With eyes of regret her vision followed Fred at his leaving, and 155

OPPOSITES 

because she really did like him or at least cared more for him than she did for any other man, past or present, she was almost persuaded for a moment, a brief fraction of a minute, to call him back, but her pride would not let her do so, and he was gone, and with a smile masking regret for her meanness and the conscious contempt for herself, she gayly bade Spencer find a well-located table for the picnic luncheon, and she and her mother followed him into the woods. 

To all appearances Reta was excessively gay and quite contented, but Mrs. Jameson knew her Reta and was well aware that her demeanor was but a blind. 

Likewise was she cognizant of her daughter's overpowering weakness, and on that Norwald picnic day she felt very sorry for Fred, as well as for self-willed

Reta. She knew that Reta would not enjoy the day, and she felt very sure that Fred was hurt. She liked the boy, for in her opinion he was a most manly fellow, in fact, the nearest in manners to a perfect man she had known since her own husband some years before had passed on and left her. Spencer, rather pleased that he was for a time at least to be in the company of Reta and that thereby he was assured of a good luncheon, even though surprised at the turn of affairs, was inclined to be somewhat elated, and as a natural consequence his mood was of the jolly sort, in which Reta assumed a sense of delight, but her mother to Spencer was only polite. She was thinking not only of Fred

Davis, with his injured feelings, but of the sad future Reta was building for herself on the non-dependable foundation of stubborn selfishness.

After a short walk the three selected a table well located as to shade and accessibility to the various points they would be most likely to visit during the day and evening. When all had been carefully and satisfactorily arranged, Mrs. Jameson then made inquiry as to what the other two should next do. Spencer of

OPPOSITES 

157 course desired to see the ball game, but, feeling that

Reta would not be overly much interested in base ball on that particular day, hesitated to say so, and as a result of the question there ensued a rather awkward silence. 

After a time Mrs. Jameson broke the spell by saying, "As you young folk doubtless wish to see the game, but will be favoring opposite sides, I suggest that you go over to the field together. You can there both find friends favoring your respective clubs and sit with them. Then after the game you can meet at some prearranged place and return with Fred in time for luncheon. I deem it advisable that I remain here and read or stroll around not too far distant from our table. 

The embarrassing situation thus relieved, Reta and Spencer together set out for the base ball field, nearing which they found that the teams were already practising. Among the players Reta saw Fred. Her first impulse was to seek a seat near the bench of the Northwald players. Then the unyielding spirit of that unfortunately stubborn girl mounted high, and as she and her companion stood at the point where it was necessary to pass either to the side of the Northwald rooters or to their opponents' section, Spencer with a mingled sense of politeness and a desire to make the most of the opportunity so unexpectedly presented said to Reta, "Why not come with me on our side and do your rooting for Northwald there?" 

Reta replied, "Why not come with me on our side and you do your rooting for Southwald there?" it. 

Perry Spencer knew the girl's weak point and played "I dare you to come with me. I hazard the guess you are afraid to let Fred see you on the Southwald side." 

That won, for Reta would not take a dare. She would not let anyone even dream nor hold the slight-

OPPOSITES 

est suspicion that her will could bow to friend or foe, unless possibly to her mother. Most extreme measures were sometimes hers to impress upon the world the indisputable fact that in her there was no fear of displeasing man or woman. Although not sensing it, she had met defeat in the person of Perry Spencer, for to his wiley ways her will had bowed to its conqueror.

Had she cared for this young man in a serious sort of way, it is more than probable that she would have scented the smoke of battle and have been her usual selfish self. Be that true or otherwise, they were in a short while seated among the staunchest of Southwald rooters, where be it said to the credit of this foolishly selfish girl that as her conscience condemningly whispered she felt sufficiently the prick to feel a bit ashamed, so much so, in fact, that she made every effort to be concealed in the crowd from any glance that Fred might cast her way.

In sports she always had a favorite and never hesitated to let the whole world know where her favor lay, but on the day of that Fall Festival none could tell by sound or sign just which team she wanted to win. At times self-reproachfully silent; at times animately conversational, but never of the game; at times in hard endeavor to convince herself it was not that she had done Fred a wrong that she suffered the tearing tortures of a guilty conscience, but rather that disloyalty to Northwald was the basis for self accusing. At times other moods; and while remorse, regret, excuse, extenuation, humility, defiance, at times each held its sway, her mind and heart were on one individual—a base ball player in a Northwald uniform.

And so it was that while the unhappy Reta by open show displayed neither choice nor favor, yet her heart was clutched with frenzied fear when Southwald seemed about to win, followed by quickening of the pulse when Northwald held advantage, and then, yes,

OPPOSITES 

159 then, when Fred Davis rose to heights supreme the quickening was further quickened. 

But with it all Reta closely watched the game play by play and as she watched she suffered. And so it continued with her until came the final inning. Then as victory's tide rose for or receded from Fred and the faithful fellows back of him she played the game with

Northwald, moving towards the seat on her right, then towards the one on her other side. 

So it came to pass that in an unguarded moment she revealed herself to the vision of Fred, which previously she had been so successful in evading, and it was this sight of her on the Southwald side that brought about the boy's undoing. With the crashing of that pitiless ball against his unprotected skull would have come the loss of the game for Northwald, had it not been for

Fred's automatic grasping of the speed-spent sphere. 

And then came the first time that Reta Jameson told herself, her very selfish self, that she had indeed been. at fault; that she had been grievously, selfishly wrong. 

With remorse-seared soul, a heart clamped tight with frightful fear, choked for air as breath came only in hindered gasps, eyes by films of horror dimmed that faintly perceived the panic stricken multitude, Reta, quaking, shaking, shivering in the throes of relentless accusation, denounced herself as the one guilty of murdering Fred Davis. And then in spite of the terror and torture that rended and tore at her hapless self, she wanted to go to him ere they took him away, but Spencer knew it were better not so, and while she pleadingly argued with Perry a doctor had reached the fallen Davis, and had given instructions to have him taken to the Norwald Hospital. So in a carriage they placed him and with the doctor doing what he could as they rode on the way in a short space of time they had the unfortunate fellow in that institution. Whether his skull had been fractured could not at once be ascer-

OPPOSITES 

tained. During the night as he remained unconscious the doctor and nurse did all possible for him, and before morning it had been fully determined that the skull was fractured, and that there was concussion of the brain. All during those long dark hours he lay there so still and so white. Physically perfect just a few hours before-then all through that night so still, so white.

"With the crash of that pitiless ball against his unprotected skull..." (p. 159)

CHAPTER XXIII 

The next day Reta called at the hospital to see Fred, but was informed that it was the edict of those in authority that none but professional ones should enter his room. 

For several days Fred Davis, that product of nature hitherto so physically perfect, lingered on the border line-lingered on that narrow space, just a scant short stretch removed from the place of unsolved mysteries, from that land to which so many have gone, waiting to be joined sooner or later by those who have stayed here for a while. On the shores of the Styx a boat straining to go, tugged at its moorings, while ready oarsmen impatiently waited to propel Fred Davis across. So sure were these boatmen, so swarthy and huge, that the time of his leaving was almost at hand, they held sturdy oars in readiness that no time might be lost in his journey to that nearby but yet far away land, that land unexplored except by those never returning to relate of their findings. But as the bargue muchly used rose and fell with the tide, and restless waves slapped its sides with impatience, the nurses and doctor refused to loosen the hold with which they clung to delay the last voyage of Fred Davis. 'Twas a slender, light thread they held in their hands, and while quite well aware that their grip must be firm and their manner of holding be steady, yet by experience it was fixed in their minds to be not overstrong in resisting, for by the exercise of unwise endeavor they might easily snap those light, slight strands, and thereby lose the hold on their patient. And so these boatmen waited in their craft on the Styx, while human 162

OPPOSITES 

163 science never ceased its vigil, and while boatmen waited and science watched, one day the object of both waiting and watching opened his eyes and saw the world of the living, whereupon those oarsmen so swarthy and huge pulled anchor and were soon lost in the mist of the Styx, for they knew that in the passing of Fred 

Davis there had come a postponing, and yet the joy in that the boatmen had gone was marred and was dimmed, for while the body lived the brain was as of one born on the day of his awakening. No memory remained. His past was as though it had never been. 

He could converse, but only of the immediate present. 

No face did he recognize; no voice was familiar.

While his body gathered strength his mind knew only the things that had happened since he returned to consciousness, and those things were not remembered long.

Only a perfect physical make-up and a clean life had conquered over dissolution, but the crushed skull had failed to protect the thinking and reasoning part of the man, and the happenings of the past were interred in the cemetery of utter forgetfulness. After a time, in hope that in a tiny cell of his brain some slumbering vision might but await a view of some familiar scene to rouse his latent mind, they took Fred in the doctor's carriage and drove around the town. They took him out to the mill where he had been employed. 

That brought back no memory of other days. They took him to the club rooms. That quickened no livening wave of the past for him. And then they took him to the ball field, but he evidenced not that he had ever seen the place. And at a later date while a game was being played they took him again to the grounds, in the hope that it might stir some sleeping portion of the brain to reason's wakefulness, but he asked what it was that those men did. A base ball game to him was that day as though he had never seen bat or a ball.

Days became weeks and weeks were absorbed by

OPPOSITES 

He months, and, while he had recovered a fullness of physical strength, his mind was being made over. knew not how to read, neither did he possess the knowledge of writing. He could, however, talk, but only of things that were of the current day.. It baffled the local medical fraternity, in consequence of which leading authorities of Big City were called to Norwald for consultation. Those wise men could neither help nor explain. The members of the boy's family could not be reached. No one in Norwald knew aught of their location, other than that shortly before the close of August they had set out for a world's tour. All the town sympathized and tried to help, but without apparent avail.

During it all one person suffered mentally quite much, much more than any other in Norwald. Her name was Reta. Quite well she knew, and her conscience would not let her forget if she desired, what had contributed most to the unfortunate plight of 

Fred Davis. Once, only once, had Mrs. Jameson spoken to her wilful daughter of the incidents of that fateful festival day, but she received no response, for the sorrows of remorse and the pangs of regret had sealed Reta's lips with a stamp of self accusation, and in her suffering she chose to suffer alone and in silence. 

So it was that from that time forth the subject was not mentioned in the Jameson home, never, except when one would ask, "Is there anything new today?" and the invariable reply, "Nothing," followed by unexpressed sadness and unuttered regret. However, the reticent Reta had been doing much thinking, as a result of which she had framed that which she hoped might restore to Fred his memory, but then as with her hope there was associated a fear that what she had in mind might have an effect contrary to that so intensely desired she for a time continued silent. 

But there came a day when to Mrs. Jameson's in-

OPPOSITES 

165 quiry, "Is there anything new today?" Reta made a new reply. "There is no change in his condition, but I have been thinking, Mother, that possibly I may be able to help." "And how, Daughter?" "As I was so largely responsible for it all, it is quite probable I was in his mind but a short time previous to the accident. Possibly if he were to see me or to hear my voice, he might by some chance catch a glimpse of the past, and once with the happenings of today a contact were formed with events now gone, it might be further strengthened until the welding of a connecting link might be successfully accomplished.' 

"Well, Reta, even if without a favorable outcome, I feel sure no ill effect can result from such an experiment."

As a result of this conversation it was arranged that on a certain day the doctor should take Fred out to the park and that Reta should be awaiting him at the spot where they parted on the morning of the Fall 

Festival day, he going to the ball grounds and she with

Spencer to the picnic section. Reta was at the appointed place when the Doctor and Fred arrived. Although the latter seemed to study Reta and appeared to take more interest in conversing with her than he had with anyone since the fateful ball game, still he failed to recognize her, and when she spoke of happenings prior to the day of the Festival, he was at loss for a reply. 

Discouraged, the doctor was about to return to the hospital with the unfortunate Fred, when Reta offered another suggestion. It was this: That she should occupy the identical seat she held when Fred was struck down by that batted ball. She knew he saw her there that day, for she had read it in his glance. The doctor being disposed to try anything that had aught to recommend it as a possible means of restoring to the

OPPOSITES 

boy a normal condition of mind readily consented to follow the suggestion.

So the three of them proceeded to the ball park. 

Guided by her memory, Reta sat in the exact seat she had occupied that other day. The doctor placed Fred in the pitcher's box. He gave him a ball and then directed his gaze toward Reta. For a long time intensely Fred looked without changing glance or direction of same. It seemed a hopeful sign, but after some minutes he dropped the ball to the ground, walked to the doctor and inquired as to when he should have his next reading lesson. Reta was insistent that Fred's slight show of interest was more than hopeful and indicated something helpful might be accomplished. The doctor drove them both back to town, leaving Reta at her home, which Fred gave no sign of recognizing, and returned him to the hospital.

CHAPTER XXIV 

That night, following the departure of his last patient, the doctor locked himself in his office, gave word that he was to be undisturbed, and, with firm determination to reason out the salvation of Fred Davis, there he remained throughout the night, and when the morning came he was well pleased with the result of his supreme effort to discover a way to bring back a straying mind. Having reached a conclusion, without breakfast or sleep, he set out to do much phoning and considerable driving from place to place, and when came nightfall he had all plans laid out and well arranged.

As a result of all this, on the following Saturday he had as many as possible of the people of Norwald, both North and South, assembled at the ball field and all seated as nearly as possible as they were on Festival Day. He had the players of Southwald there. He had the players of Northwald there. He had Reta there, seated in the Southwald section with Spencer. 

He had the bases occupied by the Southwald players as they were at the close of the last inning of the ball game. He had Brooks at the bat. He had the umpires in position and just previous to placing Davis in the box he had him saturated with water as on the day of the game, and then he gave Fred the ball. As he did so he directed his gaze towards Reta. Again on the part of Fred came that effort at concentration. He was then told that the batter had three balls and two strikes, that two men were out, that the score was one to nothing in favor of Northwald and that three men were on the bases, and he was then told to pitch to 167

OPPOSITES 

Brooks, the batter. For a time he stood and held the ball. Then he looked around the field and then towards the Southwald section. Then again he surveyed his fielders. Then he pitched the ball and Brooks hit it towards the pitcher's box, but lightly, very lightly, did he hit it. As he did Fred quickly raising his hands in front of his face dropped on his back, holding out his hands as he fell, and then closed them tight on what apparently to him was a base ball. Then he lay there still, very, very still. The doctor and players ran to him and raised him to his feet. For a moment he looked uncertainly around, seeming hazy and dazed. 

Then he spoke. His mind showed signs of clearing. 

For the first time in many days his words concerned happenings with a bearing on events of previous days. 

Looking straight at Brewer, the home plate umpire, he said, "Did I catch it, Bill, before it touched the ground?" Being assured that he did, Fred spoke: "Well!" he said, "that wins the game all right, but, boys, I am so tired. I could not have pitched another ball. I was through, but I just simply had to make good for you fellows. Your confidence in me was great. I'll take a shower to freshen myself and then have luncheon."

By that time Reta was on the field. As Fred perceived her approach he advanced to meet her. He caught her smile and said, "I am glad to see you smile, Reta. I rather feared you had turned against Northwald, but I see now how it was. You merely sat on the Southwald side because Spencer was over there and you had to have some one to explain the plays." "I am very sorry.' 

"Forget it, Reta. Tell your mother I'll be over to luncheon just as soon as I can get a shower. This has been one hot day, although it does seem cooler now." 

Reta was not prepared for this situation, but in a moment replied: "Why-Oh, yes, I had not told you,

OPPOSITES 

169 but we have decided to have luncheon at home. Dr. 

Warren is here with his carriage. He says he will drive us over."

And so it was they took him to the Jameson home, and there it was that but two topics of conversation held sway, one, led by the doctor, of the accident of Festival Day, with its subsequent happenings. This was in the presence of all. The other had for participants but two, in which Reta acknowledged to Fred her full responsibility for his sufferings, and promised how different she would be from that time on and forever afterwards. 

Soon word of Fred's recovery of memory was in possession of all Norwald. Many called in felicitation, but none saw him, for the doctor had so decreed. After a time the faithful physician returned his patient to the hospital, where he had him remain until the following day, and then called to take him home.

CHAPTER XXV 

Days became weeks and the months rolled_on through the autumn time until winter came. The 

Jamesons had gone to their home in Mohntown. 

Fred's mother and step-father had extended their stay abroad and were not expected back for some months. 

Fred had some time previous returned to the mill where he was in charge of the selling force. For some reason he was finding it convenient and pleasing to go to Mohntown at least twice each week. Week-ends he invariably spent there, where after a time he made additional acquaintanceships. Reta's sister, Laine, who was two years younger than Reta and who had been taking music in Germany, had returned home.

Fred and she became friends who mutually saw much good in each other. Laine Jameson admired Fred, and at times reproached Reta for her occasional symptoms of returning selfishness. In fact, on one occasion. she told Reta quite pointedly that Fred Davis was much too valuable a prize for any girl to lose, and she had other than financial assets in mind when she said it. 

To tell of Laine Jameson and justly describe the girl was beyond the flow of the keenest of pens. "Not divinely beautiful, but heavenly sweet," might well be said. Of stature not tall, in truth, mayhap shy of average height, losing one might say an inch, in that she stooped her slender shoulders, oh, just the least bit. Some said 'twas due to close applying to study, but mine is a contrary view in that I believe it was by ready endeavor to be more than kind, for being ever anxious to assist by word as well as by deed, her hand was extended to those in need, and so in earnest eager- 170

OPPOSITES 

171 ness to greet and to speak she inclined her neck when extending her hand. Face rather small, of round symmetrical lines, reminded one of winter and spring time as well, white as the virgin snow, with the blush of the early June rose. Eyes that smiled through Heaven's blue, nose saucily tilted a bit and dotted with drops from the field's buttercup. Nature, plucking a crimson, rambling rose, had planted it under her upturned nose, and the petals parted at her ready smile to show two even rows of glistening pearls. And above it all was her crowning glory of bountiful copper silk strands that glimmered gloriously in the light of the sun, and that shone to make day in the darkness of night. 

Then, too, Fred met the Burlinson family, in which were two sisters who lived with their widowed mother. 

The Jamesons and Burlinsons were intimately acquainted, and the four daughters, Reta and Laine

Jameson and Mary and Helen Burlinson, were much in the company of the other. They were communicants of the same church. They were of the same social set and possessed and enjoyed much in common. 

Mary Burlinson was an out-standing character. Rare modesty and reserve were hers. Possessed of a standard of honor exceptionally high, between what was right and what wrong she drew a line more closely defined than even that set by the good-living people and right-living people of Mohntown. Passing strange as it may be and as unbelievable as it may seem, none there was who could tell of any lie, white or black, that Mary Burlinson had ever told or even performed. 

Her sense of justice was of the superlative. Her sole known weakness and one which brought to her oft times much suffering of mind and heart was supersensitiveness. Many a word and many an act, never intended to hurt or wound, brought tears to the eyes of Mary Burlinson. 

What shall we say of Mary as regards her physical

OPPOSITES 

appearance? Sturdy of build and medium height. A face that reflected a healthy body. Clear skin-cheeks full of blood-large brown eyes, honest always, could not deceive, eyes that spoke in a kindly way, eyes that had a soft appeal; hair of the chestnut or of the next darker shade; a Cupid-bow mouth of red cherry tint; in all a good wholesome face of a very good girl. 

Helen was of the negative sort. Slight of build, lightly constructed, a little girl in every way, short and lean, small of feature, eyes almost void of shade; might have been gray with the blue washed out. Timid, retiring, chief characteristic loyalty to Mary, and a true friend to everybody.

While it cannot be disputed that the Jameson and Burlinson girls were almost inseparable, still it is likewise true that there existed an unexplained something spoiling by a shadow of a shade the fullness of a perfect harmony; yes, there was a something by many unsuspected, but still not unnoticed by a few, that prevented theirs being a flawless friendship.

Through the winter and the spring there were parties and social affairs, in attendance at which invariably were found the Jameson girls, the Burlinson girls and 

Fred Davis. And so it went without much to vary or alter conditions until summer time came, when the Jamesons went to their summer home in Norwald, while the Burlinsons remained in Mohntown. It was arranged, however, that before the summer's ending the Burlinson girls should pay the Jamesons a visit. 

When the Jamesons departed for Norwald, Fred thereupon and without delay ceased his visits to Mohntown. And so it was at the birth of that particular summer, or at least so it was with our quintette of young friends.

CHAPTER XXVI 

Now it so happened in those days closely following the departure of the Jamesons that Mary met a youth who had come to Mohntown. None seemed to know why and none seemed to know when he had come there, but some there were who said that after meeting Mary he liked the place so well that he decided to spend at least some if not much of his time in the home town of Mary. His was a culture a bit more than extreme. 

While apparently possessed of substantial financial means, the source of his income was one more thing none seemed to know nor able to learn. Some days and some nights he would be absent from Mohntown, but on every evening that Mohntown held him he was in the company of Mary. In many respects his distinctive qualities were similar to Mary's. He was most times quiet almost to reticence. He spoke little or nought of himself. He was much alone, except when with Mary. His whereabouts when absent from Mohntown he imparted to none. Evidently there was some bothersome trouble that refused to vacate his worried mind which being observed by many, including Mary, she one evening made bold to inquire what lay so heavy upon him and rendered him so sad appearing, to which questioning he became most evasive. 

He liked Mary, yes, he liked her very much. He liked her in that she was different from any girl he had ever met. She was so sincere, so true. He felt satisfied, too, that she could be trusted with any secret, but he had a very particular reason for wishing to withhold his very special secret from Mary as well as from the entire world. He felt, too, that did she have 173

OPPOSITES 

knowledge of that which at times did depress him she would keenly suffer from the knowing. Ceaselessly he tried forgetting, but could not. There were times he was encouraged in the hope that Mary might be his means of forgetting, just as quickly followed by the knowledge that his hope was but a false and futile one, for well he knew to tell her his secret sorrow, the sharing of it by two would render it less liable of being dismissed from the memory than if held by one alone. So he did not reveal the cause of his heavy dejection, at least not until quite a spell following Mary's first bid for his confidence.

But then came the night previous to Mary's departure for Norwald. Mary and he had spent the afternoon of that day together. They had gone to a nearby park and there had sat under the trees close to where the band was playing to the delight of many, but whether those two heard the music none can say but themselves, yet it is true they talked but little as under the trees they sat that summer afternoon. In the evening they dined at the park restaurant, where few words were exchanged as they ate, and yet they seemed mutually glad to have the company of each other. Following dinner they strolled off to the lake side, and found there a bench, apart from others of its kind, which they proceeded to occupy. Off in the distance the band still played, but it is quite certain that just then those two heard it not. Boats passed by on the lake, but the boats of a certainty they did not see. For quite a while they sat there, saying little and yet quite pleased that they, just they two, were there, or at least it seemed they were pleased. And then Mary in her honest, open way, without subterfuge or scheming said, "We are both strangely silent, you and I today. Just why it is I only partly know. Tomorrow I am going away for a time, and when two have been in the other's company as frequently as you and I have been during

OPPOSITES 

175 this summer, and the companionship has been so congenial that we both desire it to continue, then I think you will not deny that it will be with keen regret on the part of us both that there has come an interruption to whatever pleasure there has been in our associating one with the other. I am honest when I say I shall miss your companionship; you have been so kind to me. I believe that you, too, will miss our walks, our talks and our association with each other, but there is yet a something that is not explained. I am coming back again, and I believe you too intend returning to Mohntown occasionally, even if you do not remain here permanently. Fully believing that you and I are not taking final leave of each other, it cannot be that this parting which is only temporary is all that moves you to silent sadness. I hope you will pardon this openness and frankness of speech, but it is better that we do so speak as to remove all doubt and possible misunderstanding as to our meanings and intentions. "I know there is something that is eating away your heart. You are so good, it is not a crime or a wrong you have done that is destroying your peace of mind. 

I cannot believe that death has claimed one that you loved, as I am satisfied you would not have hesitated. to confide that. Your health cannot be the source of your worry, as your very appearance belies that. "No, it is something other than any of the things that I have mentioned. I wish you could tell me. I promise that I'll never betray your secret, whatever it may be. You and I have been such close friends that 

I confidently believe I might help you in your trial. I am not prying; it is not curiosity prompting me to drag from you something that you are guarding so closely. 

Honestly it is just because I want to be a help to you. 99 To this he made no reply. In truth he did not speak of aught during the space of many watch ticks. While he was silent she knew that his mind was actively

OPPOSITES 

working and that he was fighting out something within himself. She knew a contest was on with his heart as its battle ground. For a while, for quite a long while, they sat in silence on that bench by the lake side. Then observing a softening of facial lines, hard and hitherto unyielding, she was well aware that if ever he told the time was just then. So, reaching out and taking his hand, softly she said, "Tell me; you will feel better if you do. I know I can help you. 99 Although he did not take his hand away, she knew he had not fully surrendered, for he merely allowed his hand to remain in hers; that was all, just merely allowed it to remain. There was no reciprocal grasp. "I hope you will pardon me," she said, "I have presumed too much. I hope you do not misunderstand me.

I thought I was the friend to whom you could and would reveal that which is gnawing at and devouring your very existence. I believed that the only remedy was for you to confide in someone; I thought I was that one. If I am mistaken, please forgive. I shall not mention the matter again. Isn't the reflection on the water gorgeous?" "Miss Burlinson, Mary, I am a bore. I fear I have offended you, although I did not so intend. You are different, so different, from other girls. I believe you are the only person in the whole world who could speak to me as you have done tonight without losing my faith in your intentions, and to prove that I do believe in you, as difficult as it may be to do so, I am going to tell you my story, and further than that I must add that you are the only person in the whole wide world to whom I could or should tell it. This last remark may need some explaining, which I am going to do by relating my narrative in the same open manner you have used in speaking to me tonight. I am going to bare this secret of mine and you shall know it void of cape and bare of cover. For me to reveal that which you

OPPOSITES 

177 are soon to hear must convince you of my high regard for you and of my confidence in you, as well as that in my heart there must be a great liking for you and when you have heard me through, you will be well satisfied of all of that, but yet, Mary, that of which I should and shall speak is of my feeling for another, and what may seem still more strange is that I shall not speak of a buried feeling, but of one that still lives. 

With knowledge of all this, if you prefer not to hear, let me know. Tell me now, Mary.'

An embarrassing silence ensued, broken in the end by Mary, as gently she said, "Tell me. It will be best all around."

Then he held her hands very tight in his, but his eyes failed to meet those of Mary's. As a matter of fact, apparently there was nothing at all near at hand that he saw, for his gaze seemed fixed on the nothingness of the obscured spaces on yonder lakeside.

CHAPTER XXVII 

"Well, Mary, the story had its beginning in my early days, yes, while I was still in the high school of my old home town. My father was not wealthy, in fact, quite removed from financial independence. Most of the pupils in my class, it so happened, were born of parents well to do. I was welcome to associate with all members of the class and every one of them was kind and friendly to me, but certain pleasures were theirs that the means of my parents would not allow, which difference I felt most keenly. In the whole class there was not a snob, but still I always felt an existing line that divided me from most of the others. "There was a girl in that class-not particularly pretty, but with the sweetest face I ever have seen.

During classes I liked to watch that face. It dispelled many a cloud. Its owner did not pay any particular attention to me, at least not any more than the other girls did, but unknown to her she was my angel of light. She was unaware that I worshipped her; she could not be otherwise; I never told her. I never told anyone. "Many school days became memory days, and in due time we were seniors. It was in the winter preceding the June when we should pass out of the old school.

There was much snow that season, and many members of the class owned their own horses and sleighs. This angel of mine was one of these fortunate ones, and had the family coachman to drive her wherever she desired to go. "It so happened that on Saturdays I did whatever I could toward earning a little money to help my father 178

OPPOSITES 

179 keep me in school. So I worked and saved, while 

Father deprived himself of not a few necessities that I might get the education he desired for me. But I digress and must return to my story. "During that particular winter which I mentioned my Saturday's work took me several miles from my home town. I went early in the morning and on foot to my place of employment, and I walked home late in the darkness of the evening time. "One Saturday I had finished my work, and, as I was trudging homeward over the hard, frozen roads, I heard behind me the tinkling of sleigh-bells, and moved to the side of the road to let the sleigh pass by, but as it came near it first slackened its pace, then came to a stop beside me, and as a voice called my name, my eyes in seeking the speaker beheld my angel in the rear seat of the sleigh. Mary, that evening she was more than adorable. The clear and crisp winter air had brought to her cheeks, usually pale, an abundance of richest crimson. Her eyes, always kind, shone bright that night, as with a cheery smile, revealing two rows of even teeth white as the glistening snow, she bade me enter the sleigh. In response to her invitation I proceeded to seat myself by the coachman, but she instantly let me know that it was by her that she desired me to sit. She was exceedingly pleasant, and during the entire ride she chatted with me as though for a number of years we had been closest of friends. 

For a time I was ill at ease, but her quiet and pleasing manner soon dissipated my embarrassment that must have been apparent to her. The drive was all too short. 

I had expected that she would take me to the street where she should turn to go direct to her home. I did not observe that we had passed that point until she inquired as to the location of my home, learning which she directed the coachman to drive there. It took but a short time, a very short time, to arrive at my resi-

OPPOSITES 

dence. Most gratefully I expressed my appreciation and then leaving with me an ever living 'Good night,' she was gone and on her homeward way. 

As they drove away I stood and watched my angel until the sleigh had passed out of sight. 66 

As I entered my home my father, observing my elevation of spirits, inquired as to the cause, to which questioning, I avoided making reply. 66 'My boy, you act as though you were in love, and even better as though your sweetheart had accepted you,' he teasingly said. "I made no answer. I ate but little; I went early to my room. For hours I was unable to sleep, but when slumber came delightful were my dreams, for in those dreams I saw snow and a sleigh and a sweet angel of light. "During the remainder of the school term I saw Estelle (for that was her name) almost daily, although there was little opportunity to do other than adore and worship her at a distance. We were graduated from high school the following spring. During the summer 

I saw her but once. I met her one evening near her residence. As we were going in the same direction I walked with her. Arriving at her home, we stood and talked a bit, then she invited me to enter, which with mingled embarrassment and pleasure I did. "Estelle's family was a wealthy one. Her father had retired from business. His health had been poor, in consequence of which he traveled much, hoping to regain thereby what close application to the acquiring of wealth had taken from him. Her mother was haughty and proud. On the night of my visit she was polite but not cordial. I did not remain long. As I left the house, Estelle invited me to call there again. I never did. "The following fall I entered college. My father had stinted, worked and saved that I might do so. On

OPPOSITES 

181 one pretext or another I wrote to Estelle on several occasions. As she always replied, I was encouraged. "Then one day, one cold, bleak, black day, a girl who had been a member of my high school class back in the old town chanced to be in the city where my chosen college was located. Meeting me on the street, she rendered that fateful day still more cold, still more bleak and still more black. By the usual method of telling things obtained in confidence that one must not repeat, she told me she did not wish to hurt my feelings, but thought there was something I should know. 

She said she was quite sure I would tell no one that which she would reveal to me, and then proceeded to confide in me a story to the effect that Estelle's mother had said (to whom she said it I never yet have learned) that I was a most estimable young man, but that she had higher ambitions for Estelle's future. 

I never wrote to Estelle again, nor have I seen her since. My last farewell was said at the front door of her palatial home on that summer night long years ago. "That is my story, Mary. Odd for me to tell you this, isn't it? But you wanted to know what was in my mind and in my heart and I wanted you to know, but it was difficult to tell, especially difficult to tell you. 

Knowing my secret, Mary, do you think it best we should not see each other again? That would be hard for me, for I really do like you a lot, a tremendous lot."

Once again a painful silence held sway, as before broken by Mary. "You have been frank with me, so must I be with you. I am very fond of you, so much so that had you asked a certain question I most likely should have answered 'yes', but now I must be a questioner-just one question I must ask: Do you still care for Estelle?" "Mary, she is often in my thoughts; there is something that will not let me forget her. While you have

OPPOSITES 

brought much happiness into my life and I care a lot for you, still it is true that it is with the most pleasant recollections that I still think of Estelle. I think it best that I not ask you what at times has been in mind. and on my tongue, but that we part with pleasing memories of the days we have spent together. We may meet again. Meanwhile, if you will permit it, I'll write you from time to time and hope to hear from you in return. Let it be just that way for the present, and I feel sure the matter will work out its own solution to the best interests of us both. It is an odd request, Mary, a very odd request, but it will please me if you will grant it. Will you, Mary?" "Yes, your request is indeed odd, most odd, but let me consider a bit before answering. I feel that Estelle was somehow meant for you, but you-you must lack something a something that did you but possess it, you would go to her. I somehow believe she would say 'yes', but then, too, in frankness I must say thatwell, on second thought, I think this one time frankness might not be construed as such. Probably you are right in that by separating, but not entirely severing the tie between us, the problem may solve itself. When do you go ?" "Mary, tonight is the last time I may see you for a long while, but I can never forget you. I shall always think of you in the kindliest sort of way. The hour is quite late, Mary. Let us be going." 

The band had long since ceased playing; boats no longer passed by on the lake. Eventime had turned to night time and night time was growing old. Thus it was as those two turned homeward; few words were spoken on the way, and when they reached Mary's house night time had become early morning time. Then at the door they paused; they clasped hands.
