183 

Mary said, "Good night, good bye until we meet again.' 

He said, "Good night, not good bye. You are going to hear from me. I hope you will let me have word from you. I trust you have not lost faith in me. 

Mary, with her soft brown eyes looking full into his, replied: "I have all faith in and all respect for you. 

Releasing his hand she passed within the house. He stood for a space, then slowly made his way up the street to his boarding house and to his room. The following day he left Mohntown and Mohntown saw him not again for many days.

CHAPTER XXVIII 

Meanwhile in Norwald the summer passed much as had previous summers. A few new faces were seen; a few old ones were missing. The baseball schedules had been completed and the Fall Festival had been observed. Fred Davis performed valuable service for the Northwald Ball Team, and his pitching, in fact his general all around playing was largely instrumental in again bringing to Northwald the baseball championship.

Reta and Fred were much together and the people of Norwald took it as a settled fact that soon Reta would become Mrs. Davis and remain with Fred in Norwald. 

The Burlinson girls while in Norwald spent most of their time in the company of the Jameson sisters. 

Then came the time when that summer had a place only on the pages of memory's book. Once again the 

Jamesons as well as the Burlinsons were back in Mohntown, while Fred remained in Norwald, resuming, of course, his visits to the home of Reta.

Still the Jameson and Burlinson sisters remained almost inseparable. Of course, others too of both sexes were often in their company, the personnel of the men varying from time to time, except that Fred was always one of the party, he having become quite well acquainted with all four of the girls.

Those close to Reta observed a change in her that promised better things. Seemingly she had conquered her inborn selfishness. She had learned to see good in others. She evidenced a willingness to bend her own will, especially where Fred was concerned, and none of these things was unnoticed. 184

OPPOSITES 

185 

It has been said, "True love never runs smooth." If such be so, then there was not true love between Reta and Fred, for ever since the injury to Fred there had been harmony, apparently perfect, between them. Regardless of whether they were in love or not, the river on which they floated their canoe was entirely void of disturbing waves. Not the slightest ripple interfered with the course on life's waters which their love boat pursued. Reta seemed most happy and Fred quite content. All seemed well, and the folk of Mohntown and Norwald looked each day for the announcement of their engagement. So it might have been, but one day the unexpected happened.

In the world are many types, possessed as they are of numerous and varied qualities-some fixed and some changeable. Of a certain type are those who believe they are gaining most pleasure for themselves when creating unhappiness for others. Making others unhappy not because of spite, not because of pique, not because of malice nor because of hate; but just why 'tis so seems impossible to explain. It is a part of their nature, and sometimes it seems they do certain things without consciousness of so doing, and with absolute absence of slightest motive. 

Into the lives of Reta and Fred there came one day just one of that sort. Once before he held a part in making for the unhappiness of both Reta and Fred. It was his taunt on that tragic Festival Day that resulted in Reta's occupying a seat on the Southwald side of the ball field, which incident was almost directly responsible for Fred's accident. 

Perry Spencer one Sunday visited Mohntown, and on the afternoon of that day he called at the home of the Jamesons, and there accepted an invitation to remain to the evening meal. He had a way of being present at meal time.

It had been the custom of Fred to spend Sunday

OPPOSITES 

afternoons with Reta and with her attend the church of which Reta was a member. For some reason on the 

Sunday of Spencer's visit to the Jamesons Fred had been detained in Norwald; he telegraphed Reta that it would be impossible for him to be in Mohntown previous to church time, but that he would meet her at the church following the closing of the services. 

Following tea time Mrs. Jameson, her two daughters and Spencer gathered in the library. After a time 

Reta explained to Perry that she and Laine intended attending church and added that they would be glad to have him go with them, to which invitation he responded, "If a certain young man were in town, I doubt whether you would invite me to be your escort this evening.' 99 "I am not doing that now. As a matter of fact I do expect to meet a certain young man after church." "Which being the case, makes me an interloper." "The conditions do not and cannot possibly be construed to warrant such an opinion. Laine, you and I can attend the services and later be joined by Fred without in any sense making of you an interloper. The four of us can return to our home and you may remain with us until train time, unless you prefer not to do so." "You do not quite understand me, Reta. I called today to see you, and if I go to church with you it must be as your escort to and from that place." 

She said: "Don't be absurd, Perry, I am expecting to meet Fred." "You seem to be very fearful of offending Fred." "You are discussing a matter that does not concern you." "Well, just the same, I believe you are fearful of incurring Fred's displeasure. 

Laine interrupted to say, "Perry, I think you are assuming entirely too much in more than one respect. 

I expect to accompany whomsoever goes to church,

OPPOSITES 

187 whether it be Reta and you or just Reta, and I further expect to return home with Reta and Fred. I do not consider myself an interloper, regardless of who is in the party. Neither will Reta nor Fred," to which 

Perry responded: "Always a champion for Fred. However, I dare Reta to accept me as her escort home tonight. You should not object. It would give you an opportunity to have Fred as your escort." 

Laine in puzzled tones inquired: "Perry, are you serious or are you simply trifling?" "Whatever I am, I dare Reta. She is, I believe, afraid to offend Fred and thereby lose him." 

Reta fell for the ruse, and Perry Spencer had made for himself a coup. "I'll prove to you that whither or with whom I go or whatever else I do tonight or at any other time is not subject to the will or whim of any man. If you have the courage you may accompany me both to and from church tonight; I accept your invitation and your challenge as well." "Why, Reta," exclaimed Laine, "do not do that.”" "Well, Fred should have been here yesterday or at latest early today." 

Laine in a sadly warning manner replied, "Reta, I fear you are drifting back into the clutches of your one great weakness." "If you believe that to be true, and if you so desire, you may tell Fred of my shameful actions. You seem quite interested in his welfare. You may have him for tonight, if you wish. You have my consent." 

To this Laine heatedly interjected, "Reta, Fred is not one to be handed about at the wish or choice of anyone. He has his own mind and can choose for himself." "Laine, your attitude makes me still more determined. I cannot be crossed or defied. I am going to

OPPOSITES 

church with Perry and he has my permission to accompany me home.

For the first time Mrs. Jameson took part in the discussion. All the while she had been intently listening, and as the three were about to depart she called 

Reta aside and said: "Do not be foolish, Reta. You made one mistake, almost a fatal one where Fred was concerned; don't repeat it." 

Reta kissed her mother, but made no reply.

CHAPTER XXIX 

Fred was disappointed that it was necessary to disarrange the plans he had made to go to Mohntown on that fateful Saturday, but the illness of one of his salesmen had necessitated the changing and rearranging of the itinerary of one of the other salesmen. It was this that delayed Fred's departure from Norwald until late the following afternoon.

He arrived at the church a short time previous to the close of the services. The auditorium being on the second floor, Fred waited for Reta at the foot of the stairway. That night the congregation was unusually large. As Reta did not appear for quite a spell, Fred feared she had either passed out unobserved by him, or that for some reason she had not attended church that evening. As he had about determined to go to the 

Jameson house, he saw descending the stairway Reta, Laine and Perry Spencer. As he advanced to meet them, Reta took the arm of Perry. Fred was quick to see the act, and in the seeing thereof as well as the facial expression of both Reta and that of Laine, he knew that all was not as he had expected it to be. As 

Reta reached the foot of the stairway and met Fred's surprised and questioning gaze, her lips moved but without sound; then, still clinging to the arm of Perry, who awkwardly raised his hat, she passed out of the church. Laine hesitated a moment and, as Fred, stunned and bewildered stood undetermined what to do, she advanced to his side. "Fred," she said, "come with me. As we walk home. 

I shall try to explain." 

Those two, Laine and Fred, followed Reta and 189

OPPOSITES 

Perry quite some distance in the rear of the latter pair, when after an awkward spell of silence Fred inquired as to the meaning of it all, which Laine endeavored to explain. "Fred, Reta has a quality and a weakness of which she is well aware. At times and under certain conditions she seems unable to overcome its hold upon her. 

Believing that her independence and freedom of thought and action are at stake when put to the test of choosing between following her own desire or doing as another may wish or request, she has made many mistakes, of some of which you have knowledge. She had, I hoped, overcome this unfortunate characteristic, and I believe would finally have conquered it and herself, had not Perry Spencer placed her in the position of choosing between giving evidence of fear of consequence, if she should offend you and thereby exhibit a lack of independence, or else by accepting the dare of Perry demonstrate that she is mistress of her own will."

Laine then proceeded to describe in detail the happenings that had led to Reta's actions of the evening. "Fred, I know even now she is sorry, but that indescribable 'something' that holds her fast will not allow her to repair the harm. I believe she would give much to have you with her this very moment, but that horrible 'something' is stronger than she, and unless it is conquered it will ruin her whole life.' 99 

Walking along Fred listened but made no response until they neared the Jameson home, when he halted 

Laine and said: "Laine, I appreciate all you have done. You are a real friend. I must leave you now." "Are you not going to the house?" "Laine, do you really think I would, or could, or should?"

OPPOSITES 

191 "I think if you do that things may in some way be righted." "No, Laine. I am going back to Norwald on the next train. I shall always have of you the most pleasing of thoughts. I hope you will explain matters to your mother as best you can or desire. She has always been more than kind to me. If I can at any time be of service to either you or her, simply let me know. It will be a pleasure to serve either of you. Unless Reta inquires, tell her nothing. Should she ask concerning me, tell her I accept her action as a dismissal; just that, nothing more. It is sufficient." "Fred, I am indeed sorry. Oh, I wish-." She did not finish, but took his hand and held it very tight. 

Under the strong street lamp light sad but wistful eyes peered through glistening tears. Then she did an unexpected thing. She hastily kissed the hand she held, quickly released it and fled without further word. 

Fred stood for a space, his eyes following Laine until she had entered her home, glowing with light from entrance to roof. Through a window he saw herjust a moment he watched her and then he was gone. 

He went back to Norwald, expecting that if ever he came to Mohntown it would not be for many days.

CHAPTER XXX 

As the train rolled along toward Norwald Fred's thoughts were many and varied. He considered the personal side of it. He analyzed himself and without exercise of egotism or conceit he discovered nothing in his treatment of Reta for which he could censure himself. Always he had tried to humor her; perhaps he had done so to the extreme and to his own detriment. He had endeavored to overlook her peculiar selfishness. In many ways she was totally unselfish. He placed that to her credit and then sought to find wherein he was at fault. Earnestly and fairly he judged himself, but in his treatment of Reta he could find nothing with which to accuse himself. Half aloud he uttered, "Well, after all, better now than later."

Then his thoughts turned to Laine. Somehow he could not figure Laine. She had all the good qualities of Reta, with a total absence of such ones as hurt both Reta herself and her friends. Yes, he liked Laine. 

It might have been different if he had met Laine before becoming acquainted with Reta, but it was all too late to give that a thought. Laine was a good kid, but, well that was all out of the question. Laine was not a second choice article, and anyhow he did not care to return to the Jameson house; Reta would be there. 

That would be embarrassing. Then it was rather presuming, anyhow, to think of Laine in that sort of way, but somehow he could not forget his parting with her. 192

CHAPTER XXXI 

Then as he rode he became more and more analytical. He analyzed himself, he analyzed Reta. He could see it now. There was something in the chemistry of the two that would not mix. It somehow seemed well that the thing had happened. As contemptible and unwarranted as had been Reta's actions, it was well that the break had come. Had it not or had not something similar have occurred, it was not improbable they should have some day been married. His analysis demonstrated that this would have been a mistake.

They were not intended one for the other. He had been expecting to grieve. Singularly no symptoms of grief had appeared. He was strangely relieved that everything with Reta was at an end. It was all so strange-no grief-no disappointment-no regretvery strange, but true.

Then his thoughts took another turn. He somehow thought of the church; whether it was because churches were connected with weddings, or whether the unpleasant incidents of the evening had the church as its stage setting, he did not know, but somehow his thoughts were of the church, the church that teaches love for fellow man, the church that teaches charity, not the charity that drops a clinking coin into a cup by poverty's hand thrust forth, then passes on and forgets, but the charity that is in the heart and prompts a kindly deed, the charity that is love, the charity that prompts a kindly deed, the charity that is love, the charity that prompts man to exceed the golden rule. 

What did it all mean? Was the church sincere and consistent, or did it fail in its teachings? Reta was a 193

OPPOSITES 

member of the church; she was a regular communicant; she was faithful in her attendance. Well, either the church was not properly handled or it was in the hands of the wrong people. The church never taught Reta to treat him as she had done. If it did, the church was wrong. If it did not so teach her, then whatever it did teach was of little effect.

He had expected to grieve and to pity himself for many days, but somehow he seemed glad that it was over, all over. He and Reta were strangers. Fate had been good to him. He wished Reta well, but he was glad that he knew the real Reta before it became too late. To have been even unorious would not have satisfied Reta. Of course, Laine was a good kid and he should miss seeing her once in a while, but then everything had worked out for the best. He knew it. 

The train rolled lazily into the station at Norwald. 

Fred was glad to be back. Boarding a trolley he went home, and without delay proceeded directly to his room and went straightway to bed. Yes, he had expected to grieve. He had held anticipations of a restless night, but as his head touched the refreshing cool of the soft white pillow came the thought, "Reta was not intended for me"; that was all, and he was soon fast asleep, and the sleep that he slept was deep and was sound and to body and mind most restful, and he slept continuously all through the night until morning light peeked through the pane to waken him from peaceful and health-giving slumber, and as the sun in its glory proclaimed that a new day was born, while he had expected to grieve his thoughts were least of all of Reta.

CHAPTER XXXII 

That day he went about his business much as he had done every other Monday morning, except that unconsciously he seemed to exhibit evidence of relief from some burden, a burden of which he had been unaware that he had been carrying. 

Tuesday was the same, and came Wednesday. About to leave his office, his 'phone bell rang. Taking up the receiver, he was surprised to hear the voice of Laine. "Fred, I feared I should be too late to catch you. 

I want you to come here at once. Mother is very ill.

She believes that she is dying, and has urged me to have you come so that she may see you before she passes on. Will you come, Fred?" "Laine, I am so sorry. As your mother desires it, I'll come without delay. I can catch the 5:45. I hope for the best, Laine. Bear up until I see you.' 99 

Fred did catch the 5:45, and arriving at Mohntown on schedule time went directly to the Jameson home. 

As he approached the house, his emotions being very much mingled, he endeavored to prepare himself for any situation that might present itself. As he proceeded to ascend the steps leading to the porch, Doctor 

Clayton came from the house. "How is Mrs. Jameson, Doctor?" inquired Fred. "I am sorry, but Mrs. Jameson has just passed away.

She asked for you and I regret that you did not arrive just a bit sooner. 

Fred hesitated as to his next step. The doctor evidently believing that Fred's intention would be to immediately enter the house, had not closed the door. As 

Fred deliberated, a sudden gust of air blowing the door 195

OPPOSITES 

wide open, through the doorway he perceived Laine descending the broad stairway from the floor above. 

Likewise she saw him, and as she advanced to greet him he knew quite well she was pleased he had come. 

He took her hand with the remark, "I am sorry, Laine, more so than I can tell you.' 

Attempting to release the girl's hand he found her grasp on his tightening, and just as had happened a few days before, with the clasping of hands came the filling of her eyes with teardrops. "I am glad you came, Fred," she said, kissing his hand just as she had done on that former occasion. "I am sorry that I am late, Laine.”

Still holding his hand she guided him to a divan on which she seated him, then sat down by his side. "Fred, Mother was very fond of you. The affair of Sunday night much distressed her, and the following day she suffered a stroke. Regaining consciousness today, her first words were to ask for you. Soon after 

I 'phoned you, her condition became worse. We sent for Dr. Clayton, but Mother left us soon after the doctor arrived. Before she went she beckoned me to her side and said: 'Laine, I know I am going. I want 

Fred to be one of those to carry me to my last rest. 

Arrange it as best you can. I had some hopes, Laine, you understand, I think. Good bye, Dear. Do what you can for Reta and Fred. Good bye.' Then lapsing into unconsciousness, she passed on to join Father, the man she adored." "How is Reta, Laine?" "She is quite ill. She knows she made a mistake 

Sunday night, and when she realized that Mother's illness was the result of her wilful actions, she broke and has been in her room under the doctor's care ever since. She does not know that Mother has gone." 

Fred remained in Mohntown until the next day and

OPPOSITES 

197 helped to make arrangements for the funeral. He did not see Reta, and she was unaware that he was in the house.

CHAPTER XXXIII 

On the following Saturday they buried Mrs. Jame son. Fred was present and served as a pall bearer. At the house he did not see Reta. At the cemetery their eyes met for a fleeting moment, but, of course, there was no opportunity to exchange words.

The carriage which conveyed Fred also carried a nephew of Mrs. Jameson, with whom Fred returned to the Jameson home, but did not enter the house. As he remained on the porch conversing with the girls' cousin, Laine came out. Apologizing for the interruption, she said, "I came out to thank you for being present, Fred." She then invited him to go within, but he explained that he had promised to attend a meeting at the church to discuss the advisability of holding evangelistic services, and that previous to that meeting he must get a bite to eat. As he extended his hand at parting the girl once again grasped it. Again came tears, eloquent tears with a tale to tell. Fred thought they were sorrow's tears for loss of Mother. Had the tale been told, it might have been as Fred believed, or it might have been a story of something entirely different. 

Upon leaving Laine, Fred proceeded immediately to the church, of which although not a member, he contributed generously to its support, and it was largely in consequence of his financial aid that he had been invited to attend that particular meeting. 

There was much discussion as to the wisdom of engaging an evangelist, during all of which Fred sat silent. Not being a member of the church, he felt that he was not entitled to a voice; furthermore, as he pos- 198

OPPOSITES 

199 sessed decided views which were much at variance with most of those expressed by the others in attendance at the conference, he was not desirious of jeopardizing the good will of any of the persons present by voicing his opinions.

After a time, much to his regret, the pastor requested him to make known his views. Although Fred endeavored to make it clear that he considered himself entitled to neither vote nor voice in the matter, the pastor persistently insisted, which resulted in Fred's giving utterance to the following: "My friends, whenever I express my opinion I endeavor to fully cover the subject matter at hand; with me there is no mincing of words; I try to avoid creating any mistaken impressions as to my opinions, and therefore speak in plain phrases. In fact, I make my language so clear and so plain that none can mistake my meaning. Now folks, I value the friendship of everyone present so highly I fear to risk a chance of losing the good feeling of any one of you. My views, friends, are not fully in accord with some of yours, and for that reason I hesitate to comply with your request that I discuss the matter in hand."

To this the pastor replied: "We know you so well and have such confidence in your good intentions and sincerity of purpose that we are satisfied your words would express the result of careful, honest and wise consideration that and nothing more. No one can take, that is, justly take, exception to an honest opinion. 

"Well, my friends, here goes, and possibly I may say 'Here go my friends.' I hope that at the conclusion of my talk you will still retain friendly feeling for me. If not, let the blame be on the heads of those insisting that I make known my ideas. I intend to discuss this matter fully and in a manner far different from what I have belief you expect me to do.

OPPOSITES 

I am going to dig deep; I am going to endeavor to upturn and turn up sufficient of the ground on which the church is erected that there can be no misunderstanding as to my views. "If the church were well founded on solid and proper grounds, evangelists might as well take in their shingles. If the church as a church is to accomplish anything worth while, it must have vision and that vision must be the salvation of what is known as the soul of mankind. Such a vision must be mirrored in the eye of the church. To make the vision real, in my opinion, the church must repair and reconstruct. No general of any army can gain full measure of success unless there be absolute harmony, unless every individual of that army has before him a vision of that for which every man under command of his general is fighting. Every man must be true to his flag and to his country, otherwise, victory is either unattainable or at best delayed in its fulfillment. The army as a whole must be true to the cause, but each man in the army must be true not only to the army as a whole but to every other man in the army. No business can attain its full measure of success unless there be harmony. 

Capital must recognize the full value of labor, and labor must recognize the full necessity of capital. Without that full recognition, neither can obtain the result which they both seek. The executives of every organization, possessing of necessity first a definite vision themselves, must make that vision clear to and understood by every part and unit of that organization; then, whether it be an army, a corporation, a church, or aught else, it must proceed to mend and repair and reconstruct within itself before seeking or expecting to gain and hold the respect and confidence of those outside of the organization. "Now, let us consider the church. The vision, in my opinion, as before stated, is the salvation of men's

OPPOSITES 

201 souls. The church, to bring to pass a realization of that vision to the full extent, must have those to do the work whose own manner of living entitles them to that same salvation. Among them there must be harmony; there must be no jealousy; there must be no envy; there must be charity. The faults and shortcomings of one must be unnoticed or overlooked by all of the others. "The people of a community must be attracted to a church by the lives lived by those in and of the church. "The transactions and operations of the church as a body must be such that those outside of the church should be glad and anxious to join with the body. Backbiting, pique, jealousy, self aggrandizement, must all be unknown. The pastor must be a man who has taken up the work for the sole purpose of saving souls and for no other reason. The business man, the professional man, the politician, in fact, everyone who unites with the church must do so for nothing other than the saving of souls. So must it be with every integral part of the church. "Then, when the church is right within and with itself, it must pursue the proper methods of attracting to it those in need of salvation. If such be not attracted, then they must be sought. "How to attract is the next problem. Various communities require different methods. Every church must have full confidence of not only its own community, but of every person with whom it may possibly come in contact. It can gain this only by proving its sincerity. 

A church, to attract, must be unassailable to the charge of hypocrisy. To attract, it must not be known as a rich man's church; it must not be known as a one-man church; it must not be known as a cold church; it must be nothing that repels. It must attract. "The best way to attract anyone is to give him that which pleases him most. To attract, my method

OPPOSITES 

would provide for those we seek anything not conflicting with whatever dignity or whatever sacredness is properly, remember, I say, properly, due and a part of the church. If athletics attract anyone to the church, let us have athletics as a part of the church. If motion pictures attract one to the church, let us have them. 

All of these things must, of course, be properly supervised. Whether it be a gymnasium, a baseball team, a football team, a social room, or aught else that becomes the attraction, it must be so managed and so controlled that the ones attracted cannot fail to understand that religion is a serious thing. At the same time let them know it is not a mournful matter. The one attracted must know too that the church does not teach that to be saved one must abandon all pleasure. "Then, when you of the church have been successful in attracting one by giving one what one really wants and desires, the church will hold that one always, provided one finds the church sincere. If this plan were adopted and properly pursued, no evangelist should, in my opinion, be needed. Make the one attracted love the church and that for which it stand; then the one attracted to the church will by example and endeavor do much, very much, of permanent good by gathering about him in the church those that the evangelist cannot reach by instilling fear of hell, by the shedding of tears or by creating and exciting over-wrought emotions. "It is a poor Christian that is driven into heaven by the heat of hell. It is a weak Christian that must be floated into heaven on a river of tears. Love makes the best Christian, love of the One who gave Himself to suffer that there might be salvation. Attract those outside to the church by use of any legitimate means. 

Then let them know that the whole church is founded on unselfish love. Let the church do nought to give the attracted ones the slightest thought or suspicion that

OPPOSITES 

203 it is not everything for which it claims to stand. Then no evangelist will be needed. Let me say that the evangelists may provide a means of saving some. 

Doubtless, as a result of the efforts of evangelists many will profess salvation. Doubtless, many will be carried away on a wave of emotional hysteria. But let me ask: where will many of them be within a year? In fairness 

I do say this: that if one is saved by an evangelist it is worth all the hysteria and everything that accompanies it. But everything accomplished by the paid evangelist, yes, even more than that can be attained, if all the members of the church will unite in preaching love and salvation, and then practise the love that they preach. "I hope you will pardon my frankness. You insisted that I speak. I will now retire so that if you wish to discuss what I have said you may do so more freely than if I remain. I am willing to financially support your campaign, if you decide to have one. I bid you good night. 99 Fred immediately left the auditorium, and as he was putting on his overcoat preparatory to leaving the building he heard his name spoken, and, turning, saw 

Mary Burlinson. "Fred," she said, "I wish you would walk with me to my home. As you know, there is a dark place just across the bridge over which I must pass. There is a person here tonight who has been endeavoring to pay me attention and I believe would escort me home, but I do not care for his company. In fact, I dislike his personality. You will therefore gain my everlasting gratitude, if you will see me to my home." "Why, certainly, Mary, I have sufficient time before the midnight train goes. I left the meeting for the reason that I wished to afford the committee opportunity to discuss my remarks in a manner that they might hesitate to do in my presence.' 99

OPPOSITES 

They walked leisurely to Mary's home. If Mary knew of Fred's unpleasant experience with Reta, she gave no evidence of possessing that knowledge. They discussed Mrs. Jameson's death; otherwise, the conversation was commonplace. Eventually arriving at the Burlinson home, Mary invited Fred to enter. As there remained quite some time before the departure of the train, he accepted the invitation. Mary's sister, Helen, was there. The two sisters, both of them, chatted with Fred until leaving time came. As he made his way to the station and while on the train which took him back to Norwald, Fred gave little thought to Mary, neither did he think of Helen.

CHAPTER XXXIV 

On the following morning Fred arose and went to the mill and there performed his duties in the usual routine manner. And thus it continued during the remainder of that week. Strange though it may seem, he thought little or nothing regarding Reta. Likewise, Mary Burlinson held very little of his meditations. If anyone in particular of the opposite sex occupied his mind, it was Laine.

However, the sun continued in its daily custom of rising and setting until by its light a new Sunday was born. Then it was that by force of habit Fred's mind reverted to thoughts of Mohntown and then in turn to Reta, but she was quite soon dismissed from consideration, but then likewise from force of habit came the church to his mind. In consideration of the church there came also the question of a sense of duty. He had pledged support to the evangelistic campaign, if such there was to be, and as he had heard nothing from Mohntown during the week he felt obligated to go there and ascertain what had been done regarding the matter of engaging an evangelist.

He arrived in Mohntown late in the afternoon, but did not call at the home of any of his friends. He partook of his evening meal at a restaurant, and then proceeded to church, which he entered simultaneously with the arrival of the pastor. 

He was informed by the clergyman that a committee which he had appointed to act for his church had arranged to carry on a joint campaign with others of the Mohntown churches. The evangelist engaged was to start his work in two weeks following special services 205

OPPOSITES 

to be held the next Sunday by all of the churches actively interested in the campaign. These services were intended to be preparatory to the work which the evangelist should personally conduct. Having acquainted 

Fred with these and some minor details, he proceeded to his study.

Fred selected a seat in a pew near the auditorium entrance. There was present a large congregation that night, numbered among which were Reta and Laine and Mary and Helen. Reta and Laine sat quite some distance from the pew occupied by Fred. For the first time since Reta had so meanly treated Fred he felt a slight touch of regret in that happening. It may have been that her presence in the same building brought back sentimental recollections of a companionship that had been on many occasions quite enjoyable, or it may have been that habit to which all of us are slaves to no small degree was responsible for the softening, for on that particular Sunday night a custom of long standing was to be broken, the custom of going from that particular church to the Jameson home, and it is quite probable that the breaking of the tie cut a trifle into the flesh as well as into the heart.

Mary and Helen, who had been rather late in arriving, sat quite near Fred. It did seem as though Mary had intended to sit elsewhere, but observing Fred had changed her plans.

The services were much as usual, except that the pastor urged the congregation to assist to the utmost in making the coming evangelistic services a success. 

As Fred was aware of nothing requiring his presence in Mohntown once the church services were at an end, he had planned to catch an early train homeward. With the furtherance of these plans in mind, he lost no time in his endeavor to put them into effect, but as he moved towards the door he was halted sufficiently long by one of the evangelistic committee to allow Mary and

OPPOSITES 

207 Helen to join him. With an apology to the man who had detained him, he hastily passed out of the auditorium, but the two girls, whom he had greeted politely and cordially, accompanied him until all three had reached a nearby street corner, where he made as though going to the railroad station, but before he could carry his desires into effect Mary, whose home lay in the opposite direction, inquired in a surprised sort of way, "Aren't you coming with us, Fred?" He replied: "I had intended taking the 9:30 train." Catching the clearly expressed disappointment in the girl's eye, he delayed his departure, and as he did Reta and

Laine passed by. Fred, although aware of their approaching, had not turned his head their way, and from him they received no recognition. The girls exchanged passing salutations, but Fred to all appearance was unaware of the nearby presence of the Jameson girls. 

Although despising himself for such treatment of Laine, he could not openly recognize her without taking notice of Reta, and this he was determined not to do. Then, whether it was just a bit of spite on Fred's part, whether it was a desire not to offend Mary, or whether so much time had been consumed that he could not then catch the early train, Fred had a change of heart as well as of mind, for as he watched the forms of Reta and Laine passing into the night he said: "Mary, I shall be glad to be permitted to once again conduct you safely over the dark bridge."

And so it was that he walked with the two girls to the Burlinson home and remained there until it was time to leave for the midnight train. Fred imagined. that Mary would have preferred Helen had not remained with them. Whether his thought was well founded only Mary knows.

CHAPTER XXXV 

Again another week, during the days of which Fred had devoted more than slight consideration to the two invitations of Mary to accompany her to her home. 

None could deny that Mary Burlinson was of a high type of girlhood. Fred was well aware of her many noble qualities, and he failed to bring to mind aught objectionable among her characteristics. Then, too, the girl was pleasing to the eye, but with it all he had an intuition that wisdom would advise his avoidance of being drawn with Mary into the current of a stream that might carry him on and on and into a port where some day it would be necessary to face an undesired situation, which he would be forced to treat in a manner to be regretted by all concerned. He admired Mary 

Burlinson; he would be glad to have her as a friend, but that was all-just a friend. 

Came another Sunday, and once again Fred journeyed to Mohntown. Riding along in the train and deeply thinking as he rode, he determined to attend some other church than was his custom. The church he finally selected was one of those which would have a part in the evangelistic campaign and where he felt sure he would receive the same information regarding the campaign as would be imparted at the First Church. 

As you may have already guessed, this change was occasioned by his desire to avoid meeting Mary, and thereby save himself possible drifting into channels of unpleasant complications. And that is how it happened that he attended the Second Church for the first time in his life. Quite many were gathered there, but he found a seat near the exit, having in mind the same 208

OPPOSITES 

209 idea as on the preceding Sunday, that of avoiding delaying his departure at the close of the services, so that he might catch the 9:30 train. Fred paid strict attention to all that was said regarding the coming campaign, in order that he might be well posted. Following the benediction he wasted no time in his departure. 

Feeling well satisfied with the success of his plans, he hastily descended the long stairway to the street level, but as he set foot on the sidewalk to his surprise and dismay he came face to face with Mary and Helen. 

Disappointed, chagrined, he realized that his well laid plans had met defeat; whether overthrown by accident or by design on the part of Mary, he could not decide, but he did know that his plans had gone awry. 

Striving to rapidly and gracefully and in good order rise above his embarrassment and confusion, and satisfied that he had regained full self control he gave ample proof of absence of tact when he said: "Why, girls, this is indeed a surprise," and then proceeded to undiplomatically flounder and blunder as he continued, "I thought you were attending the First Church tonight."

Purposely he had addressed the sisters collectively, but the reply came from Mary. Said she: "And I thought too, you would be there. Why did you come here?" "One reason is that it is closer to the station and I wanted to be quite sure of getting back to Norwald early tonight. I have a big day on hand for tomorrow." "And what are the other reasons?" 

Fred had his choice between brutal truth and polite lying. Choosing the latter, he said: 99 "Oh, nothing that would concern or interest you, Mary.' Then he lied again when he said, “I am glad we met though. But tell me how you happened to come this way?"

OPPOSITES 

Mary did not answer, for Mary was a girl never known to lie. On this occasion Helen lied for her. "Our services were short tonight, and as the evening was a pleasant one we decided to take a stroll before going home."

And then Fred added one more lie to his tally for the evening. “Well, I am glad you did, as it has been a pleasure to see you once more, but I must be going or I shall miss my train." 

Said Mary: "Fred, though the moon is full and the stars are brightly shining the big trees over the old bridge even on such a glorious night as this make it dark and dismal, and I am much afraid to pass that ugly spot on the far side, unless I am accompanied by one of the strong sex.' 

Fred then knew that his defeat was by design, but still he was determined that the defeat should not be a decisive one, neither would he submit to unconditional surrender. Quickly strategy prompted him to a bold counter-attack, so as the two girls stood facing him he stepped to the side of Helen and said, "Well, ladies, let us be going."

He had so planned that Mary should be next to the buildings with Helen between her and himself. In this arrangement they had walked but a very short distance when Mary, with the remark, "Fred, I can't hear a word you say" passed around to his other side, and Fred Davis knew that utter defeat was his portion, and that his rout had been made complete. 

As on the preceding Sunday night, Fred went to the

Burlinson home and passed the evening there until it was time to catch the midnight train. The time was spent much the same as on the other occasions, except in one respect. Helen had either been previously requested by Mary to retire early or else she felt that her presence was not especially desired by her sister,

OPPOSITES 

211 for it was not long before she stated that she was very, very tired and hoped Fred would excuse her. Soon she was gone and the other two were alone. 

Fred with questionable success endeavored as best he could to hide his emotions, for he was deeply disturbed and sorely troubled in the realization that for some reason Mary desired his company, and for that evening at least his undivided company, and what was still more disturbing apparently she took for granted that he was not unfavorable to such a condition. All the while he conversed he wondered how best to handle this new and perplexing problem. One thought occurring to him was to send the campaign committee a check to help defray the expense of the evangelistic meetings, and then discontinue his attendance at the Mohntown churches. In fact, he seriously considered terminating all visits to Mohntown, but then came the thought that if he did so the people of the First Church might believe it was because the evangelistic campaign had been instituted contrary to his desire, and that in consequence thereof he was exhibiting a spiteful spirit. 

When the time arrived for him to leave the Burlinson home that night Mary put him to a more severe test than he had even anticipated. 

She said: "Fred, why can't you come down early next 

Sunday and have dinner with us? That would enable us to attend the twilight services which are being planned."

He offered an impromptu excuse for not accepting the invitation which she brushed aside with the remark, "Why, Fred, for months you have been spending your

Sundays in Mohntown. In the past you have seemed to find this a pleasant place in which to remain over the end of the week. What has happened to change your opinion of our little village?"

Fred then knew that Mary of a certainty must be aware of his final break with Reta. She could not have

OPPOSITES 

touched him in a more vulnerable spot, for her remarks were like salt on an unhealed sore. Thus aroused by memory of Reta's cruel hurt, in a spirit of resentful pique, he exclaimed, "I'll be here at three next Sunday, if that be agreeable to you," and he hoped that 

Reta would know.

CHAPTER XXXVI 

During the ensuing week Fred was obliged to make a trip to a city in the middle west. Possessing a slight hope that he might be detained by business sufficiently long to render it impossible for him to keep his engagement with Mary for the following Sunday, he rather despised himself for the thought, yet tried to justify himself in that he felt he was involuntarily drifting somewhere against his will and contrary to his better judgment. He was, however, conscientious in that he wasted no time on the trip and was able to board a train at Pittsburgh that should enable him to arrive in Norwald on Saturday night.

Having finished reading his morning paper, he observed sitting in the chair directly in front of him a most strikingly attractive woman who spent most of her time watching the passing scenery. Fred was inclined at times to believe that she would enter into conversation with him, if he but gave her the slightest encouragement, but no word was uttered by either until the train was entering Jacktown. Then as she turned in her chair his eyes were permitted to feast upon a face so fascinatingly adorable that his innermost soul was set aflame. Even though favoring the blonde type, he could not but judge her, a decided brunette, the most beautiful woman that had ever delighted his eye. "Pardon me," she softly and irresistibly inquired, "but if you are not leaving the train at Jacktown may

I impose upon your obliging nature to keep an eye on my umbrella while I alight and send a telegram?" 

Fred hastened to assure her that to trust him with 213

OPPOSITES 

the custody of her umbrella was to confer upon him a most pleasing and acceptable honor.

Before the train came to a full stop she was waiting to alight. Stepping from the car she hurriedly entered the station, but did not go to the telegraph office. Instead she passed out of a rear door, where doubtless awaiting her coming was a man, evidently a workman, wearing a black cap, blue overalls and jumper. He immediately passed to her a small package which she placed in her handbag. A few words were exchanged, following which she returned to her seat in the Pullman. She thanked Fred for his care of the umbrella and in reply to his inquiry as to whether she had sufficient time to send the telegram replied, "Barely time, but it doubtless is on its way by this time.” 

Conversation of a casual nature occupied the time until the train arrived at Lanktown, where she alighted. 

Although Fred made no effort to learn her name, with which she had carefully avoided acquainting him, following her departure this fascinating stranger was his sole thought during the traveling of at least three score of miles. Beautifully alluring, yet that was not the thing that held his mind. Strangely secretive as to her identity, yet that probably could be satisfactorily explained. But her apparent intimate relations with the workman in the overalls and jumper, there was the rub. She, attired as Fashion's Queen-He, in the toiler's garb. But that was not all. There was something between those two, something more than to him was easily explainable. In their meeting there was more than a service by servant to a superior rendered. 

But why was he concerned? What mattered it to him whether Venus met Caliban on common ground; if proud peacock looked with favor upon warted toad; if flitting butterfly were held fast to admire a lumbering ape; if rose and lily were glad to grow among bloomless weeds? Love performs miracles, so why

OPPOSITES 

215 should he give thought to this mysterious maid and her grimy train hand? But there was this that he could not easily dismiss. As his passing fellow passenger had accepted the package from the man with whom she had doubtless pre-arranged their meeting and then returned to the train, there was in the man's following gaze that which told of admiration, love and pride in his association with the departing woman. And there was too a unique display of his feelings, for as his eyes followed her vanishing form his lips were peculiarly puckered to whistle an air unfamiliar to Fred's ears, but which he instantly knew to be notes of some love song. All very strange, mysterious, and to Fred nonexplainable. However, after considerable unsatisfactory thinking, his mind turned to thoughts of homethe factory and other things, and in due time he was in Norwald.

CHAPTER XXXVII 

The next day, in accordance with his promise, Fred called at Mary's home. For the first time he met her mother, who was an invalid. She was of a type so seldom met today, of a retiring disposition, polite, mild, and growing old, oh, so very gracefully. She was not one of the mothers endeavoring to appear the age of her daughters, either in dress, manner or otherwise. Greeting Fred most cordially, she expressed regret that she could not attend the evangelistic services. 

Through Fred's mind flashed the thought, "With a church composed of women of such a type as hers evangelists would not be necessary, nor could they be deemed necessary by even the most radical evangelistic supporter. 

Helen, Mary and Fred that night attended the opening evangelistic meeting which was held in an immense tent at one end of which was a platform for the speaker and others who might be prominent in the work. In the rear of the platform and to both the right and the left of it had been erected seats for the choir. These seats were in tiers, each succeeding row being elevated above the one immediately in front of it. There had been provided several pianos and near one of them was an elevated stand immediatey in the rear of the speaker's chairs. This stand had been provided for the choir leader, and was so arranged that he might be visible to all in the choir and the entire congregation, also that in turn he might see all of them. 

One of the local ministers at great length told of the preparatory work that had been done in arranging for the meetings. He laid much stress on the unceasing 216

OPPOSITES 

217 efforts of the various pastors, especially of himself, to prepare for the work to follow. He then explained the plans for the ensuing campaign. Several of the other preachers spoke, after which the evangelist was introduced by Doctor Owens of the First Church. 

Fred, observing that Reta, Laine and Helen were in the choir, inquired of Mary as to why she had not offered her services, remarking that he had enjoyed hearing her sing on several occasions and felt sure her voice would prove an asset. Although doubtless well pleased with the compliment regarding her voice, Mary gave unmistakable evidence that the question in its entirety was not pleasing, as she replied, "Why, Fred, you would be alone if I were to take a place in the choir." "Possibly I might sing too if I were asked." "But don't you realize that in the choir we could not sit together? Don't forget that you come to Mohntown but once a week." 

To this Fred made no response. He realized that he was in a difficult situation, that he occupied a bad spot, from which to extricate himself he must summon all his diplomatic resources. As he pondered how he should release himself from the web so rapidly entangling him, his thoughts were interrupted by the choir filling the vast tent with the singing of "Guide me, O Thou Great Jehovah." Be it said to the credit of the leader he had accomplished much in the few weeks he had been training the chorus. The music was sublimely sweet and certainly appealing, and was followed by a prayer and the singing of more hymns, in which all were invited to join. Then came the high spot, the sermon of the evangelist, a man above average height; frame made to carry more than entrusted to its support; skin of a carrot shade; long nose, one of the sort always going somewhere, usually into the affairs of others; gray eyes, deep sunk, mouth wide,

OPPOSITES 

lips thin and seldom closed, being overmuch occupied with outgoing words and incoming food; abundance of corn husk hair which persisted in hiding his eyes, and which required much exercise of his camel like neck to toss it back among his other disordered locks. But that man could talk-eloquent—a born orator and something else he was an actor. In histrionic lines he might have been anything he desired. Vividly he pictured the terrors of the anguished lost soul; so well did he paint his picture of words that that vast throng was fairly fanned with the heat waves of hell. In response came the gasps of the scared and alarmed, the flowing of tears for long years restrained. Fear, horror and terror all held sway. Threatening, coaxing, warning, cajoling, he exercised all the persuasion at his command. Then he made his direct appeal. "The hour of decision is at hand. Will you tonight accept salvation or will you reject the offer, the free offer of eternal life, of an everlasting, never ceasing life, of a life of perpetual association with those blessed by God, of a life within those glorious portals of that land where death comes not, where men never hunger nor thirst? Answer me-will you stubbornly, obstinately stand out against the appeal of righteousness? Do you prefer death, everlasting death, a living death, a death wherein you shall see afar off the blessed ones living in the heart of Heaven, while you hunger and thirst in the agonies of the damned? Some there are here tonight who have accepted salvation. Now, I am going to ask all those who believe themselves saved, and not alone saved but active in the Lord's work, to arise from their seats. I ask them to stand."

Many arose to their feet, among them Reta, which particular rising Fred was interested to observe. 

Then he resumed: "Praise God for those who have seen the light and who have accepted the treasures thereby revealed to them; but still more praise in that

OPPOSITES 

219 they are not one-sided Christians, that they are not only receivers but givers, that they are not selfish of soul, but are glad to share, that they serve and are anxious that others should have the glory that is theirs." 

Pausing to sweep his dead yellow locks from his sweaty face, now turning from carrot to parsnip shade, he suddenly shrieked: "But why all these seat warmers? Why sit ye here all the day idle? As I stand here and look out over this vast sea of humanity I wonder not that this City of Mohntown is as Sodom of old. Out of thousands here assembled only a part of this ocean of humans, only a wee small boat load, know enough to get in out of the wet. The rest of you are all wet. Can it be from choice? Can it be that you were born with vacant garrets on top of stiff stubborn necks? Can it be that you are stubbornly contrary and like unto long-eared mules? Do you have so-called spines of shaky spaghetti, or are you simply damned fools, damned because you are fools or fools because you suffer yourselves to be damned? But I cannot believe that all of you sitters are unbelievers. I am going now to allow these children of God who have just stood up for Jesus to be seated. I should apologize to them for asking them to be seated among the unholy-the unbelievers -the self-condemned, but may their spiritual light illumine your souls and show you the way; may physical contact so thrill you with the realization that for once you are in good company, that you may feel the urge to come along too. They may do you some good. I hope they won't be contaminated by you. But there must be some here who believe in the birth and death, the resurrection and the ascension, some who believe, but have not openly expressed their faith and have not become identified with any church. They do not work. 

They take, but they do not give. They are the cheap

OPPOSITES 

Christians. Now, here is their big chance to come forward to join the workers, to help with their purse, to help relieve the heavy burden of at least one of these long suffering, long enduring pastors of Mohntown. 

Now, all you who believe in Christ and feel satisfied that you are saved, but have not nor do not now claim membership in any church, arise. That is the least you can do." 

None responded; there was absence of the slightest stir that might indicate any would rise. 

Evidently the evangelist was not surprised. Pausing a few moments, he said: "Can it be that so few are believers, or is it that we have here tonight the unconfessing believers, the tongue-tied, weak kneed, spindly shanked believers. Just believing enough to hope to get by, but with intestines so shriveled as to shake in cowardly dismay and when called to make a public declaration they cringe and cower in the comfortable seats provided by the godly and giving church people of Mohntown. How I wish I might have the dynamite of hell's fury to blast your lazy hides loose from your seats of ease. "But, whether you are lazy, indolent, crazy fools, or just don't care, I'm not going to give you up to the devil. If you won't help yourselves, I am going to try to help you. Among the live ones tonight, those who believe in and publicly acknowledge their Saviour, must be those with aching hearts, yearning for some loved ones outside of the fold. To these dear sisters and brothers I say there is left one hope, the hope of prayer. So won't you whose hearts are heavy because of sheep lost from your fold, won't you so signify by rising?" Many arose, among them Reta. Fred wondered for whom Reta would pray or ask others to pray.

He wondered, too, whether she oft prayed for correction of her own short comings. Following a most fervent prayer came the man's strong appeal; his master

OPPOSITES 

221 appeal: "We are gathered here tonight in apparent health, content in fancied security, snug and comfortable and free from care. We do not sense danger. We feel immune from harm. Oh yes somewhere fires may be consuming life and flames destroying property, but not here. No clanging alarm jars our ears. But listen friends, while you hear not the siren or the clanging bell, you are going to hear them. Yes, listen-let me tell you the most devastating holocaust of all times is upon you now. It rages at your doors. Listen to me, you have been playing with fire and the time for your burning is close at hand. With the fires of jealousy, envy, hatred, indifference to the rights of others, indifference and even defiance to your God, you have been consuming and destroying the temples of the Most 

Holy; with fires of sinful deeds and loathsome habits you have been laying low the green trees of physical as well as of immortal life. With the fire of disregard for rights of others, of neighbor, of state and nation, you have been charring the timbers, cracking the stones and melting the metal structures of a Christian nation. 

Yes, you salute your flag and you sing of its glory and yet in treason to your banner, in your hypocrisy you defy the laws of the country for which your ensign stands, and in so-doing with unpatriotic fire you make black dust of the red, white and blue. Yes, yes, the fire of lust, the fire of criminal neglect, the fire of mammon, the fire of deceit is in you and of you, and tonight the heat of the fire is all about you-hell's fire is on you. Will you cringingly throw yourselves flat on the ground while the flames consume and devour you, or will you stand on your feet like women and men, and allow the blood of the Lamb to quench these flames by Satan sent, and be saved from the fire that even this very minute with tongues of flame is reaching out to destroy you? Don't you feel the heat on your back, don't you hear the crackling, don't you see those yel-

OPPOSITES 

low waves as the fiendish fire approaches? Will you stay its oncoming or will you die? The answer is yours. 

Make your own decision. Hear me in the names of loved ones gone before. Hear me in the name of sainted mother and father. Hear me now and be saved. Say to the world I believe, I want salvation, say it tonight, not only in word, but in action, and come to this platform where I may greet you in the name of the Lord. 

Come!" Then following a momentary pause he shouted, "Delays are dangerous." Then a warning cry, "Tomorrow may be too late."

Some went forward, some moved rapidly, almost running. Some went with a wondering gaze, some hesitatingly. Some wept as they walked. 

Then the evangelist called on local persons he had trained to serve as his workers to go among the congregation and make personal appeals. Moved by these close-at-hand entreaties, many more responded by going forward. 

Once again the choir sang. The leader had done his work well. The members of that tremendously large chorus sang with an appeal almost irresistible. As the workers passed pleadingly about through that vast throng, the choir in plaintive tones added to the imploring urge with "Almost Persuaded, Almost, But Lost.' 99 The evangelist then called on certain ones of the choir selected for that particular work to pass through the aisles, urging them as they went to sing as never before. So, as they made their way through that quickened throng, gravely they sang that warning song, "Almost, but lost." Through a megaphone the leader called "Sing it, sing it, that lost souls may be saved from encroaching damnation."

Among these specially selected singers was Reta who passed through an aisle quite close to Mary and Fred. 

As she did, Fred had two thoughts which concerned

OPPOSITES 

223 Reta. He wondered if she saw him with Mary. He also wondered how one with so saintly a countenance as was hers that night, and how one with eyes and voice so eloquent in their appeal, could be so stubbornly and unreasonably selfish. And as he continued to wonder the meeting soon came to a close. 

Fred escorted Mary and Helen to their home where with them and their mother he discussed the meeting with great fairness. Giving credit where he believed it was due and refraining from whatever adverse criticism he might have had in his mind, he was most careful that his criticism should not be influenced by his personal opinion regarding evangelists.

The discussion proved most engaging, in fact so absorbing that Helen and Mrs. Burlinson did not retire as early as was their custom, in truth so absorbing did it become that Fred lost all touch with time until it was too late to catch the last train to Norwald, whereupon Mrs. Burlinson invited him to stay at her home, but he politely declined and went to the principal hotel.

There was much on his mind as he walked from the 

Burlinson home to the hotel that night. He could plainly see and feel that he was rapidly drifting into a vortex from the suffocating suction of which it would. be almost impossible to save himself.

Arriving at the hotel, he registered, went directly to his room and immediately retired, but, although tired and weary, the whirling of his worried thoughts made of sleep for him a far off thing. After tossing about on his bed for what seemed hours, he arose, re-dressed and descended to the lobby, intending to take an open air stroll, in the hope that thereby might be produced a condition conducive to sleep. As the elevator had suspended operations for the night, it was necessary that he descend by exercise of his own power. The lights in the halls and lobby had been dimmed. As

OPPOSITES 

he came to the foot of the stairway he saw at the far end of the lobby a man who was apparently endeavoring to open the cigar case. Thinking a sneak thief was at work, Fred approached the man whom as he neared the stand he was amazed to recognize as the leader of the choir, the man who had been so largely responsible for the delightfully appealing music rendered that very night by the choir in the evangelist's tent. 

The man seeing Fred as he approached said, "How annoying it is that these stands close so early at night. 

I wanted a smoke and found no salesman here. I intended taking a package of cigarettes and leaving the price inside the case." "Why not carry out your intentions?" "I find the case locked. not trust its guests.' 99 

Seemingly this hotel does "Hotels have had some experiences that prompt precaution." "Probably so, but that creates a hardship for the honest guests; neither does it provide me with my smoke." 

Fred replied, "Sorry I have no cigarettes. Probably the clerk at the desk may have one." 99 "I had not thought of that. I have had a hard night and nothing soothes my nerves as does a cigarette.' "Am I mistaken in believing you to be the choir leader at the evangelist's tent?" 

The man hesitated, but finally said, "You are right, I am." "Don't you think you are taking a chance? Some one else might have seen you as I did and judged you a sneak thief." "Oh, no one could think that of me." 

"Possibly not, but you gave me just that impression when I saw you endeavoring to open the cigar case.' "Oh, but I could explain all that away, especially when my identity became known."

OPPOSITES 

225 "Possibly so. It is more probable, however, that they would have censured a man in your position all the more for resorting to such actions." "What do you mean, sir?" "Just this-you are a man, a human being, but you are also or claim to be a saver of souls. You have a most pleasing personality. You know how to assist others to sing so appealingly and so persuasively those good old Gospel songs. You may have been the means of accomplishing some good tonight. For permanent good to be accomplished by you the people must have confidence in your sincerity. Doubtless, you heard your evangelist himself speak of the harm of cigarette smoking. You are one of his assistants and are supposed to believe in and support his teachings. By working with him you give tacit testimony of agreeing with all that he preaches, and yet you not only smoke cigarettes, but endeavor to secure possession of them in a doubtful manner. Were those who were influenced by the preaching and singing tonight to know of your habits, they could not be censured for lack of confidence in the sincerity of this whole movement.' "You are indeed insulting, Sir." 99 "I think not. Although I almost invariably speak in a manner to make myself understood, I never intentionally insult. I still claim there is evidence of a lack of sincerity and consistency on your part-that is, with regard to cigarette smoking. You seem inclined to believe that I have insulted you. Let us put it to the test. If I am not right in my assumption I am willing to admit that you are sincere and I'll gladly apologize to you. If my claim that you are not sincere stands the test, then you must admit that I am justified in what I have said to you and about you. This is my proposition. At tomorrow night's meeting you tell the congregation exactly what happened tonight. Tell the people all that I have said and all that you have said. I agree

OPPOSITES 

to tell nothing so long as you stick strictly to facts. 

When you have placed the matter before them ask them if they approve of your action; ask them whether they believe you are sincere; ask them if they believe 

I have insulted you. I am willing to abide by their verdict. If they uphold you, I promise to publicly apologize to you in the presence of the entire congregation. 

If they say you are wrong, you must admit it, and promise that you will be sincere in all your words and actions now and ever after. Do you agree to this?" "I have no inclination or desire to discuss this matter further."

Fred said nothing more to the man, but abruptly turned and ascended the stairway to the floor above where was located the office for the reception of guests. 

As he turned the matter over in his mind and deliberated as to whether it was proper to report the cigar case incident to the night clerk or simply let the matter rest and die, the latter came from behind the desk and asked, "Is there anything I can do for you, Mr. 

Davis?" to which Fred replied: "No, there is nothing you can do for me. Something has just happened that has caused me to wonder what is the right thing for me to do in regard to reporting it to the hotel management." "Of what nature, Mr. Davis?" "Well, it was in regard to the action of one of guests. 

your "You don't mean the choir leader who is here with the evangelist, do you?" "Why do you ask that?" "Well, he was at the desk a short while ago complaining because the cigar stand was closed and explaining to me that he was a hay fever victim and claimed that from the effects of his ailment there was nothing that would relieve him except a cigarette. Not having a cigarette myself, I could not give him one,

OPPOSITES 

227 and, as the cigar stand is not operated by the hotel management but is leased to someone else, I had no access to it. Then, too, one of the housekeepers had reported to me earlier in the evening that the room of this particular gentleman was literally covered with cigarette butts. She was much agitated, because there had been no care taken to protect the carpet, and that even the bed showed indications that the singer had been smoking while reclining." "Well, that is the very subject that is on my mind, so I might as well tell you of my very recent experience with that self same gentleman.'

Whereupon Fred repeated his conversation with the choir leader. 

Having heard Fred through the clerk exclaimed, "Why, that fellow is little better than a thief! I told him the case was locked. While we are on this subject, Mr. Davis, I must be careful not to offend you. I know you are interested in the evangelistic campaign, but these fellows do a lot of things that offset much of the possible good they do." "You won't offend me. I have an opinion that I imagine is similar to yours regarding evangelists; I might say nearly all of them, very nearly all of them, are not what they claim to be." "Well, that being so, I'll relate something that confirms your opinion at least so far as this outfit is concerned. When it was decided that the churches should conduct this campaign, a committee called on Mr. 

Black, our manager, and inquired if he would be willing to entertain, on a complimentary basis, the evangelist at our hotel. Mr. Black explained to the committee that at the time of the evangelistic campaign there would be several conventions in the house which would require all of our rooms for several weeks, including yesterday. He said he would be glad to entertain the evangelist and give him a room without

OPPOSITES 

charge, beginning tonight, until the close of the meetings. The members of the committee, seemingly a trifle disappointed, said that they would take care of the matter in some other way, and, with a clear understanding with Mr. Black that the evangelist would not be here until tonight, they left the hotel. "Two weeks ago, while I was waiting for the last train to come in, I received a telephone call from the evangelist stating that he was at the railroad station with his family and that our bus had not taken care of him, having left the station while he was 'phoning to one of the members of the campaign committee.

When he 'phoned me, our bus had already reached the hotel and the chauffeur had gone for the night. I so informed Mr. Evangelist and also told him that we had no accommodations at the hotel for him. He resented this very much and insisted that I call up the manager. Although as courteously as possible I tried to explain to him that our arrangements with the committee were for him to come to our hotel at a later date, I was not successful in getting him to either believe me or to understand me, which, I do not know. "I suggested that he call Mr. Swinton, who I believe is the chairman of the committee and who had arranged for his stay at the hotel. At this suggestion with a bang he hung up the receiver. I intended to ask Mr.

Black about the matter in the morning, but in a short while I saw approaching the desk two men, two ladies, a boy and a girl. Following them was a taxi driver.

One of the men and the chauffeur were engaged in a heated argument. As they came nearer to the desk I heard one of the men say, "You can collect that from the campaign committee," to which the chauffeur replied, 'Look here, I don't know your campaign committee and I ain't collecting this from anybody but you. 

You come across, or you and your bunch will have more excitement than you have seen since you tried to make

OPPOSITES 

229 a sucker out of Jack Peters, the taxi driver in Waltontown. I got your pedigree. Your record is here ahead of you.' "I interrupted the discussion and inquired as to the cause of the trouble. I gathered that the man who seemed to be the leader of the party was the evangelist, and that he had engaged the taxi driver to bring himself and his associates to the hotel. His reply to my inquiry as to why he did not pay the taxi fare was to the effect that the hotel ought to do that, as the bus had left him and his party at the railroad station. I reminded him that the reservation made for him was for a later date, and for him only. To this he replied: 'You are quite insolent and impudent. I shall be compelled to report you to the management.' "At this point, the taxi driver interjected, 'Do I git my money or don't I? I'll give you five seconds to come across or I start to clean up.' "The evangelist then called upon me for protection, but the result of it all was that he finally paid the charges, complaining about the amount of it, and the chauffeur left. "I then endeavored to make clear to the evangelist that we had no accommodations for him, showing him our reservation book and also the memorandum I had received from Mr. Black. You certainly can double up some of the people who are already here, and take care of us.' "I told him that such a thing was impossible. In turn he endeavored to make me believe that men attending conventions were all very friendly with one another, and would be more than glad to give way to a clergyman. there has been one room reserved—a single room for yourself, while you have brought with you five other persons. Do you expect us to take care of them too?' "I certainly do. They are a part of our organization which is here to save your wicked town. One of the ladies is my wife. The boy and girl are my children. The other gentleman is the leader of the choir, and the lady with him is his wife. It is unreasonable to even consider separating us.' "Well, Mr. Davis, in my hotel experience I have been up against some tight squeezes, but that one had me. Every bedroom in the house was occupied, and there I was at 1:30 in the morning with an unreasonable and selfish man and five others on my hands. I finally told them that I would screen off various parts of our convention hall and arrange the necessary furniture so that they might be able to sleep until morning, when the matter would be presented to Mr. Black for his attention. They objected very strenuously to such an arrangement and insisted that they have rooms at once and rooms with bath at that. I told them that 

I would make private as possible three places in different parts of the hall and would give them the old style wash bowl and pitcher. At the conclusion of an indignation meeting, in which they all took a part, they decided to accept my proposition. "As soon as possible in the morning I got in touch with Mr. Black. He immediately came down to the hotel and arranged an interview with the local committee, which in the meantime had been in touch with the evangelist. He discovered that what they really wanted amounted to four sleeping rooms with private baths, and another room which the evangelist desired to use as a study. On receiving this information from 

Mr. Black, I asked him for instructions, receiving this reply, 'I have arranged to take them to my private

231 home until the convention is over and then give them what they want here.' "This did not please me overly much, but as Mr.
