Little more was said, but Fred went to his room more firmly convinced than ever that he was right in his opinion regarding evangelistic campaigns. As Fred was preparing to retire he heard someone moving about in the next room. Then he heard a whistle, a most peculiar whistle, the air of which he could not name, and yet there was something familiar about it. 

Where had he heard that peculiar whistle, that strange air? Suddenly it came to him that as he ascended the steps following his animated talk with the choir leader the latter had whistled in that same peculiar manner the very same air that now came to his ear through the wall from the adjoining room.

CHAPTER XXXVIII 

Quite naturally the evangelistic services eventually came to a conclusion. Although Fred exhibited little interest in them, yet every Sunday evening while they continued he was present with Mary. The campaign was conducted much the same as most of these three ring religious revivals provided for those of which "one is born every minute," and the results were to all intents and purposes the same. Following the close of the last meeting at which the main, if not the sole aim, was to collect the sheckles, Fred had accompanied

Mary to her home. Helen had remained for a time with them, discussing the campaign, but as Fred did not seem to be desirous of expressing an opinion regarding either the evangelist or his doings, Helen took it that evidently he and Mary would rather have her absence than her presence, and with this impression firmly fixed in her mind she accordingly and forthwith did retire. 

During the entire evening Mary had evidenced a certain overmuch abstraction, regarding the responsibility for which she was quite reticent. Being pressed by 

Fred for an explanation, she quite suddenly exclaimed, "I have a confession to make. I have been corresponding for sometime with a gentleman, and after much thought have concluded that the only proper thing to do is that I should tell you. The campaign has done at least one thing for me. It has made me see that I am wrong in concealing this from you.' 99 

Fred said, "I don't see that you are under obligation to tell me that, Mary. I have no claim that should entitle me to dictate with whom you should carry on correspondence." 232

OPPOSITES 

233 "But, Fred, I have a different view from you. When two persons are associated as we have been for quite sometime, it is only fair that the attention of one to the other should be undivided." "I do not quite agree with you. You may have known this young man longer than you have been acquainted with me, and even if such were not the case he might have just as much claim to your favor as I." "Fred, you do not seem to understand me. You and

I, to the exclusion of others, have been much together. 

In fact, you come regularly and frequently to Mohntown for one particular purpose, and were it not for that purpose our town would seldom, if ever, see you. 

Is that true?" "Mary, to quite an extent you are correct in your statement, but still I fail to believe that conditions are so serious as to forbid your corresponding with some one else whom you like. If you did not like him, you certainly would not correspond with him." "Fred, I may be rather peculiar about some things. 

I try to be above board in everything. Possibly I go to the extreme. I am inclined to the belief that when two persons pay as much attention to each other as you and I have been doing, the impression created by each upon the other must be most favorable. There has been sufficient time for us to study and to learn each other. Evidently both of us seem to desire our pleasant relations to continue. That being the case, I do not believe any outside influence should be permitted to affect whatever tie there may be between us." "Mary, I fear you are taking our friendship too seriously. I like you, and I admire you, but I am quite convinced that you regard certain relations as established rather than prospective, and, if I have led you to believe in the existence of those things which your remarks seem to imply, it might be better that our

OPPOSITES 

present relations, pleasant though they be, should be discontinued." "Fred, I cannot understand you. You say you like me and admire me, and yet you want to break with me." "Since you see things in the light your attitude leads me to believe you do, it might be better so. Better now than later." 

Mary said nothing, but two tiny tear drops coursed down two cheeks and fell on the interlaced fingers of two white hands, and in those tears lay a story far more eloquently told than could have been expressed by tongue or pen. "I am sorry, Mary. I did not mean to hurt you; my thoughts were what I considered as best for both of us. 

As I said before, I like you a lot, in fact, too much not to be frank with you. I like being in your company, I more than admire your many sterling qualities, but I am not sure that it all means to me just what it seems to mean to you. However, let us forget the entire discussion, and see what happens." "Fred, to be fair with you and with myself, I intend writing to this other young man and tell him that I think it wise that he should not reply to what I desire should be my last letter to him. As it is now, I am not fair to him; I am not fair to you. I think he cares for me. He is and has been quite a distance from here, and I have not seen him for a long while. I see you frequently and am always glad when you visit me. 

Somehow I feel it would be best to do as my present impulse tells me to do." "You may be right, Mary, and somehow I regret that my views are dissimilar to yours. At the same time, if we are to continue our pleasant association then I must be just as fair as you are. There is a little girl I met years ago. I have seen her a few times since then. I have corresponded with her since my latter

OPPOSITES 

235 college days. If you are going to stand by what you say, I must not be outdone in fair dealing, and shall pursue the same course with her as you are doing with your friend. I feel I am wrong in doing this; still I bow my will to yours. May you be right, and may it be that my little friend will not care very much."

Mary made it more than plain to Fred that she was beyond description delighted with this attitude of his. 

Following this victory of Mary's, it was soon time for him to leave. During his walk to the station and all the way home in the train the only thing of which he felt sure was that his mind was in a vortex of puzzling perplexities. He felt at times that he had made a serious mistake in complying with the proposition made by Mary and yet somehow he could see no help for himself. Had she been a different sort of a girl, it would have been easy to withstand her. But she was so true, so sincere, so consistent in everything, that opposed to his best judment he was compelled to believe that whatever she did was proper and that it was right for him to do whatever she desired.

CHAPTER XXXIX 

Time passed and with it many things once seemingly of vast importance had passed into the land of the forgotten. The evangelistic services had become a thing simply for rare retrospection. A few of the converts still attended church, but most of them had gradually drifted back into the channels of their former days, and were being carried along by the same old currents.

Fred continued his visits to the Burlinson home, and when Mary or Fred was invited to a social function, the other was also expected to be present. For some reason the Jameson girls had been absorbed by another set. Fred saw them at the church, but it might have been lack of inclination or want of opportunity, but he was never near enough to speak to either of them, nor did he make any endeavor to be advantageously placed for conversation with them, although it is more than probable that had Laine been apart from Reta, words might have passed between her and Fred.

The following summer the Burlinson girls did not go to Norwald, but Fred continued his visits to Mohntown. It was during this summer that Reta became acquainted with a newcomer to Norwald and after a high-speed courtship they eloped before the season had come to an end. Much might be told of Reta's later life, but with that unfortunate girl's elopement she passed out of our story. 

During all the time we have been learning of Fred, his mother and stepfather had been traveling abroad. 

After quite an extended tour they returned one winter immediately before Christmas. In a very short while 236

OPPOSITES 

237 in some way word reached the ears of Fred's mother, as word has a mysteriously unexplainable way of doing, that Fred was engaged to be married to Mary. She asked him one day if this were true, to which question she received a reply decidedly negative. "My boy," she said, "you are making a mistake, you are fair neither to Miss Burlinson nor to yourself. 

From what I hear you are much in her company. You must think a great deal of her or you would not occupy so much of her time. If such be the case, you should be engaged, if she is willing, or else you should change the order of things. It does not look well for a young lady and young man to be so much in the company of each other and for so long a time, unless their intentions are serious." "Mother, I am in sore perplexity. I hope you can help me." He immediately proceeded to relate his experience with Reta, and how it was that he came to be so much with Mary. "I admire her very, very much, Mother. In some ways she is quite different from any girl I ever met and in every way that she is different she is better than the others. She has the reputation of being absolutely truthful. She is frank, almost to the extreme. Sometimes I think she passes even the bounds of the extreme. 

She is absolutely candid in everything that she has to say. There is no trace of selfishness and there is no suspicion of meanness in her character. She is sympathetic and she is very kind to everyone. She should make some man a perfect wife." "Then, why do you hesitate, Fred?" "It is hard to explain. I did not seek her companionship and while I value her friendship and always enjoy her company, there is something that tells me she was not intended for me.” "Then, why do you continue to see her? The longer you allow this matter to endure the more difficult it will

OPPOSITES 

be to terminate your relations. The young lady must think much of you and doubtless is expecting and waiting for you to ask her to marry you. If you do not intend to do so, you must let her know." "That is exactly what is troubling me, Mother. More than once I have determined to do that which I believe is the advisable thing to do, but when I see her, talk with her and watch her honest eyes, and listen to her honest speech, I haven't the heart to do that which I know would hurt her very much.' 

"Fred, you are making a mistake—a very grave mistake. It must be rectified at once. You must reason this matter out for yourself as to whether you want 

Miss Burlinson for your wife or whether you do not. 

If you do, and feel sure you can make her happy and be happy with her, tell her so on your next visit to 

Mohntown. If you think it would be a mistake to marry her, tell her so as kindly as you can. Ask her forgiveness for having consumed so much of her time, and then come back to your mother.”

Fred was much impressed by this admonition and advice. He well knew what he should do, in fact, had known it for a long while, but for some reason unexplainable he had been unable to bring himself to the point where he could do the proper thing. During the remainder of that week he sought to strengthen himself against that mysterious something that always held him back when he was about to broach the troublesome subject to Mary.

In accordance with his well established custom, on the following Sunday Fred's usual trip to Mohntown was made. Boarding the train, he felt fully determined that he was en route to the Burlinson home for the last time. On and on rolled the train, decreasing mile by mile the distance separating him and Mary. Rapidly ran the train, easing its pace only to stop at occasional stations or slacking its speed to cautiously cross

OPPOSITES 

239 bridges or round snaky curves; then resuming its rush past familiar woodland scenes and loud speaking bill boards, with every word of which Fred had become perfectly acquainted. Further from Norwald drew the steel steed, puffing, snorting and leaving behind a storm of cinders and black, sooty clouds; nearer, nearer it drew to the station of Mohntown, and as mileage traveled grew more the determination of the traveler grew less, and as Fred alighted from the train his will was as the escaped steam from the locomotive's boiler. 

Instead of going directly to the Burlinson home, he aimlessly strayed through unfamiliar streets, none of which led in the direction of Mary's abode. Following this useless, valueless waste of time, he abruptly was reminded that in the name of common politeness he must without further delay proceed to the residence of the Burlinsons. Then came the determination to adhere to his resolution and bravely, firmly and rapidly he made his way to the home of Mary. 

As he ascended the steps leading to the porch, Mary came out of the house to meet him. "Why, Fred, why are you so tardy? Was the train late? We have company today. Last night our cousins from the West dropped in on us unexpectedly, and they are so anxious to meet you. Mother has been telling them of 'the perfect young man who calls here,' so be on your best behavior and don't disappoint them." 

Here was an unexpected condition confronting Fred, and with its uprising, the fortification which he had built to defend himself against his own weakness immediately crumbled and scattered in cowardly dust.

He tried to alibi his oozing courage with the explanation that no opportunity would be presented to discuss with Mary the subject uppermost in his mind. 

Without offering any explanation for his delayed arrival, and with only casual remarks, he entered the house, where he met the cousins, whom he greeted in a

OPPOSITES 

most cordial manner, but it was only with great difficulty that it was possible for him to frame his mind to conform to the part in the picture the Burlinsons expected him to portray. For Mrs. Burlinson he had the utmost respect and highest regard. Then, too, he wished not to disprove the complimentary remarks she had made concerning him, and so, largely because of 

Mrs. Burlinson, he forced himself to register delight that he was with Mary that day. In truth, once Fred had gained full control of himself, the visit did become a very pleasant one, even in spite of many things, some in jest, and some in earnestness, that were said that could but give to everyone present, except Fred himself, the impression that at no distant date Mary would become Mrs. Fred.

Quite convinced that the happenings of that Sunday had been in accordance with a decree of fate, Fred returned to his home in Norwald, firm in the belief that it would be best to let things materialize as most of the people of Norwald and Mohntown thought they would. None of this did he mention to his mother, although she had expected to receive from him a report of the result of that particular visit to Mohntown. The calendar released more than one month of days before Fred again made mention of Mary Burlinson to his mother, and during all that time relations between Mary and Fred continued with scarce any variation.

CHAPTER XL 

One summer evening of the following year it so happened that Mary and Fred paid a visit to the same. park to which Mary on a previous occasion had gone. with another young man. It also happened that they sat on the same bench that had been occupied by Mary and her friend on that night of a previous summer. 

Also that in their youthful orbs was imaged the limpid waters of the very same lake, on the placid surface of which boats passed by just as they passed before the vision of Mary and her companion of the other time; likewise in refulgent glory did the same old moon arise and likewise with shimmering sheen did it shine just as on that other night—on that other night of some years gone. Fully cognizant was Mary that all of this was an old picture for her, all old, except the one who shared the scene. Her companion of that other night had candidly revealed to her that in his mind, if not in his heart, one other than herself held fast a sacred place. Well she recalled how she had implored he lay bare the hidden troubling of his soulthat sacred secret so long guarded and well concealed. 

Likewise was she reminded that since that other night there had come between them-between her and the youth of the other night-something unexpectedsomething most certainly unforseen. The parting of that other night now seemed the final one; since then their eyes had never met, and although messages regular and full of good feeling continued for quite a spell, even this had come to the ceasing time, when Mary had written this former friend that she had found a new. 

But let us return to this new summer night. Closely 241

OPPOSITES 

observing Mary in the light of the moon, Fred beheld a new Mary, a different Mary than the one of previous days, and he wondered what had brought the change. 

And as he wondered and pondered, of a sudden he made bold to ask her "Why?" 

Had not Mary been the girl that she was, frank, candid, open, honest, either she would have become an evasive Mary, or else she would have lied; but Mary told the unvarnished, unqualified truth regarding that other evening in the park. With the exception of the other man's identity, nothing remained unrevealed, and as the final word of her story was spoken, gently, softly, confidingly, she whispered, “But, Fred, everything is so changed. With you tonight it is all so different from that other night."

Somehow, whether it was the witchery of the full moon, whether it was the sparkle of fairyland lights on the far side of the lake, whether it was the spell of the electrically decorated boats so sinuously gliding along, whether he was made captive that night by the lure of feminine appeal, whether it was her frankness, or whether it was fate, or whatever it was, somehow 

Fred felt closer to Mary at that particular moment than ever before. Then that something which had so long held him back seemed dissipated and dissolved, and while he still refrained from saying that which he might have said, had it not been for the restraining force that had held him fast so long, the influence of that new something would have had him say that for which Mary was evidently waiting, but when on the point of uttering those fateful words so long withheld, still another influence caused him to hesitate, and while he did so it was Mary who spoke her mind. "Fred," she said, “we have been acquainted for quite a long while. We understand each other, I believe, thoroughly. I have given up a friendship which 

I really cherished, and I believe that you have done

OPPOSITES 

243 something similar. We have been in each other's company at least once a week for several years. I know you well enough and care for you more than enough to go on through life with you the other six days of each week. I really believe that you feel the same toward me, but that something has been withholding you from acknowledging it to yourself and making it known to me. I hope you will pardon this frankness and seeming abandonment of maidenly reserve, but why can't you and I be frank and honest with each other in all things and at all times?" 

Fred was at a loss for a reply. Thoughts, very many thoughts strange thoughts, conflicting thoughts, flashed rapidly through his madly muddled mind, one thought so swiftly succeeding another that it was impossible for him to frame into words anything that any one particular line of thought might have suggested. If he were to surrender to Mary's startling proposal, he feared eventually both would be quite unhappy, for that "Something" that had previously held him in check again asserted itself. In opposition to this was the thought that having allowed matters to drift into such a perplexing and embarrassing condition, he would be a cad and despicable among men if he were then to cut off from Mary without any other reason than the one which he was at a loss to explain to even himself.

Not able to find the proper thing to say, he acted and did the worst possible thing he could have done, that is, if it was his desire to follow the dictates of that something which had guided and held him tight for so long a time. He reached and took Mary's hand. "I am so glad, Fred," she said, "I knew it would come to this, but you, who are so brave in other things, have been so timid, so very timid in letting love have its way and its sway."

And then, as hand in hand and in silence they sat

OPPOSITES 

there on that bench in the park, Mary sublimely happy, and Fred uncertain and hopeful, the moon, the moon of all ages, the same lunar light that had been a party and a witness to innumerable scenes of a similar sort, rose high in Mary's heaven of light; then it was that as in the full silver glow of the most gorgeous and most bewitching moon of all times, as more and more boats glided gracefully by, as the music of the distant band floated through the trees in tones low and sweet, then it was that Mary released her left hand, which Fred had held in his right, and from the bag she carried she removed a tiny plush box. "Fred," she said, "in this box is a ring which my grandmother wore. It was given her by my grandfather when they were betrothed. It does seem strange for me to provide the ring, but won't you take it and place it where it belongs?" 

Fred, who might have pitched baseball on a world's series championship team, Fred, who, when at college, had smashed and battered the opposing lines of the strongest varsity elevens of his day, Fred, who had stroked college crews to championships, Fred, who had never stepped back from any man or any problem, Fred, the all-brave, the all-courageous, Fred, who knew there was some reason why he should not do that thing, did just that which he knew he should not do, and placed the ring on the third finger of Mary's left hand. 

As on the bench they continued to sit, while Fred was ill at ease, yet there was a certain relief from the oppressive suspense that had held him so fast for many weeks. Mary was supremely happy. Whether it was the spell of the moon's silver light, or whether it was that every last single boat had abandoned the lake, leaving it to serve those two as a rippling mirror to image the heavens and the farther shore, or whatever it may have been, for quite a while they sat there alone

OPPOSITES 

245 and whispered sweet nothings. Then they went home.

The next day Fred told his mother, and she seemed well pleased, not so much in that Fred was really engaged to be married, but that the matter, so long in abeyance, seemed to have been brought to a definite and mutual understanding.

CHAPTER XLI 

The announcement of the engagement was made at a party given by Mrs. Burlinson to a great number of 

Mary's friends, but the Jameson girls were not among those invited. Those present did all possible to appear surprised, but their very manner indicated that they had been expecting just what had happened, and had been wondering what had caused the delay. Among the guests there was a unanimity freely expressed to the effect that one of the very finest girls and that one of the most admirable of men they had ever known would some day be joined in what they felt sure would be a perfect marriage. Following the announcement, Fred was the recipient of many congratulatory messages and expressions of well wishes. Glad as he was to receive the goodwill words, yet there was an unexplainable something which oft times made him wonder if he had acted wisely and well. 

Time went on, just as time has always done, and while on all occasions and under all circumstances Fred was more than kind to Mary, yet, as in former days, oft times there came that same mysterious, unseen force that did most strenuously assert itself, and by its influence produce in him a certain involuntary restraint. 

Oft, while on the train, following a visit to Mary, he debated with himself as to whether or not he was traveling on a track leading to a destination where he should find the wrecked lives of the two persons at that time most interested in his future. This he never mentioned to his mother, but with himself he had many a fight and strenuous struggle, always terminating with censure for himself in that he allowed such a condition to prevail. 246

OPPOSITES 

247 Once married to Mary, he told himself all might be well, but "Something" else invariably whispered that such a union would not be an act of wisdom. Just why, the whisper never made clear. But with all of his lingering doubts he was powerless to dismiss from his mind the belief that, should he fail to keep faith with and marry Mary, in his own estimation he would be nought other than a cad, and that by all the people within the confines of Norwald and Mohntown he would be a man loathed, shunned and despised. An abundance of courage would be required, regardless of which course he pursued.

For a year it so continued. When in Marys' company his sole endeavor was to please her, but to her at times it was obvious that his mind seemed heavily burdened with a problem not concerned with the subject which they at the moment discussed. Semi-occasionally she mentioned this to him. In turn he pretended ignorance of anything that could possibly give her cause to so question him.

One night as Fred sat in the reception hall of the Burlinson home, awaiting the appearance of Mary, the cloud of that unexplainable "something" settled heavily on and about him, and in the blackness and weight of its depressing influence he was more disturbed than ever before.

None of this was lost to Mary, as she at length entered to greet Fred; so in her usual out-spoken way she inquired in a worried, troubled sort of way, "Fred, won't you tell me what is on your mind? I know there is something that is bothering you." "Nothing bothering me, Mary." "But your thoughts seem so far off, so very far away. Do you know that in the very short while I have been in your company tonight I have asked you three times whether you intend attending the Ralston party.

OPPOSITES 

I don't believe you were even aware of my presence, as you made not the slightest reply." "Pardon me, Mary. I really did not hear you.' "Then your thoughts must have been of far away things, otherwise you would have heard and answered me. 99 "Mary, I have had a hard week. Two of the salesmen have been sick, and one of our best men has resigned; the whole thing has me somewhat perplexed. 

Samples are all ready for the annual western trip and it appears now that three territories cannot be covered." "I am sorry, Fred. I regret that I spoke as I did. 

I hope you will forgive me.

Their chairs were very close together, as she raised her hand and affectionately smoothed the crown of his head. "Don't worry, Fred. Everything will come out all right." 

Although seemingly not unappreciative of her kindly attention, he did not respond as she had hoped and expected he would. Disappointed and just a bit hurt, she arose and passed to the rear of his chair; for a space of a few moments she stood there; then, taking his head in her two hands, she drew it back and placed a kiss on his aching and feverish forehead. "There, Fred, that will remove all your worries."

Instead of helping, as Mary intended, it produced contrary effects, for it was more than apparent that 

Fred was now in combat with himself. "Why, my dear boy, I have not offended you, have

I? There is nothing and there is nobody under the broad Heavens that could make me intentionally do that. Come now, Fred, let us be happy the little while that remains before train time." "Mary, I am a brute. I am; I know I am. There

OPPOSITES 

249 is something I should say to you, but I am much too cowardly to say it; yet, I am unfair not to say it." "Why, I fail to understand you, Fred. Your business worries must be most distressing. I never saw you like this." "No, Mary, there is still something more than business cares." 

"Then tell me, Dear." "The trouble is I just can't tell you, Mary. I am much too fond of you to let you know." "Is there something I have done for which you should take me to task, and yet withhold the censure?" "No, all the trouble is with myself and within myself." "Then you must tell me. I have a right to know." "I acknowledge your right, but I lack the courage to speak. 

"Come, Fred, you are needlessly exaggerating some mere trifle." "No, it is not a trifle; it is something that vitally touches both your life and my life." "Then, in that case, you must-you must tell me," she exclaimed, as taking his right hand in both of hers and sinking her knees into the rug at his side she added the appeal of her eloquent eyes to her imploring words of sound. Of his duty Fred was fully aware, but her very kindness held him fast from wounding, possibly mortally hurting this strangely candid girl. He could not stab her with that knife of doubt, with that sword of double edge, the blade that had been hanging over his own harassed head for, oh, so many days, for well he knew should it fall on her the cut would be ragged, deep and wide.

Rising from his chair, he gently, very gently raised the sadly stricken Mary to her feet; then placing his arms around her he kissed her trembling lips. "Mary, I must go now. For leaving you so, I detest

OPPOSITES 

myself, but you must not worry. Just forget what has happened tonight. I promise to explain it all when I see you again. You are the sweetest girl I ever met.' 

He kissed her again. .99 "Good night, Mary, and God bless you,"—and he was gone.

CHAPTER XLII 

Feeling for himself a loathing contempt, Fred staggered as a drunken man as he made his troubled way down the street, and then like a crash of thunder's light there came to him that which might be called courage, but if courage it was at all it was courage of a cowardly sort. Passing a corner drug store and espying a public phone therein, an impulse seized him to acquaint Mary as briefly as possible with that which he had refused to communicate when in her presence. Quickly entering a booth, he was successful in having Mary answer his call before she had repaired to her room. "Mary," he said, "I know I am brutal; I admit I am a coward, but I was not able to tell you face to face that which you should know." "Fred, I wish you would come back and tell me here.

I cannot very well talk with you over the 'phone, as 

Mother and Helen might easily hear what I say.' "Mary, I know fully what I should do, but I lack the courage to do it. Furthermore, I have only a short time in which to catch the last train. You must hear me, Mary. I believe our engagement is all a mistake." 

Mary made no reply. "I am very fond of you, but there is something that holds me fast, something that hangs over me and that persists in telling me that we could not be happy as man and wife. It tells me that we are not intended for each other. I have done wrong in not telling you before." 

Guiltily anxious, he waited for Mary to speak.

Through a suspense of eternity he suffered, until came a voice, "Your time is up. Another nickel, please." 251

OPPOSITES 

Quickly he deposited the coin, then came Mary's response. Between sobs, in choking tones he heard: "I can't talk tonight, Fred. Come down next week and let us then discuss the matter. Good night, Fred." 

That was all.

CHAPTER XLIII 

During the following week Fred performed as one whose thoughts were far removed from things at hand. 

Physically a Robot, mentally he functioned as though far at sea or high in hazy clouds. That week he did some remarkably strange things-things very, very strange for Fred Davis to do. Usually so methodical and always master of the tasks in hand, during each succeeding hour of those days invariably he changed his mind where consideration of Mary was concerned. 

Many letters he wrote and then in turn destroyed each one. One of these letters was of apologetic regrets. 

One explained in a nebulous way why it would be best if his decision of the previous Sunday should stand.

During that week he wrote many other letters, but mailed none to Mary. Neither did he receive any from her.

Other weeks quite frequently he visited Mohntown between Sundays; during that week he did not go at all. 

The following Sunday he went on his usual train. 

When he alighted from the car he found Mary was at the station awaiting his coming. Following an exchange of words that meant really nothing, Mary said, "Fred

I deemed it best to meet you here that we might talk over matters somewhere other than at home. I have told the family nothing of what happened last week. 

I hope you approve of my idea and hope too that you are not offended that I came to meet you." "I have no reason to be offended, Mary, and I heartily agree with your suggestion that we discuss the matter elsewhere than at your house. Where do you propose going?" "Do you object to going out to the park where we 253

OPPOSITES 

can find a bench in a quiet spot and there talk things Over?" "Why, no, Mary, I am agreeable to that, but what explanation will you give your mother and Helen for so long a time absenting yourself?" "I can 'phone them that we are going out to the park for a while."

She did so, adding that they might be a trifle late for the evening meal.

Whether it was by design, or by fate, or by accident, they took possession of the same bench on which Mary had sat on at least two other occasions. For a while nothing of the thing they both had in mind was mentioned. 

Eventually Mary said: "Fred, I have no desire to thrust myself upon you, neither would I hold you to anything which is contrary to your desires or to your better judgment. I can only say I fail to bring to mind anything that has occurred that should or could create a change in your feelings toward me." "Mary, it is not that. I think a surpassing lot of you. In many respects you are the finest girl I ever met. I can honestly say I care more for you than any one I know, and yet there is something, a mysterious and persistent something, some strange, unseen force, mighty in combat and beyond my ken to understand; a feeling I cannot explain. Time after time it warningly tells me our affair is all a mistake; that we are not intended for each other, and that happiness cannot come out of a union between us." "Well, Fred," spoke Mary, and then paused and sat quite still; then reached down; it seemed as though involuntarily and absent-mindedly done-maybe it was -and she plucked a daisy that grew by the side of the bench.

Then said she: "It is all a mystery to me, this feeling (or may I call it lack of feeling?) of yours. I have

OPPOSITES 

255 admired you ever since I knew you. I thought you different from other men. You seemed so noble and so true, and when you gave me cause to believe that you cared for me, I was very glad, oh, so glad. Then that night when we sat together here on this same bench, this very same bench, you made me more than happy. I thought then you really loved me. Your remarks of a week ago came as a surprise and a shock. 

Did you feel that I had taken a lot for granted and had presumed much more than I should? All through the week I have been turning it over in my mind. Mother and Sister have asked if we had quarreled. Naturally and truthfully I told them that we had not. My unhappiness was not altogether because I feared I should lose or had already lost your love, but because of a shaming thought, the thought that if you had ceased to care for me it might have been that you believed me too forward, or of impoverished maidenly modesty."

As all the while she talked, she plucked one by one the petals from the daisy she held, and as she finished the destruction of one flower she forthwith plucked another and treated it as the previous one. 

When she had finished speaking, Fred said: "Mary, there is nothing I can add to what I have already said. 

I wish I might in some way either cast aside this darksome doubt, or else find something to help me to determine the proper path to pursue. 99 Mary removed another daisy from its stem. "Fred, this may seem ridiculous to you and probably it is, but in your expressed wish that something might help you there came to me the thought that possibly this little daisy might be of some assistance. You doubtless have seen done many times just what I have been doing; pulling out one at a time the petals of a daisy, alternating with each petal the words: 'He loves me, he loves me not.' If the number of petals be odd

OPPOSITES 

in number, the last petal plucked, of course, expresses the words: 'He loves me', or, if even in number, 'He loves me not'." "Is it not a hazardous responsibility to place on one little daisy?" "Well, Fred, you seem quite superstitious regarding that 'feeling' or that 'something' you mentioned, and so am I. Instead of one daisy, let us use three, the answer of the majority to help you decide what you believe is best to do, whether we go hand in hand through life together, or whether we just clasp hands in a final parting and say a last good bye." 

"Mary, it seems like making a serious matter trifling, but if you desire to let the daisy determine what we should do, I am willing. You pluck the petals.

Mary slowly removed from a daisy she held petal by petal, one petal at a time. "He loves me, he loves me not, he loves me." 

The number was odd. 99 "That is one in the affirmative," said Fred. "If you are willing, let us accept the decision of the one daisy.' "No," said Mary, "we agreed on three, so let it be just as agreed." 

So she plucked another daisy. "He loves me, he loves me not." 

The number was even. "It all depends now on this one little flower," said 

Mary. "It holds the fate of two lives." "He loves me not, he loves me, he loves me not."

As Mary reached to pluck what seemed the final petal, she was filled first with joy, then with dismay. 

Another petal, or what had once been one, remained to be plucked, and Mary noted that it was much withered and almost ready to drop. "What shall we call it, Fred?" "It all rather bears out my fears, Mary. It tells us 'yes', but that last petal is not white like the rest, and

OPPOSITES 

257 it is sadly shriveled and nigh to its death, but yet it is a petal, and Mary-the daisies have decided. My dear, if you are willing, we shall be married soon. You can name the day at your convenience, or even now. I am glad it is settled, even though done in what seems to be a rather foolish sort of way. I was not man enough to decide in a manly manner, but now that it is all settled, I hope and pray that all will be well.” 

Mary slipped her hand into Fred's. "I love you, Fred. I know you are trying only to avoid unhappiness for me, but the flowers I feel sure told the truth. But as much as I want you, I believe it is well that you and I should wait for awhile." 

And so, hand in hand, those two, Mary and Fred, sat on a bench in the park and watched the day's sun lay itself down to sleep among the far distant hills, and then as for a few fleeting moments it hesitated to turn out its bedside light, those two beheld across the sky and reflected in the waters of the silvered lake impossible shades, colors like unto those of which the artist dreams, and which for ages his brush has failed to produce. Thus had the sun in the fullness and splendor of its luminous glory said "Good night" to those two, to Mary and Fred, on the bench in the park. Then as held fast in silent and spell bound adoration, they watched the gentle hand of nature prepare to tuck in the covers of the eventide bed, Mary observed that a tiny speck of cloud had passed over just a trifle of an edge of their sun and had obscured a bit of its light. "A beautiful sunset, Mary!" said Fred. "Yes, Fred, but I wish that cloud had remained away."

It was too late to attend church services when Mary and Fred arrived at the Burlinson home. Mrs. Burlinson and Helen had already eaten their evening meal, and Helen had departed for church. Mrs. Burlinson, observing quite a change in Mary's demeanor from

OPPOSITES 

that of the past week, remarked: "Fred, you should come to Mohntown more frequently. You produce such a difference in Mary. I was really concerned about her the past few days; I feared she was ill, or that you two had quarreled, but I see now I can put my fears behind me. Doubtless, it was the tonic of your presence that she needed."

After Mrs. Burlinson had retired, Mary's and 

Fred's discussion was of future things, and among matters given consideration was the approximate time of the wedding.

CHAPTER XLIV 

The passing of the days held nothing of unusual moment until the arrival of the marriage day. Due to the fact that Mrs. Burlinson had been steadily even though slowly declining in health, a rather quiet wedding was observed at the Burlinson home. Relatives and only a few close friends were present. Mary looked charming and Fred, if possible, more handsome than ever.

Following the ceremony and reception, they departed for a honeymoon in the mountains. They were gone a full month, and on their return took up their abode in Mohntown, where Fred had built their new home. The letters sent from their mountain retreat indicated perfect happiness. Those who met them, following their return, could see nothing but a blissful future for a well-mated pair, and quite well did their opinions seem founded, for Mary appeared to live in an atmosphere of rapturous joy, while Fred went about smiling, and with the bearing of a man happy and content. Unfeigned and unpretended was this ecstasy of 

Mary, for her heart was happy and her soul was glad, but, while to all appearances Fred's matrimonial venture had brought him nought but joy and bliss, and while his wife was a delightful companion, Fred knew, although Mary did not suspect, that the "something" which had warned him and had held him back had been a real thing, a genuine "something", the warning of which it would have been well to have heeded. Fred doubted not that somewhere in the world there was someone for whom Mary would have been the perfect mate, but that Mary's someone was not himself. 259

OPPOSITES 

While satisfied that their lives would prove passably serene, and that there would be no distressing ruptures, the sound reasoning of the man made it clear to him that Mary was not the woman he should have married, and in his self analysis it was an easy thing to determine that he was not the man to make her the perfect husband. There was one thing, even if no more than one, that made all of this plain for Fred to see and understand. In Mary's mind existed no suspicion of Fred's fear. To her there could exist not a single weak or imperfect link in their love so perfect and flawless. 

Time eased away, and Mr. and Mrs. Fred Davis to the people of Mohntown continued to constitute a couple ideal and happy. Outside of business hours they were always together; Fred's path through life was 

Mary's path. Mary, doubtless, was much in love with her husband and fully believed that her love was fully reciprocated. Nothing appeared to give her cause for doubt, although in Fred's mind was ever the consciousness that although he cared much for Mary there still existed that "something", making him realize that as a perfect couple their title was flimsy and faulty. They had a beautiful home, those two, just those two. They had friends who visited them and they had their servants who performed well and faithfully their respective duties about the house, but it was Fred's and 

Mary's home, only Fred's and Mary's home, the home of just those two-just two-a family of two-only two.

Fred no longer spent much time at the mill in Norwald. He had resigned his position there to establish a business for himself. Instead of being sales-manager of the Norwald concern, he had entered into contracts with several manufacturing concerns including the Norwald mill to sell their entire productions. He established his headquarters in Mohntown, and from that

OPPOSITES 

261 city he directed the salesmen whom he employed. Occasionally he visited the various mills, but this did not prevent him from spending almost his entire time in the city where he and Mary lived. From the very first his was a successful business, and he advanced rapidly in the accumulation of wealth and in the good will of the community. By many he was considered the leading citizen of Mohntown. He was respected and held in high esteem by everyone who knew him. He was urged to enter politics but declined. Social life made claims on him, but he yielded only where Mary desired that he should, which was seldom, for Mary was rather a selfish wife in that she wanted Fred to spend as much of his time as possible alone with her. She had many friends, and she was well liked, but somehow she did not avail herself of the opportunity to climb the social scale as well she might, had it not been that she wanted Fred's time and attention exclusively for herself.

And so they lived on in their magnificent home, not the old home, but a new palatial mansion which Fred erected, and in that spacious palace lived two-just those two-only two.

CHAPTER XLV 

Fred's rapidly increasing business as a matter of course demanded additional employes from time to time. Occasionally he added to his selling force, but as much of his business was transacted by mail he was constantly enlarging his clerical staff, most of the members of which in the early days of Fred's commercial career were known as stenographers, but in later days. as secretaries, at least, that is what they termed themselves. 

Fred had in his employ one girl, who was really a real secretary-a secretary in every sense of the word. 

She knew the details of his office work as well as he did. 

She handled the mail with very little assistance from him, and even dictated letters to the other "secretaries", most of whom were jealous of her, and in that jealousy they lost their opportunity for advancement. 

Rather than endeavor to master the details of the business, they occupied much of their time in adversely criticising and attacking the girl who had applied herself to the advancement of Fred's interests, and in doing so rendered herself invaluable to him. 

There came a time when at least one of the others might have been chosen for advancement, had she been prepared to take over the duties of Fred's secretary, because one day the latter became suddenly ill and in a very short time died. Fred gave careful consideration to the qualifications of all the other girls, as a result of which he found none equipped or prepared to take a higher position. Several were afforded trials, all of whom failed. None of his business associates or friends had anyone to recommend. He then inserted 262

OPPOSITES 

263 an advertisement in the Help Wanted column of the local newspaper, in which he mentioned a time for those seeking the position to report at his offices. He arranged that his assistant chief clerk should first interview all applicants, then that any measuring even approximately to the required standard should be passed on to the chief clerk, who should examine them as to their proficiency in typing and stenography. Those surviving the latter test were to be admitted to Fred's private office for final interview.

Many applied, most of whom did not pass the assistant clerk. Twelve there were who were admitted to 

Fred's private office for final interview. Of these nine were soon eliminated. After carefully and closely interrogating the remaining three he had them in turn retire from the room and several times return for further tests. He gave them shorthand dictation; he dictated direct to the typewriter. He gave them dictaphone tests. Everyone of them seemed possessed of the essential qualifications, but as Fred was engaging only one secretary he was obliged to disappoint two of the applicants, but which one to choose it was of exceeding difficulty for him to decide.

Following quite some consideration alone and then in consultation with the chief clerk and assistant, he had practically decided to select one as his secretary and to place the remaining two in other positions. In fact, he did explain to two of the applicants that he could not engage all three as his secretary, but that he could, and would gladly give them positions as stenographers in one of the other offices, in each of which cases the opportunity was accepted. The third girl then being invited to return to Fred's office for final interview, he asked her some additional questions, and then touched on the matter of salary, which being satisfactorily agreed upon he was on the very point of clos-

ing the matter by engaging her when the conversation was interrupted by the telephone bell.

Mahlon. I saw your ad in this morning's paper, but as I live in the suburbs and owning no car, it was necessary for me to trolley to Mohntown. Something went wrong with the electric current and I was delayed. I have just arrived in town and am phoning from the corner of Third and Spruce streets. If you have not already engaged a secretary, I hope you will grant me an interview." 99

Hardly had Fred replaced the telephone receiver than there came a knock on his office door, and in response to "Come in," Mazie Mahlon entered into the life of Fred Davis. Totally at loss for explanation, he felt himself ill at ease as how to receive this latest applicant for a position in his office. Never wanting for poise or self control, whether in presence of woman or man, about this girl there was an atmosphere of doubt holding Fred fast in the clutch of embarrassing silence. There was that which whispered "Caution",

OPPOSITES 

265, as came a warning voice to his ear; there was dread of a direful happening, as something told of danger very near, and yet before either had uttered a sound, within the innermost minds of both was firmly fixed the knowledge that Mazie Mahlon was destined to be Fred 

Davis's secretary. Mazie Mahlon, of a type to Fred hitherto unknown, not tall of stature and yet appearing shorter than her actual height; shoulders rather broad for a female frame, and chest that just escaped being flat; a hat of green felt held captive most of her hair, only one elusive chestnut curl circling around her right temple; eyes, yes, of course, she had eyes, but as for me I hold not the skill to describe those strangely tinted orbs; they might have been green; at times one might have seen in them a reddish hue; brows quite light, in fact, almost white; nose of the Grecian mould; mouth peculiar to itself, none like it before nor since; when open, which it was during most of her waking time, 'twas mighty fair to see, for upper lip was full of color and shaped like outstretched wings; but with closed mouth her lower lip, quite thin and paler than its mate, detracted much from the attractiveness of the mouth in its entirety. The weakness of a receding chin was quite offset by the strength of a rugged jaw, decidedly firm and square.

As the door closed behind her, she did not speak at once, but she smiled in a familiar sort of way, even as though she and Fred had been acquainted for a spell of many days.

The silence was eventually broken by Fred, as he said, "Won't you be seated, Miss Mahlon?" "Yes, thank you," she replied, suiting the action to the answer.

Although she was not in stylish attire, her clothes were neat and clean. "It's raining dreadfully outside," she said; "A horrible day for one to wear one's best clothes."

OPPOSITES 

Closely she seemed to be studying Fred. The chair in which she sat being a rather high one, not allowing her feet to touch the floor, she drew one of them up and sat on it, following this by arranging her skirt carefully about the other one.

Fred inquired very closely as to her experience, previous places of employment and reasons for leaving same. Satisfied thus far, he then stressed the importance of good health, explaining that the duties of his secretary required quite close application throughout the entire year, with no days off except Sundays, holidays and vacation times. Stating that while he was opposed to encroaching upon the time of his employes, he also wanted it well understood that his office was not a place for one observing standard time in the morning and daylight saving time in the afternoon. 

To all of this her reply was to the effect that she had been sick but once in her life, and that she had fully recovered from that attack. "There is another matter which I would touch," he said. CHAPTER XLVI 

On the appointed Monday morning Fred and the chief clerk were in the private office when Miss Mahlon entered. As she accepted Fred's greeting and introduction to the chief clerk, she was perfectly at ease, so very much so, that Fred wondered at himself for having engaged the girl, and yet, even then, he felt powerless to exhibit any objection to her manner, her attitude or aught else. Inquiring where she should place her hat and coat and being so informed, she proceeded to remove them, in the performance of which Mazie 

Mahlon presented to Fred the first startling surprise that he was to receive at her hands. On the occasion of their previous interview her hat concealed all of her hair with the exception of a single curl. On that Monday morning, as she stood with her head uncovered, to

Fred's shocked amazement there was revealed a mass of bushy chestnut locks, apparently untrained, and certainly in wild disorder. Well covering his surprise, he invited the girl to be seated, adding that he would give her some dictation in a very few minutes.

The very first day she proved herself quite capable and her work was most neat in appearance. Once or twice she was not quite sure that she perfectly understood her notes, and did not hesitate to seek Fred's assistance that the substance matter in question should be made perfectly clear to her. Fred was pleased with this exhibition of interest as well as good judgment. 

As time went on she made herself more and more valuable. Her eyes were seldom turned towards the office clock. When time came to close the office, if there were important letters still unfinished, she did 269

OPPOSITES 

not allow them to remain so. This as well as other rare but desirable attributes rendered her services more than satisfactory to her employer. The only thing that impressed Fred as possibly preventing a credit of one hundred percent was the fact that she was prone to voluntarily enter into conversation with him on various subjects which were entirely foreign to business. From week to week these conversations became a little more lengthy, and much more personal. A favorite time for these talks was following office hours. In course of some of these unbusinesslike talks she told him much of herself, and then, not having been discouraged by 

Fred, she made bold to ask him of his personal self, which strangely true received no resentment from him. 

In time he took an interest in the things she imparted to him of her own life. Many times he sought for an explanation of this most unusual condition that had developed in his relations with this girl, and the only answer he gave himself was that her loyalty to his interests and her faithfulness to the duties of her position had produced in him a justifiable interest in her affairs.

Her existence had not been a happy one, she claimed, explaining that conditions in her home had been such that she had been denied many of the privileges granted by other parents to their daughters. She told him that she was required to account to both her mother and her father for every minute of every twenty-four hours, and that as pay day succeeded pay day there was exacted from her every penny that she earned. 

One day she apologized for the clothes she wore. "If only I could keep part of my wages," she said, "I should be able to make a better appearance in your office."

Fred replied to the effect that he was perfectly satisfied with her clothing and appearance, and that all he

7 desired in that respect was that she shou'd l aya he neat and tidy. "But, oh," she said, "you always look so up-to-date and handsome; you make me feel just that much more inferior in appearance, and the contrast arouses me to rebel at my misfortune." 

At that particular moment Fred might and should have seen a danger sign, but somehow the influence. that had controlled him ever since his first meeting with Mazie Mahlon continued to hold sway, and that strong man made no effort to save himself from future embarrassment and possible disaster. Under the spell of his secretary he was blind to impending danger, and although the semaphore was red and dead against him, either he saw it not or else was determined to ignore and disregard the signal of warning. comment.

Their conversations became more frequent and longer extended. On several occasions, entering his office unexpectedly, he observed that his secretary, while steadily pounding the keys of the typewriter, gave evidence of silent weeping, but, although he observed her apparent distress, he made no until one day, noticing copious tears coursing down the girl's face and dropping on her desk, solicitously he inquired if she were ill. Receiving a negative reply, soothingly and tenderly he did all that any man could do to comfort any girl, as well as to ascertain the cause of her seeming grief.

At the same table I ate my lunch today, there sat a man and a woman. With them was a young girl. I am sure she is their daughter. I believe she is about my age. they showered upon the girl with the dissimilar conditions in my own home." "Is not your mother kind to you?" "Yes, she is kind. I always have missed the affection of a father and, Mr. Davis, it hurts me so much when I hear other girls addressing their fathers as 'Dad' and 'Daddy', and see the responding gleam of pride in their parents' eyes. Since I was a very small girl I remember only once, just one lone occasion, when my father kissed me. 

That was one day when I lay sick in a hospital. They thought I was dying and Father came with Mother to see me. That day he did lean over and kiss me on the forehead. I often think about it and wish, oh, how I wish, that I could have someone I could call ‘Daddy', a daddy who would kiss me, if only just once in a while."

Just at that moment the conversation was interrupted by someone entering the office, and the subject was not resumed that day.

It so happened that as Miss Mahlon returned from luncheon one day of the following week she observed

Fred looking out through one of the windows of his private office. Although his gaze was in the general direction of the out-door world, evidently his vision was not held by anything in particular.

As he shook his head very, very slowly, she returned to her chair and hastily wrote a note: "I fear I have hurt you, my dear. Forgive me. I would not injure you for the whole world." She handed him the note and once again resumed her chair.

Reading the message, but making no reply, he seemed to be straining under the pull of conflicting emotions. Like a man far at sea without chart or compass, he sat struggling for control of self. With eyes held fast by that little piece of paper, in rapid succession he experienced joy, anxiety, surprise, dismay, as all the while, silent and still, unmoving, concerned, Mazie Mahlon, fearing the consequence of that little note, awaited the sentence that she felt Fred would eventually pronounce.

At the end of one of those eternities that humans sometimes endure, he said in a low tone, "Miss Mahlon, I have no children and never had." 

Relieved in mind and heart she softly murmured, “I am sorry," then reaching out her hand and taking the note she tore it into a hundred little pieces of meaningless paper, which she flung through a window to the winds of Heaven.

At that moment the phone bell rang. It was a long distance call for Fred, and quite some time was consumed because of the ensuing conversation. portance on your mind, I shall be glad to give you a few minutes."
