Turning his head, he looked her full in the face, but the eyes he saw were not her eyes. Yes, it was her face, but with eyes not hers; but, then, how could that be? 

Eyes are not grafted, transplanted or exchanged at will. Therefore, it could not be her face, and if not her face, then it was not his secretary. Yet her hair, her dress, her figure, her face, all but her eyes. What transformation was this? Then, as he wondered and pondered, he realized that it was Miss Mahlon, but by some strange happening her eyes were changed. And such eyes were these adopted ones-different in form, for these new eyes were full and opened wide-these eyes had color that one would know-eyes of a beautiful hazel hue, but it was the expression of those lustrous orbs that held and thrilled his wondering soul. 

Gazing, staring and speechless, he sat in the spell; then by a mighty effort recovering his poise and escaping the charm, he arose, strode to the door opening from his office to the main hallway, placed his hand on the knob of the door and said in tones of finality, "Don't you worry about what you said today. Everything is all right. You had better go now. Your folks will be concerned because of your late home coming," and thereupon he opened the door. 

She made no response to his latter remark, but simply said, "Good night, Mr. Davis. You look rather weary and worn; take good care of yourself, will you? 

Good night," passed through the door and was gone. "Good night," he had said, in reply to her parting

OPPOSITES 

277 salutation; then stood and watched her pass down the corridor until she turned the corner in the direction of the elevator. Even after she was lost to his vision, he continued to stand there; for quite some minutes he stood there, motionless as one of stone. Then he returned to his chair, sat down, and although he had planned to attack an accumulation of important work, beyond taking up a pile of papers and laying it down again, he did nothing with the big job that confronted him. For over an hour he sat and did nought but think and think of the strange girl that had come into his life and who, in spite of himself, seemed to be weaving her way into his every thought; and into his very life. had she made her way to such an extent that he was hindered and hampered in everything that he did or even attempted to do. Despite determined efforts, he could not dismiss her from his mind. 

Until she had written that little note, there had been nothing said or done by either of them that was not above and beyond suspicion, and yet this girl, whether present or absent, somehow made him feel glad that she occupied his thoughts, in fact, she had become his sole thought.

Having accomplished nothing towards disposing of the matters that he had intended to give his attention, he finally closed his desk and went home. 

That night in Mary's presence, although he tried hard to be his usual self, he felt very ill at ease. Evidently Mary detected nothing different from his usual manner, for had such been the case doubtless her frankness would have impelled her to make mention of it. 

Fred slept but little that night. Failing to dismiss the girl from his thoughts, he tried in vain to explain the matter to himself. He could simply not understand why it was that a girl who had entered his office not many weeks since, and of whom his first impression was not entirely favorable, could so hold the place in his mind

OPPOSITES 

that she did. He knew many girls more beautiful, all of whom dressed with better taste as well as wore more attractive clothes. She was not polished and she was at times rather careless in the use, even if not lacking in the knowledge of good English. Some of her ways appeared rather selfish. Her manners were not of the best, and yet this girl, in spite of all his efforts to the contrary, was occupying more of his thoughts than anything or anybody else in his life, past or present. To a certain extent, because of her, he was even neglecting his business, for there were times when thoughts of her made it impossible for him to concentrate on important problems that arose at the office. Tossing in his bed, one minute he determined to dispense with her services; the next minute he resolved to have a plain talk with her and let her understand that their relations and their conversations at all times must be strictly of a business nature. Then would come the fear that possibly he might offend her, and thereby not only lose her services, which were valuable, but that she might pass out of his life. This was something that he certainly must not allow to come to pass. One moment he seemed to hate and loathe her with all the bitterness at his command; then he felt sorry for her and despised himself for his unkind sentiment; then came a feeling which he could not describe and which he refused to admit was affection for her. There was only one thought he had that was not contradicted by some other thought, and that was that he was sorry he ever had met her.

As wakefully, feverishly he sought for relief, across his mind flashed the verse: "Vice is a monster of so frightful mien, that to be hated needs but to be seen, but seen too oft, familiar with her face, first we endure, then pity, then embrace." But then this could not apply to Mazie. She was not vice, but then on the other hand was it not true that first he had endured her; that then the stories of her home life had enlisted his sympathy

OPPOSITES 

279 and he had pitied her; then, that although he had not physically embraced her, he could not help but know that the firm, fast grip and grasp she held on his life and feelings embraced him, even though he did not physically embrace her.

CHAPTER XLVII 

With the new day's dawning Fred had no nearer reached a conclusion as to how to dispose of or take care of the matter that so stubbornly and persistently vexed and perplexed him than he had the evening previous. Mary, observing that he looked quite tired and worn, inquired if he were ill, to which he replied that as there had been considerable on his mind he had slept but little during the night. Then, to avoid further questioning, he stated that there were some matters at the office left over from the preceding day that required his immediate attention, and that to afford them proper attention he must hasten through his breakfast, which he proceeded to do. Mary was quite concerned about him he seemed so different from his usual self. As he did not seem disposed to talk, she refrained from further questioning, but as he kissed her good bye and walked down the street, it was with a curious and anxious gaze that her eyes followed him until he had passed from her sight. She observed that his step lacked its usual elasticity, and Mary said to herself, "Poor Fred is working too hard and too late. He should arrange to take a vacation." 

Fred arrived at the office earlier than usual and by pure force of will compelled himself to take up the matter that he had abandoned unfinished the night before. As he was alone and not obliged to answer phone calls, interview visitors, or be interrupted by any of the employes, he was encouraged by the undisturbed headway he was enabled to accomplish. There was not the distracting "click click" of the typewriter; neither was there the hum of voices, either in the building or rising 280

OPPOSITES 

281 from the street below. Pleased because of some problems solved and applying himself to overcoming others, he was aroused from deep concentration by the unwelcome voice of Miss Mahlon. 

He had not heard her enter, but her words "Good morning, Mr. Davis, I hope you are feeling better this morning" broke in upon a stillness which had been so welcome and so helpful to him.

She had arrived at least a quarter of an hour earlier than customary, and the sudden and unexpected interruption of his work, which had been so pleasingly absorbing, surprised and startled Fred, who in a muddled state of confusion jumped quickly to his feet, advanced rapidly towards the girl, clasped her right hand as he would that of a long absent friend, and nervously exclaimed, "Good morning, Miss Mahlon. I am quite well this morning, thank you. I am very glad to see you. How are you today?" Then, with her hand clasped in his, all of his good resolutions evaporated into vaporous nothings. "You don't look well this morning, Mr. Davis. Does your head hurt you? Your eyes are so heavy. I fear you must be suffering from a headache." "Oh, nothing worth mentioning. I did not sleep well last night." "There was no trouble, I hope, Mr. Davis." "No, but I could not help thinking, or rather—oh, well, ah--I did not finish all that I intended to do before I went home last night. I had it on my mind that 

I must rise early this morning." "Well, you certainly do not look yourself. Just sit down a moment.' 

He obeyed as though she were the employer and he the one employed. Taking her handkerchief to the wash stand, she saturated it with water, then returned and placed it on his forehead and held it there. "Miss Mahlon, you should not do that. I am not ill."

OPPOSITES 

"Even though not ill, that will make you feel better." 

Feeling the touch of her soft hands on his head as she held there the moistened handkerchief, from which emanated a strangely sweet and potent perfume, his better judgment told him to remove those hands and the handkerchief they held, but man's weakness and the peculiar power she possessed as well as exercised made him glad that she was there with him, and that she held that cooling bit of linen to his fevered brow. 

A light, quick step in the reception office, a knock on the private office door; Miss Mahlon in phrenetic panic, gripping tight her moistened handkerchief, hastily passed to her own office on the opposite side of 

Fred's private room, as Fred all the while confusedly struggling to remove his own handkerchief from his pocket to dry his moistened head, in a muffled tone said "Come in.' Whereupon a woman entered his office. 

The woman was Mrs. Fred Davis. Apparently unmindful of the panic she had created, she said, "Fred,

I was so concerned about you, I really had to come down. Why, what is the matter with your forehead? 

It is all wet." “Why, ah, I had a headache and I just had a handkerchief saturated with cold water on it." "I am so sorry, Fred. You are working too hard. 

Let me have your handkerchief, so that I may put some more water on it for you." 

Fred looked on his desk and in the scrap basket. "Why, that is strange," he said, "I had that handkerchief on my head just before you knocked. I can't explain what has happened to it.' 99 "Oh, well, don't bother, Dear, I'll use mine," she said.

She walked into the other office where Miss Mahlon sat, for the washstand was there. While moistening her own handkerchief she detected the odor of a per-

OPPOSITES 

283 fume that to her was unfamiliar and which heavily permeated the secretary's office. 

In the meantime Fred, who had lost his head without hope of its recovery, had removed his own handkerchief from his pocket and with it was drying his forehead as Mary re-entered the room. "Why, Fred, I see you have found your handkerchief, but it does not appear very wet in spite of the fact that you have just removed it from your moistened head." "Oh, no, but this is another one," he flounderingly explained, as in embarrassment and confusion he dropped the handkerchief he was holding to the floor, and before he could reach for it Mary had recovered it, and immediately placed her own handkerchief to her husband's brow. "Just hold it there," she said, as she turned to go to the secretary's office to moisten Fred's handkerchief which she retained in her hand. 

She was at loss to understand the mysterious disappearance of Fred's missing handkerchief. She stood still midway between the two offices, held to curious wondering as to the meaning of it all. When she first entered the private office her husband's head was most unmistakably moist; therefore the handkerchief that had been applied thereto could have been removed but scarce a fleeting moment. Fred was seated when she first saw him; the lost linen must have been used where she found him, and yet in the twinkling of an eye it was gone. And that strange odor; she wondered if that could offer any clue. No, that fragrance was in the secretary's office. But, wait, it now seems close at hand. Involuntarily she raised the handkerchief she held, and from that piece of linen, the one that Fred had dropped and which she recovered from the floor, there emanated and touched her nostrils the identical scent that had attracted her in Miss Mahlon's office.

OPPOSITES 

After a moment of further hesitation, she proceeded to moisten the fragrant handkerchief that she might place it on Fred's head, and reclaim her own. As she did this her face was set in new lines, and she was thinking very hard. A new Mary was born that morning a new Mary whose heart was strangely skipping beats, and then in turn was riotously racing—a new

Mary who in quick succession registered sympathy, mystification, incredulity, mortal hurt, and hope—a very slight hope. 

It was Fred who spoke next, "I am quite all right now, Mary. I have been working very hard, but I feel much better now, so since I have much to do I'll have to get to work." "Shall I stay here just a little while? You may need me," she said. "No, I expect some callers, some buyers from New 

York. I must prepare for their coming. They will be here very soon. I appreciate your coming down, but I think it best that you allow me to get back on the job.' "Well, as you prefer, Fred. Good bye. Come home early tonight and take a good rest, Dear." 99 There was a new look in her eye as she passed out of the private office that day. As she made her way homeward, she said to herself, "I don't care for that girl. There is something about her I don't like. I wish

I could satisfactorily explain to myself the mystery of 

Fred's disappearing handkerchief and the similarity of those odors; but, anyhow, I don't like that girl.' 

After Mary's departure Fred applied himself to earnest thought, searching for an explanation that would satisfactorily explain the disappearing handkerchief, should it so happen that Mary raised the point again. Then, as buyers were expected shortly, he summoned Miss Mahlon to his office, and although doubtless she expected him to discuss with her Mary's unexpected and embarrassing visit, he did not mention it,

OPPOSITES 

285 but proceeded to dictate a number of letters, which dictation was interrupted by the arrival of the anticipated buyers. As Miss Mahlon was returning to her own office, she remarked, "I hope I have not made any trouble for you, Mr. Davis." 

He made no reply.

CHAPTER XLVIII 

Although for some days Fred made honest endeavor to confine all of his conversations with Miss Mahlon to matters of business, she was at times successful in wedging things of a personal nature into their talks. 

She was made cognizant of the fact, however, that although Fred was always courteous and polite to her, he was quite less cordial than of former days. 

Following several uneventful weeks, came one cold morning a happening that may have been by design, or it may just have appeared so. When Miss Mahlon entered the office that day Fred greeted her with a polite "Good morning, Miss Mahlon." "Good morning, Mr. Davis. Isn't it cold? I nearly froze coming into the city this morning. My fingers are so stiff I simply cannot take off my gloves. Do you mind helping me?" 

Fred, always polite, and with no thought of any thing except to comply with the request, arose and assisted his secretary to remove her gloves; he also took her hat and coat and placed them on a clothes tree in her office. "I thank you, Mr. Davis. You are very kind. I fear my hands are so cold that I cannot take dictation immediately. Maybe you think I am exaggerating. 

Just take my hand. Feel how cold it is." 

Needless to say, the request was readily granted. 

When, following a lengthy treatment of holding and stroking, the girl reluctantly pronounced both hands sufficiently warm for typing and Fred returned to his desk, Mazie Mahlon with what might have been interpreted as a lingering longing look went leisurely to 286

OPPOSITES 

287 her office, secured her note book, returned to the private office and seated herself a little closer-just an almost imperceptible trifle closer than usual to Fred and awaited dictation, but few letters were dictated that day and little work was done. There was much talking, but the conversation covered subjects entirely foreign to Fred's business.

For several days this procedure was repeated. When in the presence of his secretary Fred's knee was bent to his queen. Seemingly the girl's sole desire was to talk, and in spite of the fact that there was much in the way of important business that demanded this business man's attention he sat and listened to many incidents in the life of that very odd girl, and then in turn, when it became her pleasure, she drew from him many things concerning himself that he had never previously revealed to anyone. 

Returning to the office one day following lunch time, he found Miss Mahlon in tears. He was at once extremely solicitous and drew from her the remark, "You don't know how terrible it is not to have a father that you can call 'Daddy', a father that will at least kiss you good bye as you leave home in the morning and implant on your lips a good night kiss at the close of the day." "I am very sorry, Miss Mahlon, that that matter seems to distress you so much, and I regret that there is nothing I can do to alleviate your mental anguish." "But, are you sure there is nothing that you can do?" she asked. "I am sure I know of nothing that I can do to change your home conditions." "Well, you have no children of your own. You are always so good to me, much kinder than my own father, so why can't you be just sort of a daddy to me?" 

Fred vainly sought a reply.

OPPOSITES 

"Won't you?" she asked.

Curiously, anxiously, he longed to interpret the language of that strangely magnetic girl's soul; he longed to unravel the mystery of her power; he longed to unriddle the sorcery of those kaleidoscopic eyes; he longed to know why and how she held him irresistible to her whim and will.

Strong in the desire for independence and freedom of will, had he made instant reply to the girl, doubtless negative it would have been, but he delayed much too long. Already softened by lingering tears, speedily reinforced by hypnotic appeal of magic orbs, his hesitancy had made him weak, and in that his cause was lost. "Yes, little girl, I'll be a daddy to you. I cannot really take your father's place, but I am going to be kind to you, give advice when sought and endeavor to make you happy and content in your position here." 

Their conversation was interrupted at that moment, just as previous talks had been. Was some kind fate striving to save this man from the consequence of his folly? Who knows? 

When the hour arrived for Miss Mahlon to make her departure, Fred was still in conference with the salesman who had halted their talk that day, but, instead of leaving, his secretary continued writing letters, and even when these were finished she still remained in her office. 

Immediately following the departure of his salesman with whom the conference had been quite lengthy, Fred closed his desk and prepared to depart. Observing a light in the secretary's office, he went there to turn it off, and to his surprise found Miss Mahlon at her desk. "Miss Mahlon, I thought you had gone long ago." "No, I could not go without saying 'good bye' to Daddy."

OPPOSITES 

289 He helped her on with her coat and said, "Well, good night, little girl. I hope you will be happy until 

I see you again.' 

She did not move, but stood there as though in expectancy of something unperformed. "You may go now, unless there is something you wish to say! ?" "Is that the way daddies let little girls go?" "Just what do you mean, Miss Mahlon?"

She stood near the door with her face upturned. "Aren't you going to kiss me good night, Daddy?" she said.

He advanced to where she stood waiting and kissed her on the lips, whereupon she immediately opened the door and just as quickly passed out, saying, as she departed, "Good night, Daddy, wasn't that nice?"

Fred closed the door after her; then much concerned and strangely depressed he stood motionless, wondering what it all meant and where he was being led. 

When he had promised to be a father to the girl, he had no idea it meant just what it seemed the girl intended. Although quite true she had mentioned missing a father's affection, he had no thought that he would be expected to supply that parental deficiency. 

However, he palliated his mind a trifle with the thought that probably there was no real harm in what had been done.

As he approached his home, power of speech seemed to desert him. Suffering keenly the pangs of self-rebuke, he endeavored to comfort himself in the thought that he had done nothing that was wrong, not really wrong. Entering the house, he made an earnest effort to be pleasant, but his assumed manner was much at variance with the turbulent uprising of his sadly disturbed state of mind.

His conscience was actively asserting itself. He feared Mary would detect that he was not at ease. He

OPPOSITES 

did his utmost to dissemble. He rather overdid it, but luckily for him Mary took his exurberance of pleasantry for the reaction of over-wrought and straining nerves. "Let us have a little game of chess," she said, thinking it might assist him to concentrate on something which might take his mind from business cares and worries. 

Mary and Fred spent many of their evenings playing chess together. Both were fond of the game. Fred had taught Mary how to play and she had been an apt pupil. She had, however, not yet succeeded in winning from him, except when he permitted her to do so, but in that she was not deceived, for she understood the game sufficiently well to realize that her victories were not won by means of her own merit. 

Being especially anxious that night to please Mary, Fred readily assented, but, as all the while they played, it was impossible for him to banish thoughts of Miss Mahlon, and the incidents of the day continued uppermost in his mind. They played several games, all of which Mary won, not so much through her own skill, as through the extremely bad playing of Fred. His mistakes at times were almost ludicrous; in fact, so much so, that Mary was able to see quite readily that it was not because Fred was endeavoring to throw the games to her that she was the victor, but because his mind was elsewhere than on the chess board. 

Finally she said, "I think we had better stop playing, Fred. There is certainly something wrong with you. have been noticing it for sometime past. I think you must be over-worked. Can't you arrange to go away for awhile?" "I do not believe I can, Mary. There is much that requires my attention, and this is really the busiest time of the whole year. There are a number of things that no one can handle but myself.'

OPPOSITES 

291 "You told me that you had an excellent chief clerk." "Yes, I have, but his experience is not along the line of handling a corps of salesmen." "Well, can't you lay your plans in advance and leave the details in the hands of your secretary?" "No, I do not believe I can do that. She has not been with me very long." "You used to tell me how capable she was, and until recently you were constantly lauding her loyalty to you.' 99 Although Mary had not previously touched upon the subject, both she and Fred were quite mindful that Miss Mahlon had not held a place in their conversations since the embarrassing handkerchief incident. "Is she just as satisfactory as she was, Fred?" "Oh, yes, yes. Why do you ask?" "Well, you had not spoken of her to me for quite a while, and well, Fred, I wish you would please me and be careful of that girl. I do not believe she means you any good." "Why, Mary, what do you mean? You never saw her but once." "I know, but that was sufficient for me. Somehow I believe she is a dangerous person. Won't you promise me that you will be careful?" "Do not be foolish, Mary. You have hardly even seen the girl." "True, but sometimes I have intuition or what sometimes is called 'a hunch'. I do not like her and would be just as well satisfied if you were to dispense with her services." 

Fred wondered how much Mary knew or thought she knew. "Mary, just because you have a fancy, a totally unwarranted one, you do not expect me to lose the services of the best secretary I ever had?"

OPPOSITES 

"Is she really better than Miss Galeton, your previous secretary?" "Yes, I think she is. She has all the good qualities of Miss Galeton, and then some others that I never expected to find in any secretary." "That may be so, Fred. It may be that my fears are not well founded, but I do wish that you would be careful. There are so many designing women, and just my brief observation of her and the few times she has answered my phone calls during your absence from the office have somehow caused me to feel apprehensive." "Oh, just forget it, Mary. It is all nonsense. I am going to bed." "I did not wish to offend you, Dear. My motives were of the best." "Oh, that's all right, Mary, only don't worry about anything connected with my business. That is my end of our affairs and I'll take care of it." "All right, Fred. Let us retire. Probably you can have a good night's rest and rise well fortified for the work of tomorrow." 

Fred slept and did not waken throughout the entire night. In his sleep he dreamed and all his dreams had as a party to them Miss Mahlon. There were times when Mary was a part of his vision, but always Miss Mahlon held a place in the pictures of his slumber. 

When he arose the next morning he seemed much refreshed, and Mary remarked to him that he appeared rested. However, one of the dreams, leaving a better defined impression in his memory than is usual with dreams, had left with him a definite and reasonable suggestion, in consequence of which he intended to tell 

Miss Mahlon of that particular vision of his sleeping hours, with the firm hope that it might be the means of avoiding impending disaster.

With definite design he walked to the office that morning. He sought the benefit of cool, fresh air, for

OPPOSITES 

293 he believed that he should be in the best possible condition both physically and mentally to best handle what might prove to be a mighty big job. He did not fully understand Miss Mahlon. He was not satisfied as to just what was in her mind concerning him. Sometimes he thought she had some well laid plan, the outcome of which she hoped would be beneficial to her, and expensive to him. There were times when he believed that she was genuinely fond of him. That particular thought was invariably followed by a realization that he was a husband; moreover, that he was quite a few years the senior of the girl. But whatever her motive might be, or whether she was entirely without motive, as he walked he felt he must terminate the more than friendly relation that had been created between them. 

He did not wish to lose her services, but he was strong in determination that if she remained everything must be on a strictly business basis. Making his way toward the office, he framed in his mind many times just what he intended saying to her, but just as often as he built, just as often did he tear down and start to rebuild, so that when he had reached his place of business, there was nothing of a definite nature fixed for him to say or do. Entering the private office, he found Miss Mahlon already there. She greeted him with "Good morning, Daddy. I just felt that you would be here early this morning, and since I wanted to be here when you came in I made it a point to arrive still earlier than you did." 

He merely answered, "Good morning, Miss Mahlon. I did not realize I was early. As a matter of fact, having walked the entire distance, I rather expected to be late. Had I not come on foot, I should have been here quite a bit before this." "The walk has done you good," she said. "You look still more handsome than ever. But why does Daddy call me 'Miss Mahlon'? Should not a daddy call his adopted daughter by her Christian name?"

OPPOSITES 

"I imagine he should, but I want to discuss that little matter with you, Miss Mahlon."

In view of her reception of him, it was hard, very hard, especially at that particular moment, for him to take the stand which he had determined he must, but his walk in the open air had apparently well equipped him to lay before the girl that which was uppermost in his mind.

After a bit of hesitation, he resumed: "I have something I must say to you. This morning I have been giving much deliberation to matters concerning you and me, and I confess I am just a trifle troubled. Nothing very serious has happened yet, but I wish to avoid anything that might be embarrassing, if not worse, for both of us." 99 "Then you do not like me, after all?" "On the contrary, Miss Mahlon, I fear I like you just a little too much." "Why all this change of heart and attitude, Mr. 

Davis? Yesterday you were quite willing, yes, seemingly pleased, to be my daddy, and even kissed me good night. Today I take it you wish to dispense with my services." "Not at all. One reason I am discussing this matter with you is because I do want to retain you here, but, as I see it, the only way for you to remain is as my secretary.' 99 "No more?" she asked. "No more," he replied. "Well, then, Mr. Davis, I think it would be well if you were to secure a new secretary to take my place in two weeks, unless you desire that I go sooner."

He pictured the passing of this girl. He visualized their parting, an irrevocable parting of permanency, and in prospect he felt relief. During his walk of the morning he had realized with startling vividness the course of the stream in which he was drifting. All

OPPOSITES 

295 waters flow towards the sea, and he knew that the vessel in which he had selected to sail was closely approaching the sea of unfaithfulness, there to be lured by the siren's song to the rocks of ruin and the treacherous shoals of shame, there where many a craft has sunk, and whence few men ever return. He heard the cracking, tearing sound of his boat upon the rocks; he felt death's breath in the water's chill, and he felt himself floundering in the trough of the sea, and then he felt himself tossed by contemptuous waves on the isle of the loathed and despised. Yes, he experienced relief in avoiding all this just in time. 

He had not replied to the girl, because he expected her to continue speaking, and then as he waited a panorama of the shame that awaited his continuance in his present course passed rapidly before the vision of his soul. Then, as there came a pause in the passing picture, he chanced to look at the secretary. He saw her with downcast eyes; he saw her in dejected and sore disappointment, and then he softened and wavered in his resolution. "I cannot allow this girl to pass out of my life; there is a tie that holds us together; in her I find an unexplainable, but, nevertheless, irresistible attraction. It cannot be beauty that draws me, for in the world are many girls more beautiful than she, yes, some close at hand in my own employ, and today her charm of person fades and she is far from at her best, and yet, although wisdom tells me that our lives should in diverging channels flow, I cannot, cannot let her go. It is not by her culture I am held, for in that she lacks most sadly. It is not to maidenly modesty I am tied, for she is possessed not of even desirable reserve. She has ability in her line, but so have other girls, and yet without a single outstanding quality to offset the traits 

I find in her distasteful, against my judgment, my better common sense, yes, even against my very will, I cannot let her go. Why? I do not know. All I can say is this:

OPPOSITES 

there is a voice that whispers to me, a voice that tells me 'twas fate that brought us two together, and that fate decrees we live our lives just side by side, for in us there lies a common destiny. 

But, wait, what of my standing in the community? 

Can fate confuse my neighbor's eyes? Can fate make me respected by my fellow men? Can fate make me false to a wife, and paint me white to the world at large. May I be low and fate hold me high, or can I be unclean and fate make me pure? Always have I followed the honorable way, true to home, to wife, to country and to myself. Can fate let me so continue, and yet retain this girl in other than a secretary's place? 

No, it cannot be done. Fate must bow to what is right.

To the world I must remain an honest man. Though this girl be hurt in pride, and though I refuse to accept. this woman so freely offered me by fate, and choose that my purity must be white through and throughnot black within, with core of smut, and coated with covering of white-and in my so choosing reject the pleasures by fate so freely offered, I must be brave and fight my fate-my destiny defy. Yes, I must stand fast and be that which manly men would have me be." 

Then as he observed his secretary in her chair, silent and waiting for his answer, he felt that he had won his fight for right and cleanly living, and was quite convinced that the time was close at hand when he must take his stand and speak his mind just as the visions of his sleep had prompted him to do. So it was that at last he faced the girl and told her. "Miss Mahlon, I do not wish to lose your services.

I want you to remain as my secretary; I want to have all respect for you; I want to be kind and considerate, but before we discuss aught else, I want to tell you of a little dream I had last night, and then when I have finished I want you to tell me just how it impresses you, because it may have come to my sleeping mind as

OPPOSITES 

297 an urge to present to you conditions as they now appear to me. "I dreamed that I was dressed in white, in a white robe, and wearing a white turban. My shoes were white and I wore white gloves. I dreamed that I journeyed a long distance. I remember walking along a road, a very long road, and although I traveled far 

I was not tired. And as I made my way along I beheld that the sky was blue, that the sun in silvery brightness shone on all beneath, and that green trees grew on both sides of the road. Broad stretches of emerald fields there were to please the eye and to ease the restless minds of men. The cottages that I passed were clean and everyone well kept, each with beautiful flowers adorning perfect lawns. Little children sang and played and called cheering words as I passed them by, and I was happy and content and at peace with all the world. 

And although I walked many miles along the road, my white robe and even my white shoes showed no trace of dirt. "Then, at the end of the road, I came to my office, and as I entered I felt well satisfied and pleased with the journey I had made and the office itself seemed glad to welcome me. Without removing my turban I proceeded to wash my hands, but to my surprise my hands were white and clean and even the gloves that had covered them were spotless as could be. Then I returned the gloves to my hands and sat down in yonder chair, only that chair in my dream was white. All the office had become pure white, and as I sat you entered and you too were attired in white. Your dress, your hat, your gloves, all were white, of purest white, and as you came in your face was glad, but soon was your smile dispelled, for seating yourself in your chair, which too was white, you told me again that same story of your sad home life and the unhappiness of it all. And as you talked you seemed so young and but a

OPPOSITES 

very little girl, and I was a man who appeared quite old-of many more years than I own. And then I bade you come to me, and when you did I raised you up and placed you on my knee. And again you repeated your most sad tale, and I felt so sorry for you that I promised to be more than good to you and to aid you all I could. I wanted your life to be a gladsome one, free from sadness and worry and care. And this I told you in a gentle way, and then I smoothed your hair and I dried your eyes with my handkerchief, and endeavored to have you smile. And then after all this I found that my 'kerchief so white before it was used had turned to the blackness of starless night in the moisture of your tears. And then I awoke.' 

For a heavy space of time silence held them both. 

Then came a most surprising thing. Miss Mahlon, seemingly quite impressed by Fred's recital of his dream, suddenly raised her head and opened wide her eyes which she fastened full on his, and as he in turn returned the gaze he felt the spell of those hypnotic orbs which had as on those other not to be forgotten occasions so strangely and quickly changed their appearance. 

She said, "Mr. Davis, Daddy, before I go home tonight won't you let me sit on your lap as I did in the dream, for you are going to be kind to me and a real daddy to me, aren't you? Should I cry and should you dry my tears away, I know your handkerchief will remain white." 

Fred, overwhelmed, was for a few moments without power of utterance. First inclined to be angry, he was quickly changed by the compelling lure of those eyes, and so quickly subdued and held so fast that he was compelled to meekly and humbly say: "All right, Miss 

Mahlon. I grant your request. I hope only good comes out of it." True, 'twas rather grudgingly said, but, nevertheless, those were Fred's very words.

OPPOSITES 

299 Fred Davis-a man of physical power and fitness; 

Fred Davis-a marvel in the mercantile sphere; Fred

Davis, mentally keen and most alert-this was the Fred Davis who, when subjected to the inducing influence of this strange girl's hypnotic orbs, yielded to her wish or whim as all the while he wondered at the weakness of his will, of a will erstwhile unyielding and unbending to the pressure of the strongest men. 

And while he wondered at it all, he was quite well convinced that there existed a strange, strange something that linked him to this girl, and that under its influence he was powerless to resist whatever she desired. 

During the rest of the day Fred's time was fully occupied with visiting business men. During that fateful day in the life of Fred Davis, Mona Lisa was no more a puzzle than was the unreadable countenance of 

Mazie Mahlon.

The end of the business time having duly arrived, all the office attaches having departed, leaving Fred and his secretary alone, Fred remindful of his promise of the morning and with the hope that there was some way to avoid its fulfillment, made a pretense of working at his desk, even after his secretary had closed hers. Carefully he evaded speaking to the girl, hoping that eventually she would depart without exacting of him the performance of his foolish promise, but she did not go; instead, after repeated visits to his office and disappointed in his seeming indifference quite querulously she inquired: "Is there anything with which I can help you, Mr. Davis? You seem so overwhelmingly occupied with serious problems.' 

"No, I'll soon be through; yon need not wait." "Have you forgotten so soon?" she said. "Forgotten?" "Yes, forgotten the fulfillment of your dreams?"

All the while he had been bending low over his desk

OPPOSITES 

examining samples of various materials. At her testy inquiry, so irresistible in its rebuke, he raised his eyes to hers; quite cognizant that the crucial test was upon him, he realized that subjecting his will to the power of her eyes had been a tactless blunder. His first impulse to seek release from his foolish promise was quickly dismissed unuttered. Those eyes, so plaintive, so innocent, and, although he did not know it, so commanding, could not be denied their appeal; no, he could neither repudiate nor seek release from his given word, but with the endeavor to compromise his better judgment with the hope that by making good his promise he might in some way turn his dream to some good effect, he said: "Miss Mahlon, I am going to keep my promise and hope that by its fulfillment good, and only good, may result for you and for me."

Seating himself in a chair, invisible to possible occupants of surrounding buildings, for a time he hesitated and reflected, for he was sorely ill at ease. While he suffered, Mazie Mahlon expectantly stood waiting. "Well, Miss Mahlon," he said, "I am ready to fulfill my promise. You may come and sit here."

Before doing so she turned off the office light, although there still remained sufficient illumination from the outside to prevent total darkness. As the girl seated herself on Fred's lap, he said, "Miss Mahlon, this is a rather awkward situation for both of us. I fear we are drifting into a channel that will bring us to a destination not at all desired by either of us. It is as a daddy that I take you on my lap tonight, and it is in a daddy-like way that I intend making an endeavor to have you see things as I see them. În a fatherly sort of way I am quite willing, in fact, quite anxious to assist you. I want to be more than kind to you, and from time to time it is my intention to advance you in a financial way. I have it in mind to do everything possible for you that I can properly do. When-

301 ever it can be arranged I intend to see that you have some of the pleasures of life that hitherto you have been denied. If I can advise you or assist you in any way, I shall be glad to do so, but I fear we have started as daddy and daughter just a bit in the wrong way." "Just what do you mean?" "Well, last night our kiss seared itself into my brain and scorched my very soul, and with its warmth still on my lips I was sorry for the happening." "I am sorry. I did not think my kiss would be so objectionable.' 

"It was not because it was your kiss. It was because of my obligations as a married man. 99 "Then you would not kiss me again?" "I think it better that I should not." "When you promised to take me on your lap tonight, had you then determined not to kiss me again?" "I gave my consent, thinking I might be able to talk to you as a father would to his little girl, and in that light make clear to you our affair as it appears to me. 

Just consider my dream. I am sure it was intended for some very good purpose. You and I both were dressed entirely in white. Each of us in the eyes of the other was white and pure. Let us make the dream come true." "I believe I am learning to read your thoughts, Mr.

properly can, but I fear that, like all things good or bad, our affection could not remain stationary, but would either die and thereby come to an end, or else grow in strength to such an extent that it might possibly pass out of the realms of innocence into the land of things to be regretted." "If you have confidence in me and in what I say and you yourself are really what you claim to be and what

"Quite well do I know that, but why can't I be just as your own child would be? I do so want you to be my own daddy." "Miss Mahlon, this all seems to me to be quite impossible." "Then you refuse?" "I believe on his lips. 

I must-" She pressed a halting finger "Don't finish that," she said. 

With speech suppressed, he found himself once more held fast by the fascination of those overpowering eyes and as he quickly yielded to her spell, she was not slow to catch his sign of weakening and having observed, delayed not long to place both arms around him; then with the fullness of her warm, moist lips a lingering kiss she gave him. Feebly, half-heartedly he drew away, but once again her gaze of charm fastened itself upon him, and Fred Davis knew that he had lost, for without further hesitancy he drew her mouth quite close to his and for a spell lived in a world of ecstasy.

OPPOSITES 

303 Then abruptly releasing the girl, in a matter of fact way he said: "Now, let us go home." 'Twas uttered as one for whom a bitter dose having been prescribed, and having been taken with its ac companying effect having run its course, was relieved to know that it was done and hoped for no repitition. 

But she did not go-not just then. Furiously, rapidly, heatedly, and with all the passion of her soul, she feverishly kissed him on the lips, on the cheeks, on forehead, then nestled her face on the crown of his head; once again pressing fiery lips close to his, she held him tight in the grasp of her passion; so did she hold him until quite gently but very firmly he took her two arms, one in each of his hands, and forthwith deliberately and with an air of finality raised her from his lap. Without a word, choking and panting, she anxiously wondered if she had gone too far, and in the doing had lost her advantage. "You had better go, Miss Mahlon."

She hesitated, then put on her coat and her hat. "Good night, Daddy," she said, and quickly passed out of the door.

Slumped loose and limp in the depth of his chair, Fred Davis, writhing under the stinging remorse of self accusation and the scorching retribution of a tormenting hell, with eyes fixed fast on the closed door, prayed in the agony of his soul that for the last time he had seen that door close or open on Mazie Mahlon. 

Condemning himself without restraint, censuring both the girl and himself, he determined that on the morrow, should she return, he would turn her away from his pathway of life with the hope that the parting would cover a period of eternity. Fear of fatal consequences was upon him. In a little while, if not already come to pass, his name would be a lucious sweetmeat for the scandal mongers' tongues.

At length arising, many times back and forth, to

OPPOSITES 

and fro, he strode the office floor that night. Observing that all of the lights were out, he turned them on, every one, and entered the secretary's office, and there beholding his features in a mirror's glass in shame and disgust he dropped his self-censuring eyes, for he could not face himself. Sinking into a chair and burying his head in trembling arms flung on a nearby desk, in the rue of repentant regret he sobbed and sobbed and sobbed. How long in the remorse and condemnation of Aceldama he sat that night, how long in the field of blood, while from his veins of self respect and esteem the oozing fluid ebbed away, the man never knew, but while he suffered there came the sound of a telephone ring. "Who could it be? Was the girl coming back and first wished to assure herself that he was still there? 

But she should not come back. No! No! This girl who had mounted him on the black steed of disgrace should not return to spur him on and drive him to his doom." Hoping it was not his Delilah that called, hesitatingly, tremulously he raised the receiver, but uttered not a sound until there came to his ear: "Is that you, Fred?" The sound of Mary's voice was a relief. "Yes, yes. I am coming home." "You are not sick, are you?" "No, but I have been detained." "Have you had callers, or have you been working over your samples?" "Someone was here and I just could not get away. 

I am leaving the office immediately."

CHAPTER XLIX 

Arriving home he felt still more depressed and much more disgraced than even on the preceding night. He knew that he had lost something that had been worth while; something that could not be recovered; conscience told him that he should no longer claim for himself a place in the community as the esteemed and honored citizen he had hitherto considered himself to be. He tried to master his emotions, but he was unsuccessful in his efforts to hide from Mary's eyes the fact that something had brought upon him a mental state of distress, intense and unyielding. To her anxious inquiries as to whether he were ill or had business worries, he gave a negative reply, and endeavored to convey the impression that he had been working just a trifle too long and too hard, with which explanation she appeared satisfied. 

With the plea that he was very tired, Fred retired early. Instead of the sleep that he sought, his lot was the remorse of the damned. Leaving home the following morning, he determined that he would pay Miss 

Mahlon a full month's salary and let her go. As he entered his office door, he had not the remotest doubt that he would do just that thing. She arrived soon after he had seated himself at his desk. He was looking out of the window as he heard her walk from her office into his and proceed to her customary chair for dictation, where she seated herself, with the remark, "Good morning, Daddy. I am all ready to go to work." 

He turned, strong in the determination that she should never work for him again. But how frail a 305

OPPOSITES 

thing is the resolution of men, even strong men, when matched with woman's lure and wile. Fred's gaze encountering that of Mazie Mahlon, came a strange feeling to possess him, and something plainly told himself he would not dismiss her who he had determined should from that day forth be for him his erstwhile secretary, and from that something he learned too that in spite of himself he cared more for that girl than any living being. Realizing that his castle of resolution was made of crumbling stone joined with sandless mortar, he well knew that his will was but a wizened thing, so without word or ado he proceeded to dictate some letters.

CHAPTER L 

Days became just parts of weeks, and weeks were absorbed by months. Fred had resigned himself to the belief that Mazie Mahlon was an unsought but nevertheless indispensable part of his life. As his secretary she continued more than faithful to her duties. Fred was kind to her; from time to time he made her gifts; he arranged for her to attend, always unaccompanied by him, many functions that afforded her pleasure. 

There was the customary good night kiss, but aside from that there was on his part no action of endearment.

One evening, following a meeting of one of the civic organizations in which Fred was quite active, two friends walked home with him. Why they accompanied him Fred was for a time unable to understand, as their homes lay in an opposite direction, but following several awkward attempts to introduce the subject one of the men named Walton said, "Fred, Jack and 

I have purposely come with you tonight to touch on what we consider an important matter. You have in your employ I understand, a stenographer named 

Mazie Mahlon. I am quite sure you do not know her reputation or you would not have her in your office." "Why, man, I do not understand. Miss Mahlon has been with me for quite a while. She is punctual, efficient, capable, faithful and loyal. Never have I heard a word against her character. I did engage her without asking for reference, but later learned from her where she formerly had been employed and I wrote to those concerns, everyone of which replied in most favorable terms." 307

OPPOSITES 

"Yes," said Walton, "many concerns do that when making replies in black and white. It would have been better had you made personal calls." “Oh, I have full confidence in the sincerity of the men who replied to my inquiries regarding Miss Mahlon. We have exchanged information regarding employes with all of them on many occasions, and never have I had incorrect or evasive replies from any of them." "Did you inquire of Wahl and Wahl regarding her?" "No, I did not. I did not know she had been employed by them.” "Well, she was," said the one called Jack. "I think it would be well if you were to see Harry Wahl." "Can't you tell me yourself what you believe he would say?" "No, I think it would be better if you were to get your information direct from Wahl and Wahl." 

Arriving at Fred's home, he invited the men to enter, which invitation they declined with thanks, at the same time expressing hope that Fred feel no offense at their interfering with his business affairs. "Oh, that is all right, boys," he said, "only I think you are quite mistaken. However, I thank you for your interest and believe I can clear up the matter satisfactorily." 

Once more Miss Mahlon, this time indirectly, caused 

Fred a night of wakeful worry.

When at length she entered the office, his immediate and only salutation was, "Miss Mahlon, there is a very important matter I wish to discuss with you.' "You will wait till I remove my coat and hat, won't you?" 99

309 "I think it better to talk first."

The red mounted to the roots of her hair. "Yes, but why do you ask?" "Well, one reason is that when I inquired as to where you had been employed you did not mention that concern." "Are you sure?" "Yes, I am quite sure, Miss Mahlon." "Well, it was some little while ago." "Tell me why you left there." "Well, it was all on account of some false stories that were told. When I learned of it, rather than have trouble, I resigned." "Is that all?" "Yes, that is all." "Can you tell me what those stories were?" "Why do you ask that? Don't you have confidence in me?" OPPOSITES 

the slightest move to turn it, but facing Fred, she said: "Mr. Davis, I thank you for all you have done for me, but I am satisfied you would feel more comfortable if I were to leave." "You have not received your salary, Miss Mahlon." “Oh, you have already more than amply paid me many times over for everything I have done, and I deem it best that I go and go without the least delay.” 

Fred remained seated awaiting the girl's departure, but as she did not go immediately he raised his eyes. 

It might have been in curiosity to ascertain why she did not fit her actions to her words; it might have been he assumed she had a parting word as yet unsaid; but, be the motive for his doing what it may, he found himself with eyes fast riveted by the power of this mesmeric maid, in whose strangely luminous orbs there lived the fire that cindered his will to let this parting be their final one, and in turn rekindled the longing urge of passion's flame to take this girl for his very own, even though in the censuring blaze of the world's condemnation they burn together at the same stake. 

So it was that feverishly he strode across the floor, loosed her hand from the knob of the door, removed her coat and took off her hat, and then in tones apologetically soft and gently warm he said in a semi-whispered way, "Mazie, I believe in you. I cannot let you go. I want you here now and always," and then for the first time he took the initiative kiss.

CHAPTER LI 

During the ensuing days when alone Fred made and subsequently broke the same resolution many times. 

On numerous occasions he determined that he would rid himself of Miss Mahlon. Through a variety of channels gossip brought to his ears additional stories. regarding his secretary, but in spite of all of that every resolution that she should pass out of his existence was made to be superseded by another determination that he must stand by her, even against the world, if that be necessary. He spent every hour of one long night endeavoring to believe that the stories he had heard of her were true, but on the following day when in her presence he despised himself for his unbelief.

In spite of many long continued efforts he was never able to explain to himself his intense, passionate love for her and his undivertible devotion to her. 

One night at leave taking time, for a few fleeting moments, a passing doubt possessed him, and he asked himself: "Why should this girl hold me so?" It so happened that holding her in his arms, following the customary good night kiss, he chanced to scan her upturned face and saw that her eyes were closed, and that without the fire of her luminous orbs her features were flat and dull; her face, a pasty mass; colorless lashes on tight drawn lids, with lustreless, pallid brows, accentuated the missing light of her eyes; mouth knotted and strangely drawn; chin pronouncedly weak and receding straight to her throat. And as contemplating this drab mistake of a face, he once again inquired: "Why should this girl hold me?" And then flashed a new answer to it all, and the answer was of a ques- 311

OPPOSITES 

tioning sort. "Can it be pity that stirs my heart and animates my interest in this girl? Can it all be a miserable mistake in that I have construed sympathy to be love? Was the explanation close at hand?" And then as he wondered what to say her eyes were opened wide and her face had become a different face, an animated face without a tinge of gray. 'Twas like unto the glorious radiance of a noon day sun melting and dissipating chilly storm clouds that it might brighten even the dingiest and darkest corner of all the earth and by its warmth and its light drive all shadows of doubt away. And he said to his questioning soul: "There is but one thing I can say. This girl is my life."

In the community and in his home and among his friends and associates Fred Davis had become a changed man-a much changed man, but none explained the change. They saw him as a new Fred 

Davis-a different Fred Davis. His former and famous customary smile was most times missing, although occasionally he did force a very wry substitute for what with him had been formerly habitual. In appearance and in manner he was much, very much older. 

Although continuing as kind to Mary as ever he had been, it oft times seemed that the kindness was from momentum of habit or assumed by force of will. Mary could not define the change in her husband other than he was lacking in interest, in fact, totally void of interest in everything unless it be something apart from his home.

And so it continued through the winter months and during the springtime and the summer days and the ensuing fall and winter season until summer came again.

CHAPTER LII 

"I knowed he wouldn't be here. We been waitin' fer him more'n an hour now. Don't suppose he's gonna cross us, do you?" The foregoing conversation took place in an old frame building that once had been used by a railroad company to house some of its employes over night. 

The men who had most used it were those who worked on coal trains in a bituminous district. The building was two stories in height. On the upper floor were series of bunks; on the lower floor could also be seen some bunks and the remains of a long table which formerly had been used as a dining table. An old stove stood on one side of the room and in it could be seen pieces of charred wood and lumps of burning and smoking soft coal. There was a door in the rear of the room leading to what apparently had been at one time a kitchen. The building did not occupy its original site, which had been in the valley land and along a railroad, but had been moved some years since to its present location, high on the mountain side. 

One of the speakers was tall and angular, with a countenance of most forbidding features. As much of his face as was not covered with repulsive looking sores was dirty tallow yellow. Apparently it had been a long time since his skin had been clear of blemishes, to which doubtless could be laid that uncomplimentary nickname of "Scab".

The other man, not as tall as his companion, but more thickly set, was crowned with an abundance of black hair, black as the coal in the surrounding moun-

OPPOSITES 

315 tains. His brows were bushy black and his eyes like inky beads. Swarthy and dingy, his blackness was accentuated and emphasized by a coally beard that no razor was ever made to tame. However, with it all, there was that about the man bespeaking a character at least one shade whiter than the unscrupulous qualities of his partner. 

It was a night in January, and a snow storm which after threatening for several days had broken during the afternoon with the ferocity of a giant blizzard.

The wind came down from the north and, being trapped in the valley changed its course to follow the river bed. 

Being thus confined, its force concentrated and thereby much increased swept down between the mountains, swooping suddenly upon the frame abode of the two men, the velocity of the blow causing the house in its unpreparedness to tremble and to shake as though it would suffer severance from its foundation and hurl itself to dark and distant depths, but by some rare fortune it withstood the shock all through the day until dark of night pushed aside the light, and as hours passed the push of the storm increased in fury and in power.

Through the windows of the building nothing had been visible for hours past, the snow having caked itself into a solid mass against the glass. Two lanterns were all that lighted the interior of the room. 

Following one sudden and most vicious attack which caused every timber in the house to groan and creak, Black said: "Tell me, Scab, while yer knockin' Grif, do you think he could make his way up here even if he wanted to ?" "You may be right, Black, but somehow I don't feel just right 'bout Grif. He gits the last turn of the wheel in this game, and if he wants to throw us, how do you lay stoppin' him?”

OPPOSITES 

"Well, we all gotta take a chance. I been takin' chances on you fer a long while. Mebbe you think you been takin' a chance on me, too. I often think what a clumsy game it was, too, askin' a guy to give you five pennies for a nickel and tell him you wanted a penny to put in a chewin' gum slot. I laff yet when I think of Scab Boley chewin' gum. But Grif was wise to the phoney coin and instead of handin' you over to the railroad dick on the station platform he called you back of the buildin' and tipped you to drop the game if that was the best you could play it, and then laid it to you that if you wanted him to go 'long he'd show you how to make 'em fancy. He didn't know you, but he didn't turn you neither, so why you got yer hammer out for him tonight? 319 oak, but saw nothing and they heard nothing except the alternate howl and shrill scream of the storm crazed wind. "Git a shovel, Scab," said Black.

As the latter turned to secure one, a sudden gust of wind scooping up some of the snow by the side of the oak uncovered part of a man's arm. "Come on, Scab," said Black, as he rushed through the snow and deep into the drift, and grabbed the extended arm. With the aid of Scab he was able after a time to get the stricken Grif out of the snow and into the house. He had not lost full consciousness, but was too cold to move or speak. They laid him on the big table, which they pushed over by the side of the stove, into which they threw fresh fuel and to which they applied full draft. They removed Grif's snow-caked top coat and his shoes and socks, following which they wrapped him in blankets, then vigorously rubbed his wrists and his ankles.

As Scab continued to work on him, Black heated some coffee. By the time the beverage was ready to drink, Grif was able to move his head and drink the full contents of a cup, but it was a full hour before he could speak, and then in a weak, chattering sort of a way, he asked for his coat. "Yer coat's all soaked," said Black; "we're tryin' to dry it by the stove." "I think I left a message in one of my pockets. The telegraph operator at Trentville gave it to me. I want to read it over again so that I may be sure of what it says.' 

"Scab, bring him the coat," said Black.

Scab took the coat from the back of the chair where it had been placed to dry, but instead of bringing it directly to Grif who still lay on the table he passed out of the vision of the half frozen man, as well as that of Black. As he did so, he rapidly passed his hand

OPPOSITES 

into various pockets of the garment until rewarded by finding in one of the pockets an envelope such as used by telegraph operators in railroad stations. Grif, while still lying on the table carefully searched the pockets and was much disappointed at finding nothing there. 

Again and again vainly he sought for the missing message, then returned the coat to Black with the remark, "I must have lost my telegram in the storm."

Scab was silent. "Well," said Black, "Yer here and 'pears like yer goin' to stay, fer you can't git down the mountain tonight. You'll have to bunk here with us. We ought be talkin' over matters anyhow. 99 

Scab spoke for the first time. "Yeah, let's git somewhere. Did you bring back our split o' the swag?" "Yes, I have it in my money belt, but I can't get at it until I get loose from my clothes. I feel a little bit too chilly yet for that." "I might get you some whiskey," said Scab. "You know I never touch it," said Grif. "Say, Scab," said Black, "I been tellin' you to lay off that stuff; you ain't none too bright nohow, an' when

321 we're puttin' off our deals, 'speshly the big one, I'm tellin' you you want to keep yer nut clear. If you got any rum about the joint, spill it. Sound thinkin' and liquor don't mix." Proceeding to put his words into action, Black led the way to the door opening into the kitchen, which he swung on its hinges, and the three men passed through. 

Once inside the room, Black advanced to what was apparently a wall directly opposite the door through which they had just passed. He reached high his hand and felt carefully over the plastered wall. After a time, finding the spot he sought, he pressed one finger against what must have been a concealed button, for a section of the wall swung slowly inward, revealing a small aperture on one side of which hung a key. Black removed the key from the hook on which it was supported and carried it to what had the appearance of a wide and long crack in the wall, repaired with plaster of Paris. Very carefully he placed the key that he carried against a particular spot in the plaster of Paris, pushed it gently, and a section of the wall moved first inward and then rose slowly inside of the rest of the wall. He further inserted the key into the key hole, pushing it upward instead of turning it. As he did so, another section of the wall descended. The rising of one section and the lowering of another produced an opening the width of an ordinary door, through which the men proceeded to another room. In this room were crucibles, weighing machines, measures of all sorts and many peculiar looking instruments. It might have been a chemist's laboratory; it might have been a manufac turing jeweler's shop, but whatever it was there was much evidence of considerable work having been done there with chemicals, as well as with numerous tools that were much the worse for wear. "You can see we been workin'," said Scab. "With what result?" asked Grif.

329 "When we show you, you'll think we earned all we git." "Is everything ready?" asked Grif. "Yeah," said Black, "far as we're concerned, we're all set." "Let me see the result of your work." Scab. Scab placed within the glass several carefully measured liquids, then returned the glass to Black, who poured the contents on the shelf from which he had taken the glass. The shelf rapidly disappeared, revealing in the wall what appeared to be the dial of a safe door. Black dexterously and rapidly spun the dial back and forth, then gently pushed on the wall a bit above the dial, as a result of which the great door of an immense safe swung slowly inward, revealing to their eyes an immense opening in the mountain side. 

The first thing that attracted the eyes was what was undoubtedly a portion of a vein of coal inside of which had been constructed a large room, the floor, the ceiling and three sides of which were of hard cement. Grif evidently had expected to see a particular something, for after carefully inspecting the opening he inquired: "And where is it ?" 

Black replied: "Grif, we bin playin' this thing safe.

OPPOSITES 

It weren't likely that nobody would work their way so far back as to reach our vault, but we wasn't havin' all our trouble fer nothin', so we made it surer'n sure. 

Now, Scab, fer the last step." "I'm all ready," said Scab. He held in his hand an immense electric drill, which he rapidly ran around the concrete floor. He knew just where to drill, for the circle that he cut released a big stone slab that had been buried in the concrete. The three men pulled it out from the floor, uncovering a large hole containing a box, which they quickly brought to the surface and carried into the kitchen. 

The box appeared to be covered with some sort of metal and the top was secured by locks on all four sides. Black, from what seemed to be another secret opening in the wall, secured a key, with which he proceeded to unlock the lid of the box, within which was a display of jewels and jewelry that would have delighted the eye of the most fastidious child of wealth and luxury. For a few moments Grif feasted his gaze on this unusual display, and then with unsuppressed enthusiasm exclaimed: "Boys, I congratulate you. You have done a good job." 

Black replied: "Thanks, Grif, but you don't know just how good a job we done yit." With these words he reached into the box and lifted therefrom two very long strings of pearls, both with diamond set clasps. "Come back inta the workshop with me. We got a 'lectric light there. The minin' comp'ny is good' nough to pervide it, only we never send 'em a letter o' thanks, 'cause they don't know they're doin' us the favor." 

Black, carrying the two strings of pearls, proceeded to the workshop, followed by the other two. "Now, Grif, which un belongs to the traveler in 

Europe an' which un are you seein' fer the first time?"

Grif took the two strings, held them up to the light, weighed them carefully in his hands, laid them side by

333 side, balanced them on the scales, carefully felt them with the ends of his fingers, and, after a long attempt to distinguish between the two, handed them back to

335 "Are any of these real?" asked Grif. "No, all our own make." "Beware of imitations," said Grif, with half a smile. Mr. Woolworth also rendered valuable aid, but 

I am sure he will never ask for a cut. Of course he got his in what he would insist was a legitimate way. 

Some of the merchandise he provided was rather nifty too. You might say Uncle Sam's P. O. Department was also an accessory, but of course he won't admit it. 

But Quesne's help was quite essential, and as he has put through our other deals and is the big factor in the swinging of the pearl deal I think I ought to cut with him anyhow. If you are not willing to make it a fourway split, I'll give Quesne half of mine.” "Oh, make it a four cut," said Black, who did not wish to take any chances of losing Quesne's assistance in the big deal.

Scab, not so keen and not so far sighted, and opposed to any division into four parts, unless four were really participating in the transaction remarked "You took moughty good care not to divvy with me and Black on that Christmas deal. Musta cleaned up good tooslicken nickel and dime stuff fer the real goods." 

However after some further discussion, during which Scab intimated once more that Black was working hand in hand with Grif, he finally was persuaded

OPPOSITES 

that it would be most advantageous to hold the friendly interest of Quesne, whereupon Grif placed the rings and brooches in his money belt, which he strapped around his body, shortly after which all three crawled into bunks.

In the morning the sun was brightly shining, but the drifts had piled high. The three men spent the day gambling until the mountain side was enveloped in darkness; then Grif carefully and cautiously made his way down to the town in the valley, and a train going east carried him miles away from the headquarters of his two partners.

CHAPTER LIII 

The clock in the old town hall of Jacktown tolled twelve times, announcing to all within its hearing that a year was dying, that twelve months of joy and of sorrow, of accomplishments and of disappointments had become history, and that another year with its hopes and its fears was being born. There was the ringing of many bells and toots and blasts from all the factory whistles. There were pistol shots and many exclamations of "Happy New Year" with the ready response of "The same to you, and many of them."

In the private office of the post office sat four men. 

Doubtless, the noisy welcome to the incoming year reached their ears, but they paid it no heed. To them did not even occur the exchange of the season's greeting, for their thoughts were of things concerning not the passing of time.

At one end of a long office table sat the borough postmaster; at the other end was seated a man of uncertain type, probably best described as tall and angular. To tell of his face would be for me well nigh impossible, and since no good purpose would be accomplished in so doing, it is best left unattempted, except to mention just this: plainly in his features might be discerned deep lines of disappointment, but it was a disappointment that did not kill, for his eyes shone bright as living sparks, reflecting the hope that lay in his soul, a hope that was brave and refused to die. With brows of a thinker and eyes of keen and far distant penetration, his facial contour and general expression defied anyone to read them.

Sitting between the two men, one on the right and 337

OPPOSITES 

one on the left, were two others, who from their conversation might have been respectively described as the head of some organization and his assistant. 

Said the postmaster: "I have done my best to find any mail going through my office that was not addressed to someone whom I really knew." 

Said the man on his right: "Simpkins, your best is not good enough. Pardon my candor, but in such a problem as yours there must be no mincing of words, and none of us, no matter what may be the provocation, must allow his feelings to be hurt, for our remarks are not intended as personal. This case must be handled as though the four of us were not ourselves. 

But to get back to the matter in hand, it is my opinion there must be a leak in your office, or this thing could not continue as it has. What is your opinion, Laird?” "Inspector, there are just two ends we must tie together. I have the connecting link, but I have not been able to find the other two links which the known link would unite." "Why is that?" said Simpkins. "If we know one of the gang, why can't we grab him and squeeze the missing information out of the rascal?"

The one who was evidently an assistant inspector said: "Simpkins, your question demonstrates to me just why you have been unable to do anything to assist in this matter." "You don't know the kind of fellows that are playing this game," said the Inspector. "It's this way, Mr. Simpkins," said Laird, in a manner not at all unkind. "I am quite satisfied I know just who is handling the middle of these various operations, but unless I can trace him one way or another and can connect him with somebody else engaged in these crimes I can't prove a thing. And this man knows that as well as I do. I am satisfied in my own mind that the counterfeit nickels as well as the five, ten and

OPPOSITES 

339 twenty dollar notes all came from the same place. I also firmly believe that the gold-copper trick that was put over on the mint was done by the same birds. It takes a number of workers-three-four or five or more to be successful in operations like the ones I have just mentioned, and it is not often that you find so many bright crooks in one vicinity. They are associated together and have contact with one another only when necessary. Another thing, gangs of that nature keep clear of other gangs or groups of crooks; they don't encroach on a gang already working in a certain territory. It is safer not to do so. One gang cannot afford to incur the enmity of another gang, and too much of one crooked game in one vicinity is liable to bring about disclosure. Usually these individuals associate with one another only when it is advantageous to do so, and the various gangs act along similar lines. 

Has anything new happened within the last forty-eight hours, Mr. Simpkins?" "No, nothing," said Simpkins. "The last thing that happened was the Brownwell case. To make it quite clear, I'll repeat it. Mrs. Brownwell bought a new diamond brooch from Oldwell in Big City. She arranged to have it sent by registered mail to her home in Jacktown. Upon receiving the package, she immediately claimed the brooch which it contained was not the one she had purchased. The Oldwell Company in turn insisted that it had mailed the identical brooch selected by Mrs. Brownwell. The clerk who sold the brooch claims he personally took it directly to the mailing department; the young lady who packed it did so in the presence of the clerk. Mrs. Brownwell expected to go to Big City yesterday, taking with her the brooch she received, but I have not heard from her since she told me of said intended visit." "I am not very well sold on what I am about to suggest," said the Inspector, "and it is only because it is

OPPOSITES 

absolutely necessary that I be in Washington before noon tomorrow that I do suggest it. My thought is to call Mrs. Brownwell on the phone now and ask her if she went to Big City yesterday. If she volunteers anything, listen, but don't ask her any further questions. I prefer talking to her face to face, if possible to do so without revealing that I represent the government, but I cannot well do that tonight. Therefore I must be satisfied with a phone call." "I suggest," said Laird, "that Simpkins phone Mrs. 

Brownwell, stating that he had been expecting to hear from her, and that not having done so he felt he should phone her, as he was anxious to know if she had accomplished anything with Oldwell's. Apologize for calling at this time of night, but explain that you thought the noise must have kept everyone awake, and that as tomorrow, or rather, today, is a holiday, you feared she might not be home during the day time. I agree with the Inspector that it is well not to ask any questions, but merely listen to what she has to say. 

Have you an extension to this phone, Mr. Simpkins?" "I have none attached, but there are several instruments that can be attached in a very few minutes. I believe it might be well to connect one of them and let the Inspector listen in."

This was expeditiously done, and as Simpkins was about to put in his call the phone bell rang, whereupon he removed the receiver from the hook. "Yes-yes-I was about to call you. Yes-yes— 

Don't do anything more until you hear from me.
