CHAPTER LIV 

On the following Saturday Simpkins went to Big

City and followed instructions as outlined by Laird. 

He saw that the box was placed in the baggage car, and took a seat in an adjoining day coach. Soon after the train started the baggage man made his way into the mail car. "Hello, Jack," said he to the mail clerk, who was busy sorting the mail. "How are things? You appear rather busy." "Nothing unusual, just the usual Saturday night mail." "Strange, isn't it," said the baggage man, "what chances some persons take? So many of them do their shopping on Saturday, and if they buy anything valuable in Big City it is usually sent by registered mail, or by express. In either case, it lies around in the post office or in the express office through Sunday until some time on Monday." "Yes, it is strange, but I don't believe many persons give the matter of safety much thought." "No, I don't suppose they do. The Christmas season being over, there is not much of that sort of mail now, is there?" "No, only an occasional package that shows any real value." "You have to be mighty careful of such packages, don't you, Jack?" "Yes, I do. I am held responsible, but as nobody comes into this car except you once in a while I feel little concern." 99 "Your remark makes me think how lonesome it must 344

I must go get them." "I'll go with you. Your buddy in the box is not very sociable." "No, stay here, those leather mail bags of yours with their stuffy, disagreeable odor nauseate me at times, especially in winter time when we get so little fresh air. I thought I heard something drop in your car. Must have been my keys. I'll be right back."

Following his return after a brief absence, the two men discussed matters of little or no importance until, following a pause in the conversation, which the baggage man seemed pleased to create, the latter said: "I believe this is a good opportuniy for us both to steal a little rest. We have a full hour and a half before the next stop." "9 The mail clerk taking this as a gentle hint that his companion preferred to be alone, returned to his own car. The baggage man immediately locked the door of his car and proceeded to unfold a cot, which was

OPPOSITES 

out of sight of anyone who might look through either door. However, instead of lying down, he quickly drew from pockets in his coat several small packages. 

He also took from what appeared to be a secret compartment in the side of the car a package, which he carefully unwrapped, thereby revealing a number of finger rings and other pieces of jewelry. He then proceeded to open some of the small boxes which he had removed from his pockets, but evidently the contents of none of them met with his approval, for he immediately resealed the packages, making them appear as neat as they originally were. This he did with all of the packages except one, which he carefully opened and removed what appeared to be a platinum ring set with an unusually large diamond. He then took a ring from the large package which had been concealed in the car and carefully compared this with the other ring. The examination was evidently to his satisfaction, for instead of replacing the two rings in their respective original boxes he reversed them, returned the boxes to his pocket which had previously held them and once more placed the large package in its hiding place.

He had nothing consigned to the next station stop, and upon the arrival of the train there he was in the mail car. Carefully watching the mail clerk hand out some mail bags, he took from his pocket several boxes and placed them in the safe that occupied a corner of the mail car. After the train started he remained a very few minutes, then returned to his own car, and the two men did not see each other again that night. 

Pushing its way through the darkness, on and on rolled the train. All passengers had retired and everything had assumed the stilly monotony of a moonless night time. On and on rushed that steed of steel, seemingly serene in the safety of its precious cargo of human souls. Smoothly it forced its way and all seemed

OPPOSITES 

347 well, when suddenly, startlingly, out on the night sounded the shrill shriek of the engine whistle, the grinding of brakes, a crushing crunching of railroad ties, absence of light, then a snapping sound, as the locomotive and part of the train broke loose from the rest and down, down, down it plunged and rolled until checked at the foot of an embankment, and then all was still and dark again. No moon, no stars, no light. 

Then, after a space came agonized cries of pain and distress and calls for much needed aid; some pinned fast and unable to move appealed for help of succoring hand; others there were, unhurt themselves, who groped through the lightless night, blindly searching and frantically calling for loved ones lost in the wreck.

Simpkins' car was one of those that had taken the fall, but fortunately he was unhurt, and after a time was able to extricate himself from the wreckage, whereupon he immediately proceeded to go about helping others as well as he could in the dark.

There was not even the smallest sort of settlement for miles around, not even an isolated farm house; there was no station near. One of the train crew started down the track, hoping to get aid in some way from somewhere. Simpkins, working as best he could in the darkness, assisted several persons from the ruins. As he was making his way about administering aid, he was suddenly jostled, almost thrown from his feet, having come in bodily contact with a man much larger than himself, and as at that precise moment some live coals from the locomotive having come in touch with wood from the broken cars a flame rose high and sufficiently lighted the scene that Simpkins was enabled to recognize the man who bumped him so hard, and in a surprised tone, said: "Why, Laird, how did you come here?" "I assure you I did not come here voluntarily. I imagine you are not here from choice either," said

OPPOSITES 

Laird, "but do not ask questions now. Let us do what we can to help."

In some way word spread, as word so often does without anyone knowing just how, that there had been a railroad wreck. From somewhere a doctor arriving on the scene immediately drafted several of the uninjured ones to assist. Laird and Simpkins were already doing what they could. As the latter was searching through the debris for some that might be injured and helpless, he abruptly stopped and going hastily to 

Laird, who was carrying in his arms a little child crying for its mother, exclaimed: "Laird, I just thought of the corpse. 99 "I think we should give our assistance to the living instead of the dead." "That is true, but it is also horrible to think of anyone, dead or alive, helpless in those consuming flames. 

Don't you think we should make some attempt to remove the corpse from that flaming car?" "There isn't a chance, Simpkins. 

"What about the baggage master? Do you suppose he got out?" "I think it hardly possible. Undoubtedly, he was in the car when it rolled down the embankment, and there isn't a thing we can do to help him."

After a time a wrecking crew appeared on the scene, and by daybreak so much of the wreck had been removed that if any bodies were still in the mass of tangled iron, steel and charred wood, their remains could be uncovered. Several bodies were found and a few piles of charred bones were disclosed. At length, Simpkins and Laird, being satisfied that there was nothing further for them to do availed themselves of the services of a special train to proceed to Jacktown. 

To their surprise they found the Inspector in Simpkin's office awaiting the latter's coming. Arriving at 

Jacktown, he had been informed at the postoffice that

OPPOSITES 

349 Simpkins had gone to Big City, but was expected to return on the Night Hawk. First annoyed at the failure of that train to arrive on time, and then alarmed when word came through telling of the disaster, he determined to wait until he should receive definite information of the postmaster's fate. First expressing to

Simpkins in terms of relieved anxiety his unconcealed delight that he had escaped unscathed, he addressed the other man: "Quite surprised to see you, Laird. It is not necessary to ask whence you came. Your begrimed face and hands, as well as sadly bedraggled attire, bear unmistakable even though mute testimony that you have been in that disastrous wreck. Anything new in our case to report?" "Yes, but I don't think it is going to help us much." "Whatever it is I believe it can wait until you have opportunity to wash up and change your clothes."

An apartment on one of the upper postoffice floors had been placed at Laird's disposal. Satisfied that soap and water were desirable, he saw wisdom in the suggestion of the Inspector, to which he responded: "If you will come with me, I'll endeavor to be more presentable as well as more comfortable, and at the same time we can talk over matters." "All right, Laird." Then, sensing that the latter wished to see him alone, he turned to Simpkins with the suggestion: "You had better go home now and clean up. Your wife is doubtless anxious to see you and satisfy herself she has a husband who is still intact.” 

Following Simpkins' departure the two other men ascended to Laird's apartment; then, after the latter had allowed a bath to do its best for him and he had attired himself in clothing whole and clean, he joined the Inspector in what had been arranged as a living room. "I see you have something on your mind, Laird. 

What is it?"

OPPOSITES 

"My suspicions as to one of the connecting links has been confirmed." "Do you mind telling me who it is?" "No, but conditions for solving the mystery are little improved. The wreck has I fear upset my plans." "In what way?" "I believe the link is dead. He was in the baggage car when the wreck occurred. That car was totally consumed by fire, and I don't see how he could possibly have escaped." "Was there anything in particular that confirmed your suspicions ?" "Yes, I had the goods on him. I saw him do his work.' 

"How did you accomplish that?" "Well, I'll tell you. Simpkins was shipping the body of a deceased friend to Jacktown. It was necessary of course, to place it in the baggage car. By a certain fortunate arrangement I was able to travel in that same baggage car. I saw the baggage master on some pretext get the mail clerk into the baggage car and have him stay there while he returned to the mail car, where he remained a short while before rejoining the mail clerk in the baggage car. After the latter had returned to his car the baggage master, who is our connecting link, removed from his pocket several small packages which I believe he secured in the mail car during his little visit there in the absence of the mail clerk. 

These he opened, the contents of some of which evidently did not meet with his approval. These unsatisfactory ones he carefully resealed. The last one he opened was doubtless according to desired specifications. To me it appeared like a platinum ring containing a large diamond. Very carefully he compared it with a ring that he had concealed, along with a lot of other jewelry, in the baggage car. He substituted his ring for one taken from the mail car and then resealed

OPPOSITES 

351 the package. The stolen ring he put in a pocket of his coat, and then placed what was doubtless phoney stuff in the hiding place. He managed to return the appropriated packages to the mail car during a station stop and while the mail clerk was busy unloading some mail. 

Not long after that the wreck occurred. The baggage car rolled down a very steep and long hill, turning over several times in its descent. The last I saw of the baggage master he was lying in one corner, unconscious and bleeding from a deep cut imposed, I take it, by a piece of broken glass. I believe an artery was cut, because with every heart beat the man's life blood spurted and splashed with slowly diminishing force. "How did you get out?" "Well, I was lucky. As the dead box rolled over and over within the falling car it was instrumental in protecting the body inside from flying glass and other possible injuries. When the car reached the bottom of the bank the box was lying on its side with one end protruding through a car window. As the box passed through the window frame one of its ends was entirely destroyed. The broken end being in the open air made it very easy for me to crawl out. Soon after that the car caught fire, and I don't think there is much doubt as to the fate of the baggage man. My observation confirmed my previous suspicion as to the fellow's guilt, but I am just where I was when you and I last met, except that I know now what before I thought I knew. I am satisfied that I was correct in my suspicion of one connecting link, but now it is a broken link." "Do you know his name?" "No, but we can get that from the railroad company, although I think it is well not to let anyone know whom we suspect, unless we are quite sure we can use the knowledge in hand to connect some of the missing links."

OPPOSITES 

"Does Simpkins know how you worked your scheme?" "No. He is totally in the dark. He believes that a corpse has been consumed by the flames. No good can be gained by his possessing knowledge of the facts, and you and I have always agreed not to let out anything unless we can gain something worth while in return." "I do not know that there is anything further for us to discuss, Laird. You must continue on this combination of cases, and let me know your location at all times. It is advisable that you disguise a bit. Information has reached headquarters in Washington to the effect that word had leaked out that 'The Mist' was at work; so it is best to be prudent and careful." "I always try to be careful, Inspector, but while, of course, I have no consuming desire to be bumped off, my big interest in life is to serve the government or anyone who may wish to engage me to assist in running down criminals or in preventing crime. To do that I must have a whole body. Cripples can hardly do the things I am ambitious to accomplish, so I'll follow your advice."

Laird was a man who talked very little of himself. 

Likewise he discouraged others who were prone to discuss him. Little was known of his private life. None claimed knowledge of his early days. The few possesing intimate touch with him knew that he had made a deep study of psychology, criminology, laws of averages, and seemed well informed as to occultism. To those few he appeared most content when seeking most earnestly for a reason or a cause. He endeavored much to classify the relations of various causes and effects. He was a student of human nature; he was well versed in physiognomy. These things some men knew of him, but to any appreciable extent he was unsolved by everyone. To most men he was an enigma

OPPOSITES 

353 in toto. The underworld was unable to reach him by bribe, or scare him by threat. It had never successfully combatted him. To the crooked ones he was a complete mystery, and for this they called him "The Mist". Apparently he was possessed of financial resources of his own. That added to the mystery as to why he was willing to match his skill and courage against the most dangerous and unscrupulous of America's criminal class.

There was only one point upon which everyone agreed; that was that whatever he accomplished was by honest endeavor.

He and the Inspector parted that night with no definite understanding as to when they should next meet, but they had a well arranged plan for establishing and maintaining any necessary communication.

CHAPTER LV 

“E. Wesley Arnold," was the way she signed her checks; and that is the name by which she was known throughout the entire community of Elmwood, a suburb of Big City. Her parents had called her by her first name, but after they had passed to the eternal unknown she chose to use the name of "Wesley”, for she loved her mother and cherished her memory, and "Wesley" was her mother's maiden name. Of course, she also loved her father. But she still retained the name of "Arnold", which naturally was her father's name. 'Tis well known, of course, that there is always possibility that a daughter may surrender the name given her by her father, but Miss Arnold, although no longer eligible to the so-called "younger set", had not yet arrived at the time of life commonly called "middle age", and there seemed only the most remote possibility of her ever taking the name of another. 

Evidence in plenty could be provided that she cared little or nothing for companionship of those of the opposite sex; seldom was she seen with any of such. 

She had no brothers, and in fact of male relatives she had none at all. Among some of the folk of Elmwood she was know as a "man hater"; others told of an unrequited affair of the heart; then by some others 'twas said that no man had and no man ever could exist to whom Wesley Arnold would surrender the independence of the singleness she enjoyed; others, too, there were who just as confidently would tell that Wesley had a secret that she kept quite well concealed, but how they knew none could explain, for, if it were a secret she possessed, then they could have no knowl- 354

OPPOSITES 

355 edge of it, and if once they did have knowledge of what they termed "a secret", then a secret it no longer could be.

With the exception of her servants, Miss Arnold lived alone on the extensive and magnificent estate she had chosen to call Elmwood Manor, and which was one of the show places of exclusive Elmwood. As might be inferred, she was possessed of exceeding great wealth, large sums of which she diverted into channels of charity.

It was seldom that any man called at Elmwood Manor, except some representing charitable institutions, and to call it was always necessary to gain permission in advance, unless, of course, she had sufficient interest in some worthy movement to invite to her home someone representing that particular cause. 

If the mistress of Elmwood Manor had close friends of her own sex she kept the information well concealed. 

Wesley Arnold lived very much alone. While neither she nor others for her could claim beauty of face or grace of figure, yet to see her was to take at least a second look. There was something in her sad rimmed eyes that told they were kindly eyes, and one observing was compelled to know that they were true as their shade of heaven's blue. A distinctive refinement was hers not haughty-not cold-just that having undergone a refining process she had emerged a finished. product, polished, cultured, but with it all a gentle, unsnobbish manner. Pale as the lily, and yet most times tinted with a touch of the rose's blush. Rather thin lips with the faintest droop, yet with a smile undeniably kind that put one at ease in her presence and that reflected the benevolence of a heart overflowing with charitable impulses that prompted the execution of many acts of love. None met her but was cheered and benefitted by the sympathetic warmth of her unselfish soul. And yet among all of her acquaintances there

OPPOSITES 

was not one claiming to know or understand the living, breathing Wesley Arnold. 

She traveled much and she traveled far; just why she traveled so very much no one except Wesley Arnold ever knew, neither knew they why she traveled far. Some believed she enjoyed visiting new scenes and seeing strange people, but oft times she returned to places to which she had previously journeyed. 

It was quite well known that she was interested in certain of valuable things which the earth sometimes gives forth. Precious stones possessed attraction for her; one time she made a hurried trip to Africa to purchase a newly discovered and oddly beautiful diamond. 

She was a lover of pearls. If there was one particular thing that really tempted her to travel so much and so far it must have been to see, and in some cases, to gain possession of some gem holding extraordinary renown. Her judgment of precious stones was recognized by the best of experts; and on the occasion of several cases of dispute and legal entanglements she had been sought to pass her opinion. 

It happened one day that there called at Elmwood 

Manor to see Miss Arnold a most beautiful woman.

The visitor presented her card which read: "Alice 

Wonder". It told nothing more, but Miss Arnold instructed the servant to admit "Miss Wonder". Of decidedly different types were Wesley Arnold and Alice 

Wonder. Miss Arnold, light and fair; Miss Wonder, most decidedly brunette. "Miss Arnold," said the latter, "I appreciate your granting me this interview. Having heard of your kind heart and your charitable disposition, and being interested in establishing in some permanent way an institution for under-privileged girls, I am here for advice. I am not at this time soliciting financial aid, Miss 

Arnold, but am simply seeking your opinion as to whether you believe such a thing can be accomplished."

OPPOSITES 

357 "Why come to me? There are many persons better qualified to advise you than I am.” "I have come here because I have heard, and I believe it is true, that you are most charitably inclined, and that your special charities are in the interest of girlhood and of womanhood." "Even if that be true, never have I taken the initiative in any of my chosen so-called charities. Others have conceived the idea as to how they could in some way help humanity, and have brought their propositions to me. If I have seen merit in what they presented to me, I have tried in some manner to serve their projects. If you have now or will come to me later with some definite proposition which upon investigation I find worthy in its appeal, and possessing sufficient promise of successful culmination, I shall be only too glad to assist, but as my experience has not been in organizing charitable movements, I am quite sure you have come to the wrong person. 99 

Miss Wonder replied: "What I really wanted to know was whether you would be willing to aid one more charitable institution, should its object meet with your approval." 

Miss Arnold smiled. "Undoubtedly, you realize how many and how varied are the movements organized in the name of sweet charity. I have thought for quite a long while that the same good and still more good could be accomplished, if the efforts were more concentrated; certainly it would be more economical. There are so many different ways to perform charity; then, too, it seems to me as though each different charitable movement has so many, many separate and distinct bodies organized to carry on its particular charity. 

Now, in my opinion all this could be done so much less expensively and so much more effectively if some of our charitable movements were combined." "I grasp your thought, Miss Arnold," said Miss

OPPOSITES 

Wonder, "and I believe you are right, but could we not tie on to one of these other charities the work of helping the under-privileged?" "It may be so, but I think nearly all charities are extended to those who have been under-privileged or over-privileged. I am inclined to the belief that every instance of solicited assistance or aid could be classified as charity for the under or over-privileged." "Yes, I again agree with you, Miss Arnold, but your knowledge of the workings of so many different movements for helping and uplifting humanity would be such a help to me. Won't you give some consideration to my particular charity, and let me call again?" "Is there anybody else associated with you in this, Miss Wonder?" "No, it is all my own idea." "Can you give me-pardon me, but I must be plain can you give me any reference ?" "I fail to grasp the implication, Miss Arnold. I am not seeking employment; neither have I solicited financial aid. Further than that I assure you I'll never do so, at least for myself. Before a single dollar is required to even start our movement, I am confident that you will have learned to trust me. It is not my intention to even suggest that you interest yourself actively in this work until you are entirely satisfied with its worthiness of purpose and its advisability and feasibility of operation. I am interested only in seeing that the work is done. If you think my idea feasible and you desire to put it into practice you can simply dismiss me personally from further consideration, and call on those of your own choosing to carry on the movement. 

I shall be satisfied and very much gratified to see this work begun and to know that you are one of its supporters, for then I know it will be a work well done." "I hope you will pardon me, Miss Wonder. I have so many calls and demands that I must be careful and

OPPOSITES 

359 guarded, but you have already gained my confidence. 

You my call again, if it suits your convenience, let us say, Wednesday, and if agreeable to you, Miss Wonder, I shall be very happy to have you take dinner with me." Following the acceptance of this invitation, the interview soon was at an end.

CHAPTER LVI 

On the following Wednesday at the appointed hour 

Miss Wonder presented herself at Elmwood Manor where she was greeted most graciously and with exceeding cordiality by her very charming hostess, who, following dinner, escorted her guest to what she termed her "museum". There with much pride she exhibited many rare specimens of various kinds, among them being samples of wood from almost every corner of the globe; pieces of rocks and stones, some of which had been taken from a stratum long hidden from the eyes of man, as well as a variety of minerals. To have acquired her collection of jewels and precious stones would have not only exhausted the wealth of Croesus, but to seek and obtain it had entailed an almost unceasing search as well as required personal influence of no little importance. Diamonds, emeralds, rubies, sapphires and many stones almost impossible to describe were represented in that unique collection. 

But of all these she seemed more proud of the pearls she owned, which with much pleasure and pride she showed to the wondering Miss Wonder, and then in turn explained just how and where she had secured each one. "But even the finest and choicest of my pearls are not here now," she said. "I am having strung a number of perfect pearls, selected not only with respect to absence of the slightest blemish or flaw, but also as to certain connections and associations as well as to continuity of how and when and where they came into my possession. I know everyone of those pearls. You may take them and mix them and then scatter them 360

OPPOSITES 

361 far apart, but as I regain possession of them I can take each pearl in my hand and tell you its individual history in so far as I am concerned. I am having a string, a long string, made of these pearls affectionately named by me 'my sister pearls'. I want to show them to you when they are returned to me." "I certainly shall be glad to see them," said Miss 

Wonder. "Where are they now?" "They are in the hands of a man who has done considerable work for me. He and I have agreed not to reveal his identity to anyone." 99 "I should think if you did not fully trust him you would not let him have your precious pearls." "Oh, I do trust him. It was his own suggestion that we enter into such an agreement. He feels the great responsibility of having those pearls in his possession and claims he can feel safe only when no one even suspects their whereabouts." "Oh, I am sorry I asked," said Miss Wonder. "I should not have been so inquisitive." "You were perfectly justified in asking the question. 

I hope you will pardon me for not answering it with definite information."

After returning from the museum and until Miss. 

Wonder took her departure they discussed matters of charity, especially as to how it might be exercised to best benefit under-privileged girls.

CHAPTER LVII 

Following the night on which she was privileged to visit the museum, Miss Wonder made frequent visits to the home of Miss Arnold. There had been created what was evidently a warm friendship, in fact, one so firmly cemented that Miss Arnold with great faith in her new-found friend made her the recipient of many confidences, a thing superlatively surprising in that thoughts of importance had hitherto and invariably been kept and held by her as silent as a grave. 

One evening, following dinner, she said: "Miss

Wonder, I have a surprise for you. In fact, I have two surprises; one is that I have just received some information that leads me to believe I can secure something abroad, the location of which I have been striv ing to learn for quite a long, long while. I am sailing from New York in just a week.' "Oh, I am so sorry you are going," said Miss Wonder. "No, I should not say that. It sounds so selfish.

I meant I should be sorry to lose the very pleasant companionship which you have made it possible for me to enjoy. For your sake I am glad you are going, and hope that you find and are able to obtain the treasure which you seek. What is the other surprise?" "My other surprise is: I have just received word that tonight my 'sister pearls' will be here. I want you to see them before I sail." "Will they be here before I leave tonight? I am so anxious to see them." "I believe not, but the night before I sail you must arrange to have dinner with me, following which you 362

OPPOSITES 

363 shall see my beautiful 'sister pearls'. Tell me that you will be here." "It will be impossible to keep me away."

Dinner being served on the evening preceding Miss Arnold's departure, hostess and guest proceeded to the museum that Miss Wonder might have her first view of the 'sister pearls.'

The museum was constructed entirely of fire proof materials; in reality it was an immense vault containing numerous locked apertures, each of which held a steel box, each of said boxes being the reposing place of some precious possession. To open these apertures it was necessary to use a separate set of keys for each one, there being three keys to each set. Miss Arnold kept well to herself knowledge of the respective keys to use. As an additional precaution, all of these keys were deposited in another aperture, which was opened only by a key which Miss Arnold held in her physical possession day and night. This aperture was termed by its owner her key retainer. In addition to this key, it was necessary to have accurate knowledge of the proper combination to use in opening the key box when once removed from its aperture. As additional security each aperture had its own combination lock. 

As they entered the museum Miss Arnold drew from her bosom a package which she opened, the contents of which she handed her guest, with the remark: "These are my precious Sister Pearls, my dear. You may look at them while I am opening a new safety box that is to contain them while I am abroad." 

As Miss Wonder with wonderment held the pearls, there came the sound of a telephone bell, which call 

Miss Arnold proceeded to answer. 

She came back rather excitedly exclaiming: "My dear Miss Wonder, I hope you will pardon me. The station agent has just informed me that it will be necessary for me to go to New York on the night train to-

OPPOSITES 

night, as there has been an unexpected change made in the schedule. Kindly put my pearls in the box that I laid on the table, place the box in that opening in the wall and close the door while I make ready to go." 

Miss Wonder did not immediately place the pearls in the box as requested by her friend. Instead, for several minutes she stood with her eyes strangely fixed on the beautiful string which reposed in her hand; but, while apparently held fast by the object of her physical gaze, her mind's vision was focused on something far apart from those precious pearls. In the passing of a moment she had become a battle field of conflicting emotions; for a space one impulse held sway only to retreat as a contrary urge assumed the ascendancy. In rapid succession opposite impulses were repulsed. 

Should one prompting the victor be, she feared the consequence of the future years. If the other the winner of the fray should prove, she had scant hope of immediate safety of her physical self. Seemed as though the charm of the woman had aroused the jealousy of two great unseen forces, that, drawing, tugging, pulling in contrary directions, each struggled to make her will subject to its own. Alternate inclinations to yield first to one and then to the other prompting force held her swaying and irresolute between good and evil. While her eye had changed not in the fixedness of its gaze, her face clearly revealed the wavering of indecision. Suddenly caution flashed a warning, impelling her to make sure that Miss Arnold was still in her dressing room. Having ascertained this, she returned to the museum, there to resume the fencing with her conscience. Then as tick succeeded tock of the museum clock came the urge of the best within her. 'Always, always have I despised an ingrate, and this woman has been more than good and kind to me. On faith alone she has accepted me to herself. In me she has reposed a sacred confidence. To yield to this con- '""

OPPOSITES 

365 fronting temptation I can but utterly and eternally hold myself as one despicable and worthy only of contempt, and, yet, is there not another side? Must I not consider the 'others'; shall I betray them, the ones that sent me here?"

And there she stood, a statue of living indecision, yes, there she stood, outwardly motionless, within a center of tumultuous and tormenting irresolution, yes there she stood inclined to the right and then to the wrong and then to the right again. Approaching the table on which lay the open box, almost were the pearls resting within the container that waited to receive them, when came with startling suddenness the voice of Wesley Arnold, "Miss Wonder, won't you help me? 

My time is getting so short?" Abruptly roused from deep concentration, momentarily the latter halted her advance to the beckoning box, and as she endeavored to gain composure, responded: "Gladly, Miss Arnold, I'll be with you immediately." "No, no, do not come here. I am bringing something there for you to do." 

Alarmed, panic stricken, demoralized this wavering woman had now become; then within the heaving bosom of Miss Arnold's guest guilty hands thrust a string of pearls; into an opening in the wall was hurried an empty box as a strong steel door with noiseless. care was softly closed upon it. 

When Miss Arnold appeared, so thoroughly absorbed was she in preparing for her hurried leaving, she failed to observe the nervous embarrassment and quivering agitation of her over wrought guest, until in response to her inquiry: "Won't you pack these things in this overnight bag," she received the reply in a quaking tone: "Why, certainly, Miss Arnold." Then in the greenish pale of the other's face and the shaking of her limp, clammy hands, she felt that all was not well with her friend, and solicitously inquired: "Why,

OPPOSITES 

my dear, what is wrong? You are not ill, are you?" "No, no. It is simply nerves. Sometimes things that come unexpectedly and suddenly upon me affect me in just this way. The knowledge that you must go sooner than expected upset me just a bit, but I'll be all right.' With that remark she proceeded to pack the bag while Miss Arnold busied herself with other things, far too much occupied with her hasty preparations to be cognizant of her guest's near collapse. 99 "I sent word by Clara, my maid," said Miss Arnold, "to be sure to have my chauffeur with the car in readiness at once. Will you just look out of the window to see if he is there while I get on my hat and gloves?" "Yes, he is waiting," said Miss Wonder. "Will you come with me?" asked Miss Arnold. “You can go with me to the station and then William can drive you home."

The time consumed in reaching the station was not long. The train arrived in a short while and, after what appeared on both sides to be an affectionate leave taking, Miss Arnold was on her way to the other side of the world and Miss Wonder, torn with her emotions and burning with shame, was driven to her own home in the car of the woman who had been so good to her and so kind to her and whose confidence she had betrayed. Endeavoring to ease self accusation with the explanation that she had determined to place the pearls in the box, conscience answered: "But you did not. 

You had time to do so, but you did not."

CHAPTER LVIII 

Late one July afternoon a tall, well dressed man alighted from a train in Mohntown. He entered a taxi and was driven to a neat little house standing back from the street and surrounded by a garden in which were many flowers, vines, trees and bushes. A little old lady evidently was expecting him, for as he strode up the gravel pathway leading to the little house she opened the front door. Although he had at no time communicated with her and she had never seen him before, she was aware of his coming. "Come right in, sir. Your room is ready. How long are you staying?" "I really don't know, Mrs. Masters. I may be here the remainder of the summer; I may leave you in twenty-four hours. I understand, however, that the Department has paid for the room until the first of October, and it is to be at my disposal whether I am here or not. Is that correct ?" "That is true, sir. I merely asked so that I might arrange to best suit your convenience." "I am quite sure that I shall be well cared for here. 

My room, I believe, is obscured from the view of any one on the street, is it not, Mrs. Masters ?" The "Yes, it is well adapted for your purpose. lights and shadows are such that for most of the day you have a very good view of everything that passes by, while at the same time you are not visible to any one outside of the house." "That is fine. Now show me to my room."

Mrs. Masters led the man to a room on the top floor. It was just as she had described it. The light 367

OPPOSITES 

broke through the trees in such a manner that one sitting in the room could see passers-by without being observed by them. "May I bring you a cup of tea?" "No, I had something to eat and drink on the train." "Isn't there anything I can do for you?" "No, I think I shall retire early. Should a message come for me, do not hesitate to let me have it promptly, no matter what the hour may be." 

The little old lady bade him good night and as he heard her descend the stairway he opened his bag, took from it manuscript, several books and three small boxes such as are used by jewelry stores. He laid them on the top of a chiffonier, then unpacked his clothing. 

Afterward he carefully read the manuscript, then with a magnifying glass examined the contents of the three boxes. From his pocket he removed a small bottle in which he inserted a small glass rod. As he withdrew the rod from the bottle it was slightly moist. He applied it to the contents of one of the boxes, then returned the three boxes with respective original contents as well as the manuscript to the bag. Seating himself by the window he proceeded to read from one of the books, interrupting his reading from time to time to view those who passed by the house. Once he seemed to evidence a bit of interest in a lady, for as she passed he closed the book, except that he had one finger inserted at the page he was reading. For him temporarily the book had lost its interest, but like every man who has a duty to perform and who is devoted to his work and faithful to a trust he re-opened the volume, the perusal of which soon regained his undivided attention. Late into the night he read and it was not until noon of the following day that he emerged from his room.

His landlady had not disturbed him. Evidently she knew just what she should do and should not do. As

OPPOSITES 

369 he decended the stairway she asked if there was anything he especially craved for his "luncheon-breakfast", as she termed it. "Just a little fruit, soft-boiled eggs, toast and coffee." "I think you had better return to your room. I'll bring your breakfast there." "I imagine that will be just as well," he said, and suited the words to action.

In a short while she took him the meal on a tray, spread a cloth over a table and with the remark that she would bring anything else he desired, left the room. 

It was not long before he again descended to the lower floor, although this time he was changed in appearance. He was differently dressed; his hair was gray, he wore glasses and his shoulders drooped. "I do not know just when I shall return," he said. "Will you be here for the evening meal?" "I cannot say." "Well, here is a key to the front door. You may keep that while you are with me."

During the day he visited numerous places in the town. Sometimes it seemed that he was unsuccessfully looking for someone. He sat for a while in a railroad station. He visited the city library. He walked out to the municipal park and seated himself on a bench and watched the graceful gliding of boats on the glass of the mirroring lake. He entered several jewelry stores where he made some well studied purchases. Although various storekeepers endeavored to engage him in conversation, his words were few and concerned only the things he bought. Some of the town folk wondered as to the identity of the stranger, for in truth he was a stranger to all. None recognized him, none save one.

As meditating he sat on the bench in the park, a woman passed down a nearby walk. Approaching his bench, momentarily she halted her step, hesitated,

OPPOSITES 

seemed as though about to advance and speak to the man, but she did not. Instead she resumed her walk, passing very close to the bench where motionless he sat, apparently unaware of the presence of anyone. 

He did not return to his stopping place until late in the evening. When he did he found Mrs. Masters awaiting his coming. "I have two messages for you, sir, a special delivery and a telegram." 

Carefully he read both, then said: "I am leaving tonight, Mrs. Masters. My presence is not needed here, and it is best that I go some place where I may be more useful." "Important developments, I presume, sir?" "It is quite important that I should leave Mohntown." He left there that night.

CHAPTER LIX 

It was on the afternoon of an early July day that 

Miss Arnold returned from abroad. Following her arrival home, one of the first things she did was to telephone Miss Wonder, inviting her to dinner that evening, which invitation was accepted. Following their reunion, Miss Wonder endeavoring to conceal embarrassment and want of self possession bravely struggled to pay respectful attention to the strange experiences as related by her returning hostess.

Ill at ease, conscience stricken, self accused, sorely troubled and burning with feverish fear that in some manner her friend might learn that in her possession and not in their intended place were those troublesome 

Sister Pearls, it was with a burdensome pack of remorse she traveled the valley paths of gloomy Hinnom. Could she but place the Sister Pearls in their proper place in some manner to her hostess unknown, all might yet be well. Failing in this, she might confess her act of perfidy and brand herself faithless, false and most unfair, but in that she would lose not alone the trust of her worthy friend, but subject herself to the merciless disposition of the "others". She was well satisfied that during the ensuing evening Miss Arnold would repair to the museum, and just as well satisfied that she would seek her Sister Pearls in that box where she so confidently believed they had safely reposed during her most recent journey abroad. Oh, the wearing worry that her friend should find that box an empty one.

She must in some way gain access to that box and so arrange that when Wesley Arnold first opened the de- 371

OPPOSITES 

pository a string of pearls should meet her longing eyes. It must be done, but how? If she had but been strong the night her friend went away, while she might never have seen Wesley Arnold again, nevertheless she would have held Wesley Arnold's confidence and respect. But now she stood well to lose both her friend's esteem and the faltering faith of those unseen, unknown "others".

At length the dinner came to an end, and as she feared, her hostess suggested that they repair to the museum, which place held for Miss Wonder at that particular time less attraction than any place in the whole wide, wide world. Of a truth she did want to go to the museum, but she wanted to go unaccompanied alone. When she went, she wanted access to that empty box, and most emphatically must she be alone with that Sister Pearl box on the occasion of its next opening; if opened next by or in the presence of her hostess, Alice Wonder's ardent desire was to be anywhere other than in Wesley Arnold's museum. 

On various pretexts she was for a long time able to prevent the carrying out of her friend's suggestion. 

At length, no longer able without creating suspicion to defer compliance with the wish of her hostess, they proceeded to the museum. While Miss Arnold related many of her experiences abroad and told of her success in securing the immense diamond, the quest of which had been the reason for her journey, Miss Wonder was thinking hard and fast of something entirely different. How was she to get those pearls into that box? 

Taking a desperate chance, she asked if she might see how the keys were used to open the various boxes. "I am so dull about anything requiring mechanical skill," she said. "It would delight me so much if I could be successful in opening just one of those boxes myself. The last night you were home I closed the

OPPOSITES 

373 pearl box; just let me try to open it. No-do not help me. Just let me try all by myself."

Miss Arnold, unsuspecting, gave her the necessary three keys. "Now you sit over there," said Miss Wonder. "I want to do it or at least try to do it all unaided." With a smile Miss Arnold gave her the keys and sat in a chair far removed from the Sister Pearl box. Miss

Wonder fumbled with the keys. After a time she was successful in opening the aperture door, but she did not let Miss Arnold know of her success until she had drawn from the bosom of her dress a plush box and quickly placed it inside the metal box assigned to hold the Sister Pearls.

Then with a cry of exultation, which was genuine in at least one respect, she carried the box to Miss 

Arnold with the exclamation: "At last I have accomplished something of a mechanical nature !" 

Miss Arnold, taking the plush box from the iron one which contained it, hastily opened it and gazed affectionately on her Sister Pearls. "How I love them," she said, "yes, even more than any of my other treasures do I love my dear Sister Pearls." "Aren't they beautiful? I adore them," said Miss Wonder. "Yes," said Miss Arnold, as she stroked them with her hand, "they are indeed my own Sister Pearls. I could tell them in the dark." 

Miss Wonder's new found relief was again disturbed. Her tongue was silent, but her face told a troubled, puzzled tale. Miss Arnold was much too engrossed in her pearls to observe the discomfiture of her companion. Hoping against hope, Miss Wonder exclaimed: "Isn't that a beautiful box? Let me see it, won't you?" Closely examing the plush box, she was a living portrait of despair and disgust. As she returned the box without a word, Miss Arnold was still

OPPOSITES 

too completely occupied with the Sister Pearls to discern the distress of her beautiful guest. "Well, I must put them to sleep for the night," said the former, and suiting the words to action she kissed the pearls, placed them in the plush box, which she deposited in the iron one and in turn placed it in the side of the wall. Snapping the lock this time, she herself spun the dial with the remark: "How careless I was! 

While I was away only three little keys guarded my dear little Sister Pearls. The bolts controlled by the combination had not been thrown." 

With the explanation that she was suffering from a severe headache, Miss Wonder did not long remain, and was soon on her homeward way. Reaching her room she took from her bosom a plush box. Very, very carefully she examined it. Then in muffled tones she disgustedly and angrily hissed: "What a mess! What a miserable mess! For months I courted penitentiary bars, as all the while I was responsible for those innocent looking, but hell condemning pearls, and then at the last minute I foozle everything. Almost ready to throw myself on the woman's mercy, and then when fate worked my way I blundered like a brainless, stupefied fool." All the while she held in her hand what seemed a plush box, and in it a string of pearls that might well have been the Sister Pearls, for so closely did they resemble Miss Arnold's pets that Miss Wonder would have staked her all as to their being the outstanding and choicest gem of that valuable collection, were it not for one thing, a very minute, tiny thing, a very faint, but peculiar scratch her finger traced on the bottom of the plush box she held in her hand. "How I guarded those pearls! How I watched over them! My, what a fool! I had it all planned so well! So sure was I that I could reach in my bosom and find the right box, my only fear was for want of opportunity to have first access to the hole in the wall.

OPPOSITES 

375 Fixed in my memory I had the locations of the two boxes, and then with everything made easy for me I cross myself. And what will Terry say? He will swear I double-crossed him."

Deep in troubled thought, cunningly she conjured, bravely she battled, desperately she struggled to get a lift from the pit she had unwittingly dug for herself. "No, that won't do. I must either evade the whole thing or make a clean breast of it to Terry. Oh, what a fool I am! The only good I can possibly salvage from this wretched wreck is that I can still hold the confidence of Miss Arnold. Maybe that is best, after all. All the time I have been despising myself for my ingratitude to her, and now I am all upset because unintentionally I have given her back her own Sister 

Pearls. However, if it were not for Terry, I should be glad for my blunder. As it is, I must bore my way out of this blotchy, blundering mess. Even the fates try to drive me straight, but I am so tied up with Terry,

I must continue crooked in spite of all good influences. 

I had everything planned so well, I thought, but my execution was like that of a novice. I must get in touch with Terry just as soon as possible, and come clean with it. Then we'll either have to play another game, or miserably starve to death. Terry won't work at anything straight, that is, unless it is a means to a crooked end. Yet, somehow I cannot shake him off. 

If only I had not taken that first step! How true it is, as the poet said: 'Oh, what a tangled web we weave when first we practice to deceive!' Just a little thing first. Terry showed me how to cheat at cards, so that 

I might win a fifty-cent prize. But this is not getting me anywhere. I am going to bed and try to sleep, but the chances are I'll think and think and think the whole night through without coming to a conclusion, and then be so worn out in the morning that I'll be less able to think than I am now."

OPPOSITES 

But sleep did come, for after exhaustion had numbed her weary brain and soothed her tortured nerves, she slumbered until the morning came, and then only to be awakened by Miss Arnold's phoning to inquire about her aching head, and to invite her to come to dinner that night, explaining that some trunks had arrived containing things in which she felt sure Miss Wonder would be interested.

Miss Wonder replied that recently she had been suffering from so many severe headaches that she intended calling on her physician, and could not give an answer to the dinner invitation until she had her doctor's advice. Miss Arnold then suggested that as she was going out in her car she would pick up Miss Wonder and take her to the doctor's office. To this came the reply that another friend was going to stop for her, but that she would see Miss Arnold just as soon thereafter as possible.

CHAPTER LX 

Hastily leaving her apartment and hailing a passing taxi, Miss Wonder gave an address that caused the taxi driver to inquire a second time, for the address mentioned was in a negro district, whereupon she hastened to explain that she was doing charity work in behalf of an organization interested in a movement to uplift the colored race.

However, when she alighted from the taxi she did not seek admission to the house of which she had given the number to the chauffeur. Instead, she waited. until he had passed out of sight, then entered a drug store on the nearest corner. The man behind the counter evidently recognized her, but did not address her. 

He did an odd thing. He seized the left lapel of his coat with the thumb and little finger of his right hand.

Apparently noticing the sign, she proceeded through the store to the prescription department, and then from there to a room in the rear of that department. 

A man was sitting there. He wore no coat; he was without collar and tie. Doubtless, he had been ill. As she entered he greeted her with a smile, but reading her countenance his smile became a stare. For a time she made no endeavor to explain her visit, nor did he make effort to ascertain the cause of her coming or the reason for her apparent distress and despair.

Unaware of his suspicion that her mind was not well at ease, at length she solicitously said: "I hope you are better, Terry.' 99 "I was till I saw you." "Am I so horrible as that?" "You know what I mean; you're a photograph of 377

OPPOSITES 

hard luck. You're a radio of bad tidings, and although you have not made a sound your face is a loud speaker of the toughest gloom. I'm always glad to see you, old girl, but there is something wrong. Come, tell me about it." "I am so sorry, Terry, to upset you this way. If what I have to tell you could wait, I should be glad to hold it for a while. The truth is I am sorry I shall ever have to tell you. I fear your long spell in bed has taken quite too much out of you to be called upon to hear my hard luck story.' "The sooner bad news is over the better, so tell it right away, and make it good news.' "Well, Terry, it is the very worst. I got the pearls mixed." "The hyou say!" "Yes, Terry, I returned the real ones. The phoney ones I have here. There they are." "Well, if you did, you did." "I am sorry for you, Terry." "Don't be sorry for me. I've had many a tough break; I can stand one more. 99 "But how are you going to square yourself with the others? They won't believe you." "They'll have to; if they don't, what are they going to do?" "I am afraid they will do you harm." "Just leave that part to me. In our imagination you and I have had the steel bracelets on our arms more than once, and in my dreams I have become so accustomed to the clanging of chains around our ankles, that I would not hear it now, if they actually had me clamped. The doors of the pen have been yawning for us for a long while now. But somehow you and I have had just thinking skill and luck enough to fool them. 

We can do it again. We can think of some way to gag those others. They won't harm us. They have sense

OPPOSITES 

379 enough to know it wouldn't get them anything. Their stock of the mint's production is almost as low as ours, so if they harmed us they couldn't get far away. What would it get them anyhow but a chance to see how many volts they could stand. If they'll believe us we'll take them along; if they won't, we'll turn them flat.

That will make them the losers, for I believe we two can still figure a way to put the pearl deal over." 

Miss Wonder returned to her apartment and in the struggle that ensued between that pricking thing that some term conscience and her own human tempting self she realized more clearly than ever before the dual being of the human kind. Conscience pleaded that she abandon not alone the plot to regain possession of the 

Sister Pearls, but also to turn her back on her cloudy past, and let no part of it be connected with or influence the days to come. Then, with long, black, outstretched finger pointed towards her, temptation shouted its accusing words: "and you would turn traitor to your pals? You would double cross those who for years have given you everything you have had?" 

Then conscience reasoned: "Is it not better to be ungrateful, if ingratitude it can be called, and to turn aside from those who aided you only through motives that were selfish, rather than to be ungrateful to one' who has unselfishly been a friend to you and who through the goodness of her heart has been kind to you. Profit came to you only because the others needed. you in their despicable schemes." 

Then spoke the evil side. "All those words are pretty words, but they do not mean much when it comes to feeding a stomach hungrily craving for evasive food, or putting clothes on a pretty form. You and your partners, in what the world calls 'crime', have taken only from those who have more than they need, and by doing so you keep yourself and your confederates from becoming a charge on the public charity fund."

OPPOSITES 

"No, no," said conscience, "your ability, your talent and many other assets that you possess could, would and should make you independent of charity or crime. 

Even now the door of opportunity is not closed to your living down your darksome past. Apply your God given talents to honest endeavor and thereby be respected and respect yourself." 

Then with intimidating and menacing gestures and threatening and scratching tongue came the biting retort of the Black One: "And do you believe that by just a whim, a selfish, wiley whim, you can drop like last summer's fashions these long time playmates of yours? And is it your notion that these who have been your companions in crime, your co-conspirators, your hand-in-glove fellow opponents of the laws of the land -is it really your fixed point of view that these, your partners of long, long standing, can be abandoned to the dicks and the bulls? Do you believe it or is it a fancy that you can step lightly by and leave to them the brunt of the burden, while you go free with a smile? Do you suppose that you can loll in the laps of luxury, while your erstwhile pals rot in a cage? No! 

No! You might play the part of a lady, but it would be for a very short while, for even though your discarded chums were inclined to return fair dealing in exchange for your rotten play, even if your abandoned cronies were magnanimously willing to let the dead past bury itself, yet there would live a fear, a fear that in your new guise of the Godly good, or mayhap in an accidental sort of way you might by a word, even though of innocent intent, bring to pass that which might tanglingly threaten the freedom of your friends of other days. No, in your virtuous ways you could but be a menace to the liberty of these friends, your allies of lawless times. No, your righteous living would be most wearing upon the sensitive temperaments of those with whom for so long you had a common interest in wrong

OPPOSITES 

381 doing, and they never could be quite satisfied as to where your pious ways would lead you or to what dangers your rectitude would bring them and so would it be that their security and safety would demand and justice among crooks would so decree that some day you would be numbered among the strangely missing, and so complete would be your fading that to your newfound lady friend you would live only as a shady memory. You have made your bed, and have chosen your bed fellows. You must stick to them, or the sticking will be done by them, and what a sticking that will be! 

Can you picture a bright, sharp and glistening thing sticking in and through that body fair of yours? A two edged blade and a pricking point sticking through and through and then-and then imagine this; your vital form shall like a shriveled carcass be-your lustrous orbs shall lose their light, and with fast fixed gaze and glassy stare see naught but darkness of the night, and you a sweetly perfumed one a stinking dead thing will become."

Thus through the long night time hours unceasingly and unyieldingly raged this bitter battle of the promptings, all through the dark night hours until weary and worn, this strangely weak, yet wonderfully strong woman, spent in both mind and body, sank into the mercy of deep slumber, and when she awoke she beheld that the early sun had come out of the East, and glorifying her room with its morning light had centered its brightness upon the face of her mother in its frame of gold hanging on her bedroom wall.

And as with glistening eyes she looked on that sweetly sainted face it became a luminous, living thing, and in those eyes of maternal love she saw and read a message from the dead. Opportune-just in time had the mothers' admonition come. Duty was plain and daughter was determined to do as mother would have her do. So, suiting resolution to action, at once she

OPPOSITES 

arose, prepared herself a light breakfast, dressed for a visit to Miss Arnold, determined to reveal herself as a false friend, to seek forgiveness for her shameful past, and crave counsel for a future in which she might redeem herself.

Arriving at Elmwood Manor, she was disappointed in that Miss Arnold had departed within the hour. "May I ask where she has gone?" "She has gone to her home in the mountains." "Is there any reason why you cannot tell me where that is ?" "No. Miss Arnold has gone to Mount Hope." "How long will she remain?" "I do not know. She said she herself did not know." 

Miss Wonder, returning to her apartment, found awaiting her a code message, left there in some mysterious way. It was word from the one she called "Terry" that he must see her immediately. From the text she was satisfied that something unusual was pressing. Her curiosity, as well as the loyalty she considered due Terry, impelled her to go to him, secure in the belief that no matter what his attitude might be towards her new resolution, he would not do her physical harm.

She found his physical condition much improved, but he afforded her little opportunity to question him regarding that.

He always referred to those with whom he was connected in his criminal schemes as "the others". "The others," he said, "have learned the location of my hiding place, but what is still more alarming, after all these months, they have missed the pearls. I received word from them today through underground channels that they wanted me to know they had dug me out and that they believe I have already cashed in the pearls and that I am only lying low until I can escape them and make a permanent getaway. I had

OPPOSITES 

383 hoped they would not know I had the pearls until the job was completed, but my accident upset everything. 

They insist that I either produce the pearls at once, or else come across with the money for them. I can't tell them of your mistake. They would not believe it, and my life would not be worth a smooth nickel; neither would yours. Now, if you approve, I'll tell them that we're still working on the job, and that we have been delayed because of my accident. Of course, I must admit having taken the pearls the night of the blizzard in the mountains. I'll tell them I took them by mistake along with the phoney stuff, and that due to the accident I had no opportunity to return them." "All right, but I do want to tell you something else, Terry. I am worried." "Why? You never let anything trouble you before, or at least you never showed it." "No, previously I never had any fear of consequences for my wrong deeds, but it is just this, Terry: a little part of me is still good, and this woman with the pearls has awakened that sleeping good. I fought this thing out last night, and this morning I determined to go straight. I went to the pearl lady's house to see her. I had even summoned enough courage without implicating anyone to tell her just what sort of a woman I have been, but when I arrived at her home I found she had gone to the mountains." "Well, what are you going to do now?" "I do not know, Terry. The only thing that has caused me to hesitate is that I believe there is considerable loyalty due you from me.' 99 "And in view of that would you turn me flat?" "No, Terry, I would not even consider doing that, but somehow there came to me a thought that you might turn, too." "It's too late, old girl. We have been pals for a long, long time, you and I. You have been mighty

OPPOSITES 

good to me, and we have stood by each other through it all, you and I, but this crooked work has become my business, sort of a chosen profession. I hate it, but it's too late to get out of it and adapt myself to straighter things. It's a part of me and the biggest part, too. Had I driven alone in single harness through it all, I might some prosperous day have cut the straps and let the black horse run. But, besides driving a double team with you, I have been sort of going along with a coach and four. I'd mighty like to go straight,

I believe, especially since that's the way you're going to drive, but I couldn't get a white horse out of a walk before the others' would have my new nag fast by the bridle. Then what good would all my pretty resolutions avail me? 'The others' don't know your identity yet. I never told them who you were and never shall. But in spite of my believing myself well hidden here, and almost safe even from the wrath of God, they spaded me out, which only shows that if I were to drop them now they would drive right through the center of the earth, even to China, rather than forego their vengeance. "You go along, old girl, but you must go alone. Whip up your white horses and leave me here by the side of the road. I can't go with your team. Neither can you drive your creamy whites with me in your wagon.

I have kept you clear of 'the others', but they would find us both, if we tried to double harness your pale nag and my coal steed. Could not be done, old girl." 

While he talked she silently wept. "Don't do that," he said, "I'll always wish you well. If I can't make it clear to 'the others' just what happened with the pearls, I'll have to take what they choose to hand me. Anyhow, why not? Live as a crook, die as a crook. Associate with crooks, pass out as one.

For the crook, if it's not the pen or the sizzling chair or the end of a piece of hemp, he gets the gat from

OPPOSITES 

385 some other crook, or else he fades away. Maybe the rat-a-tat-tat of a shooting machine or the swish of a slashing blade; maybe he's taken for a one way ride, or he's placed on a target spot, for a crook's a crook and he'll die like one, just as crooks have always died. 

If not today, at some later time I'll go like some other crook, so, oh, well, but don't cry, old girl. Buck up. 

Shouldn't talk like that. I'm just a little tired, you know, just a little weak from my hurts, but, well, just kiss me good bye. Good luck, old girl, may you live and die like a saint."

At this the restraining flood gates broke; the dam was overrun and the river of tears was beyond control of the sobbing, sorrowful girl. "No, Terry, you may be a crook, but still you are too clean and white for me to abandon you. If you will go straight along with me, I'll carry through my good resolution, but to turn you flat, it just can't be doneyou're too clean a pal for a deal like that. Yes, it's true, Terry, you are a little late in turning your back on the threatening days of a shadowing, darksome past, but no later you than I, and I'm well convinced it's not yet too late to right about face and make yesterday's black day the last."

Unselfishly continued the discussion, he urging her to abandon him and go on the narrow way, while she as strongly resisted this unless he would travel along. 

The outcome of it all was that she won her way. Well she knew "the others" would not believe his tale regarding the pearls, and that unless he returned them to those other two his life would not be worth the lifting of a single finger. So she could not abandon Terry, for to so do she could but measure herself an accessory to his end. Yes, this now thoroughly unselfish woman won her way, and she and Terry set about to plan how best to carry on with playing the Sister Pearl_game.

It was decided that they should both go to Mount

OPPOSITES 

Hope. She would go that very night; he would go as soon as he could communicate with the others and let them know that Quesne and he were still working on the big deal.

CHAPTER LXI 

Colonial in design, perfect in proportions, magnificent in dignity, and secure in the stability of its structure, nestling in majestic comfort among the patriarchal timbers of Mount Hope's loftiest stretches, might be found by those familiar with the circuitous mountain roads the proud white mansion erected by the father of Wesley Arnold, that his family might enjoy the delights of a mountain home.

On the front porch of this Arnold residence might have been seen in the early hours of one summer evening a swinging chair with a solitary occupant, Wesley Arnold by name, and as there she sat in the twilight hour, at times silently sitting while at other times rythmically swinging, her thoughts had as much sound as those of the tongueless sphinx. Happenings of the daytime hours, if they were of concern at all to her, had been consigned to the yestertime, to the unrecallable past. It may have been that her mind reposed or it may have been that it functioned on no one particular thing, but yet one observing Miss Arnold that night, as she sat in her swinging chair, would not have been far from being right, had one searching her countenance to discover her thought have read "perplexity". Doubtless, she did not entirely miss the splendors of the setting sun as so snugly cuddled within the coziness of its fleecy coverlet it sank to sleep in its comforting couch. 

Yes, doubtless, she saw, but the full glory of its splendor was of a certainty unobserved. Then, as the king of the solar realm proceeded to puff out the light of his daytime lamp to better enjoy the solace of a sleep so justly earned and so well deserved, there came a 387

OPPOSITES 

crunching sound of rubber tires from the gravel road that led to the Arnold home. In delight and surprise 

Miss Arnold quickly arose from her swinging chair to greet Miss Wonder and ere the latter had time to alight from the car she had engaged in the distant valley to drive her to her friend, she was clasped tight in the arms of her hostess. By the first glimpse of her unexpected visitor was dispelled from the mind of Wesley Arnold the burdenings of her puzzled brain. 

With a welcome ringing true and well filled with cordiality, she exclaimed: "What a surprise!" I am so delighted to see you." "Your greeting overwhelms me. 

I feared you thought me rude when I was unable to accept your kind invitation of several days ago, but, really, conditions were such that I could not do otherwise." "It was not that you did not accept the invitation, my dear, but there was something in your manner indicating, as I thought, a change in your attitude towards me. Your voice carried a new tone that day, at least, so it sounded to me." "I'll admit I was a bit disturbed that day, but you were not in the least involved in my perturbation. I really did regret that I could not be with you." Then she lied by adding: "There was someone there in whose presence I could not well explain." "Well, let us lay aside and forget all that. I am so glad you have come to Mount Hope. When did you arrive?" "Just today." "And you are going to stay with me?" "No, I could not think of imposing further upon your generous hospitality, especially uninvited. I have engaged a room at one of the hotels." "Why not cancel the reservation and come with me ?" "I thank you, but the truth of the matter is that I

OPPOSITES 

389 have been somewhat unnerved for quite a long time and my physician ordered me here. A friend of his owns the Lookout House, and so it was that I made my reservation there. I thank you and hope just once in a while I shall be welcome here." "My dear, you are always welcome. I am all alone with the exception of the servants; in fact, I prefer to be alone." "Then why do you wish me to be here?" "That desire does not apply to you. You seem different. You are interested in the things that interest me. For years I have preferred self imposed solitary confinement, so that I might commune alone with my precious collection, my diamonds, my emeralds, and especially with my pearls. No one ever seemed to have time to even discuss my precious pets with me; no one, not a single soul, until you came into my life. You were the first to exhibit an interest in the things that have made living worth while for me, and what pleased me most was that you seemed so fond of my Sister Pearls." "I am, indeed, fond of them, Miss Arnold. I really hope you brought them here with you. I should love to see them once in a while. It gives me such pleasure just to hold them in my hands and fondle them and, somehow, because they are yours I have an added interest in them. Do you have them with you, Miss Arnold?" "Yes, I do, and I am so glad that I have! Intense, of long duration and of great depths were my ponderings before I determined to remove them from their place of security, but I did so want some companionship, and my Sister Pearls always seem to meet a desire that no humans have yet been able to supply. However, had I known you were coming, I doubt if I should have brought along the pearls.'

Had Miss Arnold been observing her visitor very

OPPOSITES 

closely, she might have observed in the latter a betrayal of half-fear, half-doubt, but none of this did she behold, nor did she catch the paling of her unexpected guest, as in puzzled agitation Miss Wonder asked herself the question: "What does this woman mean?”

Doubtless, her visitor's bewilderment had escaped 

Miss Arnold, for she said: "And now that I have you both here we three can enjoy the company of the others."

The evening passed without discussion of any important matters, but still it was quite late when Miss Wonder was returned by one of Miss Arnold's cars to her hotel.

Once in her room, there came a struggle just as on a previous occasion, when discouraged conscience had endeavored to assert itself. After this fashion did she soliloquize: "This woman, this noble woman, is so kind to me, so good, so considerate of my comfort and welfare, and in worthless me she holds such boundless confidence, how can I abuse that faith? And, yet, if I fail in my part of this damnable plot, it is not I that will be called upon to pay the price, but Terry's life will be the sacrifice. I do not know the others in his scheme. I am held in total ignorance even of their names, but from Terry I have sufficient information to know that they are unscrupulous and dangerously desperate men, and should this pearl plot fail, regardless of why, it would be impossible to make them believe other than that Terry was at fault and that he had betrayed and failed them and had carried off the spoils. "Yes, for Terry's sake I must go through with it.

Yes, for him I must carry on. In one case, it is the life of a man—a human being-that is at stake; opposed to that only the loss of a wealthy woman's pearls-her playthings, baubles, gewgaws, Sister Pearls-just as valuable when unsold as paper dolls. Yes, her play-

OPPOSITES 

391 things, that is all; the toy of today mayhap to be replaced tomorrow by another fad another passing fancy. An ingrate I must admit I am, but between ingratitude and treachery I must choose, and after all is not a life, a human life, of more value than a string of pearls? One lives and breathes and has a right to continue so to do; the other serves only as an ornamental dead-like thing, to be handled and fondled and worn to please a woman's selfish whim. In either case 

I am doing the Judas deed; I cannot escape the traitor act. Despicable, despised which e'er I do, so 'tis best, I feel, to give a man his chance to live, even though condemned for treachery to pearls without a soul.'

The following day in a secluded hidden mountain spot she and Terry by appointment met. And while he related to her his doings since last they met, she made known to him that somewhere in the Arnold mansion of Mount Hope the Sister Pearls were hidden. "Let's work fast," nervously, anxiously he implored. "Those brutes are hounding me. They refuse to believe me when I tell them of your mistake. They insist I am trying to drop them from the deal, and hold against me the fact that against their wishes and without their knowledge I carried away the pearls from their mountain retreat." "I cannot work too fast," she said. "The opportune moment may not come for quite a while. I cannot run the risk of the thing going wrong, but, Terry, for your sake I'll see this thing through, but with it all I hate myself, I despise mself. What a loathesome creature am I! How despicable must I some day appear to the owner of those pearls, and not alone to her but to myself, and I believe to even you. Think of what this woman has been to me, and then weigh and measure me-false, untrue, ungrateful, mean and low, worthy only of contemptuous disgust. Yet, Terry, for your sake, I'll never flunk this deal. I'm going on, and if

OPPOSITES 

our deal, our scheme, should fail, it cannot be because I have not tried, for, Terry, I'm going to be square with you and play my best, my fairest game to help you win. Yes, Terry, if I fail, it will be because my best is not good enough to win." 

During the ensuing days Miss Wonder quite frequently visited the Arnold home. Terry she saw nearly every day, explaining each time that the time had not arrived to consummate the plot, but while Terry became impatient at times, he retained enough confidence to believe in her and wait. He complained because "the others" were threatening him, and told of receiving word that they were coming to Mount Hope to investigate things. They intimated that while he enjoyed his summer in the mountain with the spoils of the plot of the pearls they were barely existing from day to day. "I am doing my best, Terry, but when the deal is pulled, it must be done in a safe way. You know that aside from the servants I am the only one that goes to the Arnold mansion, so that when the string is finally pulled and the loss of the pearls is discovered I shall be suspected, and unless I do the job in a sure way the whole thing will be spoiled, and the authorities will have me before a turn can possibly be made to dispose of the pearls." "Well, I do have all confidence in you, old girl. If I can only hold off those human hounds." 

One night, shortly following this conversation, while seated in her library, Miss Arnold inquired of Miss 

Wonder if she would like to see the Sister Pearls again.

Of a certainty the reply was in the affirmative. "Be comfortable here while I get them. I have a yearning to caress them, and I know you will enjoy their companionship too."

This was not what Miss Wonder wanted. She was more than anxious to know the hiding place of the

OPPOSITES 

393 pearls. However, there was nought for her to do other than to comply with her friend's suggestion and await her return.
