Miss Arnold, clasping tight to her bosom the box of precious, priceless pearls, at length came back to the sofa where the two had been sitting, and removing from the box she held those treasures so carefully strung she handed to Miss Wonder her Sister Pearls with the remark: "Don't you just simply adore them?" "I certainly do adore and love them. I fear I envy you these pearls." "What would you do if they were yours?" 

Once more of composure Miss Wonder was lacking not a little. "Did Miss Arnold really suspect her? 

Was she trying her?" And while through her mind she searched for something well contrived to say, momentarily from that string of troublesome pearls she raised her eyes, and in her lifted gaze she saw the crouching figures of two men on the limb of a nearby tree, peering through an unshaded library window. 

Checking the impulse to give vent to her fright, she shook as an ague stricken aspen leaf. Never had she seen those men before, but well she knew just who they were. In mortal funk she realized the close presence of those dreaded "others". 

As Miss Arnold solicitously inquired, "Why your agitation, my dear? Are you ill?" she likewise chanced. to look through the window and as she did, in choking tones of frantic fear she hoarsely whispered: "Did you see anybody, or did I imagine that I saw two men drop from a tree and run towards the woods?" "Yes-ah-no-I do not know whether I did. I thought I did." "Was that what upset you so?" "Yes, I think it must have been. Yes, I think it was.' 

Miss Arnold took the pearls, or what she thought was the string of Sister Pearls, with the remark: "I

OPPOSITES 

think I had better put them in their resting place. I do not like the thought of anyone prowling around. If those men saw those pearls they might take a fancy to them." Without examining its contents she closed the box and took it from the room. "I think I had better go," said Miss Wonder, as her friend returned to the library. "You are not afraid, are you?" "No, not if you will send your car.' 99 "I could not do otherwise. or would you rather stay all night with me?" "Not unless you are afraid of those men." "No, I shall caution the servants to be on guard against possible impending danger.”

And so it was that Miss Wonder left the Arnold mansion riding in Miss Arnold's car, and carrying with her in her bosom Miss Arnold's precious Sister Pearls.

CHAPTER LXII 

The trees of Mount Hope were proudly arraying themselves in the gorgeous coloring of their autumn garments; the only guests the hotels could count were those with a liking for the mountain fall time, when it happened one night that among some trees that closely grew there stood a tall man, apparently bending with weight of heavy years. His hair was of the ageing gray. Wearing tortoise shell glasses and a black felt hat that well covered his forehead, and a long black coat that accentuated his height, he leaned heavily on a stout black cane. Standing still and motionless, apparently he was listening to a voice that came from a nearby frame building, but his eyes were intently focused on something farther up the mountain side. 

His sight was held by what might well be described as a small shaky shack as from the nearer and larger building could be caught the words: "I ain't been sayin' there ain't a God, fer I knows well there be. The earth don't turn and the moon don't come on schedule time and the ocean don't rise and fall in tides and fields ain't green and flowers don't grow just chance-like. There must be a Power to tend to all this, and the Power is in a Supreme Bein'. Ye can call Him 'God'; ye can call Him what ye will, but

He works in His own way, and this Big Spirit ye calls 'God' makes nature's laws without help from anybody, so don't ye believe what peoples tells ye when they says 'I believe there ain't no God.' "If I only had words to use to make ye understand me, ye'd have a firmer belief in God than ye get from the high falutin' preacher men. Churches be all right 396

OPPOSITES 

397 in their way and the world 'd be worse without 'em, but the churches is in a rut. There be no two churches what agrees with any other churches. Not many of the peoples in the churches believes with any of the other peoples what's in the churches. I believes they all intends right, but there's one litttle thing what's got into the peoples what spoils an' upsets all the workin's. 'Tain't sech a big word, but it's moughty disturbin'. 

Just four letters: 's-e-l-f', self. Ev'ry church wants to be considered a better church than ev'ry other church, not in the good work it do, 'though it do be true it wants to do good work, but in ownin' magnificent buildin's and big org'ns and swell singers an' preachers what preach words what most folks don't understand.

That's what makes the best church to the thinkin' of most the church folks today, at least that's how it 'pears to me. "But my idea of the church and the workin's of what 

God, or the Big Spirit, wants folks to do is just to operate the Golden Rule. Instead of ev'ry feller fer himself, let ev'ry feller be fer ev'ry other feller. Instead of tryin' to beat the other feller when ye owes him a debt, do your best to pay him, and if ye can't real and hones'ly pay him it's his big job to forgive the debt till ye can pay him, and then on top of that it's best fer him to help ye, if help ye needs, even though ye still owes him the debt."

The tall man doubtless heard the discourse, but it was the shaky shack that held his interested attention. 

Abruptly was his gaze withdrawn from that building by the approaching step of an athletic appearing man who hastened down the mountain pathway. As his hurried foot falls drew nigh the elderly appearing man stepped forth from his hiding place among the trees and halted the other man by a touch on one broad and shapely shoulder, following which the athletic and younger man defensively sprang back,

OPPOSITES 

whereupon the taller one with mingled surprise and pleasure whispered in tones guarded and subdued: "Put down your fists, my man. I am too old to fight.

I merely wanted to-but say! Let me look at your face again. I think I know you. Don't they call 

Don't they call you 'Pinky'?" "Why do you ask?" "Well, tell me. Don't they call you 'Pinky'?" "They did a long time ago. But who are you?" "My man, if you are Pinky, I know I can trust you, even though I cannot reveal to you my identity. My uncle in Washington has engaged me to do a little work, and it is for that reason I cannot let you know my name. Not because I could not safely confide any secret with Pinky, but for the very good reason that it is contrary to my uncle's orders to tell secrets and give real names when no good can be accomplished by doing so. But I know you are Pinky and I promise you that some day you are going to know just who I am. But tell me, Pinky, what caused you to move so hurriedly from the proximity of that old shack?" "I was simply strolling through the mountains, as I have often done before, with no definite object in mind. 

When I came to the shack I heard male voices there. 

I stopped to listen, but was unable to understand the words. Undoubtedly, one of the men was very angry and a second one was certainly not much less so. With caution I made my way to the building and looked through a crack in the shutters. I could not see the two that did most of the talking, but I did see a face and form that were strangely familiar. As I endeavored to associate their owner with some memory of the past, he started toward the door, whereupon for sake of safety I deemed it advisable that the occupants of that shabby place should not know of my presence.' "Can you describe the man?" 99 "No, not very well, except that he was of medium height, smooth-faced, and may be forty years of age,

OPPOSITES 

399 not very stout, dark complexion, had brass buttons on his coat and wore a cap-a uniform cap-something like he wore when I saw him before, although I can't recall just when and where that was, but the buttons were not the same as he wore that other time." "Did you hear anything that was said?" "Very little. The man in uniform said: "The impression can easily be created that you two took the pearls. 

Now my advice would be to make yourselves scarce. 

We can't rush this thing. It won't do you any good to get violent. You were seen on a tree one night outside the house. Now let me handle this thing my own way, and you'll get yours.' It was then that he advanced toward the door.' 

"Thank you, Pinky. You are helping me a lot. I shall make it a point to be here every night or so. If from time to time there is anything you can and are willing to tell, meet me here. You will not be sorry, Pinky.' 

The "Pinky" of other days, with the promise to further assist his newfound acquaintance, should opportunity afford, bade him "Good night", and passed on into the night. 

Following Pinky's departure the tall, elderly appearing man proceeded down the main road, addressing himself with these words, "I am advancing, yet not advancing. Once I believed I possessed the connecting link, but that link I had good reason to feel sure was burned to death in the railroad wreck, even though the message that I received in Mohntown gave word that the self same link was in Mount Hope. That uniform mentioned by dear old 'Pinky' inclines me to the opinion that after all the Mohntown message was correct, but at the same time I cannot believe him such a fool as to wear his uniform here. However, within the past few hours I feel I have progressed in my endeavor to unravel the pearl mystery.

OPPOSITES 

"It is obvious from what Pinky has told me that the two men in the tree did not take the pearls. That seems certain, although, doubtless, they hold quite a part in the 'Plot of the Pearls'. If my baggage man is the fellow in the uniform, then my connecting link is still a living link. The men in the tree are likely one end of the chain. If I can establish that, I think I am well set to join all of the links and make my chain complete.'

A few nights later, while our tall, elderly appearing man was stationed again among the trees near the old shack, he saw approaching the man he called “Pinky”. "I have a little news for you," the latter said, "which, unless I am mistaken, will interest you not a little. I have been thinking and thinking and trying hard to place that man in the uniform. Possibly it won't help you much, but here is my story. I saw that same fellow once in a railroad station. He was talking with a very beautiful woman. She had left the train in which I was riding; because of her striking beauty I followed her with my eyes. I recall that although she had requested me to take care of her umbrella while she sent a telegram, she proceeded past the Western Union desk and out of the station through a rear door, where she was met by a man attired in overalls and jumper, on the latter of which were brass buttons. All this I observed while they conversed. Several things influenced my memory to retain the incident. First, the extraordinary beauty of the woman; second, that she lied to me regarding sending a wire; third, that one attired as was she should have an appointment with a fellow dressed as was he; fourth, that as he talked he pursed his lips in the oddest sort of a way. That was the little thing that refreshed my faulty memory. The man I saw the other night framed his lips in that same identical manner, just as though suppressing a whistle

OPPOSITES 

401 that threatened every moment to overthrow the suppressing." "Can you describe the woman?" "Well, it was quite a long while ago that I saw her and my descriptive powers are limited, but she was dark, very beautiful, and expensively and stylishly dressed. That is about as much as I now remember." 

As some of the village folk were gathering to attend services in the little chapel, the elderly man thanked Pinky for the information, and the two men walked away in opposite directions.

CHAPTER LXIII 

Imprisonment, self imposed, held Miss Wonder isolated in her hotel room as during all the days and nights following the disappearance of the Sister Pearls from the house of her friend this unhappy woman wrestled desperately, unceasingly, with her other self.

And through the wearing hours of this struggle, as within herself these wrestling, struggling forces fought, as the fortunes rose for or fell from first one and then the other, as ascendancy swayed from herself to her other self, as holds were gained and then were broken, after a long, bitter, wearing, tearing struggle, there was no decision, no victory gained. The fight was a draw. And during all this wondering as what next to do, she had no word from the owner of the missing pearls until in the late hours of one afternoon her monotonous reverie was terminated by her telephone bell. After much hesitating and with the hope that the ringing would cease, she finally answered the call, and over the wire came the voice that for days and for nights she had dreaded to hear. "Is that you, Miss Wonder? Are you ill? I have not seen you and I have not heard from you for nearly two weeks. I could not understand your silence; I thought you might have been unexpectedly called home. 

I hesitated to phone, because I felt satisfied you must have some good reason for not communicating with me. 

Naturally I disliked intruding on your privacy. I could bring nothing to mind that I had done or said to offend you. I had a thought that possibly the two men in the tree outside of the window the last night you visited me had alarmed you so much that you feared to come here 402

OPPOSITES 

403 again, and wishing not to offend me, you hesitated to tell me so.' 99 "Oh, no, Miss Arnold. I have not forgotten you. 

You never did anything to offend me. I have had you in mind more than anyone else, but I have had some trouble, in consequence of which I have worried more than I can tell you. When the phone bell rang I was just passing over in my mind whether I should call you or

This is Friday. Tuesday night I was all alone and wanted my pearls, my Sister Pearls, to keep me company for a while. When I opened their hiding place they were gone. But what is still more strange and mysterious is that in a plush box exactly like the one that held the Sister Pearls was another string. At first glance I did not detect the difference; in fact, it was not until I sat in my chair before the open fireplace in the library where a fire had been started-because, as you will recall, Tuesday night was rather

chilly that I realized the pearls I was holding were not my Sister Pearls. As you have at times seen me do, I was fondling them, but the response to which

I could not easily believe it, but yet there was something lacking. I turned on all the lights in the room and very carefully examined the pearls. They appeared just the same and to the touch they felt just like the 

Sister Pearls. Then one by one I inspected each individual pearl, for I am perfectly acquainted with every one on my own string, and I found one little pearl, near where the clasp was placed, that was not like the pearl, my little Sister Pearl, that always occupied that particular corresponding place. Trying to believe that something might have happened to have changed that one pearl, I well knew in spite of hope that such a thing was impossible. For quite a spell I was undecided as what to do. After much indecision I telephoned to my lawyer at home. After a few hours he was here. Carefully he went over the matter with me. 

I told him when I had last seen the genuine pearls. I also informed him that you were here at the time. 

Miss Wonder, Miss Wonder, are you there?" "Yes, yes, I am here. My arm was cramped and I dropped the receiver. Tell me all about it, won't you?" "I am sorry to keep you listening to my story, Miss Wonder." "Oh, don't say that. I really am much interested.

Just hold the phone a moment while I draw a chair so that I may be more comfortable. Now I am all right, Miss Arnold." "Well, just where was I? Oh, yes! I explained to my lawyer when I last saw the pearls. I told him that we had put them away carefully. I told him also of the two men in the tree. 405 "No, I am quite sure it would be impossible for me to identify them. But this is dreadful, Miss Arnold. 

I feel so terribly bad because I was the last person within your knowledge who handled or saw the pearls; but is it not possible that the wrong pearls might have been the ones we had that night?" "Oh, no! That could not be. The pearls I had in my hands that night were my own Sister Pearls." "Is there not something I can do, Miss Arnold?" "No, I placed the entire matter in the hands of my legal representative. He has handled everything for me since my parents died. He is an old friend of the family. My father took a special interest in him and aided him in his struggle to acquire a profession. I shall be guided by him in every way. Please do not mention anything about my loss to anyone. Mr. Blair, my attorney, might not like it if he knew I had told even you.' 99 "I certainly hope you get them back, Miss Arnold." "Thank you, my dear, I hope you will come to see me real soon. 99 "I shall certainly try. Good night.' 99 Miss Wonder put on her coat and hat, walked down into the valley and then along a beaten path until she reached the river bank. Arriving there at an open spot where no trees broke a view of the stream, she stood silent-motionless—with head bowed low and eyes fixed upon and held fast by the swashing, swishing dark heavy waters of the rushing river below as it pushed its monotonous way through the towering hills. 

Weighted with remorse for the wrongs of her past, especially for her part in stealing the pearls of her friend, she had reached a decision as how best to end the struggle with her other self. She had left the hotel fully resolved to to cast herself into the whirling mountain stream. She had told herself she could not go on. Should she win in her fight with her

OPPOSITES 

other self, what could she gain? To return the Sister 

Pearls to their rightful owner, while her conscience might possible be eased, what else could she win? Not the retention of Miss Arnold's friendship! Not 

Terry's good will! No, for on the contrary he would full well know she had placed his very safety in jeopardy; then too must she bear in mind that her own fate would more than probably be subject to the will of those dreaded "others." 

Should her other self overcome her in that furious wrestling, then all would be lost, including her own self respect, and in that event she and her other self both should die. She thought it all out. No matter which won, whether she or her other self, life at its best could be only a breathing death and so she decided to travel on from earth to the country of mystery and doubt, satisfied to take the step to that land beyond recall-it could not be worse she told herself than hell's torment she suffered here at least she would not be compelled to face Wesley Arnold's reproach; she would escape the hate and revenge of the relentless others. And so it was she came down to the river's bank to take her last trip on earth. She had chosen to travel by the water route and there she stood ready to go. But as she stood, she hesitated before bidding the earth adieu. And as she haltingly waited, wanting a bit of the suicide's courage, with a lack of the killer's will, as she stood watching and wondering where the waters would carry her lifeless form and whether it would ever be found and how it would look if it were; whether Terry would care, whether her friend, Miss Arnold, would forgive her, whether he-Oh, well, he would never even knowand then as she wondered what that hazy hereafter would hold for her, someone lightly touched her, laid a hand gently, oh, so gently, on her shoulder. 

Then, as turning, she beheld a kindly looking man,

OPPOSITES 

407 both elderly and very tall, she heard him softly inquire: "Pardon me, Miss, are you lost?" "Oh, no, not lost," audibly she replied. "Almost," she silently thought, then resumed, "but I do so love to watch the water at the approach of eventide."

But this man was not deceived. Observation had taught him to know many signs. Well aware was this man of the conflict that raged within her soul. "Are you ill?" he asked. "You seem so distressed." "I am not-not, oh,-not well, entirely well."

Yes, this man could tell where the cold steel blade of remorse had struck. He could hear the rending and the tearing of heart string pulled and tugged by her merciless self. He could understand the scalding tear drops that flowed from her suffering soul. 

Oh, yes, this man knew the symptons, but he did not tell her so. He simply said: "Come with me. It is not well for any lady, especially one of your apparent beauty and refinement, to be alone at night in this part of the mountains. Many dangers lurk about you. 

"No," she said, "I wish to be alone. I am not afraid." "I do not desire to impose my presence upon you, but you must pardon me if I insist that you leave this spot and allow me to accompany you as far as the road."

She knew that for the nonce her intention of self-destruction was defeated, so she went with him. 

When they came to the road they parted, but this man followed in her wake until she entered her hotel. He waited a while and then left, satisfied that she would not return to the river that night.

CHAPTER LXIV 

In the resort of Mount Hope there were two persons who slept not at all that night: One was Miss

Wonder; the other was the tall elderly man, whose night hours were well occupied with much deep thinking, the result of which must have been satisfying, for at the breaking of the morning time he told himself that he possessed a strong clue, a very strong clue to one end of the chain, which he had been so earnestly striving to complete. 

The following night he stationed himself in the midst of a clump of bushes, even taller than himself and so densely thick that he might unmolested watch the old shack on the mountain side. He was of the opinion that three men in whom he had a deep interest would again meet in their former rendezvous and that the time of their meeting was set for no distant date, but although he watched until the morning came his vigil was in vain. Two other nights his watching was likewise wasted time, but on the fourth night he was rewarded. Two men entered the old building that night at different times and coming from opposite directions. When those two had arrived he cautiously approached the shack, on one side of which was a huge rock hidden among chummy trees. In his previous reconnoitering he had selected that rock as a venient station for himself to occupy, for so close to this huge and chosen stone was a hole rotted through a board in one side of the shack that he was enabled to command a view of much of the single room which the building contained. He was unable to see the men 408

OPPOSITES 

409 distinctly, but to him their voices were clear and plain as he heard one say: "Did you see him?" and another reply: "Yeah, but some job!" "An' what's he got to say?" "He says nobody kin move the pearls right now. He says we gotta wait till the storm clears up. I told him there weren't no storm, that I didn't believe the woman on the hill even knowed she'd lost her pearls, for if she a knowed it, the papers would be full with it, for no dame whatever breathed what could keep her face shut, if she lost even one pearl. And I don't believe she could tell her pearls from the phoney ones, nohow, not the way we made 'em. I had a hard job a movin' him. 

I told him we couldn't lay down and starve in these here mountains, while we waited for his pal Quesne to get ready to get busy. With that he throwed up to me that he was snitchin' enough from the hotel for us to eat, but then I told him plain we knowed his game. He and the other feller was a gonna dump us. Told him we knowed it, and put it up to him if he wanted to prove that the pearls wasn't awready played out of our hands he'd hafta bring 'em here tonight and show 'em to us. He said he didn't know where they was; that the other guy had 'em. That satisfied me that both him and the other bloke was crooked, so I give him till eleven tonight to show the goods. Told him plain if he didn't we would send him on a little journey west where they don't come east again." "Now you're a doin' somethin'. That's the way you orter a acted long ago, if you would a listened to me instead of allus tellin' what a nice clean feller he was.' "Sh! Sh! It's almost 'leven. I hear him comin' now, I think."

The door opened and a third man entered. This man our elderly friend could plainly discern. He had

OPPOSITES 

seen that man before; he had seen him several times before. "Well," spoke one of the men, addressing the third, "did you bring the pearls?" "Now listen!" came the ready reply. "I told you

I'd have them, if possible. I told you who did have them, but that I couldn't get them until I could see him." "Well, why didn't you see him then?" "Impossible until tomorrow." 

Long wrangling ensued. Revolvers and knives were drawn. One man was intent on murder; the second man, while exhibiting lack of faith in the third, well knew that the taking of life could have no other outcome than to render them even more susceptible to the hands of the law, which at that moment hung so heavy and so threatening above them. The quarrel, therefore, became a three-cornered one, which terminated in a positive promise of the third man to produce the pearls the following night, whereupon the men departed in three different directions. 

Giving them sufficient time to be so far removed from the shack that there would be little chance of his being discovered, the elderly man leisurely made his way to his abiding place. Had his face been visible to one of a searching eye, that one might with even careless scrutiny have read a satisfied countenance that was well at ease. Yes, our friend of elderly appearance was quite well satisfied in the belief that in his possesssion was full knowledge of both ends of the chain, as well as the necessary connecting link. He slept well that night, for a series of puzzling crimes was no longer a mystery to him, at least, that was the felicitation he extended to himself as he retired soon after his return from the mountain shack.

The following day from Terry came a message to Miss Wonder urging her to meet him at an appointed

OPPOSITES 

411 place. Complying with his request, she heard from him an account of the happenings of the preceding night.

He successfully sought to make plain to her that "the others" were desperate men, that they suspected Quesne and himself of either having already disposed of the pearls, or else of having conspired to do so as soon as they felt it safe, at which time it was their intention to drop "the others" flat. He stressed the importance of showing the pearls to "the others", to prove that she and he were playing a fair game and explained that as an evidence of good faith he had consented to take the pearls with him to the shack that night, so that "the others" might know for themselves that he and Quesne had not disposed of them. "Yes," she demurred, "but I doubt whether you will bring them back." "And then you don't trust me." "Yes, Terry," she replied in a hurt tone, "I do trust you. You have no right to ask me that. I mean that once they gain possession of those pearls neither you nor I will ever see them again." "They are too wise for that," he explained. "If the authorities know of this matter at all, they know that two men were seen in the tree that night. "The others' know, too, that they were seen, even though they don't know whether they could be recognized. They would be fools to have the pearls in their possession. They have no possible channel except ourselves through which to turn them safely into cash." "Yes, but they could have a hiding place the same as I have. I do not intend to give them to you, Terry." "Old Girl, you must! We'll all sink, if you don't. 

Why, they'll resort to anything, if I don't turn up there tonight with those pearls. They'll blow the whole works." 

Irresolute she silently stood, hesitating, wavering, vaccilating; temporarily repossessing herself, she was

OPPOSITES 

engaged in some very deep thinking. Yes, her mind was at work. There was some definite planning, some sane reasoning; then her eyes expressed a solution. "Meet me at nine tonight," she said. "Will you give me the pearls?" "Meet me here at nine tonight. I promise to return you the pearls." With no other word than "Good-bye 

Terry," she left him standing there. 

And Terry left alone in their trysting place, stood and watched the woman go and when she was gone from out of his sight, he continued there to stand. He stood as a man uncertain of what to do, as a man sure of only one thing, but very sure of that; he was more than sure that the end was near, the end for better or worse. And as alone he communed with his better self, with his thoughts he painted a scene. He titled his picture "A Prophecy," and this is what it showed. 

Most of the picture was of the night; just a little showed the break of day; six figures there were, more or less distinct, of whom he chanced to be one; then one was "old girl" and "the others" were there; the fifth a tall, elderly man, and the sixth he had never seen. 

No, she was a stranger, and yet for all that he was sure he knew who she was. He had not placed her in the scene. She had painted herself in a separate group with "old girl" and the elderly one, while with “the others" he was off to one side.

Yes, that was his picture of "A Prophecy," as he stood by himself all alone. Six in the picture. The elderly one was unfamiliar, but he considered him just an intruding old busy body, but the sixth, although a stranger, had come into that picture for a purpose that was definite and plain. She had come to remind him that she owned the pearls; that the Sister Pearls were hers. “Oh, well, it was only a brain picture, but, nevertheless, she had a nerve to force her way into his private studio."

OPPOSITES 

413 However, what concerned him most was that he, the artist, was hardly in the picture at all. "Wonder if that means anything?" With that he pulled himself together, and left his "A Prophecy" behind him. 

Miss Wonder, returning to her hotel, called Miss

I shall be waiting for your car."

In a short time Miss Arnold's automobile appeared, and in a very short while Miss Arnold was greeting her guest on the porch of her mountain home. They talked of the pearls, and of them talked again and again, in fact, they talked of naught else, until a brief breathing spell was broken by Miss Wonder's inquiry: "Are you quite sure the pearls you have are not your own Sister Pearls ?" "Oh, my dear, I am quite sure!" "Won't you let me see the one little pearl that convinces you someone has imposed upon you and has substituted the other pearls for your own?" "Why, yes, I shall be very glad to do so. It is well you came tonight, though, for in a day or so my lawyer is going to have someone come and examine them to ascertain if those in my possession are really pearls at all. If they are not, the procedure of my attorney will be different from that which will be pursued if the substituted pearls are really genuine, even though not my own. But regardless of that, you may see them immediately." "You need not bring them to the library, Miss Arnold. Let me accompany you." 

Disregarding this latter suggestion, for some reason Wesley Arnold made no reply, but left the library where the two had been seated, secured a plush box from its hiding place, returned with same, handed its

OPPOSITES 

contents to Miss Wonder and proceeded to explain very explicitly and carefully why she was so sure the string was not that of her Sister Pearls. Asking many questions, Alice Wonder seemed to be examining the pearls very closely. The ensuing conversation consumed much time, and while they still discussed the all important matter, dinner was announced. "I am sorry our discussion of the pearls occupied so much of our precious time, especially since it must distress you to have their loss continually on your mind," said Miss Wonder. "Then, too, we have had so little time to talk of anything else; still, it is the subject uppermost in our minds." "Yes," said Miss Arnold, "but we must have dinner now, especially since you tell me your plans are to spend your evening elsewhere." "I am sorry for that, but I had arranged for my evening previous to my phoning you. I must not be late in getting back to the village. I'll just put the pearls in the box and return them to their hiding place." 

Suiting her words to action, she did just what she said and closed a little white iron door that concealed a hole in the wall. Miss Arnold, it seemed, would have preferred attending to the return of the pearls to their depository herself, but her guest's initiative took her unawares. 

Soon after dinner Miss Arnold's car drove Miss

Wonder back to her hotel. At the appointed time she met Terry and handed him the plush box, which he speedily opened and nervously removed the pearls. For a moment he held them as one relieved of a heavy burden; then returned them to their container. "When do I get them back, Terry?" she asked. "Watch for me as I come from the shack. I swear 

I'll bring them back, Old Girl."

415 stopped once to watch Terry pass out of her sight. 

That night the rock among the trees in the rear of the shack held two men. There was the elderly man and the one whom he had called Pinky. Two of the men who had been in the shack on the previous occasion were there again. The men on the rock could hear their subdued voices. From the conversation they gathered that Terry was later than the other two expected him to be. Time passing without his appearance, first one and then both concluded that he had intentionally failed them, but at length he came. "Well, got the goods?" one immediately demanded as he entered the shack. "Yes, I have them with me, but I want your word that I can take them back." "Ain't we played square so far? What's the use of givin' words? Let's see the pearls." "No, not till you promise. 99 The other, until then silent, eased the situation: "We promise. Let's see the pearls."

Terry produced the plush box and handed it to the last one to speak, the one who had given the promise.

It's results what counts, not words. You kin go Scot free, if yer nice and quiet, 'cause we knows you won't squeal, fer that would put you in the same boat with us. Watch yer step there. If you make a noise or start somethin', I'll drill you till you'll look like a fine gauge sieve. Now beat it."

Old Girl. For a moment he hesitated; then dashed at the man with the threatening gun. The latter fired, but the bullet missed its mark. Before he could shoot a second time, Terry had closed in and was struggling for possession of the weapon, which its owner was endeavoring to free from Terry's clutch, in order that he might once again fire. Fighting like blood thirsty beasts, kicking, biting, with loose hand striking, clawing, scratching, tearing and ripping, they battled across the room. Without advantage of his firing piece,

Terry's opponent would have been no match for him, but that gun was a menacing thing that Terry could not lightly regard; so it required that while he had his man to fight he must keep clear of the bullet end of that thing. Swaying, bending, choking, battering, they fought to a corner of the room where Terry adroitly pinned his man. Now was his chance to get that dangerous firing iron. If he could only break the other's hold on it, he would either throw it out of the door or else use it to demand the return of the pearls. With one hand on the throat of his struggling, panting foe, he sought to wrench the gun loose with his other hand. 

Furious was the battle. The weakening wretch unsuccessfully sought to break Terry's grip on his tightening wind pipe, and it seemed that Terry had won, but with the desperate strength of a drowning man the other threw all his weight at the man who was robbing him of his life's breath; the suddenness of the thing caught Terry off guard, and both men went to the floor. During the battle the third man had taken no part in the fray. He seemed quite content to keep out of harm's way. Although Terry's antagonist had called for aid, the third man seemed well satisfied to avoid the range of the gun. But as the two men wrapped tight fell towards the floor the gun with a roar burst forth. Came the spit of fire, a flaming flash, a ripping bang, and a bullet was loosed in the room, a bullet in-

tended for Terry's breast found lodging place in the heart of the other man, the one with no part in the fight, who with a look of surprise and a glance of reproach at the man with the gat in his hand shook and shivered, then slumped to the floor, a messy mass of man. Lifeless, motionless, and as inanimate as the plush box of pearls that lay at his side.

Simultaneously, without word or sign, the two men on the rock, with weapons drawn, rushed to the open doorway in the front of the building, almost colliding with the two grappled men who still clutched tight in the hold of the other fought out through the door to the steps in the open, the rotted wood of which giving way to the force of the combat, both men were hurled to the ground, so cruelly covered with ruggedly rough and hard harsh stones. The impact of the fall breaking the grip of both men, Terry's antagonist seized the opportunity to pump two cruel shots into the prone body of the man who unarmed had so bravely fought him. Once more a killer, this man hastily rising and unexpectedly being confronted by two armed strangers and his gun being empty, turned and ran from the scene. "After him!" shouted the elderly one to Pinky. "I'll take care of this fellow." 

As the silvery white moonlight fell upon the face of the fallen Terry, thereby adding to his ghastly hue, the elderly man raised him; he saw that he breathed but likewise he saw that death's hold was upon him. Hastily glancing inside of the shack he saw the prostrate form of the man who had fallen from the wild shot of his partner, while at his side on the floor lay a plush box. 

Taking sufficient time to secure the box, the elderly man returned to the stricken Terry, followed soon after by the reappearance of Pinky. "Did you get him?" "No, he fell over the cliff. It is a hundred feet down,

419 a trifle far for me to match his leap, but we can find him any time we want him. He'll wait for us." "Let us do what we can for this cuss then."

They raised the limp form with its fountain of blood and started toward the village. Reaching the road, they were halted by the approach of a most beautiful woman. "Is he hurt?" she cried. "Yes," the elderly man replied. "I fear he is very much hurt. Let us take him into the chapel. Then hasten for a doctor," he said, addressing Pinky.

They carried the bleeding Terry into the building and laid him upon a bench, while through two holes in a uniform coat came the spouting of crimson stuff. The elderly man, assisted by Miss Wonder, did what he could to check the flowing of vital blood, as all the while the unfortunate man remained unconscious. None other than the three were in the chapel. Miss Wonder found water which tenderly she placed to the helpless man's lips, which after a time seemed to revive him, as with half opened eyes he weakly inquired, "Are you with me, Old Girl?" "Yes, Dear, I am right here. Don't worry, you will be all right soon.' 99 "No, No," he said, "I'm going over. Did you get the pearls?" "Don't worry about the pearls. They are all right.
