Having questioned the elderly man and Pinky, they departed, stating that they would arrange for removal of the body.

After they had gone Pinky inquired as to further procedure, the elderly man replying to the effect that there was little to be done except to look after Miss Wonder, who at that moment was in another part of the chapel kneeling by the side of a roughly hewn pew, where with head bowed low she prayed for the soul of the departed Terry, and sought forgiveness and guid ance for her own weak self. "I have seen that lady before," remarked Pinky, "and I have also seen the man who died here tonight." "Yes, and so have I," very slowly replied the elderly man. "I have seen them both."

CHAPTER LXV 

One of the men from the prosecutor's office returning, requested that Pinky accompany him to the place where the man had fallen over the cliff. The departure of those two was almost simultaneous with the reappearance of Miss Wonder and the arrival of the coroner and an undertaker who came to remove the body from the chapel. The elderly man, fearing that Miss 

Wonder might be near to total collapse, advanced rapidly to meet her. "Is there anything I can do," he asked, "to assist you to your hotel or otherwise. The man who has just passed on was I presume very close to you." "He was," very quietly she replied. That was all. 

She made no further response. "I do not believe there is anything requiring the presence of either of us here. It is fortunate that the door was open and that we were able to bring the poor fellow in, even though we were unable to do him any permanent good. We are in a sense trespassing, and the only reason why we should remain would be to make explanation to the owner of the building. When he sees the condition of the bench he will, of course, know that something unusual has happened. At the same time it might be quite a long while before he arrives, and explanation can be made later; in fact, there is no doubt that from the newspapers, if not from some other source, he will be fully informed as to what has occurred here in his chapel during his absence." "Yes, it is best that I go. I have often wondered regarding this place. I knew they called it a chapel, 421

OPPOSITES 

but that did not create sufficient interest to bring me here, and I have never been inside the building until tonight." "Which way do you go?" he queried. "I fear you are much upset and if I may accompany you to your hotel, I shall gladly do so." "I do not know just what I shall do. I may want to walk a while. I have not fully determined. I thank you, but I am perfectly able to take care of myself." "As you please," then significantly, "but stay away from the river."

While they conversed someone unobserved had entered the room and was standing just inside of the doorway. Before they had taken a single departing step, simultaneously they knew that they were not alone. The elderly man gave no sign of recognition, but Miss Wonder stood motionless with gaze fixed full upon the man who stood just inside the open door and who likewise appeared as though, of stone. Aside from the blaze of his staring eyes, he might have well been from all appearances a figure carved out of some inanimate substance. To the elderly man came suspicion of more mystery. Impossible that Doogle should have been a link in the plot of the pearls, but it was beyond contradiction that he and this woman were not meeting for the first time. Likewise was it beyond possible doubt that their acquaintanceship had been of more than a passing sort. Held by the tableaux, he was as speechless as the sphinxlike other two and just as motionless, except that his line of vision alternated between the two.

Following an eternity of indecision, the man in the doorway was the first of the three to break the spell created by his appearance so entirely unexpected. Surprised though undoubtedly he was, yet surprise was not all that his countenance registered. Joy, embarrassment, hesitancy, wonder, incredulity, all might clearly

OPPOSITES 

423 be read as an impulse stronger than himself impelled him onward, forward, into the immediate presence of that sad but beautiful woman, who unbidden, unexpected, had entered his chapel walls. Closer and closer he approached, and then face to face, with a gladsome smile and an expression perplexed but strangely pleased, he said: "Martha, is it really you?" 

She made no reply. And while one could read that her soul was glad, still one could just as readily discern the regret she had in the meeting.

Again he spoke. This time he answered his own question, "Martha, I know it's you! Has time changed me so much, you don't know me?" 99 "No, Douglas, I do remember you, but as I had expected never to see you again, I am quite overcome.' "Then tell me, Martha, why are you here? If you did not expect to see me, why did you come here?” "I did not know that you were here. I did not come to see you." "Then why are you here?" he insisted.

At this time came an interposing of the elderly man: "I believe I can best and most fully explain that. I am sure that you are entitled to an explanation, which you shall have at the proper time. Since you two are friends, I shall not remain longer, but I must insist that before I go the lady give me her name and address." "Why must I do that?" she parried. "You are a witness to what has happened tonight, or at least to some of it. As a matter of fact the prosecutor wished to place you under bond as an important witness, but I assured him you would be present when wanted, and I have thereby made myself responsible for you." "Have you a blank card?" "I have no card, but you may write your name and

OPPOSITES 

hotel on the page of this book," indicating exactly where he wished her to do so. 

She wrote: "Miss Wonder, 'Lookout House'." "Do you promise me you will not change your address," he said, "without first seeing me?" 

As she hesitated, he warned: "If you do not so promise, I shall be obliged to immediately make known to the prosecutor that I am no longer responsible for you. 99 "Where may I find you, Sir?" 

Ignoring her question, he countered: "In case I want to communicate with you, I shall be at your hotel between nine and noon tomorrow,' whereupon he bade the two “good night," and passed out of the chapel. He knew that when he wanted Miss 

Wonder he would find her just where he wanted her to be. 

Following the departure of the elderly man, Douglas urged Martha to remain and talk with him. Obviously she wished to go, but some indescribable something, strengthened by pity for the man, influenced her to stay and seat herself on one of the benches. "Tell me, Martha," he said, as he proceeded to occupy the remaining portion of that same bench, "Isn't it true you came here to look for me?" "No, of a truth I did not, Douglas. Long ago I determined never to see you again. I did not know that you were here." "How long have you been at Mount Hope?" "Oh, for quite a little while." "Did you never see my chapel or the Mount Hope information booth?" 

"I saw them, but did not know that you were connected with them. I did not care to meet anyone." "What have you been doing all these years?" "Douglas, were I to tell you, you would be sorry you knew."

OPPOSITES 

425 "Of course, I have no right to insist, but I have thought of you every day since last I saw you years ago. I have been hoping and praying you would change your mind and come back to me. Do you prefer to tell me nothing of your doings during these long months of separation?" "I believe it best that I should not tell you. However, probably you will find out soon enough from someone else." "Just what do you mean by that?" "I might just as well tell you the story myself as explain my remark.' "But, Martha, now that I have you, you won't leave me again, will you?"

A long delayed reply came through agonizing sobs: "My dear, I must.' 99 "You must, and why? Have I become so homely, a hob goblin, uncouth, unkempt, lacking physical equipment, or do you see me growing old?" "No, it is none of those things," she said. "Then tell me what it is." "I cannot." "Martha, you must! Do you love me, Martha?" "More than all else in the world." "Then why will you not stay with me?" "I am not worthy of you," came in a smothered sob, and before he could lay a staying hand she had fled into the night. 

Unknown to her, a tall, elderly man followed her through the mountain darkness to the hotel known as "Lookout House", and in the shadow of nearby trees he was careful first to observe that none of the bedrooms was illuminated; next, shortly following Miss 

Wonder's arrival at the hotel, that a light was turned on in one of the rooms on the top floor; then, that the woman who pulled down the window shade in the room newly lighted was the same who had played such an

OPPOSITES 

important part in the happenings of that eventful night. Then he waited until the light disappeared, and he saw the shade raised and the window flung open. 

Well satisfied with his observations, he turned his back on Lookout House, and retraced his steps to the chapel. 

To the man Miss Wonder had addressed as "Douglas", he said, "I have returned here for two purposes.

One, to explain the happenings of tonight and why the lady and I uninvited occupied your chapel"; whereupon he immediately proceeded to relate as much of the tragedy as he considered wise to tell, following the conclusion of which he continued: "My second reason for coming here now is to learn from you whatever you are willing to tell me regarding the lady." "There ain't much as I can tell ye, sir. In long 'go times the lady be a close friend to me, but she ain't been a part of my life for what seems a long span of years. Ain't even seen her, didn't know she was hereabouts. I'm just interested as much as ye be to find out what she's been a-doin' and why she be in these here parts. 99 "Isn't there anything you can tell me of her past?" "Nothing as can be doin' ye good. And why can't I be turnin' to questionin' and askin' what ye be knowin' as to her?" "It is true you have the right, but as it seems as though neither of us can be of assistance to the other tonight, let us simply drop the matter for the present. 

I hope to see you again." With that he was gone.

CHAPTER LXVI 

Early in the morning, following the tragedy of the shack, an elderly gentleman might have been seen alighting from an automobile in front of the mountain home of Miss Arnold. To the servant answering his ring of the bell he stated that he had important business to discuss with Miss Arnold, but that for good and sufficient reasons he could not give his name, to which the servant replied that under such circumstances she felt quite sure Miss Arnold would find it convenient not to see him. "Tell her I have some information concerning the matter she recently discussed with her lawyer." 

Without replying the servant closed the door and after a brief space of time returned to usher the elderly man into the library, where Miss Arnold kept him waiting but a very short while. As he looked out of a window his admiration of the valley view was interrupted by a woman's voice, a voice surprisingly familiar, a voice strangely thrilling: "You wish to see me, sir?" Had he been facing the speaker when first he heard her speak, he would have been unable to hide the emotion that stirred every atom of his tingling being. As it was, trained for emergencies and surprises, he slowly turned, being careful to consume sufficient time in the turning to regain his self control. "Am I addressing Miss Wesley Arnold?" he said. "Yes. What may I do for you?" "It is quite important that we be not overheard." "Is it true that you represent my lawyer?" "Not directly, but I am importantly connected with 427

OPPOSITES 

and interested in the same case which you discussed quite recently with him." "May I ask to what case you refer?" "A string of pearls has passed from your possession, has it not?" "It has, but what interest can you have in my pearls?" "I am here to return them." "I cannot believe that you were connected in any way with the disappearance of my pearls." "I thank you for the compliment, but in this instance, at least, I am worthy of your confidence, as I had nothing to do with the loss of your pearls. I was really on the other side. Through that I was rather fortunately placed in a position that I was able to recover them-.' 

"Then you have my pearls, my Sister Pearls?" "Yes, I believe I have." "Are you willing to explain how they came into your possession?"

As briefly as possible he repeated the story, carefully avoiding all reference to any part Miss Wonder might have had in the plot or in its outcome. "I am so relieved," she said. "I had just a faint little doubt, just the faintest bit of suspicion, regarding a friend of mine. I am so glad that the doubt has been dispelled." "I am quite sure that the men from whom I recovered the pearl were no friends of yours." "Doubtless, they are the same two men Miss Wonder and I saw in a tree outside of one of my windows one night." "I am quite sure they are." "And they are both dead?" "Yes, they are both dead." "But who is the third man?" "It appears he was a bell-hop at one of the hotels.

OPPOSITES 

429 Just what his part was is very immaterial. He and the other two are beyond punishment, and I have your pearls." "Won't you let me see them? Won't you let me have them ?" "Yes, but first I must have your compliance with certain requirements. I may tell you that I represent the 

United States Government. My happening in on this case was rather accidental. Will you fill in a description of your pearls on this form which I hand you?"

She took the paper, answered all the questions asked thereon, and gave a complete description of her pearls. "I have no doubt the pearls I have in my posession belong to you. If you will sign this receipt, you may have them." She signed the form he handed her, following which he placed in her hands a plush box.

With an exclamation of joy she grasped and hurriedly opened the box. Seizing the pearls with eager fingers she caressed them as she would a child, but of a sudden a strange change took place. Happiness, gladness and exuberant joy, once animate, lay as the dust of the dead in a shroud of disappointment, dismay, and fond hopes destroyed. "Why, these are not my Sister Pearls! These are not my Sister Pearls! I could tell my children in the dark. From this string of pearls there comes no response of reciprocal love. Let me take them to the window." Carefully examining the pearls by the light of the sun, she exclaimed: "Why, this is the string that was once substituted for my own pearls. What a trick fate chooses to play with me! Is it because I am over much occupied with material things?" 

The elderly man was only momentarily puzzled, for quickly catching the light he said, "May I ask, Miss 

Arnold, where you keep your pearls?" "Yes, if you will come with me, I'll be glad to show

OPPOSITES 

you. Somehow I have confidence in you. Come with me. 99 He followed her to the room which contained the secret box. As he stood at the opposite end of the room she opened a door in the wall and withdrew an iron box, lifted its lid and with an exclamation expressing further surprise she thrust her hand into the metal container and withdrew therefrom a plush box similar to the one handed her by the elderly man. Fairly running to his side, she opened the new found box as she ran. Scarcely had she the pearls in her hands before she exulted in excited fashion: "These are my Sister Pearls! I can tell with the very first touch! My dear Sister Pearls, safe, safe, safe, but how they came here I cannot understand."

The elderly man thought he knew, but all he said was: "Fate has been kind to you, Miss Arnold. Fate may have played tricks, but in the ultimate fate has been kind to you. Fate usually is kind to the deserving." 

Intensely excited and void of speech, Wesley Arnold sank limply into a softly cushioned chair. And while this tall, elderly man stood waiting for the over wrought woman's relaxing, his fixed gaze was unceasingly upon her. Probably it was the warmth of his eyes that broke her silent spell, for in kindly tones she apologized: "You must pardon my impoliteness. I do not believe I have expressed the slightest degree of gratitude. You brought me the pearls, and I have failed in ordinary courtesy as a reward for your kindness. I have not even invited you to a chair." "I cannot blame you. You have been laboring under anxious suspense. Your nerves have been greatly overtaxed. Regardless of all that, there is no necessity for me to remain here longer. In my work there are just a very few things to be done, or I may say there are but a very few different classifications of work to be

OPPOSITES 

431 done. One is to protect property and life; one is to bring to justice offenders against the government and the state and individuals; the other is to recover things lost or stolen. In the case of the pearls, which belongs to the last mentioned class, there is no further need for me. The ones most guilty have been meted a full measure of punishment; all those implicated have been sufficiently punished, and none of them will ever annoy you again. Your property has been returned to you.

I am glad to have been of some service--at least, I thought I was-but fate returned your pearls, not I. I must bid you good morning, Miss Arnold. I have another appointment that I must keep before noon." "Somehow," she said, "I should like to talk with you again. It is not often that I care to talk with anyone, especially with men. I hope you will not think me forward, but I hope you will call again." "I promise you that, Miss Arnold," and with nothing further than formal parting remarks he was on his way to the village.

CHAPTER LXVII 

Instead of proceeding directly to "Lookout House", he stopped at Doogle's office. "I was expecting you, sir," was the greeting of 

Doogle, or Douglas, as Miss Wonder had called him. "Just why were you expecting me, my good man?” "Well, that is easy enough. It was plain to you that the lady and I were acquainted. Doubtless, you want to know all about her that is possible to learn. You were aware that in some way she was mixed up in that bad mess in the shack. Now, first of all, I don't know anything about her connection with that affair, but knowing her as I did years ago I don't believe she would willingly harm anybody or anything.' "Was she a close friend of yours?" "Well, I should say she was." While his words did not fully convey the intensity of their promptings, the elderly man was satisfied that friendship did not include all that was in the little fellow's heart. "Isn't there anything else you can tell me about this lady?" "Yes, but nothing that would be of any service to you.' 99 "Well, I thank you for this much. Good bye. I must be on my way." 

From the little fellow's office he proceeded to "Lookout House", where he found Miss Wonder awaiting his coming. "I have thought it best," he explained, after the customary exchange of greetings, "not to remain here for our conversation. I have engaged an automobile to 432

OPPOSITES 

433 take us to a spot where we can converse unmolested. 

When you are ready we shall proceed."

Miss Wonder returned to her room, hastily prepared for the unexpected motor ride, and was soon seated at the side of the elderly man on their way to the top of one of the neighboring mountains. Arriving there, they entered an observation tower which was little used at that season of the year. They did not ascend to the top of the tower, but sat on a bench just inside of the door where they might observe the approach of any unwelcome additions to their party. The elderly man instructed the chauffeur to drive off a short distance and to remain there, subject to call. "Miss Wonder, I want you to acquaint me with all information you possess regarding the matter of these pearls." "You suspect me, don't you? If you do, then you are seeking a confession from me. Do I make myself plain?" "This is my answer to your question, Miss Wonder. 

Miss Arnold has her Sister Pearls. Is that sufficient?" "You took them to her?" "I took a string of pearls to her. Her Sister Pearls were just where they should have been." “When did you learn that?" "When I took the substituted string to her." "Then she is aware that her real pearls are not lost?" "Yes, she is now cognizant of that, just as you and I are. The fact that they were in her possession when 

I called at her home clearly indicates that you had full knowledge of the whereabouts of the Sister Pearls before I informed you of same, and the ownership of such knowledge opens the way for you to tell me still more about this case.' "Why do you want to know?" "I have at least two reasons; one is that I have an

OPPOSITES 

official report to make and it will be to my credit to present a report such as will be as nearly complete as possible under existing circumstances. 

I assure you that from the fact that the pearls, the genuine pearls were actually in Miss Arnold's possession at the time the three men died, I am persuaded to do what I have never done before, and that is to make a report not to include some known facts in the case. Do you understand me?" "Yes, but what good can come ?" "Possibly no good." "Well, then, why insist that I talk?" "There are some things in connection with this case of which I certainly have no knowledge. I want you to help me make my report such as will do me credit, even though I do fail to explain how the Sister Pearls happened to be just where they should be." "What is your other reason for inquiring?" "In my official capacity, I cannot explain why." "Then you should not be so insistent that I tell you something that will not benefit you." "Miss Wonder, I do not wish to be severe; as a matter of fact, I want to be just as easy and kind with you as I possibly can. I have promised you immunity. 

All I ask is that you give me your own history and your connection with this case." "And should I refuse?" "Only this, Miss Wonder. Listen carefully now to that which you compel me to say. Recently I have been reading in the newspapers of four football players on a certain college team. They are called 'The Four 

Magicians'. In plain view of eleven opponents and while a hundred thousand eyes gaze upon them from surrounding stands, those four men so manipulate a football that no one is able to discern for a certainty which of the four has possession of the ball. Also have I read of another magician; they call him in the

OPPOSITES 

435 

Holy Book 'The Great Master'. Of Him have I read that water he turned into wine. Now, in the case of the football, there are four magicians; in the case of the wine, but one; in the case which is particularly interesting to me at this time I am not quite sure just how many magicians there were, but I think there were four who had a part in turning copper into gold and turning gold into copper. Can you tell me whether four is the correct number, Miss Wonder?"

Fully convinced that he held all the cards that were worth playing, she said: "Although you have not told me your name, or your particular place in the plan of life, your knowledge of things magic and mysterious. convinces me conclusively that you must be whatever you claim to be. "My name is of no importance, Miss Wonder, not a bit more to you than the name 'Miss Wonder' is to me. 

That is not your name, so what do names matter anyhow?"

She wondered how much he really knew. "Time is passing," he said. "My uncle in Washington has large resources, but at the same time he does not wish me to spend it unnecessarily on taxicabs and chauffeurs. However, I did believe we should be here during the time which is usually occupied in consuming a noonday meal, so I came prepared. I am going to the automobile to get a little lunch I brought along. I believe on my return you are going to tell me all about yourself."

The elderly man did more than evercise his legs, as with unusually long and hurried strides he proceeded towards the car. His mind was in operation. He was thinking hard. He condemned himself for use of the gold bar threat to loose the stubborn tongue; yet, the regret was softened in his realization that he had a duty to perform in the rendering of a report truthful and correct in every detail. Further than that, para-

OPPOSITES 

doxical as it seemed, he believed he could best protect this woman from suspicion of implication in the plot of the pearls, as well as complicity in those other crimes, by knowing fully of her connection with those other cases so closely associated with the loss of Wesley Arnold's Sister Pearls. Then, too, there was the element of curiosity that urged his investigating mind to seek for some explanation as to how and why beauty, talent and culture as personified by this 'Wonder Woman' could be associated with and cooperate in crime with men of the type he knew at least two of her confederates to be. At length, still in cogitation, he reached and removed from the automobile a very generous and appetizingly packed box, from which he removed and gave to the chauffeur a portion of its contents, carrying the balance to the observation tower. "I know, Miss Wonder, you are going to tell me all about yourself. However, I suggest that you postpone the telling until we have finished our lunch. I believe a woman as well as a man is most easily reachable through a satisfied stomach." "Yes, sometimes, probably most times, but I have been observing the contents of that luncheon box, and I have been also told that overeating oft times makes the mind sluggish and dull, in which case it might be just as well or even better if I were to talk as we enjoy the viands your thoughtfulness has provided."

Unknown, of course, to her, this was all in accord with his desires. He was satisfied that she was going to help him, but he wanted her story, if possible, to be voluntarily told. Long before and on numerous occasions experience had convinced him that women of her type, even when yielding, still prefer to believe they are having their own way, to a certain extent, at least, and that likewise they simply must exercise the contrary part of their natures.

Fortunately for the success of the man's plans, the

OPPOSITES 

437 woman did not read the mind of her companion. Had she been aware that his ardent desire of that day was that she should tell her story at the earliest possible moment, quite naturally she might have delayed the telling, but his suggestion that she withhold her tale for a time had aroused the counter-action of her femininity, and, as all the while she flattered her independence in that she was having her own way, quite true it was that by the tactful diplomacy of the man she was subservient to his will. "How far back do you wish me to go?" she asked. "Just as far as you are willing." Another master stroke. He knew she would not start just at the particular time he might suggest. "Well, I might as well show myself in some good light, so I am going to start with my early girlhoodthe days of my innocence. Just where I was born and where I spent the first days of my life can be of no interest to you. Sufficient be it to say that the earliest days of which I have recollection were spent in a Pennsylvania town. I wish I had never grown up. I with four sisters and two brothers lived there with my father and mother. Most of them have passed away. One brother is in California; one sister is living. She is married, quite happily married, and it is because of that happiness I desire so much that who I am should not be made known to the world at large. But I am speaking of the present now. I must go back to my starting point. "Father had a very good position and we had all the comforts and a few luxuries of those days, just enough of the luxuries to create a desire for more. I was a normal, healthy girl; in fact, I do not believe I ever have been sick. I loved the sunshine and the air of the great outdoors; so well did I love the vast unrestrained openness and the things by nature given that many times in the forgetfulness of a contented mind I was

OPPOSITES 

oblivious of eatingtime, and thereby missed my meals. 

It may seem strange, but it was the games of the boys that I loved to play. I seldom played jacks, and dolls were silly things to me. With the boys I spun tops, shot marbles, ran races and swam in the nearby river; 

I even played baseball and I threw a ball with as much freedom of motion as any boy in the neighborhood.

I was a happy kid. I lived in the warmth of the sunshine, and with vigorous enthusiasm inhaled the wholesome air. I played and slept and slept and played. "Of course, I attended school, although I'll confess for me in the doing of the same there was an absence of delight. Nevertheless, my clear brain made studying far short of a hardship, and while I was not enthusiastically keen for school it never became a bore. All the world seemed gay, and I was happy and well filled with contentment. "Then came melancholy days; Father was taken ill and in a short time passed on to that land where 

Mother said men like Father would be happy. I did not understand then why anyone should need to go to another land to be happy, for I was happy in that little 

Pennsylvania town. When Father died, of course, the income ceased. He had some life insurance, but there was little cash in his bank account. It took lots of money for a mother and seven children, so Mother sold the old home and moved back to the place of her childhood, and where her mother continued to live. 

They called it a village, but really it was little other than a community of farms. Mother explained how living expenses could be reduced by taking up our abode in that rural place, where we could raise much of our own food in the large garden, also that we could have our own cow. "So it came to pass that we left the town where I had spent the happy time of my early girlhood days. The night before leaving Mother took me on her knee and

OPPOSITES 

439 told me I could play just the same in the little village; in fact, she said there was much more open space for me to enjoy. Yet, when it came time to go, there was just a little something in my heart that made me very sad; a lump in my throat would not let me say

I was glad. I wanted to be with Mother and my sisters and my brothers, and yet—yet, I did not want to go. "I was not old enough to have love for anyone of the opposite sex; but, yet, there was one boy to whom 

I was strangely attached. I cared a lot for that boy. 

When I told him I was to move away, oh, so many miles away, it seemed to me he was sorry too that we had had our last play together. Yes, he and I had played together, had shot marbles from the same ring, had swum together in the same river, had climbed the same cherry trees, and it was his jack-knife that had carved my initials in the trunk of a big oak tree. "When the day came to leave the place of my childhood's fondest memories, the boy's grandmother and he accompanied our family to the railroad station. His grandmother went because he was too small to return from the station alone. She was his mother, and had been since his real mother had died some years before. 

And then as the train was almost ready to start, and as I was about to ascend the steps of the car which was to take me so far away from that little boy, I do not know just why, but I kissed him good bye. Neither of us was twelve, but I liked that little boy; I believed that he liked me, and so in just that way we two children parted. I did not see him again until quite some years later when I returned to the old town, and in some way which I need not explain I met him there. 

He was a full grown youth when next I saw him! I might say he had become a young man. He had grown sturdy and strong. While I remained in the town we were in the company of each other quite a bit. That

OPPOSITES 

childish affection of the other days had faded and passed away, but in its place had come to me a strong admiration for the grown-up boy; in fact, at the time 

I believed love for him had found a place in my heart, and I am quite sure even now that had I remained in that dear old town my childhood chum would have been my pal of today. "Then there came a day when I went back to my village home, when we-he and I—once more parted at the station, the same old station where we had parted before. I promised to write to him. This time he carried my big bag into the car and found a seat for me, and then, as the signal was given, indicating that the train was about to start, he kissed me full upon the lips, ran, and as the train moved he dropped from the car, and as the train left the old town behind I saw him standing on the station platform with his gaze following departing me; and I watched him there as we pulled away, watched until it was no longer possible for human vision his outlines to discern. "I went back to my village home, firm in the belief that I could plainly and clearly read my future time, and that in my days as yet unborn great stores of happiness were held. Yes, that was my dream, but it was all a flimsy, vacant sort of dream-just a bubble, light as air-fascinating in the delicacy of its hues and shades; glistening and bright in the light of the sun, and then at the moment of its greatest glory it vanished into vapor, just faded away, and all that was left me was a world, drab and gray. Yes, just a dream—a very unreal dream—for I learned long ago that all of my life's happiness was spent in that little old town of my childhood days. "But I digress. Arriving home I immediately wrote to him; yes, my letter was in the next eastward bound mail. He promptly replied, and for a long while

OPPOSITES 

441 letters were exchanged with great regularity and exceeding frequency. "Before proceeding further with this drama of my own life, for a drama of many acts it has been, with each act a play unto itself, and each play just another tragedy, now that I have the stage well set for you to review my play, I'll present to you quite an important character in my cast; yes ere I proceed with this exclusive and the first presentation of 'My Tragedy', there is a very important character in the play of whom you should know-a country boy, born and reared in my home village. I won't describe him, at least, not yet. However, there is one thing I must tell, because it has an important bearing on my story. 

This boy seemed fond of me, and I liked him too not a little. He belonged to that type of likeable fellows constantly getting into scrapes of a more or less nasty sort, and yet in spite of it all nevertheless likeable fellows. Well, this boy simply could not keep out of trouble. Every time he walked up the street he met trouble coming down, and soon they were arm in arm, traveling in the same direction. Oh, he was so easily influenced. One of those you know who when in certain company would do things one day that they would occupy a week in regretting; one of those boys who when with the gang would not hesitate to rob an apple orchard. I well recall one particular escapade. 

Homeward bound from a church social one night, he came upon a group of occasional companions on their way to pull a trick, as they termed it. Easily led, he was soon just one of the others. Well, what they did, while wrong, was not such a grievous crime in itself. 

It was only upon whom it was perpetrated. Like most small towns, it had one big man, the squire, president of the volunteer firemen, president of the one bank in town, proprietor of the general store, trustee of the largest church, could have been burgess or in

OPPOSITES 

fact anything within the gift of the village voters might have been his for the asking. He owned that town, and that town well knew it was owned, and by whom it was owned. But this man found it personally preferable to own each office holder than to own an office himself. Well, it so happened that this proprietor of the village and all that therein lay, likewise was the father of a daughter who had gone away to college and while there met a son of some wealthy man, the outcome of which was they were married. As it so happened, the wedding night was the same on which this young man on his way home from church fell in with the fellows on their way to pull their so-called trick, which was to reduce the stock of refreshments provided for the wedding of the big man's daughter. Of course, while really wrong, it was only a prank, the extent of the depredation being one tub of ice cream. Somehow it became known that this unfortunate boy was one of the pillagers of the ice cream, although when the story reached my ears my friend was the leader of a band of murderous marauders who had burglarized the big man's home and at the point of threatening guns had seized and carried away thousands of dollars' worth of precious wedding gifts. However, with it all, the big man was too much of a practical politician to do other than make himself still more secure in his hold on the voters. He laughed off the thing, said: "Boys will be boys," and his hold on the town was still stronger. But my boy friend had lost once more in the town's estimation. On this occasion I expostulated just as I had repeatedly done in the past. He seemed most sorry, and I believe he was at the time. Following each escapade, he would promise me severance from the gang, and for a while would hold to his promise, but only for a while until one more promise was broken. He never worked at one place

OPPOSITES 

443 any great length of time, just could not or would not hold a job. "Then one night, following a period of probation, he proposed marriage to me. Frankly I told him that

I could not even seriously consider such a thought. 

While not withholding from him the fact that I was fond of him, still I minced no words to make plain my opinion to the effect that he never had demonstrated in any measurable degree his inclination or ability to make anyone a good husband. My reply to his proposal became what might be termed a lecture. Among other things I explained that to be a husband he must be able to support a home; he must obtain and hold a position; that he must keep good company; keep out of scrapes; and a lot of other good advice I gave him. "And there was still another very good reason why on my return to the village I did not encourage him, and that was my firm belief that I had left my heart with another back in the old, old town, but on the other hand I did attend parties with him and he did call to see me at my home. "One night he told me he had secured a position on the railroad, and that he was determined to make good, determined for my sake, he said. I encouraged his 'making good', but tried to be careful not to encourage him in the belief that I could ever marry him.

I did not believe I cared sufficiently for him to be his wife; and then, too, there was that other young man for whom I cared more, very much more, and with a different sort of a caring. I made him promise that regardless of what happened, so far as I was concerned, that he would hold his job. I tried to tell him. of the young man back in the old town, but to that subject he paid little attention. "Giving him full credit, he did take the position. and appeared quite anxious to hold it. As a matter of fact, he seemed to be making good, for which I

OPPOSITES 

was glad, but fearful that he would renew his urgings that I marry him, which one evening he did. I was then convinced that there was but one way to discourage any such hopes that he possessed; so it was that I told him of having invited the young man from the old town to visit me. In spite of his evident displeasure, he promised to hold his position and continue in the path of honest toil. "For some reason the young man from the old town did not come to visit me. I thought for a long time I knew why, but quite recently I was convinced that my former belief was not well founded. This encouraged the young man of the village, and he more frequently and more earnestly urged me to marry him, but still I refused. Although the other young man failed to visit me, for a time I continued to receive letters from him. "Then there came a day and with it came a letter, and with it also a change in my whole life. The letter explained that the young man in the old town could not write me again. Regarding the contents of that letter there is no use of going into details. It merely meant that all was off-that my fondest dreams were -oh, but why repeat all that? I have already bored you with the story of my dream of dreams, of my pretty, vanishing bubble, but, look! do you see that leaf as it falls from its tree? And do you see those others that still cling to the limbs? Well, for a time my hopes were as mountain leaves, once green-the green of life, of a life full of hope for happiness, peace, joy; then came the changing of the green, and in its stead the glorious shades of the autumn time; proud leaves gorgeously robed to feast at the banquet of realized hopes, and then, well, and then came the chilling breath of death to still the hopes and stay the faith, as leaves, once green, now garbed in dirty saffron coats, dropped lifelessly to gloomy earth, where musty, cracking, ugly

OPPOSITES 

445 things they became to lie midst muggy stuff; yes, lie, decay and rot, disintegrate to dust. This dissolution of life's hopes arrived one dingy afternoon. The young man from the village called that night to find me more than sorely distressed. Naturally he sought an explanation, but even persistent seeking and seeking availed him naught. Instead of his expected displeasure, he once again urged me to become his wife. I was in the mood to do almost anything, whether foolish, reckless, or of a desperate sort. What I did do was to go away with him that very night. It required much persuasion on his part, but, nevertheless, I went. To get a marriage license the day was too far spent, but with him I traveled that very night into the State of Ohio. While traveling I had opportunity for consideration and reconsideration. I could not well go back, but at the same time I determined not to marry him. I knew marriage with him would be a mistake, and that neither of us would be happy, but, well, I stayed with him. He kept his position, which was such that he could retain it, even though changing his residence. He assumed a new name in the city to which we traveled, and, of course, I changed mine. Letters from mother I received by general delivery. "Then one day I grew sick of it all. I had not seen my mother since I had gone away. I had let her hear from me. I wrote her stating that I had gone away on account of the letter from the boy in the old town. 

I told her I could not bear to face the people in the village, who had been led to believe that I was going to marry this young man who had severed his relations with me. I told her that I had secured employment in the Ohio city. She believed that I had gone away alone, and I never told her otherwise. "One day she wrote that her health was failing, and pleaded that I return to our home. She was all alone, she said, and told of deaths in the family; of a brother

OPPOSITES 

marrying and going away. I was tired and disgusted with it all anyway, and so I resolved to go back to Mother. During all this time my companion had kept his position on the railroad. That was easily arranged, as his duties carried him into the city of our Ohio home. "Doubtless, he was sorry for my leaving. He so expressed himself, and urged me to some time return to him as his lawfully wedded wife. Unable to bring myself to the point of resuming my residence in the old village, I induced mother to move to another town, where I secured employment with a manufacturing concern. It was while there employed I met Douglas. "What I am now about to relate will doubtless seem odd to you. I know I am beautiful, and that Douglas is not handsome; I know he is not robust like other men, and, yet, within his flat, narrow chest there beats a heart so kind and brave that somehow, impossible as it may seem, I grew to love him. I had been very fond of that boy friend of mine back in the old town, but after I met Douglas I knew I had not really loved the other. I think all would have gone well, and that eventually I should have married Douglas, had not that unfair thing that sees the two sexes in different lights, that unfair, unjust thing that sets two standards for male and female; had not that most unfair thing seen fit to enter into and wreck my life. Somehow, there came to the attention of my employer and Douglas's employer too-for we worked at the same place—the story of my past. My old village companion in some way learned of this, and was fair enough to send me word. "One night Douglas proposed marriage to me. Because of the disclosure of my past, I could not accept him. That same night Mother died. "I left my home, leaving word for Douglas that I could never see him again. I had intended seeing no

OPPOSITES 

447 one that knew me, but on the train that was to carry me away I met my old village companion. Discouraged because of our separation, and having fallen in with bad company, again he had done something which, if made known he had a part in it, would have sent him to jail. He had been fair with me, and I wanted to help him, so, well, so I just stayed with him to keep him straight. It had seemed for a time that he was unable to hold permanent employment, and as he had lost much of his interest in trying to do right it was not long before the influence of his companions had drawn him into a number of things that were not honest.

After a while he was able to secure another position on the railroad. There were at least two reasons for his desire to have employment on that railroad; one was that it did produce an income; the other was that it enabled him to carry out some of the nefarious schemes and plots of his companions. I never saw them—unless- 

"Unless they were the men in the tree whom you saw looking through Miss Arnold's library window,' interrupted the elderly man. "Yes, unless those were the men. "Time and again I tried to break away from the life, but having once gone down the steps, it seems so utterly impossible to climb a ladder. You may throw mud into water and water is no longer pure, but you cannot throw water into mud and make of it a pure liquid. When once one's down, the world has no inclination to lift one up. "And so it was that our crimes, for crimes they were, became worse in character and more bold in what was attempted. I might as well tell you those other men were expert counterfeiters, and Terry-I might just as well tell you that too now, because he is dead-Terry -his name was Terrence Griffith carried the money between those others and me, for I had means for its

OPPOSITES 

circulation. Each had a part, as you seem well to know, in the copper-gold deal. I had my part; there is no use, however, to go into details concerning that, is there?" "I think not," he said. "I believe I already know." "Then came what was to be known as 'the big deal'. 

Our profits from that were to be enormous. I had promised myself and Terry had given me his pledge that the pearl deal, or as we sometimes termed it 'The Plot of the Pearls', would be the last; that we would take the spoils and go away, and thereafter live honest lives. Rather paradoxical, wasn't it?—Living honest lives on the fruits of ill-gotten gains? Part of that big deal went through, the minor part, but then the substitution of the manufactured pearls for the valuable string possessed by Miss Arnold was to be the culmination of it all. It might have been easy, but that 

Miss Arnold was such a noble character and was so kind and friendly to me that to betray her confidence was the most difficult undertaking of my whole life." "You need not go into that, Miss Wonder. She does not know and I do not intend that she ever shall know that you had any part in the crime. Is there anything more you have to tell me?" "I think not," she said. "If I were you, I should go to Douglas. He wants you." "I am not worthy of him." "Is it not true that he does know a part of your sad story?—and that in spite of that knowledge he still wants you?" "Yes, but he does not know all. He does not know the worst of me." "I think he wants you just the same."

She smiled. It was a sad smile. "That was a mistake of the telegraph operator. Terry called me his 'Queen' when we were alone, although never in public. That wire should have read 'Queen' and not 'Quesne'. Another term of affection he had for me was 'Old Girl'.

He had a way of referring to me as his 'Queen' when using the third person, but never in the presence of others." "Then tell me this: Who was 'The Mist' ?" 'The Mist' was a detective of the Secret Service in Washington. I learned in a way that I cannot reveal to you that he had been engaged on our case or rather cases. He was not a regular attaché of the Secret 

Service, but on account of his rare knowledge he was engaged by that department on special cases. I had a suspicion once as to his identity. I am not sure yet that I was wrong."

"Then you still retained a friendly feeling for him?” "Yes, I did, in spite of everything.' 

"Did you have more affection for him than for Douglas?" "No. There was a time when I thought I cared more for him than I could ever possibly care for anyone else, but I know now it was not real love. He was younger than Douglas; he was stronger; better educated; he was polished and cultured, while Douglas is not. He had wealth and the means of acquiring more, and, yet, while once he could have married me for the asking, I know now there is only one man who holds my true love, and that is the little man of the chapel. Mother used to tell me I should have admirers because I was beautiful, but that I should be careful not to marry until Mr. Right came along. She said that she believed there was just one man for every woman, and that the world was being turned topsy-turvey simply because men and women refused to wait until the union intended by nature had been formed. I believe her now; 

I know she was right. I have paid a big price to find it out, and what is even worse than paying the price is the knowledge that now I cannot take the hand of the man who is my proper opposite.' 99 "Where and when in Mount Hope did you last see your boyhood friend?" Then this was her reward for waiting. "Miss Wonder' is dead. She died the same night that the other three, failing in their magic, passed over the black line. Leonore Shelby is going to marry Douglas tonight. She is going to be the wife of the man she loves."

Leonore Shelby wept-wept like as one who experiences the passing of a heavy burden of unrest. And without a word and with absence of action on his part he allowed her, uninterrupted, to silently and softly weep until, lifting her head and gazing through the moisture of a soul relieved, she said: "You are a good man. In the kind of life that has been mine for the past few years I had been led to believe that the men of the Secret Service were heartless and without souls. 

If that belief be correct, you are in the wrong place.

Immediately they were on their way to the chapel. 

As they rode she said: "You have a right to ask anything you wish regarding 'Miss Wonder'. She is dead, but before interment I shall be glad to reply to any question that may assist you to make clear any clouded part of your almost complete solution of Miss Wonder's part in the various manipulations of the four magicians." "Thank you. Tell me then who was T. S. G. and who was Hen?" "You are not asking much. T. S. G. is the brave boy whose life was given that he might return to Miss 

Wonder the pearls. T. S. G.-Terrence S. Griffith, christened Stanley G. Terry. Hen was a mistake of the telegraph operator, just the same as was Quesne. 

I sometimes called him Honey or Hon. Is that much clear ?" "Quite so. Now, what can you or will you tell me of Terry or Hon or Stanley G. Terry?" 

"I am quite sure you know a great deal already. 

Just let me say that had his environment been different, he would have been a credit to any community. Weak under pressure of influence, he lived the life of an undesirable citizen, but he died the death of a brave and honest man. Briefly, his history was a struggle against temptation. Resistance to wrong impulses was gradually weakened until it was worn down and worn out.

OPPOSITES 

453 I have outlined to you my early association with him, followed by my first serious mistake in life. I have also explained our separation and his honorable desire towards me at that time; you are aware that he was still interested in my welfare, following my securing of employment in a certain manufacturing plant. That is evidenced by your inquiry as to the identity of 'T. S. G.'

I never received but one letter from him with that signature. I have already told you of rejoining forces with him. He made one supreme effort to go straight.

He was blessed with natural musical talent and the ability to impart his knowledge to others. He had a soul overflowing with music. It was most difficult for him to refrain from humming or whistling. Even when in conversation it seemed he required a tremendous amount of self control to restrain a whistle. His supreme effort to live rightfully and to be of service to others was in associating himself with an evangelist and taking charge of the musical part of the campaigns.

After a time he fell once more, and that was his last effort to go straight. Had I been in contact with him in those days, I might have been of some help to him, but unfortunately that was while I was at the manufacturing plant. He came to Mount Hope especially to be near me and to do his part in the big deal or the 'pearl plot'. At one time he nearly lost his life in a railroad wreck and was incapacitated for months.

Terry was just one of those unfortunates who could not be the honest man he desired to be, but with it all he died in the cause of the right." 

She paused and drew a deep breath. Before she had a chance to say more the car stopped in front of the chapel, and there bathing in the floodlight of the setting sun stood Douglas, evidently awaiting their coming. Alighting from the automobile, the elderly man dismissed the chauffeur with a generous fee, and then

OPPOSITES 

taking Leonore by the hand he guided her straight to her lover. "My friend," he said, "I have brought you the woman you want and need." "Come inside," said Douglas.

They entered the chapel. "Have you really come?" he said. "I have come," she said. "I have come; I am here with all my past, of which you know only a part, undoubtedly the best part." "Martha, in my sight you are always good." 

From the elderly man in surprise came the query: "Why do you call her 'Martha'? Is not her name

Quickly came her explanation: "My name is Leonore 

Martha. When I went back to Mother I used Martha.

I wished to change all possible of the life preceding my return. It was easy for Mother to address me as 'Martha'; she had always wanted me called by that name, but preferring Leonore myself I selfishly insisted that I be so known." The Big Spirit long ago intended you for my opposite. I cannot oppose nature, although I believe I should oppose nature if nature opposed my having you."

The elderly one taking them, those two, that woman and that man, both by their right hands, clasped those two hands firmly together in his own huge right one; then, with his left raised high in the air, upon them in tones full of feeling he pronounced his benediction: "May the blessing of Heaven and nature be upon you both now and evermore." Lowering his hand he fervently said: "You have my every good wish," and

OPPOSITES 

455 before they could do other than continue hand in hand and follow with wondering and effectionate gaze his rapidly disappearing form, he was gone. 

Within a very few days Mount Hope was much agitated to learn that Little Doogle had closed his chapel and moved away, and that with him had gone Miss Wonder, his beautiful bride. With them they carried the good wishes of all the Mount Hope folk, and Mrs. Douglas carried with her a beautiful string of pearls, a gift of the elderly man.

CHAPTER LXVIII 

Returning from the railroad station, following his "Bon Voyage" to Doogle and his bride, who had departed on a late afternoon train, our elderly friend had seated himself on the porch of Kenneth Inn, where he hoped he might encounter Pinky, and was rather surprised and much disappointed in his failure to do so. 

He had neither seen nor heard from him since the night of the tragedy in the shack, following which during the ensuing days he had neither time nor opportunity to seek him, nor was it of the utmost importance that he should, but, nevertheless, there were several little ends of string he was anxious should be tied together, in order that he should know for a certainty whether there were still others than the four magicians implicated in the 'Plot of the Pearls', the solving of which had been one of the things that had brought him to Mount Hope. Several of Pinky's remarks had led him to believe that the latter might possess knowledge of something as yet unrevealed as well as necessary to complete the chain which he was so anxious should want for no missing link.

In his quest for Pinky he chose the dinner hour as the most likely time to find him at the Inn. Rather than inquire for him at the desk, he preferred to choose a favorable location on the porch from which to watch for his appearing. Should this plan fail, he must try some other way to find him. He felt that Pinky was still stopping at the Inn, believing that he would not have departed from Mount Hope without first seeing him. From a vantage spot commanding a view of all who approached or departed from the hotel, our friend 456

OPPOSITES 

457 patiently awaited the appearance of the man who had rendered him such valuable assistance on the night of the tragedy. 

Following noon time the sun had been bright and warm, but as its daily vigil over Mount Hope had come to a close, and it had gone to brighten another part of the world, the mountain chill of autumn twilight time was in the air, and had forced all others inside the hotel, leaving alone our elderly appearing friend who in anticipation of just such a contingency was warmly dressed. It might have been that he really enjoyed the sudden drop in temperature, but it is more likely that his thoughts were so engrossed with other things that the sinking thermometer impressed him little or not at all.

Just inside of the hotel near the look-out of the elderly appearing man was an alcove. Evidently dinner was over because from the lobby there came the murmur of many voices, and there could be observed a few of the guests descending the steps to the main driveway. These latter the lone man watched closely, for he was quite anxious that Pinky should not escape his vision.

It was while thus engaged that he heard voices emanating from the alcove. Someone had raised the window overlooking the porch near where the elderly man sat, to which window raising another raised oral objection, to which protestation the one who had been guilty of admitting the fresh air retorted: "It is so stuffy in here. It has been warm and sticky all day, and now although it is rather chilly outdoors the logs in these open fireplaces have made it entirely too warm for comfort inside the Inn, and don't you know, Mother, there are more people dying every year from too little air than from too much?"

Although the daughter's remark gave a finality to the discussion of window raising, still our elderly friend

OPPOSITES 

was soon aware of the nearness of additional voices just within the window near which he was sitting, likewise was he aware that all of said voices were of feminine tone. Oft on previous occasions had he enjoyed hearing conversations for which unseen persons were responsible. Guided by their views as verbally expressed, he liked to picture each participant as painted by his brush of imagination. 

Said one voice: "I believe this is the last time I shall ever come to Mount Hope. Everything used to be so clean here, but not any more. 99 "Why, Mother, the house is just as clean as ever. 

As hotels go, it is spotless." "Oh, Daughter, you are too young to understand that I am not referring to physical cleanness. I have in mind the atmosphere." "Well, even so, what could be cleaner than this atmosphere?" "Oh, if you girls would only occupy your minds in studying the books from which we of our generation derived our valuable knowledge, you would understand that I do not mean the air when I refer to atmosphere. 

Probably I can better explain it as a moral atmosphere." 99 "I imagine you could better explain it, Mother, by saying 'the present environment of Mount Hope'.' "There you are, correcting your mother again?" "No, I was not correcting you. I was trying to understand what meaning you intended to convey. Well, be it as it may, I am going to shift atmospheres; I am going off into the far corner and borrow a cigarette from one of those boys, and do my bit in clouding the atmosphere.' 

Following a chorus of shocked "Ohs!", the mother hastened to explain to the others that her daughter was not serious in her remark and that she was simply trying to tease Mother regarding the cigarette.

OPPOSITES 

459 "I hope it is a smokeless jest. 'Where there is smoke,' you know," said another. "Girls of the present are not as they were in our younger days, you know." 

The mother's voice was resumed: "No, and I repeat 

I am not coming back to Mount Hope." 

A new voice said: "I believe you will find all resorts just the same, no better, no worse, and whatever they are the visitors make them so." 

The mother's voice replied: "It was a mistake for me to come here at all this year. I really should not have gone anywhere. I actually feel like a base sinner, having deserted my dear church back home. But, then, you see, our dear pastor, Doctor Browning, went abroad this summer. He is returning next week. In his absence I positively could not cooperate with any other leaders in the church; they are all so jealous of my standing with Dr. Browning who values my opinions so highly. So when he went abroad, while I quite well knew the church should have someone to look after its spiritual welfare, I concluded after much prayer that rather than have dissension it would be better for me to absent myself. Of course, it is going to be that much harder for the doctor and me when we return, because I am quite well aware that some of our best laid plans will be all upset, and designedly so, too. 

However, the doctor and I, with the help of the Lord, will very soon get things running in their usual systematic way again. We simply must do it, we two. We have so little help from the others."

Although the elderly man kept his eyes well fixed on the doorway of the hotel, his active mind was picturing the speaker within. He visualized her as a woman with two narrow slits just beneath her brow, only sufficiently wide to show two tiny black beads; below and between the slits was a perfect triangle for want of a better name termed a nose; mouth small and tight closed, except when exercised; lips as thin as an imaginary line;

OPPOSITES 

chin sharp and running to a point. Her smile, if and when, a pitiable effort, straining lips, cheeks slightly elevated, no joy in those slits still tighter closed, and with even less mirth than that of the mournful and melancholy mummy of a deserted village. Her sigh told him of her self satisfaction.

Of the daughter he had not heard sufficient to paint a mind's picture, but he was quite satisfied that the girl thoroughly understood her mother. 

Said one of the others: "I rather like this place. To those of us who are of the observing sort the spice of its scandal makes our stay here quite interesting. To those most active in its scandalous happenings it is only occasionally of satisfied and pleasing interest, for their interest is lost in the heat of the spice, actual contact with which leaves them burnt scars for their pains, but to those having no part in stirring its dirt, it is for them simply to stop, look and listen, as the spice all the while brings to their nostrils a sweet smelling savour." "And likewise 'tis true, my dear," said a third, "that oft times dormant scandal, being stirred, riseth and stinketh unto Heaven." 

A fourth inquired: "Now, just what is the latest thing in scandal? I do so want to keep up-to-date." "Well, I really don't know just what is the latest," said the self important mother. "As a matter of fact, I am not aware whether you know all the happenings of the early summer.' 

Interposed another: "I am sure I do not. I did not come till the summer was over." 

From the motherly one: "Well, I really think you should be informed as to at least some of these horrible happenings. You might accidentally be thrown into contact with some of these most ordinary persons. 

Now, first, I must tell you something of a very young girl. She was secretary to a very nice, wealthy man-

OPPOSITES 

461 At this point a voice interjected: "Well, who were they? Tell us their names.' 99 "I really do not think I should. One must be careful when mentioning real names. You know oft times such persons, especially adventuresses, take highly respectable persons into court, and the juries of today are so vulgar. What chance would you or I or any one of us have in the courts of today? The juries like the girl of the flapper type; with dress to her knees, and with her eyes playing full on the twelve who are to render a verdict, we of the eminently respectable have no hope for justice. Respectability such as we represent would seem drab and of no interest to the juries of this decade. No, we cannot mention names, but I really think for your own welfare you should have some knowledge as to just who some of these persons are, so if you will listen attentively you may be able to gain possession of some valuable clues as to identity. "So, if there are no more interruptions," she resumed, with a resounding snap of her jaw, "I shall endeavor to finish telling of this insolent girl who has deigned to thrust herself into the respectability hitherto maintained untarnished by the visitors of Mount Hope.

This girl's reputation it seems was not of the best before she met her employer. However, by the wiles that such beings use she wove herself into his affections and wrapped poisonous tentacles about him. Until he met her he had been a most exemplary husband. He was devoted to his wife and considered by many as the leading citizen of his community. But, then the story goes that for some reason this girl decided to come away. Maybe she was tired of the man, maybe she had her eyes on another prospective victim, maybe she had secured from the man in question everything obtainable. Anyhow, she came to Mount Hope. She keeps close to her room most of the time. That may indicate there is just a little bit of shame left in her

OPPOSITES 

composition, or she may have other reasons for desiring seclusion." "Have you ever seen her?" "No, but I know she is here. She has her meals served in her room." "And that is all you know?" "Isn't that enough? To think that the management of this hotel permits such persons to be here! Here, mind you, right under the very same roof that protects us! It is an insult to us, whose families have made this place a success. I did think of inviting Doctor Browning to come up here over the week-end;you know, he doesn't really start to preach immediately following his return-but I could not bring him here. 

He might actually touch elbows with that low woman!" "I do not see much in that story that is exciting. I am still waiting for my thrill," said another. "Is that the scandal that has smirched the good name of 

Mount Hope?" "Oh, no, that is only part of it." 

Some one yawned. "Well, if you haven't anything with a better kick to it than that, I was going off to another group. Probably they have some inside information about the things that have made the air of 

Mount Hope unfit to breathe." "Don't let us delay your departure. I have other things to tell, to which of course you are not required to listen." "May I inquire how you get all your information?" "You may inquire, but I am not going to tell. I have ways of obtaining information and learning things by means that are perfectly honorable, too. You know things of this sort are often brought unsolicited to a person beyond reproach, in order that they may be handled and treated to the best interest of a community." "Lord help a community that is placed in your care,'

OPPOSITES 

463 was the parting shot of one voice, as its owner evidently left the group. 

Pausing to recover from that last shaft, the motherly one said: "Yes, and I might tell something about her, too, but I am too much of a Christian to retaliate."

From the one who had but recently come to Mount 

Hope came the request for more scandal. "Well, then, there is another woman here. Just why she came I have not been able to fathom. She arrived without her husband, and what information I can gather leads one to believe that she is in love with another man. My source of information seems to believe she thought that the other man was here. Like the girl of whom I just told you, she, too, keeps to her room; so evidently she has not found the object of her search, and is sulking in her room because of her unrewarded quest.' 99 "Have you seen this one?" inquired one of the others. "No, but I know she is here." "Nothing very startling yet," said another. 

Quite nettled, the motherly one retorted: "Oh, I know lots, but if you are going to treat what I tell you with such contempt I must refuse to tell you any more." "Won't you tell us just a little more?" coaxed another. "Well, there was a man who arrived in Mount Hope during that terrific storm on that dark day last July.

He was injured and was taken to the hospital. After he had to some extent recovered he came to Kenneth 

Inn. They say he was married, but that he was in love with a girl much younger than himself. They say he quarreled with his wife, and then came here thinking he might find the young girl. Before he found her, in some way he was injured again. He is adorably handsome and, oh, he has such a kind face. I saw him once.

of information, I could not believe that he had ever done anything wrong. He spoke to me once.' 99 "And why did he speak to you?" burst simultaneously from the patient listeners. "Why shouldn't he? He looks like a gentleman and knows a lady when he sees one. Let us resume where I was interrupted. He spoke to me once, and I had an opportunity to study his physiognomy. It is hard to believe that he ever did anything wrong, and yet my information is so sound and so sure. I do not know whether I ought to tell you or not, you seem so incredulous and so unappreciative, but I am always forgiving someone, and I am going to forgive you. If you will allow me to continue without further interruption, I am willing to tell the rest. It seems that he was mixed up in that terrible affair out near the chapel the other night. I understand he received some sort of a wound, and is confined to his room right here in this hotel." "Which is his room?" as one person they exclaimed. "Can't we go to him?" "I'll tell you his room. It is number twenty-two, but no one is allowed to see him. I do not know what excuse any of you could frame anyhow." "Well, nothing very thrilling in that," complained one of the others. "Now, can't you give us something worth while? Tell us a real one." "I'll tell you one bit more, and if that is not to your liking you must get your information elsewhere." "Oh," said one of the others, "don't misunderstand us and don't misjudge us. Nobody has been caught doing wrong. Is not that true?” 

To this the motherly one cried: "My, how incred-

OPPOSITES 

465 ulous you are! Don't you ever believe anything unless you see it?" "I don't always believe all I see," retorted one lady who had been taking but a small part in the discussion. "Well, I am going to tell you something that to me seems mysterious. No one yet has been able to solve it. On the day of the big storm in July a man registered here and went out for a walk. He was injured in the storm, too; no one seems to know to what extent, but he was brought back to the hotel. They said that the little spiritualist down at the chapel and he were in league to promote some sort of a scheme, telling fortunes or something like that-no one seems to know just what. And then one of the bell hops caught a very beautiful woman in his room one night. They say the little spiritualist and the man quarreled about that woman, and that soon after that the man disappeared. 

Where he went no one seems to know. The man at the railroad ticket office said he had not gone out on a train. He did not have an automobile of his own, and he could not walk very far because he was badly hurt.

Some folks say that the little spiritualist had something to do with his disappearing, and that he was very jealous of him on account of that very beautiful woman. They say that she was a bad sort. I just heard a rumor today that she and the little spiritualist had been married. If that is true, it does look as though the man who disappeared did so because either the little old spiritualist or he and the woman collectively found it convenient not to have him around any more. 

He may have had some money they wanted; possibly not, but the whole thing does possess an odd aspect. 99 The talking continued, but for how long our elderly friend cannot inform us, for convinced that he should not see Pinky that night he returned to his room in the village. He was quite concerned regarding Pinky, too, for he had little doubt as to the identity of the injured
