man referred to by the virtuous mother as occupying room twenty-two. While turning over in his mind thoughts of Pinky, he had the air of a man who has visioned the accomplishment of a big undertaking.

CHAPTER LXIX 

"Glad you-all is getting all right, suh. You-all sho' did have a mighty close call, suh." 

These few, but expressive words, as a colored waiter carried the remains of a meal from a hotel bed room, through the windows of which could be observed on the far reaching canvas of near and distant mountain foliage indescribable wonders with the brush of autumn painted.

There, in delicate tints and beauteous shades, high lights and deep shadows, was such a picture as to draw unstinted words of profuse praise from the most fastidious critic of a cynical age. Gorgeous, glorious in its wealth of coloring, it was such as to bring worldly men to their knees in supplication, as in awe and solemn adoration they realized how feeble is man, and how insignificant his works, as compared to the wonders wrought by nature's art and controlled beyond the Heavens.

The remarks of the waiter were addressed to a man somewhat above average height, broad of shoulder, and deep of chest, of athletic mould, a man apparently of great strength and physical resistance, and yet, in his features indescribable lines of suffering showed; yes, at that very moment either he was temporarily indisposed or convalescent from some serious illness or bodily injury that had exacted a heavy toll. Also beyond possibility of successful argument to the contrary, some extreme mental strain that had drawn heavily upon his hitherto seemingly inexhaustible reserve had been scarce endured, for about the temples of his blondly handsome head his hair showed streaks of 467

OPPOSITES 

decided gray, a condition unusual in one of his complexion and seeming years; neither did certain deep lines belong in the features of one his age and bearing, for his was the frame of an outdoor man who had devoted much time and attention to athletics. Then, too, there was the clearness of eye that goes only with the fellow who has kept physically fit and clean. All of this was evident, in spite of the pallor's telling that for a spell of indefinite duration he had associated little or not at all with the health-giving tonic of God's free sunshine.

A smile and a slight wave of the hand was all the response accorded the waiter man, who carefully and quietly closing the door left with his tray.

The man wearily turned his eyes with a yearning look to the scene beyond his window. Then, with his face in his hands, slowly, very slowly, but, oh, so feelingly, he softly said: "Oh, man, lay aside thy palet, thy easel and thy brush. Ease thy ambition; it brings thee disappointed hopes, for at thy best thy brush falls short and cannot match the shades, the tints, the intermingling colors of the Master's touch; so bow thy head in awe and reverently worship Nature's Artist as all the while His holy brush prevails."

For a brief spell he contemplated the scene, then buried his face deep in his hands as they lay on a table before him. For a very long time he sat just like that until disturbed by a knock on his bedroom door. 

In response to his uncertain "Come in", the door opened to admit another man. As the visitor who was our elderly appearing gentleman entered the room, and before he had opportunity to address the man at the table, doors of two communicating rooms swung slowly open. In one room stood a woman of middle age, black of hair and dark of eye, of about average height; and in the other doorway, a younger woman, somewhat

OPPOSITES 

469 shorter than the first, chestnut-brown of hair and eye of hazel hue. 

Unaware of the presence of the women, the man paid undivided though silent attention to his male visitor, as though expecting the latter to address him, which our elderly friend was much too surprised and amazed to do. As though searching for explanation of a seemingly odd and embarrassing situation, the eyes of the elder man traveled from one woman to the other. 

Following this changing gaze, the man at the table was made cognizant of the presence of the other two. While giving signs of recognition, not a move did he make other than turning his head in unison with the elderly man. Then, as the three unbidden guests, embarrassed and perplexed, stood motionless and ill at ease, the center of their concentrated attention sat still and speechless, and to all appearance a very weak man. 

After what seemed an interminable time, the latter very slowly arose. Standing for a few brief spaces as though to summon his reserve forces, both mental and physical, and with a gesture refusing proffered assistance from the other man, he made it perfectly plain with a look of unalterable decision and a step although slow, was firm, that he was determined to go where he willed to go, and having so done, which was to the younger woman, he seized her two hands in both of his and stood there unsteadily swaying. 

But while he held the hands of the younger one, it was the other woman who was first to speak. "And then I am right, after all," she said. "Another has taken my place." 

At the moment resisting the attempt of the younger woman to withdraw her hands, he made no immediate reply. When at length he did speak, he said: "You are wrong, Mary. No one has taken your place. The place you have hitherto had you might

OPPOSITES 

still hold, were it not that you of your own accord went away." 99 "Yes, but what brought you here?" "I came here, following the realization that you desired a change in the order of things.' 99 "Your implication is beyond my comprehension. 

Will you kindly explain?" "I can explain, but this is hardly the proper place to do so, or, rather, the immediate conditions are not exactly favorable for such an explanation. In turn, will you explain to me just how and why without permission you enter my room.' "9" "It was through no design of mine that I was assigned to a room adjoining yours, and it was not until 

I learned that you had been seriously wounded that I determined to see if there was something I might do for you." "I do appreciate that, Mary. I thank you, but I am getting along quite nicely now and I do not believe there is a thing that you can do for me." "But am I not your wife? Is that other woman to take my place and care for you?" "The doctor is caring for me. He alone and without assistance is doing all that is necessary.' "I see. It is the end of everything," she said, and with no further word she forthwith returned to the room whence she came; then, following a brief pause, the key on her side of the door was turned, whereupon the man turning to the younger woman, said: "I am sorry this has happened; but, tell me, why did you choose to come to my room unannounced?" "I came for the same reason as that expressed by your wife. I had expected and had hoped never to see you again, but when I learned that you had been injured, and as I thought by an act of fate I had been placed in a room next to yours, I could not resist the impulse or desire to tender my assistance."

47.1 "I thank you for your good intentions, but I believe there is nothing that you can do—at least now. I shall see you again, I hope. It is best that you now return to your room.'

The young girl having immediately complied with his suggestion, the man wearily dragged himself back to his chair. Turning to the elderly appearing man, he said: "Won't you be seated?"

Accepting the belated invitation, the other replied: "I thank you. I am more than sorry for your accident, of which I had no knowledge until last night. Tell me how it happened." "Oh, it is simply nothing. Just a trifle. Had I been in my customary good condition, I could easily have thrown it off.” "But you are not telling me about it." "Well, really there isn't very much to tell. When on the night of the tragedy of the shack I chased that murderous villain, I learned that he was possessed of a spark of sentiment in his forbidding nature. Doubtless, believing it true that actions do speak louder than words, his last act preceding his departure over the cliff was to turn, and as a final farewell his endeavor to reach my heart was most forcefully touching, but his aim was a trifle faulty, and while he left with me a well marked and lasting impression of his brotherly love it was emplanted a bit removed from my center seat of affection. By the way, I retain in my trunk the token of his love and esteem, on the blade of which is quite legibly engraved 'Ralph C. Gage'." "Why did you not tell me of this the night it occurred?" "You had enough other trouble at that time; then, too, I could not delay securing the doctor to do what he could for that poor cuss who was shot; following that it was necessary to get in touch with the authorities. Anyhow, I considered my hurts trifling."

let that pass, at least for the present. I am going to take you back to my present home town, and tell you of some of the happenings there that led to my coming to Mount Hope. For obvious reasons I must be very careful in my narrative of this particular period of my life. "You have seen my wife. Her character is one of the most beautiful it has been my privilege to ever know. Had she married the right man, a happy union would have been hers to share. But I was not that right man. Even though fully aware that our marriage was a mistake, my umost endeavor was to be more than kind to my wife. I was, I believe, not failing in duty as a husband, and for years I was absolutely true in every sense of the word. Yes, faithful and true, until one day there came into my life a woman, another woman, but even then as today's world views faithlessness I was not untrue. But a girl-a woman-did enter my life and with that entering came a change in the order of my living. I rather doubt whether I should tell you this; it may seem like abusing the confidence of the girl, but I have said I would tell my story, and as nothing can matter very much now you shall hear of why I am here. "My first impression of the girl was that she was not particularly attractive, and was overly self possessed. 

In the beginning my feeling for her was one solely of pity. Until I met her, she and happiness had never lived on the same street. After a time she gradually grew confidential; she told me of some of her troubles, thereby enlisting the sympathetic part of my nature. 

I was truly sorry for her-genuinely sorry, that is all; or, at least, all then. With sympathy awakened, I did more or less in various ways to bring some brightness into a life seemingly so dull and so absolutely sheared of cheer. Home conditions seemed uppermost in the girl's thoughts, conditions such as to make home for

OPPOSITES 

481 her a place intolerable, not so much because of deeds of unkindness, but rather for want of loving acts and words. She craved for the affection due from parents to those for whose existence they are responsible. 

Yes, this girl was sad in that she yearned in vain for the want of a mother's and a father's affection. She laid considerable stress on the unhappy relations with her father, which she claimed were most difficult to endure. Not only was he lacking in paternal love, but was most times severely cold. "As I entered my office one day I found this girl weeping and more sad and depressed than ever I had known her to be." "Oh, then I take it she was an employe of yours?" "Yes, my secretary. I somehow believed that you were aware of that. However, as I was saying, she was more than usually distressed that day. Between sobs she told that the breaking point had come; that her heart was broken; said that other girls had fathers they could and did address in the closely affectionate terms of 'Daddy'-fathers who were glad to kiss and caress them, and that it was all wrong-unjust-unfair -that, while she had parents, she was without a daddy. Try as I did to comfort her, she was inconsolable. The outcome of it all was that I agreed to be a daddy to her, a father of a sort. Let me make myself plain. I had no other thought than to advise her, help her and supply the place of parent in a fatherly way just as far as I could so do without actually being her parent. I swear to you my motives were pure and good. I had already lost sight of the fact that she was homely and that she did not dress as well as many other girls. I could not-or rather did not-see her unattractiveness. I enjoyed conversing with her, and in spite of her almost chronic despondency I was glad when my office held her presence. But during all of

OPPOSITES 

this I can truthfully say that I neglected no duty at home. "One night-how I have many times cursed that night, and then in turn with equal fervency have blessed it-she sat on my lap. I'm truthful when I tell you that when I took her on my knees I believed it was only as a parent would so do. I was quite a bit her senior in age, you'll understand. I swear that my intentions were good-my motives pure-but somehow on that fateful night before we parted our lips in kisses met, and in that moment there was awakened in my understanding something previously unknown. For days I did not recognize this new found, sweetly bitter, pleasure that was mine. All that was mine to know was that I was superlatively happy when in her presence and strangely depressed when she was gone. "Following the night of that fateful event, there came the passing of not many days when on the tongues of tale bearers and gossip mongers came to my ears disturbing stories, cowardly attacks, assailing the girl's character. In the repulsing of these assassins of good names and fair reputations, I lost friendships more or less worth while. In defiance of the scandal slingers, I retained my secretary, and our very friendly relations continued. In my home, while on the surface conditions appeared unchanged, yet, while in the presence of my wife, I felt myself a cheat, an embezzler, as one unfaithful to a trust, and in those days the light of my secret soul went out, and I was as one enveloped in the murky darkness of a heavy darksome cloud. "Time after time I battled with myself. I fought against the influence, or call it what you will, that was slowly but just as surely weighting me down to the level of self contempt. With my baser part I struggled, and then in the end would resign myself to drifting along with the tide, resigned to follow the course of my strange infatuation, let it carry me where it might.

OPPOSITES 

483 "Then, one night, in contemplation as to how it would all end, I was made bold in the cause of right, fairness and decency, and regardless of my feeling for the girl I determined to terminate the whole unwholesome affair. In desperation for a motive, I strove to make myself believe in the truth of the unsavoury stories told me of my secretary. Firmly did I resolve that the following day must close and unresumably terminate relations of every sort between that girl and me. But with the coming of the morrow and in her presence my good intentions faltered, my resolution was as though never made, and my strong determination of the previous night had become as the spine. of a gummy jelly fish. "Nothing of importance happened, at least, nothing that I now recall, until one day my secretary informed me she was leaving my employ. She freely made known the deepness of her feeling for me, and stated that it was because of that caring she was going away. She did not indicate where she intended going, but did make plain that once she departed it was her desire that our parting should be permanent. She claimed it was all for my sake. I freely admit it seemed hard, but made myself satisfied that it was best for us both that all our mutual relations, business and otherwise, should cease, and so it was I lost the best private secretary I ever had. "After her departure the general routine of my home continued unaltered; but, while I continued to perform my domestic duties as I believed correct, somehow my wife seemed changed. She did not actually find fault with me, for she was never a fault finder, and she never nagged, but one night she mentioned that I seemed to be losing interest in her. As a result of said remark, I redoubled my efforts to please her, in spite of which she seemed each day to grow more changed and very sad. She was never angry; she was never

OPPOSITES 

known to lose her temper. But, regardless of my efforts, the household held a changed atmosphere. Because of my endeavors to reach a solution to the problem that confronted me, my business suffered. I knew in my heart that I loved the girl who had gone away, but just as firmly was I convinced that considering a divorce was out of the question. I had no grounds on which I could obtain one. I lacked the courage to unbosom my secret to my wife, and even though to have done so should have persuaded her it would be advisable that we dissolve our matrimonial state, how could 

I know that the young girl would marry me? I tried earnestly, but unsucessfully, to dismiss her from my thoughts. I endeavored more than ever to be kind to my wife; I think I overdid it. Nothing seemed natural. 

Now, don't think I am trying to alibi myself, but I do think, had it not been that all through our married days there had been only two of us, just the two of us, that a condition might have been created that would have forestalled and prevented the deplorable unhappiness that entered and prevailed in our home. "One day my wife came to my office. I could not just understand her that day. She seemed to be so changed, so unlike herself. I never had seen her in such a mood. After talking about nothing in particular, but still voluntarily or otherwise making it very evident that there was a troublesome something on her mind, she asked when my secretary would return. When in reply I told her I did not believe she would ever return, she seemed incredulous, and in a very odd sort of way said: 'Aren't you sorry? She was such a satisfactory secretary.' "Determined to bring the matter to a head at once, I replied: 'My dear, what is on your mind? You have come here today for some specific purpose. Tell me what it is.' 'Oh, nothing at all. A wife can come to her husband's office in a wifely sort of a way, can she not?'

OPPOSITES 

485 'Quite true,' I replied, 'but there is something disturbing you, something that you are turning over in your mind and from which you wish relief, and yet for some reason you do not seem to know just how to seek the remedy.' Without reply she walked toward a window, looked down on the street, and then in a forced, offhand manner remarked, 'Whom do you think I saw today?' I replied: 'I do not know. I imagine you saw a number of persons.' 'Yes, I did, but there was one person I saw for the first time in a long, long while.' 'Can't you tell me who it was?' 'Yes, I can, but I am asking you to guess first.' I named a number of persons 

I thought it might have been. She said: 'I do not believe you can ever guess. Don't you remember the young man with whom I ceased corresponding on your account?' 'Yes,' I replied, 'I do. Where did you see him?' She told me she had been strolling out through our municipal park and had seen him there. 'Were you talking with him?' I asked. 'No, I was not quite sure at first it was he,' she replied, 'but walking off quite a distance, I watched him until he arose from a bench which he had been occupying. I then followed him to a house on Mulberry Street. I know his walk; it was he.' 'Why did you not invite him to dinner tonight?' I asked. My wife seized the opportunity to make her departure. After she had gone, I had the office boy usher in my old friend. I was glad to see him, and did my best to make it evident to him. 'Just as handsome as ever,' he said to me. 'You must be enjoying good health.' Then walking to a side of the office which permitted the rays of the sun to pass through the window full upon my face, he supplemented: 'Yes, but while physically you are great, my boy, there is something on your mind that is wearing and tearing and ripping you asunder.' 'I am a very busy man, Mr. Choose. Business cares insist on taking their toll, you know,' I said. "He informed me he had been quite successful with a mine in New Mexico and had come East to buy some new pumps. Following a discussion regarding his mines, he continued: 'I don't suppose you ever went in for clairvoyancy?' 'No,' I replied, 'but I have often thought of it because of your connection with it.' 'You, no doubt, recall your vision of the wreck of the Valencia?' 'Never can forget it.' 'I suppose you read just how the test proved true, did you not?' OPPOSITES 

487 'I doubt that you yourself have followed the subject as closely as you once did. Now have you?' He came back with the remark: 'Well, I am not as active in any respect as I once was. I am getting older, you know.

But I have not abandoned the subject entirely. I keep on playing with it once in a while.' 'How would you like to make some sort of a test right now?' 'Bully,' he said. 'What have you got around here that I could work on?' "As I turned my eyes I saw upon the floor the photograph of a man. It was one I had seen at home on several occasions; as a matter of fact, it was of the man about whom my wife had been speaking while in my office that morning. Doubtless, she had dropped it on the office floor. Choose, also seeing the picture and sitting closer to it than I, reached down and took it in his hand. 'Will we be undisturbed here for a while?' he said. "That can be easily arranged.' I summoned the office boy and instructed him to allow no one to disturb us for at least an hour. 'Come, sit here,' said Choose, 'and swing your chair around so that your back is to the window. Now, take this picture in your hand.' 

Performing those same manipulations that I still remember so distinctly, he after a time asked me what I saw. I was largely under his influence, but that which was presented to my vision I preferred not to reveal to him, and made no reply, in consequence of which evasion, he said: 'Something wrong with you, my boy,' and immediately terminated the test. 'Let me have that picture,' he commanded. 'Give me your comfortable chair. I have been able to place myself in a clairvoyant condition on previous occasions, but if you will follow instructions you can be of assistance to me.' "Following his instructions, I observed after a time a change in his facial expression and, doubt if you will,

his eye balls. The pupils appeared like lenses focused on a far away scene, of which I saw sufficient to know that the clairvoyant gaze of my friend Choose was firmly fixed on the resort of Mount Hope. I see appear to be hotels. I see a man and now I see a woman. The woman is unsuccessfully searching for the man. There are influences keeping them apart. They are not human influences, rather forces controlled by nature's laws.' "At that interesting juncture the telephone bell rang, and I was obliged to answer it. It was a rather lengthy conversation. When I hung up my receiver Choose was no longer in a clairvoyant condition. 'I am sorry we were interrupted,' I said. 'I had a telephone call. I had given instructions that we should not be disturbed, but this was a matter that demanded immediate attention and my chief clerk used his good judgment in disregarding my orders. It seems she has a vision of the lady who was in your office this morning. The rider has lost the reins.' At that moment something in the picture was visible to me. I recognized the woman on the frenzied horse. That was all I saw, for Choose just then resumed his normal state. I was positive that there was something else he saw, but he refused to admit it. "Following dinner, Choose was almost immediately required to take a train in order to keep an appointment made for early morning of the following day. I accompanied him to the railroad station. Following his departure I sought opportunity to think and to think alone, for which solitary meditation I returned to my office and there mentally rehearsed the happenings of the day. It was quite plain to me that my wife and I had come to a parting of the ways; possibly not the sort of parting visible to the eyes of the world, but certainly as viewed by the two persons most concerned; for as to her part she seemed firmly and unalterably of the belief that she no longer held her husband's love, and I in turn was well convinced that an image of the man whose photograph she had dropped was indelibly fixed upon her heart. Just the truth of a part of this would have been sufficient cause for considerable perturbation of mind; but, even if all of it and more were so, it would be very minor as viewed in the light of the danger threatening my former secretary, for she was the girl on the frightened horse, as revealed in the clairvoyant picture. “Arriving home that night I was surprised to learn that my wife had not yet returned. I retired immediately, but my desire for slumber was not satisfied.

Weary in body and troubled in mind, tossing and turning, I missed touching no part of the entire bed. 

Finally, despairing of early sleep, I arose and dressed, intending to walk out in the open or try to read. Ascertaining that my wife was still absent, I was convinced that Choose's picture was right, and that she had gone in search of the other man, in which case no effort

OPPOSITES 

491 would be made on my part to circumvent her. If 

Choose's test were true, even at that moment she was on her way to Mount Hope, to which place on the instant I determined to go, not with the thought of following my missing wife, but to obey the irresistible urge to hasten to the girl who needed me. Whether the runaway horse was a thing of the past, whether coincident with the vision of Choose's clairvoyant eye, or whether it was a picture of a future fatality, I felt I was needed in Mount Hope. If not already a consummated casualty, I must, if possible, prevent its happening, or, if too late for that, I must render to the girl any needed help or assistance. I went to my garage; my wife's car was missing. Returning again to the house I packed a small trunk and a bag, carried them out to my car and placed them therein. Leaving a note on the dining room table to the effect that I had gone away to be absent for several days, I was soon burning the road in the direction of Mount Hope. "With moonlight all the way and encountering little traffic, I was able to accomplish rapid progress. Consulting my watch while driving I observed that if I should reach Waldensburg within the next half hour, I might be able to leave my car there and catch the early morning train for Mount Hope. Being successful in this, I aroused the night man at the garage; told him 

I might not return for the car for some time; left sufficient money to pay storage for quite a while, and was just able to dash across the street to the railroad station in time to board a train, which enabled me before the hour of noon to inhale the exhilarating ozone of 

Mount Hope. "Eating a hurried restaurant luncheon, which was also my breakfast, I proceeded in quest of my former secretary. During the afternoon I walked quite a distance along the mountain road, but saw nothing of the girl I was seeking. About to go back to the railroad

OPPOSITES 

station to have my trunk and bag sent up to a small boarding house where I had stopped on a previous visit to Mount Hope, the worst storm I ever have experienced broke upon me. Except when there came lightning flashes, eyes were of little value. Fearing that I might emerge second best in an encounter with an automobile, I crouched by a wall along the side of the mountain, when to my startled vision by a sudden flood of light there was revealed the man of whose picture I have told you, and before I could collect my scattered wits it seemed he leaped full upon me from the top of the wall, following which I remember nothing of the happenings of that day or any other day until very recently. "In the course of time I recovered sufficiently to be brought to a room in Kenneth Inn. Soon after that I met you. Until today I had not seen my wife since her last visit to my office, nor had I seen the girl since she left my employ. I think you know the rest." "I believe I do. I think a good purpose will be served in your having been so open with me. I want to see you again; in fact, I am inclined to believe I am going to see you quite frequently."

Nothing of further moment was said and the elderly appearing man left, leaving with the other his best wishes for a speedy recovery.

CHAPTER LXX 

That night as dusk was closing in and upon the village of Mount Hope, and as the sun was taking its last lingering glimpse of the mountain top, our elderly appearing friend stood on the porch of Miss Arnold's home. Evidently the latter had witnessed his approach, for she stood awaiting his coming. "Shall we sit on the porch?" she asked, "or would you prefer to go inside. I often sit here," she continued, "at sunset time."

Gathering that she preferred to remain on the porch, he replied, "I believe it is well for most people to spend as much time as possible in the open air." 

"I am of the same opinion, but then, too, care must also be exercised, so I'll return to the house for a wrap. 

I imagine you prefer not to be relieved of your coat.' 

Having returned to the porch, she said, "I am glad you came; I have so few visitors, although it is from preference that I don't have more callers." "In due consideration of which I appreciate the favor you show me." "I do not know just why," she said, "but I have such unusual confidence in you; then too, of course, you performed a great service for me." "As a matter of fact, I did you no great service, Miss Arnold." "I think you did. I know you did.” "No, the pearls I brought you were not really the pearls that were missing-your 'sister pearls'." "Well, you believed that they were, and after all when we judge service that which counts most is not what one really does but what one really intends and 493

OPPOSITES 

tries to the full limit to do. Some things are accomplished by one accidentally and sometimes by one with little effort. Sometimes one puts one's whole mind, one's whole body, and one's very blood into an effort to accomplish a purpose, and then fails. I prefer the latter one. Your intentions were to return to me my 'sister pearls'. From what I have gathered you went to extreme measures to get them. You thought and I thought too, at first, that you had been successful in returning the genuine pearls.' 

"Well, be that as it may, Miss Arnold. You have your pearls, and that is all in which we are really interested, after all." "Is there anything you can tell me regarding the mystery of this whole matter?” "It is no longer a mystery," he said. "It was the scheme of three men, all of whom are dead." "I was much concerned about Miss Wonder. I fear she is inclined to believe I had a suspicion that she was implicated in the loss of my pearls." "Why do you say that?" "She has not been to see me and I have not heard from her since the tragedy of the shack." "I think I can satisfactorily explain that, Miss Arnold. In the first place, I have every reason to believe that she as much as anyone else wanted you to have your sister pearls'. That you have not heard from her I think is because she has been married and gone away." 99 "Why did she not let me know of her wedding?" "Hers is a most extraordinarily peculiar case. She married the little fellow who had the information office and who conducted some sort of religious services in the place he called his chapel. It seems that they knew each other quite intimately long ago and had been separated for some years. There are many circumstances connected with the case that cannot help but

OPPOSITES 

495 convince the most incredulous that theirs is true love on the part of both.” "It must have been true love; she was a most beautiful woman, while he was not the least bit attractive in any way. How can we account for such an attachment ?" "It simply bears out a theory that some of us have, that there is really a Jack for every Jill; in other words, for every person, or at least for every person one hundred per cent normal, there is a person of the opposite sex intended by nature to be the mate of that particular person. 

"That is an odd theory." "Nevertheless, I with others believe it to be the only sound theory for the mating of humans. I am quite convinced it is flawless. Only such marriages can be the completely happy ones." "What of the people who never marry?" "Oh, we have more than one answer to that," he explained. "Sometimes through death one may lose one's opposite before the two meet. Sometimes parental influence brings about a mismated union before the two opposites of nature have formed a contact. That is a very unhappy union, more especially so if one meets one's perfect opposite after being married to one who is the choice of parents or of someone other than the ones most vitally interested." "Then it it your belief that there are never any really truly happy marriages unless the opposites, as you term them, marry?" "I do not go that far. There are marriages that are more or less happy where the two united are not the ones intended by nature to be joined in matrimony, but their happiness is not a complete happiness. There may not be discord, and there may actually be a contented happiness, but both are losing the full happiness that nature intended them to enjoy.'

OPPOSITES 

"I am interested in this subject," came softly and subdued. "May I make bold to ask just why?" "You may. Again permit me to say for some reason 

I have every confidence in you. You are one of the few persons with whom I converse that I do so because 

I enjoy so doing." "You know very little of me." "True, but somehow you instil confidence. You create a feeling that what one tells will be held sacred and kept secret. You are a man whose opinions appeal to me because I believe they have been well and soundly reasoned before a decision has been reached. 

This theory of yours regarding the opposites is a new one to me, and yet unknowingly I think I have always believed in it.". "I hope you won't think me presuming, Miss Arnold. If I am, you must pardon me. I take it you have never married. Is it too much for me to inquire why?" "No. I was hoping you would ask me that. Our conversation seemed to lead to it, and somehow I wanted to tell you. You carry me on and on. All that you say on any subject interests me, and I feel quite sure if I tell you my story you may say something that will be of a satisfying nature to me.' 

"I have every reason to hope that it may be so." 

He waited for a reply.

For a brief spell of silence she seemed to be preparing herself to tell something that undoubtedly would be the first time that anyone had been favored with its narration. Apparently she was searching for a beginning. The first sentence was so difficult to frame, and her initial words were undoubtedly a surprise to the man. It can hardly be contradicted they were a surprise to herself. "I have known love," she said, "and for very good reasons known only to myself I have never married.

OPPOSITES 

497 Those two things convince me that your theory is entirely correct. I might have married. More than one man gave me reason to believe by actions and words he craved me for his wife. Among them were men who have since married, and I believe happily so. Some of them were wealthy men. All of them were educated men; none of them but what had reputations above reproach. But I had loved before I met any of them. While I was very fond of some of these men and respected all of them, I could not marry any of them. There was something that told me it would not be just the right thing to do. You would like to ask me why I never married the one that I loved." "I hesitate to ask it, but I should like to know.' "I am going to tell you. I met a boy one time; I met him in my school-time years. I did not then know 

I loved him, but somehow I took pride in his school work accomplishments. There was rivalry among several boys in my class as to who should lead in studies. It seemed strange to me, but I was always glad when this particular boy was the winner. I thought at the time it was because he was not as well supplied with worldly goods as some of the other boys. 

In everything he was always fair; he saw the good in others. When not at school, he was always employed in some capacity. He was liked by the entire class and seemed to be popular in the neighborhood where he lived. I have not seen him for a number of years. I really believe there was a time when he was interested in me, but for some reason that interest either ceased or was held in restraint. It was not until after I had lost touch with him that I realized that I had been caring for him all the time. I had a suspicion as to why he voluntarily ceased to keep in touch with me. There is no good purpose to be served in mentioning that. 

Whether I am right or wrong, I have always believed in being faithful to a trust and to a cause. After a

OPPOSITES 

time, and having thoroughly considered the matter from all angles, I was convinced that I loved the boy, and that had he pressed his suit he would have been my husband. I believed it to be my duty to be faithful to the result of my reasoning. And that is why I never married. I suppose now I never shall. "My parents left me with an abundance of means to obtain worldly necessities as well as luxuries. I have tried to be charitable and assist worthy movements. 

Sometimes I feel that I have fallen short. One should have something to occupy one's mind and attention when one has wealth at one's disposal. Wealth is a dangerous thing to possess. Sometimes it leads one into a negative existence. In that case the possessor of the wealth simply drifts along in idle uselessness.

Idleness when supported by wealth is almost a calamity. When the mind is not occupied with planning commendable things, it becomes soft prey for the tempter. 

Wealth, luxury, idleness, invariably conspire toward disaster. I found it, or I deemed it, necessary that I should do something to occupy my mind, and having some knowledge of precious stones and jewelry, as many of them had been lavished upon me by my parents, and being of a nature to investigate and observe rather than to simply take for granted, I had become by study, investigation and search, quite an expert along the line of precious jewels and curios. When 

I was not endeavoring in some way to help some widow or orphans or to do something to alleviate the sufferings of unfortunates, I devoted my time to searching for curious things. I gained possession of quite a number of perfect jewels. I was years in gathering my collection. I had determined that when the collection of my desire was really completed that I should not renew my search for similar things. I had concluded that the one I loved, even if he had not forgotten me, certainly did not care for me, at least, not sufficiently

OPPOSITES 

499 to come to me, so the aim of my remaining life was to redouble my efforts to alleviate suffering. That is my story." "And now that you have your pearls in your possession, is it still your intention to confine yourself to charitable works?" "Yes." "Should the man you love be successful in finding you, would it be too late?" "No. While life lasts that will never be too late." "Will you pardon me, Miss Arnold? I have a telephone call I should like to make."

She directed him to a private booth inside the house. "When you have finished I think you can find your way back to the porch. I'll await you there."

He entered the telephone booth. He made a brief call, but that was not all he did. However, little time was consumed before his return to the porch, but in the meantime, however, it had grown quite dark. "Did you find your party?" she asked. "Yes, I did. As a result of my call I find I can stay but a short while longer. If you will come inside there is something I am desirous of saying to you.' 99 "There is no one here within the hearing of our voices." "No, but what I have to say cannot well be said here." "I do not understand your reasoning, but I have confidence that you know what is best."

She arose and entered the reception hall. He followed close in her wake. "Shall we sit here or in the library?" she asked. "If you have no objection, let us go to the library."

She had not faced him since she left the porch. He had taken care that she did not, but as he uttered those last words there was something in the tone of his voice that caused her to turn in speechless amazement. A

OPPOSITES 

person less self-possessed might have done any or all of a number of things, as she beheld him with a smile on his lips, waiting expectantly for her to speak. 

Following an eternity of silence, in tremulous tones, she said: "I do not understand. I cannot understand. 

How did you come here, and is it really you? And where is but was it you all the time?" "I am just who I am, Estelle."

Then overflowing her mind came the remembrance of her words regarding the eternal love of her heart.

Her flushed face evidenced her embarrassment. "I am sorry," he said. "I did not intend to play such a trick. It was not my desire that tonight you should know my identity, but when you related to me what you did I could not longer delay making myself known to you." "But think of what I told you! I have bared to you my innermost soul. It seems so unmaidenly." "But had you not done so, I could never have known." "Let us repair to the library," she said.

Arriving there, he drew two chairs close together. "You have told me what has given me the greatest happiness of all my life," he said. 517 that in Fred Davis's heart I was no longer his wife, in which event it was but natural I believe that I should turn toward another man. A woman is a proud sort of a being. She cannot bear scorn; she cannot bear the slight of a man; she cannot bear the humiliation of being thrust aside for another; so if she is so confronted, either her lot is to pine and fade and wither away, or else she seeks for vengeance. Oft times she retaliates by transference of affections to another man. And so it was with me, for I made my heart believe that fate had returned me the other man at the opportune time. When he did come back from the bathroom, where he remained for some little while, Mrs. Davis stood looking out of a window. Doubtless, her thoughts were of things far removed from Kenneth Inn. She was oblivious of the other's presence until in a gentle way he said: "Mary." 

Turning in amazed and startled confusion, she would have fallen, had she not clasped a chair. Then, as he hastened to her side and supported her swaying form, she gasped in strangled tones: “Tell me how and when did you come here? I have been wanting you so much. He said he would bring you. 

Where did he go?" Then nestling close she softly murmured: "So he was mistaken after all, but I hardly thought the fates would fail me." Probably my mother had a liking for me, possibly she considered she loved me, but the atmosphere of that household was bereft and bare of mutual family affection, and all the while I was longing for someone to love me. I am not entering into details, but there came a day, one of those bitter sweet days, that my eyes opened to the realization that I was in love with my employer. It came as one happy shock, and later upon me was borne the startling and surprising knowledge that in some way I had brought him to love me. The world would ever contend as well as believe that it was your treasure chest, not you, Miss Mahlon sought; likewise upon her brow in the eyes of the world would eternally rest the scarlet stain; and though in the years that are yet to come, happiness be yours and hers, even then, though the venomous tongue be stilled, yes even then would the hugger-muggers point, yes, even then would fingers accusingly point, but not towards you the man in the case, but towards her, the girl you know how it is-it's the woman that carries the scorn. 

You as a man, a wealthy man, the world would accept, but what would its lasting verdict be of her? Are you willing to let her sacrifice herself for that?" "You make it hard for me. I know my answer, if I had but myself to consider. I know what nature wants me to do, and yet—and yet—should I bring the woman that I love, even though she became my wife, out into the open, where all the scoffing world might hold her in contempt? It is indeed most hard to know just what my duty is. I am going to ask you to leave me now. 

You have done all that you could. I believe you are

OPPOSITES 

539 sincere and that your motives have been good, but I must fight this thing out alone, all by myself. I hope some day to meet you again, and I hope when I do I may know just who you are. 99 "I hope you may. Once I am entirely released from every obligation of secrecy to the United States Government, I shall be glad to let all who care to know me become acquainted with just who I am. There is only one person in the whole world that knows for a certainty the name I bore and received from my parents. That person is to be my wife. She knows and has a right to know. In the meantime, judge wisely and decide according to your conscience. Good-bye, Fred." Then as a sudden thought aside he turned: "May I ask one little thing about Doogle or Douglas before I go?" "Yes, go on. Just a diversion before I settle down to the struggle I have before me." "Only this, Fred. Can you reconcile Doogle's dialects?" "I think to a certain extent I can by telling of an experience of mine. Following my introduction into the mysteries of clairvoyancy, hypnotism and kindred things, Douglas invited me to his home one night to make some hypnotic tests. He had as a subject a man. named McGlathery, an illiterate old fellow, the father of an exceptionally bright son, who was closely in touch with local and state politics. Well, I conceived the idea of endeavoring to bring McGlathery to the hypnotic state, and by proper suggestion have him believe he was the Congressman of our district. I was more than successful, and was surprised in the fact that my subject not only portrayed the mannerisms of the Congressman, but delivered a masterpiece of political oratory, using the best of English; his words, his sentences, his rhetoric all being equal to the best efforts

OPPOSITES 

of the Congressman himself. That may in a way explain the changes in Doogle's language.' 

"Thank you, Fred, I must be on my way. God bless you. Good-bye." "Good-bye," the other said, then locked the door behind his departing visitor. 

Many times Fred Davis paced his room that night; at the table in the middle of the room he would sit, then would pace the floor again. Many times he chose a course to pursue and just as many times changed his mind. And then at the close of a long, long night, as the sun shed light on a new born morn, then and only then was it that this very, very tired man, quite wearied and worn had successfully struggled and reasoned his way to an unalterable conclusion. With features fixed, with jaws firm and set, with eyes looking straight ahead, with mind determined and with steady step, no power on earth could change him then. He approached one of the communicating doors, turned the thumb latch and likewise turned the key. That key he threw far out through a window of his room and watched it disappear 'neath the dark waters of the rapidly flowing river. Thus was his room made secure against intrusion from one adjoining room. Then he strode to the opposite door. He knocked, but to his knocking there came no response. Again he tapped-the same result.

He turned the knob of that second door, but the key in that lock was turned. He knocked again and called her name, and the door was opened wide; so he took her in his big, strong arms and kissed away her tears.

CHAPTER LXXI 

'Twas one springtime day, when two persons, a woman and a man, returned to the scenes of their childhood days. Things were much changed since last they had seen the place of their nativity. The streets, which in those yester years had seemed so wide to cross, with sidewalks stretching far apart had become as narrow lanes. Many buildings of those other days had been replaced by modern ones. Of the old structures there were still remaining some, many of which so imposing to their childhood eyes, were to their vision of that later day painfully void of significance and unworthy of even the turning of a passing head. And to those two some things there were while still extant to them were as things no more; yes, to their eyes matured by fleeting years, by the experiences of life and by their observing of other worlds were as though they never had been. And so it is that just like that life's passing happens so.

Those two had means with which to ride, but they chose to walk about the town. Many persons they saw, but none they recognized, and they were familiar to none. They went out to the old school, the place that in other days so formidable had seemed, the place that had been such a bugbear to them, especially on examination days; the place that on young minds had inflicted sore trials and tribulations, but the place which with it all had been the source of so much youthful pleasure and happiness. But how had the old school house become shriveled and shrunken! Hard indeed it was to realize that before them was the three story structure of brick that in those other days had from its 541

OPPOSITES 

lofty height looked down upon them with an air of dignity and a spirit of restraint, yet with much encouragement. But on that day, as those two gazed on the old high school, it seemed as though from the ground where they stood they might have touched the old roof with their hands without overmuch straining or reaching Time, indeed, had worked changes. But in the presence of that old brick school house, so strangely small and shriveled, there came not to them a spirit of superiority, for as standing there, those two, that matured woman and that matured man, with her hand in his, and with their heads reverently bowed they gave full evidence that all they saw was the quiet dignity of age, all they felt was solemn adoration, and all they knew was that it was there they had met, and so it was that from their hearts and out of their minds there emanated that day messages of deep and everlasting gratitude. Silent messages they were, but messages that in their silent eloquence gave humble expression of the deep respect that was theirs as reverently they stood in the hallowed presence of that old brick school house. 

Then they walked to the outskirts of the town and they saw where one time a sleigh had skimmed along the old turnpike, and they saw where one night that sleigh had stopped; where a young girl on the rear seat of that sleigh had invited a youth who was trudging on foot to sit at her side and ride in her sleigh, something they were both glad to remember.

Next, they went to a church which the girl in her childhood days had quite frequently attended. The same clergyman was still there, but like the rest of the town he had changed with the years. Once tall and strong and athletic; now aged and bent. He was expecting them. He gave them his blessing and he married that woman to that man.

Then soon they left the old town and motored away to the mountains. Arriving at their destination late in

OPPOSITES 

543 the afternoon and having registered at the only hotel for miles around, they drove their car to the peak of Mount Content, from which point of view they could in one direction still see deep into the distant valley, while in the opposite direction was open to their vision vast stretches of the boundless west. There, on the very mountain top, all alone, those two, each happy in the presence of the other, remained until suddenly made awake to the approach of eventide. In truth, down in the faintly visioned valley land, the sparkling and flickering village lights, piercing the spreading shadows of the mountain side, told them that down there it had already become night time. "That is the past," said the man. "That is what has gone before. It represents what is behind us. The same light can never shine twice on what is already done. The life of an act performed is but a part of memory.' 

Turning, they faced the west again, where to the end of the trail for that day's sun there was yet a span or two to run. Glorious, gorgeous the sight, for viewed from that mighty mountain height was revealed midst the splendor of a setting sun a cloudless, spotless skynot even a creamy, fleecy speck to float before the view. "That," said the man, "is our today and our tomorrow.' 

"Our today," said the woman, "has been a perfect day." "Before us are many perfect days," said the man. "Yes, our future will be one continuous perfect day."

About to step into the automobile, as of one mind they hesitated; then with eyes fixed on the glories of the wondrous sunset, they stood together there, hand in hand, of one thought and of one mind. 

Then said the woman: "May we turn to the past just once again?"

OPPOSITES 

"Yes, just once," the man replied. "Only once, for 

I want to forget the past." "You have told me a little of the happenings of those days in Mount Hope. Can't you tell me a little more?" asked the woman. "What would you know?" "I would know more of Miss Wonder. Somehow she is always in my mind. I was quite interested in her, and I believe she was rather fond of me. Yet she went away without a word. I have not seen her and I have not heard from her or of her since she paid that last visit to my mountain home." "I do not know just what you want to know. I am going to tell you something about her though which probably you should know. I was acquainted with Miss 

Wonder before I met you. She and I were kids together in the old town. While she was still a child her family moved away. Later she came back for a very brief spell, during which she and I were oft times together, but after a while it all came to an end.” "Was there anything serious between you?" "Not really serious. We were just very good friends." "And you did not love her?" "No, I did not. You know my theory regarding love. You know my firm belief in the 'Opposites'. 

There can be but one perfect opposite." "Yes, I should not have asked that question.” "I can say this: Miss Wonder's life helps materially in bearing out my belief in the 'Opposites'. I am going to pass over a big part of her life. There is much of it I do not know. But it so happened that she met some years ago the little old fellow who had the information office and chapel at Mount Hope. He was a considerable number of years older than your friend, Miss Wonder. It is needless for me to tell you that she was beautiful; you know that; it is not necessary for me

OPPOSITES 

545 to say to you that it is almost impossible to believe that he was a man that would in any way attract her, yet they were drawn together. Doubtless, they would have been married years ago, but a little of her past came to the ears of Douglas." "And he broke things off on that account?" "No, he did not. She went away.' "Then what?" 99 "Well, they came together again following and through the tragedy of the shack." "And he still wanted her?" "He still wanted her and she wanted him." "Was it not strange just how she happened to be on the scene at the time of the tragedy?" "Yes, it was rather odd." "She really must have loved him to have taken him, just one of those characters of a mountain resort." "Yes, and he really must have loved her, taking her as she was." 

She raised her eyebrows, but there was something in his face that told her he would have no explaining words to speak. 

What the man did say was: "This is almost proof positive that my belief in the opposites is right.' 99 "What further argument have you in favor of that pet theory of yours?" "This is a painful thought to me, and after I have once discussed it I am going to endeavor to bury it down in the cemetery of unpleasant memory. Pinky

Williams, a fellow I knew in college, was one of the finest specimens of manhood it was ever my privilege to meet; a handsome fellow-a real man among men; he was made out of a mold that was physically perfect; as fair and as square a chap as ever played a game. 

Whether it was on the gridiron, on a baseball diamond, or in the game of life, Pinky Williams fought hard, but he always fought an honest fight. He married, I

OPPOSITES 

I believe, against his better judgment. I do not care to cast adverse reflections on his wife; on the contrary, really believe she was just as good a woman in her heart as he was a man, but they were never intended for each other. When a good woman and a good man, each of them as nearly perfect as it is possible for humans to be, are married and the union is not a satisfactory one, then there is something aside from their personal selves that is at fault. There is something missing in the combination, and in my belief the wanting thing is that they are not nature's opposites. There may be a man in the world that was intended by nature as a husband for Pinky's wife, but there was a weakness in Mary Burlinson's makeup, a weakness that probably would destroy the possibility of absolute happiness for any pair of which she should be a part. The weakness is that she, like so many of today, seemed to believe that a home consists of just a woman and a man, some sticks of furniture, an automobile, some servants, and a house. There was someone else in the world intended for him. You may say it was simply an accident that brought him and that other someone together; I say it was nature and the forces of nature that contrived to make contact between Pinky and his secretary.' 99 "Did you ever learn the result of that affair?" "I never did; in fact, I left it all in his hands. I gave him my advice, the following of which I am sure was the only proper thing to do, even though his marriage was opposed to my own theory of the opposites; nevertheless, their unfortunate case bears out and establishes the reasonableness of what I have believed to be the only correct plan for mating and marrying. "Then, too, Estelle, your case and mine. I did not know I loved you when we were still in school. Had my mind been more mature, I should have been able to recognize just what my feelings towards you meant,

OPPOSITES 

547 but the ride in the sleigh was the one thing needed to awaken my dormant senses and to make me understand. I have loved you ever since. I never married because of my memory of you, and that memory dates back to even an earlier time than when I worked out the theory of opposites. I prefer not to discuss the other side of our case. Maybe you have something to say, my dear." "Ours is the one case that compels me to believe that your theory is right. There was a certain feeling 

I held for you in my girlhood days. I felt very kindly toward you. For a time I thought that was all it was, simply a feeling of kindness, but when I went away to college, which brought about a separation or rather a condition that precluded close association, that yearning to see you convinced me that my feeling for you was love. When you seemingly avoided me, which I interpreted as a desire on your part to sever all relations with me, I was at first a bit angry and then I was hurt. Had it not been for the love implanted in my heart, I should have tried to forget you. But love it must have been; deep, deep love, or you must have passed from my thoughts long ago. I never knew of my mother's remarks with reference to you until informed of same by you a few days ago. Sometimes I had a feeling that seemed more than a hope that we should again come together. No man, no woman, can wait and wait as long for each other as we have done, unless influenced by the prompting of love by nature sent, which after all is the only true love, unassailable and unending." "Then we are agreed on that?" "How could we be otherwise? Dear, I wonder if a dream I once had was just a dream, or whether your forces of the opposites were at work on the night of the strange dream of which I would tell you. Would you like to hear it?"

OPPOSITES 

"Yes, Estelle, I am always more than glad to have you tell me anything that it pleases you to tell." "Well, as I have said, it was a very strange dream.

It was while I was in Mount Hope. As I recall the vision, I was walking along the mountain road in 

Mount Hope; something I never did; I always rode. 

I saw a beautiful woman resembling Miss Wonder, a colored man who was not really a negro, and two rough looking fellows attacking two other men, one of whom bore a huge cross. Then there were three others, a handsome blonde man, a woman and quite a young girl, who while they offered no hurtful violence they assisted in forming a human circle to imprison the two men. To me it seemed that the man bearing the cross was you, and yet he did not resemble you aside from the matter of height. The second man subjected to the attacks of the others was a frail little fellow, might have been much like Doogle, as I picture him from your description. As the others overpowered you two, I attempted to hasten to your assistance, but stumbled and fell. You of the dream then attempted to aid me, but one of the others struck you a most cruel and wicked blow, following which the little old fellow put all the others to flight. That is all I recall, probably all that I dreamed." "And you dreamed that, Estelle? My dear girl, the forces were working that night. Your dream was almost identical with one of mine, except that in my dream the two rough looking fellows were missing.

At no distant date we must further discuss those two dreams, my dear. When we do, you are going to agree with me, I am confident, that my belief in the theory of the opposites is well founded." "Every belief of yours is well founded, my opposite husband, but now before we return to the hotel I want you to promise me that your special services for the government are forever ended.'

OPPOSITES 

549 "I promise you that, Estelle. As a result of my studies, I feel quite pleased that I was able to make myself useful, but in doing so I almost lost my identity.

Having assumed various characters and make-believes, I myself at times almost forgot just who I was. I remarked one time (I think it was to Pinky) that you were the only person who really knew my identity. I was mistaken in that."

He abruptly halted, then with an odd movement of his right hand he tapped his forehead with the forefinger thereof. Several times he repeated the tapping, then remarked: "I am just reminded that I promised to reveal my identity to Pinky. This I must do tomorrow."

Just to digress sufficiently to let it be known that the promise to Pinky was not an idle one, would state that but a few days later Fred Davis received a letter worded as follows: "My dear Pinky:- 

I am hereby making good the promise that you should some day be made acquainted with the identity of the old codger who deigned to address you as 'Pinky'. 

Well, here goes: Do you recall the long, lanky lad who did his best to hold down first base for the University of X when you pitched our team through a long and difficult schedule without a defeat or even a tie? I am he.

And further to elucidate: Through long, hard application to study I was successful in acquiring certain knowledge that enabled me to be more or less useful in secret service work of our Federal Government. I take it you recall the make-up man of our dramatic club, and being yourself an important member of our cast, well know that the lanky first baseman and the makeup man were one. That gift of make-up was most helpful in my efforts to serve Uncle Sam.

OPPOSITES 

Upon entering the secret service I chose at once to hide my real identity at all times. It was more safe. 

So I carried various names from time to time and changed my face and figure, and in the play of 'Passing Time' the stage of life has seen me in many parts. Yes, in many parts for I found it very much safer. 

Mrs. Davis recalls a man by the name of Dixon

Lenz. He met her in Mohntown. She had in her possession a photograph of him taken without his knowledge or consent. Score one against the secret service man, and one in favor of Mary Burlinson, later Mary 

Davis. You may tell Mary; not necessary to tell Dixon

Lenz. He alreadys knows. 'Tis he that confesses. 

Mrs. Davis later saw a man of the name of Laird, Owen Laird; she saw him in Mohntown Park one day; believed he was her old friend, Dixon Lenz; also believed that Owen Laird was not conscious of her presence, but in this she was mistaken, although I must admit she has a keen sense of perception, for Dixon 

Lenz was Owen Laird. Whereof I speak I know, for 

I was present in the park that day.

When Owen Laird departed from Mohntown, because of instructions from his venerable uncle, as well as having received a message from Mrs. Davis, signed, 'Mary,' he never returned to Mohntown and never will. He was lost on a railroad train between Mohntown and Mount Hope, but on the same train a full grown man was born who arriving at Kenneth Inn wrote on the hotel register: Stanton Ware. I can imitate that signature any time.

Then came the storm of the daytime night, in which Stanton Ware was washed up and washed out, and was for a spell without a name, except to the beautiful Miss Wonder, who knew a certain youth in his boyhood days, whose name she applied to the man who appropriated the contents of the room formerly occupied by Stanton Ware.

OPPOSITES 

551 

Later among the trees of Mount Hope, Pinky Fred 

Williams Davis was hailed one night by an elderly, stooping, presuming person, who had the effrontery to call Mr. Frederick Davis by the familiar handle of Pinky. 

And now, my dear old Pinky, I am just myself. Once again sign papers by the same name as the lanky first baseman. I am married to the owner of the 'Sister 

Pearls' the sweetheart of my school-time days-the one and only girl I ever loved, and I am feeling as young as when I played ball with you on the varsity team at X, Sir.

Hoping that the outcome of the decision you made following the various visits of the elderly man to rooms twenty-two and twenty-four of Kenneth Inn, as well as those of Dixon Lenz to those same numbered rooms and to room number twenty as well, has already demonstrated to you that your judgment was sound and your decision wise, and with every good wish, I remain, as ever, The Long Lanky Lad who tried to play first." 

Pardon the digression, dear reader. "As I was saying, Estelle dear, my perfectly opposite and oppositely perfect wife, I was mistaken in believing you the only person in possession of my real name. Dearie, I shudder when I think of how I almost lost you through losing myself, but as I was about to say, in the accident of the daytime night at Mount Hope my disguise was destroyed, and while Miss Wonder recognized me I doubt whether she associated me with the elderly man. But let us forget all of that."

There was a moment's pause; then he resumed speaking. "See," he said, "it is now quite dark in the valley. It is night there, but before us-in front of uslies the day with its light time. Past years are goneunrecallably gone, save as memories, some of them hideously nightmarish, some gloriously beautiful,

OPPOSITES 

nevertheless they are nought but memories. Among those memories are contained the days of our cruel separation. Let us try to forget, and turn our eyes from the deep, dark night of the unhappy past, so that we may gaze hopefully, faithfully, confidently into the luminous days of our future."

When they changed their vision from the shadowed valley land and once again cast their eyes to the west, the day's sun was closely approaching the beckoning horizon. "See, with our backs to the past we can see before us nothing but joy and light and happiness, and for every year of the past assigned us to endure, two years of the perfect future await us. And, Estelle, as I behold this day's sun seeking its rest, its well earned rest, I, too, see its wedding gift. 'Tis a beautiful painting of many shades, colored and tinted on the broad canvas of the western sky, and its title is 'A Promise,' and in this picture painted by nature for you and for me I interpret its promise. It tells me although the sun with its light now is passing and will leave us for a while, that just so surely as it has its setting, so too must it have its rising; it tells me that in the fullness of its luminous glory it will return to shine on your days and on my days our days. It gives me the promise of day time, cloudless, undimmed and rich in the glow of refulgent joy. It assures me that out of obedience to the laws of the Opposites shall come to us the reward of satisfaction, unqualified, and of happiness, unalloyed. "That is the promise, Estelle. That is nature's wedding gift sent from out of the skies. No night time; all day time. Light, happiness, joy." "Yes, Dear," she softly murmured, "your picture is a true picture. We have waited a long while for this, but the future cannot be other than just as you and the picture say."

Then they stood there, those two, that woman and

OPPOSITES 

553 that man; alone they stood hand in hand, and watched the sun go down, and while slowly the dark blanket of night was spread over the earth of that woman and that man, still happy were they in the thought that brief would be their time of waiting before this same sun must again appear to brighten and lighten the earth and the daughters and sons thereof; likewise joyous were they in the faith that in their souls there would continue a light, a light unceasing, unshaded and undimmed, a light that would unfailingly and forever illumine and make day time of the pathway that together she and he as one should travel until should come the day, that day of days, when those two, that woman and that man, still hand in hand, should stand in the sheen of that glorious and eternal light, that light known only in that Land where the sun never sets and where darkness and sadness are unknown. "And God said, 'Let there be light,' and there was light."
